# Changelog

## 2.0.0 — 2026-10-30

### Added
- A new core record, COR-046, rules that a decision record refers to a thing the way it means it — by the role something plays where the role is the point, by name where the name is the point. One question separates them: would this sentence become false if the name changed, with no decision changing? If yes, a role was meant and a name was written.
It protects the property that makes a record worth trusting: it stays true without being edited. A path or a function name is the fastest-moving part of any project, so a record citing one goes wrong on the first rename while the decision it records has not changed — and nobody notices, because nobody re-reads accepted records looking for drift.
This is self-executing on sync: records you author from now on are held to it, and the reviewer that audits records against the methodology's disciplines applies it. Existing records convert on contact rather than in a sweep, since turning a name into a role needs someone who knows which role the author meant.
The record-system specification gains the neighbouring rule it already owned, now stated plainly: editing in place means editing in place — a correction is folded into the body, not appended as an amendment section, a dated correction marker, or a "previously we believed" passage. Two in-body markers are named as correct and must not be stripped: the superseded-by line, and a forward `(refinement per <record>)` pointer to a later record that extends this one. Both point at another record rather than at a discarded belief.
The spec's Decision-section guidance also now says what the corpus actually does — a numbered list rather than `D1, D2, …` labels. Older records using the D-form are unaffected and their labels remain valid addresses. ([#794])
- The methodology now declares what it requires of the harness that hosts it, as observable behaviour in a new core schema pair shipping with its first entry, under a new accepted core record on harness requirements. ([#868])
- The friction change check of COR-050 ships as `pkit friction check [--base REF] [--json]`, the first command of a new `friction` group. It works from git alone and writes nothing: the working tree, uncommitted and untracked work included, against the merge-base of REF (default `origin/main`, or `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`), both sides discovered in their own declared places. Every artefact whose anchor changed — a path matched by a changed file, a record whose file changed, another artefact whose content changed, with the cascade stopping where a revalidation leaves content alone — must carry one of three answers in the same change: updated, unchanged with a new justification, or a deferral introduced for that anchor; a new artefact counts as revalidated, and a changed anchor list or a move needs a revalidation. It reports friction, a marker bump with nothing behind it, dead anchors of the change (an unresolved kind separately), an outdated base and front matter that does not parse, upstream first along artefact anchors, and `--json` emits a stable document (artefact, location, kind, anchor, answer, message per finding) for other components. `friction.mode` decides the exit: `warning` reports and passes; `enforcing` fails on friction, dead anchors, unresolved kinds and bumps, never on an outdated base; with no place declared or nothing carrying the container the check is dormant. Capability-registered anchor kinds are refused unless their resolver declares no network egress; none are registered yet. The record resolver and artefact discovery gain a listing seam so a commit's files are read without checking anything out.
- The first backbone file schema ships: `.pkit/schemas/backbone/container.schema.json` fixes the methodology's front-matter container (`pkit:`) for a document's front matter and for a collection entry, with the `friction` block modelled strictly (COR-050, COR-053 point 10, ADR-056). `pkit schemas validate` now load-checks every schema under `.pkit/schemas/backbone/` — a malformed one is reported against its own path — and counts them in its summary. A new `project_kit.backbone_schemas` module loads a backbone schema by kind from the project tree, applies the container's discrimination rule (functionality blocks by name, role blocks by their versioned point blocks, anything else an unknown-key error naming the nearest known key, role blocks without an active provider reported as orphans) and carries the one unknown-key renderer the class shares. Nothing user-invocable changes yet; the front-matter reader and `pkit validate` pass follow with their Tasks.
- The whole-repository friction check of COR-050 ships as `pkit friction check --all [--json]`. Every artefact in the declared places at HEAD is checked against the current history. Its revalidation point — the last commit, renames followed, in which the parsed `at` changed, or the commit that introduced its block — and each deferral's point — the commit that first introduced the entry, rewording aside — are derived from git, per file and no further back than the oldest point in question, from one log matched in memory. An anchor is stale when a commit after the point touched it beyond what a deferral covers, and a move after the point with no revalidation is stale too. It reports stale and deferred debt with their origins (author, date, change), every dead anchor and unresolved kind, over-broad anchors (a path matching more than half of the tracked files) and the two measures — unanchored artefacts and uncovered surface, excluded paths ignored — upstream first, and never fails: exit 0 in either mode. A shallow clone whose history stops before a point reports the artefact as unreachable rather than guessing. `--json` emits a stable document in the change check's style, with each finding's origin, each artefact's points and state, and the measures. Dormant while no place is declared.
- The lifecycle's ownership module answers a third question beside write authority and tier: `is_synced_copy(target_root, path)` — does this path arrive as a copy a sync makes from the methodology's source? It is sync-managed content in a repository that is not the methodology's own source (`is_methodology_source`, the same two markers the entry-point router uses), so it keys on origin and on the repository, never on the path. Documentation places ask it: a synced copy is never a place, while the methodology's source repository, where nothing is copied, may declare its `.pkit/` READMEs.
- `pkit status` shows every recorded documentation location, not only those outside the internal root: an `inside root` list comes before the `outside root` one (COR-049 point 7). A new `Friction` section shows the friction mode with its source and each declared place, surface entry and excluded path — the project's in written order, then each capability's, resolved under the internal root and tagged with the capability — and says so when no place is declared (COR-050 point 14).
- The backbone configuration file (`.pkit/project/config.yaml`) now has a schema and `pkit validate` checks it (COR-048 point 4, ADR-056). The second backbone file schema, `.pkit/schemas/backbone/config.schema.json`, fixes the keys the core records own — `name`, `docs` (the `user` and `internal` roots, COR-049), `friction` (`mode`, `status-job`, `places`, `surface`, `exclude`, COR-050) and `connections` (`providers`, `selections`, COR-053 and COR-052) — plus the reserved `project` block, which is accepted as any mapping. An unknown key at any backbone-owned level is refused with the nearest known key suggested; a wrong type or an invalid enum value is refused; the file carries no version key. `pkit validate` gains a configuration pass reported under a "configuration" heading: schema findings, then repository checks — a documentation root must resolve inside the repository and outside `.pkit/` (an absent root warns), a friction pattern must stay inside the repository (one matching nothing warns), and each connection entry must name an installed capability (whether it provides the role is reported as unverifiable until package metadata declares connections). Only errors fail the command; an absent or empty file means defaults apply. A configuration file created by the backbone now opens with the editor directive pointing at the shipped schema. Documented in the CLI reference's new "Configuration file" section.
- `pkit validate` now runs a `friction` pass (COR-050 point 12). It discovers artefacts only in the declared places — the `friction.places` of `.pkit/project/config.yaml` and, for each installed capability, the string-shaped `friction.places` of its `package.yaml` under the internal documentation root (object-shaped capability places follow in #1025) — reading a Markdown document's front matter or each keyed entry of a collection file, and never front matter elsewhere or a plain YAML file, and never a file that resolves outside the repository. It fails validation on front matter in a place that does not parse, a malformed `friction` block (the container schema's errors, surfaced against the artefact), a dangling deferral naming no anchor of the artefact, and a cycle between artefacts through `anchors.artefact` (the cycle's path is shown); an invalid `friction.mode` and a settings path outside the repository are the configuration pass's findings, which now also follow links. With no places declared, or no artefact carrying the container and no unparsable file, the pass prints only its counts. The discovery model lives in `project_kit.friction_discovery` and the findings in `project_kit.friction_validate`; the friction checks themselves (the change check and the whole-repository check) arrive with their own Tasks.
- The package-metadata schema ships: `.pkit/schemas/backbone/package.schema.json` type-checks every field the shipped `package.yaml` files use — both `schema_version` values 1 and 2 are accepted, nothing is bumped — and the blocks the newer records add: `connections` (roles, `extension-points {accepts, offers}`, `extensions {contributes, subscribes, depends-on}` with `depends-on` marked generated, per COR-053 point 3), `docs.locations` (COR-049 point 4) and `friction {places, surface}` (COR-050 points 1 and 8). The schema is permissive on unknown keys: a new `project_kit.package_validate` module reports an unknown key as a WARNING naming the nearest known key, through the shared renderer of ADR-056, and adds the repository checks — a declared point sits under a provided role, an accepted data point's companion schema exists under the capability's `schemas/`, every filler, emitter and subscriber command exists in `commands:`, command scripts exist, documentation locations and friction places are relative sub-paths. `pkit validate` gains a "packages" pass over every registered component's package file; errors fail it, warnings only print. The install-time self-consistency check of an incubated capability now runs the same validator (its previous messages kept, each prefixed by its location in the file — a missing version reports the schema's required-property error when the schema is present —, its refusals widened by the new checks). Refusing unknown keys is Task #999; the checks that need the wiring resolver are Task #983 and have named hooks.
- Two reading commands join the `friction` command group, both views of the whole-repository check that write nothing. `pkit friction debt [--json]` lists the stale and deferred debt oldest first, each with its kind, artefact, anchor and origin — commit, author, date and change — and a deferral's reason; its entries are exactly the stale and deferred findings `pkit friction check --all` reports, and artefacts a shallow clone cannot judge are named apart. `pkit friction explain <artefact> [--json]` shows one artefact's anchors, its revalidation and deferral points, what changed since each — every changed anchor with the commits behind it — and, for each finding, what clears it: the exact `pkit friction revalidate … --outcome …` or `pkit friction defer … --anchor … --reason …` command, or the edit it needs where no writer answers it. The artefact is named as the writers name one, by location or id, and looked up at HEAD.
- A rule that fills an inherited extension point anchors to the rule it fills (COR-051 point 7): `pkit validate`'s `rule-sets` pass fails a fill whose rule carries no `artefact` anchor naming that rule — bare, or as it is cited (`living-docs:RS-LDOC-003`) — so a change to the inherited rule flags the fill as friction (`unanchored-fill`, naming the anchor to add). An orphaned fill is still only reported, and asks for no anchor. A rule's cited source takes the verdict the friction checks give an anchor of its kind, from the one anchor-kind registry (COR-051 point 5): unregistered, refused without the query contract, or failing closed until registered resolvers run — reported as an unresolved kind, as before, with the registry's reason. `path`, `record` and `artefact` are reported as anchor kinds, never source kinds. `pkit status` gains a `Rule sets` section: the rule sets and rules found, how many inheritance pins were checked, and each pin whose inherited set has moved to another major — beside the `versions` failure `pkit validate` already reports on it — with its fix, the edit to `inherits` that re-pins it; the validate message names the same edit.
- The documentation roots work as COR-049 decides. `docs.user` and `docs.internal` resolve from `.pkit/project/config.yaml` (both default to `docs/`, each reported with its source), and every location the methodology has to choose derives from the internal root by precedence explicit > derived > conventional default: `pkit agents reconcile` and `adopt` now resolve the conventional `architecture-docs` / `adr-records` directories from the root (`<internal>/architecture`, `<internal>/architecture/decisions` — unchanged under the default root) and record the chosen location as an explicit overlay value on first use, so a later root change moves nothing already chosen. A capability's sub-paths are read from its package metadata (`docs.locations`) and its chosen locations are recorded in `.pkit/capabilities/<name>/project/docs-locations.yaml`. `pkit status` gains a `Documentation` section listing both roots and every recorded location outside the internal root. One consent-gated primitive now serves every writer of the configuration file — it validates the result against the schema before writing, keeps the file's header, and refuses a non-interactive run without `--yes`, naming the command to run — and a new `pkit config set <key> <value> [--yes]` command uses it for backbone-owned scalar keys. No migration: an absent key means the defaults and existing overlay values stay authoritative.
- The connection-points wiring is resolved and validated (COR-053 point 7). One resolver, `project_kit.connections`, computes from package metadata and the configuration's `connections` selections alone — no filler command runs, no process definition is parsed — which capability is the active provider of each qualified role, the points each active role defines, and every contribution, subscription and `depends-on` entry against its point (compatible when the version integers are equal, inert otherwise); the graph, the status report and the install / uninstall plans are to read the same `Wiring`. `pkit validate` reports it under a `connections` heading and fails on a role provided twice with no `connections.providers` selection, an unmet mandatory mark (the side carrying it has the error, the side it targets is warned), a mandatory cycle, two providers of one point and version whose companion schemas differ, and a `single` point with several contributors and no selection, each with the fix named. A new `versions` heading checks every version relation deterministically and labels each finding with its relation: `requires_backbone` against the installed backbone, `requires_capabilities` ranges against installed versions, contributions and subscriptions against point versions, and `depends-on` entries against the offered process interface's version; project filler versions and rule-set pins are named hooks until their files have schemas. The configuration pass now checks its connection entries for real: a provider selection must name a capability that provides the role, a contributor selection a `single` data point and one of its contributors — the earlier "cannot verify" information lines are gone.
- An agent's front matter may declare the model it runs on (`model:`) and the effort it reasons at (`effort:`), and an adopter may override either per agent in the overlay under `overrides.<agent>.model` / `.effort` — the two keys are reserved in that block and are not overlay categories. Both are optional and default to inherit: nothing is carried and the harness default applies, so an adopter who sets nothing sees no change, and the shipped agents set neither. Precedence per key: the overlay override, else the front matter, else inherit. Accepted values are the harness's own — `model`: `inherit`, an alias (`sonnet`, `opus`, `haiku`, `fable`, `best`, `opusplan`, `sonnet[1m]`, `opus[1m]`, `fable[1m]`) or a full `claude-…` model name; `effort`: `inherit`, `low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh`, `max` (the same levels `review-pr --effort` takes, which wins over an agent's effort at run time). `pkit agents` shows each agent's effective model and effort on its row, and `pkit validate` (the `refs` member) reports a value outside the vocabulary as an error, at the agent file or at the overlay. The fresh-install overlay seed shows the override shape in its commented example.
- Data points now resolve to a value (#994). A project answers a data point with a filler file at `<internal-root>/pkit/fillers/<publisher>/<role>/<point>.yaml`, in the new `backbone/filler.schema.json` envelope — `schema_version`, `value`, and `remove` for removal overrides, each with its reason. A data point may declare `combination: additive` beside `single` (now the default when none is declared) and `union`, a `default` with its participation (`always` or `alone`) and an `inert` policy (`fallback` or `fail`); a contribution may write its data as `value` instead of naming a command filler. Precedence runs project filler, then contributions, then the default; `union` merges by id with whole-entry overrides, `additive` merges in precedence order and refuses any collision, and an inert filler never promotes an `alone` default nor leaves a partial value. Command fillers take no parameter: run with `--json` alone, offline-marked and bounded, they print the filler envelope, and a command without `query-contract: true` is reported and never run. `pkit validate` reports the resolution under `connections`, and `pkit status` gains a "Data points" section showing how each point resolved and why. COR-052 point 6 now records that a filler takes no parameter: a question with an argument is a process point.
- Rule-set files are validated (COR-051). A Markdown file in a `rule-sets/` folder is a rule-set file, bound by where it is: the backbone's `.pkit/rule-sets/`, an installed capability's `.pkit/capabilities/<name>/rule-sets/`, `rule-sets/` under the internal documentation root (`docs/rule-sets/` by default), or a declared place whose path has a `rule-sets` segment; a `README.md` there is a signpost, not a rule set. The new `.pkit/schemas/backbone/rule-set.schema.json` fixes its front matter — `rule-set`, `version`, `inherits` (`<SET>@<major>`, with `<component>:` in front for a method rule set), `scope`, and a `rules` map from each `RS-<SET>-NNN` id to its `status`, `origin`, `offers`, `fills`, `successor` and container — and refuses unknown keys, naming the nearest known one. `pkit validate` gains a `rule-sets` pass (`project_kit.rule_sets`): the schema; the join between the data and the body's id-headed sections; well-formed ids that carry their set's name and are written once; unique set names; the container inside each rule; a complete origin on every accepted rule, whose cited decision record exists and is accepted; successors in the same set or one inheriting it; and inheritance — a pinned set that exists, no redefined inherited id, fills only of offered points and at most once along a chain, a method set never inheriting a project one, and a capability's set inheriting another capability's only when that dependency is declared. The pinned major is a version relation: the `versions` heading counts the rule-set pins and fails on one whose inherited set has moved to another major, naming it (`[rule-set pin]`). A rule without a status is proposed. An unresolved source kind and an orphaned fill are reported, never failed. Rules are friction artefacts: the friction pass walks the rule-set places too, reading each rule as one entry, and leaves a rule-set file's own findings to the new pass. `RS-<SET>-NNN`, `RS-<SET>-NNN#<point>` and `[<component>:RS-<SET>-NNN]` are citation tokens: `pkit refs validate` resolves them in agent and skill bodies, `pkit refs lookup` prints where a rule lives, and `pkit decisions validate` reports a rule id claimed twice across the rule sets.
- The three writers of COR-050 ship in the `friction` command group, each naming what it writes and writing only with consent: `--yes`, a terminal confirmation shown with the diff, or `--dry-run` to see the diff and write nothing; a non-interactive run without either refuses and names the command. `pkit friction revalidate <artefact> --outcome updated|unchanged [--because <text>] [--keep <anchor>]...` rewrites the `revalidated` block with a fresh `at`, the outcome and, for `unchanged`, a justification that must differ from the one written; it re-states only the deferrals kept by `--keep` or confirmed at the prompt and removes the rest. `pkit friction defer <artefact> --anchor <anchor> --reason <text>` adds or rewords one deferral, kept sorted, without touching `at`, and refuses an anchor the artefact does not carry. `pkit friction record-status <artefact>` writes the tool-written `last-check` from the whole-repository check at HEAD, only when the state changes. Each rewrites one key of the block and leaves every other byte of the file as it was, in block or flow style, and reads the result back before writing, so it never writes a block `pkit validate` would refuse. The after-merge job that would run `record-status` is not shipped.
- The wiring of connection points is drawn, shown and selected (#997). `pkit connections graph` renders the one wiring graph in the process graph's text, flow, mermaid and `--json` formats: the process definitions' derived and annotated edges together with the wiring resolver's edges — `accepts`, `emits` and `offers` from each point's definer, `contributes`, `subscribes` and `fills` from its counterparts — each saying how it stands (`active`, `conflict`, `bound`, `inert (version)`, …), with `--kind` to narrow it to data, process or event edges. `pkit process graph` now renders that same graph filtered to its process edges, so nothing it drew is removed and the two never disagree; its `--source` filter accepts `resolved`. `pkit connections providers set <role> <capability>` writes the provider selection through the consent-gated configuration writer after checking that the capability declares the role, showing the diff first (`--dry-run` stops there, `--yes` writes). A role conflict in `pkit validate` and `pkit status` now names that exact command for each provider, and `pkit status` gains a "Connections" section showing each role's provider, conflict or orphaned state and, per point, the bound and inert fillers and counterparts and every unmet mandatory mark.
- Every agent keeps its intermediate files — pull-request and issue bodies, scripts, captured output, notes — in one workspace, `.agent-workspace/` at the repository root, and nowhere else outside the repository: a new tool-hygiene rule in `rules/core.md` (rule 16; the communication and cross-repository rules move to 17–19). `pkit init` and `pkit sync` (self-host included) create the folder and add `/.agent-workspace/` to the repository's local exclude file in the common git directory, so one entry covers every worktree and nothing there reaches a commit; both are idempotent, and outside a git repository the folder is created and the exclusion skipped with a note; a symlink where the folder belongs is refused, never adopted. The exclude file covers one clone, so `pkit init` also recommends adding `/.agent-workspace/` to the project's committed `.gitignore`, which pkit never writes itself. `pkit status` reports the workspace and whether git ignores it, naming `pkit sync` when either is missing, and reports a symlink in its place. Every core agent body tells its agent to keep intermediate files there, and `pkit new agent` stamps the same section into a new agent.
- The permission catalog gains a `workspace` privilege, granted to every agent by all three shipped profiles: reading, creating and editing files in the agent workspace (`.agent-workspace/` of the project root or of another working tree of the same repository) with the file tools — `Read`, `Write`, `Edit`, `MultiEdit`, `NotebookEdit` — no longer prompts once enforcement is on. It is a path-scoped allow: the new `recognize.path` recognizer matches a file tool only when its target lies inside the folder, so outside it the grant has no opinion. The folder counts only as the checkout holds it — a symlinked `.agent-workspace`, a folder entry that is absolute or climbs out with `..`, a directory whose `.git` pointer git never registered as a worktree, a repository or worktree nested inside the folder, and a file hard-linked into it from elsewhere are never the workspace. A shell command is never the workspace's — a redirect, a here-document or an `rm` into the folder is judged exactly as before. The grant is hook-enforced and never realized as a session-wide rule; `pkit permissions catalog` and `overview` show the folder, `pkit permissions probe` demonstrates the verdict, and `pkit permissions diagnose report` lists a prompt for the workspace first, as a defect.
- The friction pass of `pkit validate` refuses a synced tree as a place (COR-050 point 14, refined): a file a declared place matches — the project's or a capability's, each glob expanded and each match tested — that a sync writes into the repository is no longer walked, and the place is a `synced-place` error at its declaration naming the synced matches, dormant or not; the place's other matches are still walked. The question is the tree's own ownership predicate's (`is_synced_copy` in `.pkit/lifecycle/ownership.py`), keyed on a capability's recorded origin and on whether the repository is the methodology's source, so a backbone area's or a `kit-shipped` capability's README is refused in an adopter and remains a place in the source repository. A tree without the ownership module gets an `ownership-unavailable` report and every match is walked. The backbone now loads the tree's ownership module through one loader, which leaves no bytecode cache in the tree.
- Every query command is provisioned before it runs offline: `pkit init` and `pkit sync` (self-host included), for each command a registered capability or adapter declares with `query-contract: true` whose script carries inline script metadata, resolve the script's environment once, online, with `uv sync --script`, which never runs the script. One line per command says what happened — provisioned, already provisioned (the script resolves offline, so nothing is fetched and a re-run is a no-op), nothing to provision, or a warning naming the command when it cannot be provisioned; the warning never fails the step, so init and sync keep working offline. A query whose environment is not provisioned is now named as such rather than as the exit it shows as: `pkit validate` reports "environment not provisioned — run `pkit sync`" for a validator, and a command filler's inert reason says the same, distinct from a command that answered nothing. A fresh clone or a pipeline no longer needs to run a query command by hand before `pkit validate`; running `pkit sync` on checkout is enough.
- A changeset can now declare that its component needs the backbone the release ships, with `requires_backbone: release` (top-level or under `custom:`), and `pkit release apply` raises the lower bound of that component's `requires_backbone` range to that backbone version: the new one when the backbone moves in the same release, else the current one. The raise is raise-only and never automatic, runs after the upper-bound broaden, and is not skipped by `--no-broaden`. Before writing anything, `apply` computes every raised range as it will write it and refuses the release if one would admit no backbone; the broaden and the raise both rewrite the top-level key, never a comment that mentions it. `pkit release plan` shows each floor under the component's bump, says "raised" only when the range changes, and adds a notice when the backbone does not move in that release, since the current backbone may lack the change the component needs; `plan --json` carries each raise, and the release PR's body lists it. `pkit release lint`, and `plan` and `apply` themselves, refuse the field on a backbone changeset, on a `none` changeset, on an unknown component or one that is not a capability or adapter with a range of `">=X.Y.Z,<A.B.C"` or `">=X.Y.Z"`, with any value but `release`, and in a release that would ship a pre-release backbone. The lint's escape hatch does not cover the field, since an invalid one blocks every later release.
`pkit validate` warns, without failing, when an installed component's `runtime_ignore` still declares the process journals, whose ignore line the backbone owns: while journals are committed the render leaves such an entry out, and the warning names the fix for where the package comes from: upgrade a methodology-shipped component together with the backbone, move an externally sourced one's pin to a release that drops the entry, or drop the entry from the project's own package.
- `pkit friction artefacts --at <rev>` reads one commit instead of the working tree — its configuration, its installed capabilities and their places, its files — from git objects, so a capability's script reads what the default branch or a merge-base holds through the one discovery rather than listing the commit itself. Each artefact in the `--json` document now carries its friction block's `anchors` by kind. A new top-level group, `pkit docs`, holds one command: `pkit docs record-location <capability> <name>` records where a capability's documentation location now lies (COR-049 point 5), once, in the capability's `project/docs-locations.yaml` — running it is the consent, so it asks nothing, and `--dry-run` shows it first. A capability's stamping command runs it when it places the first document there.
- `pkit new agent <namespace> <name>` accepts a capability name as the namespace, the way `pkit new decision <capability>` does: the agent stamps under `.pkit/capabilities/<capability>/agents/` (with `--with-storyboard`, in folder form with a sibling storyboard whose consumer namespace is the capability's name), the capability must exist — an unknown name is refused with the capabilities that do — and its `agents/` folder is created on first use; `core` and `project` are unchanged. The command now refuses a name already taken in any capability as well as in core or project. `pkit new storyboard agent <name>` finds an agent in a capability's `agents/` folder too, in the deploy's order (project, core, then capabilities by name), and a new `--namespace <ns>` option pins where to look. `pkit refs validate` checks a capability agent's storyboards as it checks those under `.pkit/agents/` — the storyboard names its agent by the capability, the agent declares the storyboard back, and an undeclared storyboard in the folder is reported — comparing a declared entry with the file by the path it resolves to, so the bare sibling form and the project-root-relative form name the same storyboard. The agents README, the `agent-author` and `storyboard-author` skills and the CLI reference document the capability namespace and the storyboard reference convention: declare a storyboard by its bare sibling filename; the deploy rewrites it to the source path in the deployed copy.
- A new read command, `pkit friction artefacts [--json]`, prints where the artefacts are, from the one artefact discovery `pkit validate` and the friction checks read: the documentation roots; each declared place, the project's and each capability's, with the location and root a capability place names, the files it matches and the skips validation applies (a synced copy, a place outside the repository, a malformed declaration); every file read, with the places matching it, its rule-set claim, whether `friction.exclude` leaves it out and its front matter's own fields; and every artefact. The JSON document is stable (`schema_version: 1`): it is how a capability's own script reads the places without importing the backbone or walking them itself. Read-only; exit 1 when the configuration cannot be read, 2 on a usage error.
- `pkit friction explain <artefact> --json` carries what a capability's script reading an artefact's change would otherwise compute again. Each commit in a finding's `commits` has `paths`: what the check read as the change — for a path anchor the paths it matches that the commit touched, excluded paths and the artefact's own file left out; for a record or artefact anchor the file it names; for a move the artefact's file under both names — so a reader runs `git show <commit> -- <paths>` and matches nothing itself. Each path anchor has `files` (`{point, head, excluded}`): the files it stands on at the revalidation point and at HEAD, matched as the check decides a dead anchor, under HEAD's exclusions — the two trees' view, not the change, which `commits[].paths` gives — and the files its glob covers at HEAD that `friction.exclude` leaves out. The artefact's `body` is the text its content is compared on: for a collection entry, the section headed by its id. One key holds more than it did: a dead path anchor's finding, whose `commits` were always empty, now lists where its files went — for each file it matched, the last commit that touched it, its removal or rename away, whether before or after the revalidation point; a deferral of a dead anchor still lists none. Both checks now say which way a path anchor is dead: `matches no file`, or `matches only excluded files (<n>)`, where both read "matches no file (or only excluded ones)" before. The human view lists where a dead path anchor's files went.
- A changeset's `requires_backbone` can now name an already-shipped backbone, an `X.Y.Z` that `CHANGELOG.md` records as released at or below the current `.pkit/VERSION`, for a component whose need an older backbone meets, so its floor is no longer raised to the current backbone down to the patch; `release` keeps naming the backbone the release ships, and a version above the current backbone, one never released, a pre-release or one with a leading zero is refused by `pkit release lint`, `plan` and `apply`, each saying how to fix it. The floor goes to the highest backbone a component's changesets name. `pkit release apply` states each raised floor at the end of the component's changelog entry ("Requires backbone >=X.Y.Z."), warns before confirming when `--no-broaden` leaves a raised range excluding the backbone it ships, and `plan --json` carries the declared backbone, whether `release` or an explicit version set it, and that sentence beside each raise. `pkit release check` now also refuses a pull request that declares a floor for a component it neither changes nor releases, whatever its labels; a floor-only `patch` changeset for the component is the way to declare a need found after it shipped.
- An artefact with nothing to anchor to may carry `unanchored-because: <reason>` in its `friction` block instead of anchors — the reason a person accepted it with none, such as a person's part in the work that no code carries out (COR-050 point 1). The container schema admits the key, non-empty text; `pkit validate`'s friction member refuses it beside anchors (`unanchored-beside-anchors`, point 12), and the friction writers refuse to write over such a block. The unanchored measure counts only the artefacts without a reason (point 8): `pkit friction check --all` lists those accepted with a reason apart, each with its reason, under `Accepted unanchored`, and its `--json` gains `measures.accepted_unanchored` (`artefact`, `location`, `reason`) beside `measures.unanchored`, which now holds those without a reason alone. `pkit friction debt` shows the same measure after the debt — those without a reason counted and named in its title line, the accepted apart — and its `--json` gains `counts.unanchored`, `unanchored` and `accepted_unanchored`. `pkit friction explain` shows an artefact's reason under its state, and its `--json` gains `unanchored_because`; `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives every artefact its `unanchored_because` (the document's `schema_version` stays 1: a key added).
- A capability may declare folders of held documents — files that belong to it but are not artefacts, such as a log of reviews it carried out — in its package metadata, as `friction.held: [{location, path, description}]`: `path` a folder, never a glob, inside the declared location `location` names (COR-050 point 1). No place — a documentation root, another capability's place or the project's — walks a held file, so it is never an artefact, and neither the unanchored measure nor uncovered surface counts it. A held folder is bounded: one that equals or encloses a documentation root or another declaration's place, or shares a file with its own capability's place, another held folder or a rule-set folder, holds nothing, and `pkit validate`'s packages member refuses it at its entry. `pkit friction artefacts --json` declares each held folder under a new `held` key, as a place is declared — owner, location, resolved path, the files it holds and why it is skipped (`malformed`, `outside-repository`, `overlap`) — and lists each held file under `held_files` with its folder, the places that match it, its front matter's own fields and why it is unreadable; the document's `schema_version` stays 1 (keys added). `pkit validate`'s friction member refuses, dormant or not, a held folder it cannot read (`malformed-held`) or that leaves the repository (`held-outside-repository`), a held document whose front matter does not parse (`held-unparsable-front-matter`), and a friction block anywhere in a held document (`held-friction-block`). The friction writers and `pkit friction explain`, named a held document, say which capability holds it.
- One meaning of the default branch for every reader of settled state (COR-054, #1128). The backbone configuration gains `repository.default-branch` — the branch the project's pull requests merge into, declared once, `main` when absent, a branch name as git accepts one and not a reference. It resolves to the remote-tracking reference of the branch's upstream, else `origin/<name>`, and to the local branch only when there is no remote, which every reader says on a `warning:` line; when nothing resolves it is reported with the fixes, never guessed. Any branch named as a base resolves the same way. New `pkit repository base [--base <ref>] [--json]` reads the default branch and the base a comparison reads — `--base`, else `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`, else the default branch — with its commit and where HEAD left it, so a capability's script never resolves a base or computes a merge-base itself. The friction change check, `pkit migrations check-diff` and `pkit release check` default to that base where they used to take a fixed `origin/main`.
- `pkit decisions validate`, and the `decisions` member of `pkit validate`, warn of each line of a record that narrates its own revision instead of stating what is true: an amendment heading or marker (`## Amendment (…)`, `**Amendment 1**`, `**Amended by …**`), a revision stamped with an issue number or a date (`**Update (#252)**`, `(clarified, #813)`), and change-log phrasing ("this record originally…", "previously we believed…"). Each is a warning naming the file and line, never an error, so the command's exit code is unchanged: the shapes are read from prose, and a gate that fails on a heuristic reading of prose fails for the wrong reasons. A superseded record, fenced blocks and inline code spans are not read, nor is a record that arrives as a synced copy — a core record, or a kit-shipped capability's, in an adopting project — since it is refined where it is authored. The two permitted in-body markers — a superseded-by line and a `(refinement per <record>)` pointer — match none of the shapes. The decisions README's "Refining an accepted record" is the one place the refine-in-place rule is stated, and gains its steps: fold a correction into the section it belongs to, with no revision marker or residue of the change, and supersede only when the decision is overturned. The `architect` agent and the `decision-author` skill point at it rather than restate it.
- `pkit release merge` lands a release PR through the base branch's merge queue where it has one, instead of merging around it and then deleting the head branch of a PR the queue had only taken in, which closed it unmerged. It refuses unless the queue squashes from the PR title and body, and refuses to enqueue again a head the queue already dropped unless `--force` is passed; it enqueues the PR pinned to the head whose checks it read, reports where it stands and waits for the merge — as long as the queue estimates plus 2 minutes, at most 30, or `--wait-minutes`, `--no-wait` not at all. Without a queue the direct merge is now pinned to that head too. When it cannot read how the base merges it refuses, merging nothing. The head branch is deleted only once GitHub reports the PR merged, and the local branch only when nothing on it is missing from the merge; a run on a release PR that has already merged runs that clean-up alone. A run that returns while the queue holds the PR, or after a direct merge it could not confirm, exits 4 with nothing deleted, and running it again completes it; a PR the queue drops, or whose head moved, exits 3. The merge mechanic behind it is the backbone's, once, and a new command exposes it to capability scripts on GitHub, through `gh`: `pkit pull-request` reads where a PR stands with its base's queue and the repository's squash-commit defaults, squash-merges, enqueues, waits for the queue's merge and takes a PR out of the queue, each with a `--json` document. ([#1207])
- `pkit process move` takes `--from <state>`: the state the subject held before the caller applied the move's domain side-effect. A wrapper that writes its side-effect first and journals second, as the process README's seam-ordering contract has it, finds live detection already reporting the target, and the engine took that for the origin: a move into a state with no transition to itself was refused, and one into a state that has one was journaled as that self-loop. With `--from` the move is validated and journaled from the stated origin, gates included. The engine accepts the stated origin only while live detection places the subject at it or at the target, and refuses otherwise, naming all three. Without `--from` a move starts at the live position, as before. The seam-ordering contract now has the wrapper pass the origin it read.
- `pkit process move` takes `--reason <text>`: why the caller took the move, recorded on its journal entry as given — a move one subject's move caused in another, say. The engine reads it for nothing, so it never changes whether a move is allowed, and with journal logging off it is not recorded anywhere. `pkit process status` shows it after the move it explains. The process shape contract's journal entry gains the `reason` field.
- A process definition's states may detect in a second mode, `classified` (COR-033 point 5, ADR-062). Under `inferred` each state's predicate answers "is the subject in this state?" with `{result, reason}`; under `classified` a predicate answers "which state is the subject in?" with `{state, reason}`, and the states that name it — one command under one `with` mapping — share its one answer, so the engine runs it once each time it resolves a subject's position rather than once per state. `state: null` with a reason that is not blank places the subject in none of its states, and `status` shows that reason under "no position" — `status --json` as `position.placed_nowhere`; any other answer the engine cannot read — no `state`, a blank reason, an id that is not one of its states, the wrong type — leaves each of its states indeterminate, never "none", with the answer quoted in the reason. Every state of a definition declares the same mode: a definition that mixes modes resolves no position and says why, and `pkit validate`'s `process` member reports it as an error, as it does `classified` detections that name one command under different `with` mappings; the shape contract's schema states the one-mode rule too. Gates, invariants, `resume_when`, cascade membership and the hand-off seams read their answers exactly as before. A detection that cannot be evaluated for several states is now shown once, with the states it leaves indeterminate listed beside it, in the narrative views and in a refusal; `status --json` keeps one `position.unevaluated` entry per state. Fixed alongside: from an indeterminate position `status` listed a `from: "*"` move as permitted although `can-move` and `move` refuse it; it now lists it refused, with `can-move`'s reason. Existing `inferred` definitions are read as before.
- A capability registers an anchor kind with its resolver under `friction.kinds` in its package metadata: a mapping from the kind to an entry whose `command` names the `commands:` leaf that resolves it. The `packages` member of `pkit validate` reports, at the registration, a resolver whose command names no leaf or whose leaf does not declare `query-contract: true`, a kind the backbone resolves itself (`path`, `record`, `artefact`), a kind two installed capabilities register, and a kind an adapter registers; the friction checks and rule-set validation refuse each before any run. A resolver that may run is run under the query policy — `--json`, `--` and then the anchor value as its arguments, the value last so that none is read as an option, from the project root, the offline marker set, the base override removed, bounded by thirty seconds — once per anchor value for a check, and not again in that check once it overran its bound. It answers `{"paths": [...]}`: every file whose content decides what the value denotes. The change check and validation read the answer against the working tree's listing; `friction check --all`, `explain`, `debt` and `record-status` read it against HEAD's files, so a path HEAD does not hold is no answer. The change check asks an artefact when the diff changed the content of a file the answer names, and the whole-repository check finds it stale when a commit after its revalidation point did; the files count as anchored surface, and an anchor whose resolver names more than half of the tracked files is over-broad. Validation admits an anchor, and a deferral, of any kind a word names, and the friction writers write them: the container schema leaves the anchor kinds open, and the `friction` member reports, and never fails, an anchor whose kind nothing installed resolves (`unresolved-kind`). The change check gains two finding kinds. `no-answer` — a resolver exited abnormally, overran its bound, was not provisioned or answered in another shape — fails in enforcing mode whoever added the anchor, and says to run again, then `pkit sync` or mend the resolver. `dead-unattributed` — an anchor the diff kept whose resolver names no file — is reported and never failed: a resolver is asked about the head alone, so a change that deletes what a kept anchor denotes passes the change check and the whole-repository check reports the anchor dead. An anchor the diff added that resolves to nothing fails as `dead-anchor`, and `unresolved-kind` fails when the diff added the anchor or took the kind's resolver away, read from the base commit's registrations. An artefact with an anchor that cannot be resolved is not judged: `friction check --all`, `explain` and `debt` give it the state `unresolved`, never `current`, with the finding `unresolved-kind` or `no-answer`, and `record-status` refuses it. `schema_version` stays `1` in every reading document: kinds and one state are added, and a reader treats a finding kind or a state it does not know as not judged. Rule-set validation resolves a cited source through its kind's resolver and fails when the resolver names no file (`missing-source`) or gives no answer (`unanswered-source`); a source kind with no resolver that may run stays a report. `pkit init` and `pkit sync` provision a resolver's script as they provision a validator's. COR-050 points 2, 5, 7 and 12 state what a resolver answers and what the change check fails.
- Every change the backbone makes on the hosting service now runs the cross-repository guard itself (COR-039, ADR-061 point 6). `pkit pull-request merge`, `enqueue` and `dequeue` compare the session's anchor with the repository of the working directory before the request; `pkit release merge`, `release publish-notes`, `version tag --push`, `version untag --push` and `release apply --tag --push` do so once, at their entry, before anything is read from the service or sent to it. A tag made or removed only locally runs no guard. In the session's own repository nothing changes. In another, the guard asks on standard error where standard input and standard error are both a terminal, and refuses otherwise, making no request; each of these commands takes `--allow-foreign-repo` to confirm the change. With no session's anchor — a plain terminal, a pipeline — the guard does not fire and no flag is needed; a git that cannot be asked warns and goes on. A refused `pull-request` request answers `accepted: false` with `reason_kind: "foreign-repository"`, and every request document gains `reason_kind` and `guard` — `verdict` (the comparison alone: `same-repo`, `diverged` or `undetermined`), `undetermined_kind`, `anchor`, `target`, and `cleared` (how the guard let the request through, null when it refused) — as additions: `schema_version` stays 1. A dry run never asks: it ends as a run with nobody to ask would, refused in another repository without the flag. The guard is an interlock, not a boundary: a set `GH_REPO`, which sends `gh` elsewhere than the working directory's remote, is not compared. ([#1277])
- `pkit validate`'s `process` member reports, as an error, an `inferred` definition in which two or more states name one detection predicate — the same command under the same `with` mapping, the key the engine's answer memo runs a predicate under. Such a predicate runs once and each of those states reads the same answer, so none of them but the one declared first can be the position; the finding names the states and the command. The usual way there is a classifier's command named under `inferred`, where every subject resolves to "no position" and nothing said why. One command under different `with` mappings is not reported, nor are the states of a `classified` definition, which share a classifier by design; a definition that mixes modes is reported for the mix alone.
- `pkit pull-request delete-branch <n> --expect <sha>` deletes a merged pull request's head branch on the hosting service, and only while its tip is `<sha>`, so a push made after the merge is not lost and a newer pull request's branch of the same name is not deleted. `<sha>` must be the head the pull request merged at, named in full (40 or 64 hexadecimal characters; anything else is a usage error, exit 2). It runs the cross-repository guard first and takes `--allow-foreign-repo`. It refuses a pull request that has not merged (`not-merged`), one whose head is in a fork (`cross-repository`), a `<sha>` that is not the pull request's head (`expect-mismatch`), and a reading it cannot take (`unreadable`), counts of open pull requests included. It keeps a branch whose tip moved (`tip-moved`); one another open pull request uses as its head or as its base (`open-pull-request`); one the service answers the deletion of with an error (`deletion-refused`); and one still in place after a deletion that got no usable answer (`unanswered`). It reports `gone` when the branch is not there. When a deletion gets no usable answer and the branch cannot be read since, it reports `unconfirmed`, with no tip, since whether the branch was deleted is not known. The deletion is one compare-and-delete request (GitHub's `updateRefs` with the expected tip); a host whose API lacks that mutation keeps the branch every time. With `--json` it writes one document: `outcome` (`deleted`, `kept`, `gone`, `refused`, `unconfirmed`), `branch`, `expected`, `merged_head`, `tip`, `reason_kind`, `reason` and `guard`. It exits 0 when deleted, kept or gone, and 1 when refused or unconfirmed. `pkit release merge` deletes a release pull request's head branch through it, after the merge. What became of the branch is said in one line, which names the command that deletes it later where it was not deleted, and none of it fails the run. The local branch is deleted only once the remote one was deleted or is gone. `pkit pull-request read` reports the head's ref name (`head_ref`) and whether the head is in another repository (`cross_repository`), as additions: `schema_version` stays 1.
- The document `pkit connections resolve <address> --json` prints gains `outcome`: how the resolution of the point ended, as one value from a closed set, beside `why`, which stays the sentence for people. `resolved` — the point has a value, which may be empty (an inert filler among several that answered leaves it `resolved`; each filler's `state` says so); `undefined` — no active provider defines the point (`defined: false`); `unfilled` — no filler is declared and the point has no default (a contribution not delivered, because its capability is not the selected provider of its role, declares nothing); `no-answer` — every filler meant to answer was inert; `inert-fail` — a filler meant to answer was inert under the `fail` inert policy; `collision` — the fillers' entries collide under the point's combination; `selection-needed` — several capabilities contribute to a `single` point and none is selected; `selection-unmatched` — the contributor selection names a capability that does not contribute to the point; `definer-defect` — the definer's own declaration cannot be applied (its companion schema, or a combination policy the backbone does not know). A reader dispatches on `outcome`, never on `why`'s wording, and reads a value it does not know as "could not tell" — never as `resolved`, never as `unfilled`. The structured fields alone could not tell a collision from a point nothing fills: a point that does not resolve lists every filler that answered as `passed over`. The document also gains `schema_version` (`1`), under the rule every reading document follows: raised when a key or an `outcome` value a reader relies on changes its meaning or goes, never for one added; a document without it reads as version 1. The human output is unchanged.
- `pkit pull-request land <n> --head <sha> --subject <text>` performs the landing sequence in one command. It runs the cross-repository guard once, takes one reading, applies the caller's options to it (`--seconds`, `--allow-dropped-head`, `--admin`, `--direct-only`, `--queued-bad-shape refuse|warn`, `--no-request`, `--allow-foreign-repo`), sends the direct squash merge or the enqueue pinned to `<sha>`, waits, and takes the pull request out of the queue when a reading finds another head. It refuses in one order — `request-not-allowed` (with `--no-request`, which allows no merge and no enqueue: a pull request queued at `<sha>` is waited for, one queued at another head still taken out), then `admin-on-queue`, `queue-not-allowed`, `queue-not-squash`, `dropped-head`, `squash-defaults`, the defaults read last — and no option makes an administrator merge on a base with a queue, or hands a pull request to a queue that would not make its squash commit. Only an open pull request gets a request. It never deletes a branch. With `--json` it writes a `reading`, a `requesting` line before each request is sent, a `requested` line once it is settled, and one `end` document whose `ended` is one of twelve kinds, every key in every document: its `reason_kind`, `sent`, `dequeue`, `shape`, `warnings` (`queue-not-squash`, `squash-defaults`, `unreadable`, `auto-merge-armed` for auto-merge left armed on a pull request it held whose direct merge fails or is unconfirmed, `enqueued-instead` for a direct merge the service queued — for a pull request auto-merge held, a queue entry it did not have), `guard`, `merged_head`, `merge_commit` and `bound_seconds`, the longest the landing can run with those options. A merged end always names `merged_head`. `pull_request_landing.decode_end` decodes the end strictly by its `ended`. A write that fails before any request ends the landing with nothing sent; once a request has gone out, it stops the writing, not the landing. `--dry-run` reads and judges, sends nothing, never asks, and ends `planned` with what it would do. Exits are for a person; callers read the document. `--head` must be a full commit id, and the sequence refuses any other form itself, imported as by command. The reading gains `merge_commit`; `wait_for_merge` gains `on_reading`. `pkit release merge` now lands through the sequence, planning it first, and decides on the decoded end document. Its outcomes change: every landing run reads once more first; a pull request auto-merge holds on a base without a queue is gated and merged directly, and where its requirements are unmet gh refuses the merge, exit 1, with a warning that auto-merge is still armed, which an unconfirmed merge of such a pull request gives too, for the case that the merge was not made; a head that moved between its view and the landing's reading exits 3 with nothing sent, where it used to exit 1; a base changed to one whose queue would not make the release's commit is refused at the landing's reading, exit 1; a queue switched on after its view is enqueued into, the defaults read; a direct merge GitHub queued instead is waited for with the `enqueued-instead` warning in release's words — this run did not judge the commit the queue makes, and what to read to judge it; a release pull request closed without merging after its view exits 0, "nothing to merge" — exit 0 does not mean merged, so a script reads `pkit pull-request read <n> --json` — where it used to exit 1; a direct merge that merged at another head deletes the head branch at the head that merged, with a warning; a pull request the plan finds queued that leaves the queue before the landing is refused, nothing sent, exit 1; a head its view names in another form than a full commit id, or a view `gh` answers with something that is not JSON, ends the run having asked nothing. `pkit pull-request` takes an HTTP 408 for no answer, a dequeue that could not read the pull request before sending anything says `reason_kind: unreadable`, and the readings that settle a request stop at their stated most.
- `pkit friction check` lists the answers a change wrote: every revalidation, deferral and reason for having no anchors, with its words, derived from each artefact carrying the block against its counterpart at the merge-base — never composed. `--json` gains a top-level `answers` key (`[]` while dormant; `schema_version` stays 1), each entry with `artefact`, `location`, `answer` (`updated`, `unchanged`, `deferred`, `unanchored` or `null`), `anchor`, `reason`, `kept` (the deferrals a revalidation kept), `asked` (whether the diff asked for it), `status` (`stands`, `bump` or `edited`) and `new`; the human view ends with "Answers written in this change", every reason in full, any character that is not printable shown escaped (`\x1b`, `\u202e`) there and in the findings. Every deferral entry is read, so an anchor deferred twice lists each entry's words. `--json` also gains `unreadable`, the head's files whose front matter does not parse. `--head <rev>` checks that commit, read from git objects, instead of the working tree: the merge-base is taken against it, uncommitted work is not read, and its rule-set folders, installed capabilities and registered anchor kinds are that commit's, so a pull request's head can be checked from any checkout; a resolver of a registered kind runs only from a checkout at that commit and is refused elsewhere. `--head` is refused with `--all`, and a revision that names no commit exits 1. Discovery over any commit — the whole-repository check, `friction artefacts --at` — reads which folders hold rule sets from that commit. `pkit validate` reports an anchor deferred twice in one block (`duplicate-deferral`, an error; COR-050 point 4). `pkit friction revalidate` and `defer`, refusing to write off a terminal without `--yes`, no longer print a ready-made `--yes` command: they name the dry run and say that an answer on an artefact may be a person's decision. `record-status` refuses as before.
- Whether an agent may run a friction writer is recorded. COR-050 point 3 says who decides an answer on an artefact. An agent making a change may write, without asking first, the answers the change check asks of that change, and the revalidation of a combined state it owes after a conflict. A reviewer other than that agent, one the project chooses, checks every `unchanged` and `deferred` reason among them against the change, from the change check's own list, and blocks the merge when one does not hold. Two cases leave a change's answers unchecked by any reviewer, a limit the record accepts: a person skipped the review on record, or the project chose no such reviewer. A deferral holds only if it names an open follow-up. For each document revalidated as `updated`, the change's author says in the pull request what changed in it, in one sentence. The reviewer checks it against the diff, and blocks the merge when a document has no sentence or its sentence does not match what changed. The person who authorises the merge is told those sentences and which documents are left out of date until which follow-up, as information: a change with answers merges under the same authorisation as any other. Every other answer, and every reason for having no anchors (point 1), is a person's decision, accepted before it is written. COR-013 says a write made by one of the methodology's mutating commands is the command's and is not declared in `owns`: the agent's body, or a skill it hands the work to, names the command and the conditions for running it. The agents README gains "Friction writers" — an agent making a change answers what its own change owes, and a reviewer other than it checks those answers, unless a person skipped the review on record or the project chose no such reviewer; an agent whose task is the answer runs a writer only when its body, or a skill its body hands the work to, names it, it can ask the person, the person was shown every word first, and it is not a reviewer — and its `owns` bullet says the same of command-routed writes. The core rules gain rule 20, "A reviewer checks the answers your change owes, and a person decides any other answer on an artefact", and the `agent-author` skill says that a path an agent writes only through a mutating command it runs is not listed among the files it owns: the body names the command and the conditions for running it.
- `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives two more keys on each artefact. `span` is, for a collection entry, the lines of its file that its section spans, `{first_line, last_line}`. These are exactly the text friction compares as the entry's body, so a script reads the section without finding it itself. It is `null` for a document and for an entry with no section. `in_scope_of` lists the rule-set files whose scope covers the artefact's file, in path order. A scope is read as a place is, and an artefact that no rule set covers has an empty list. Both keys are added, so the document's `schema_version` stays 1.
- The backbone answers which accepted rules bind on an artefact (COR-051 point 7, #1408). `pkit rule-sets reach` gives the answer at the working tree, or at a commit with `--at`. Each artefact on which a rule binds is listed with each rule's status, the sets whose scope it binds through, the extension points it fills, the parts of it checks enforce, and its text. `pkit rule-sets altered` lists each artefact a change altered, with the rules that bind on it at the base and at the head. It pairs artefacts as the change check does, so a move that keeps the content, or a change that only writes a revalidation, alters nothing. Neither command runs a query. A route starts at a scoped set of the project's own and follows its pins, and no rule binds through a pin behind its set. A rule set may declare, under `checks`, the part of a rule a check enforces. `pkit validate`'s `rule-sets` member now fails an artefact reached through routes that pin one set at different majors, or whose most specific sets bring different fills for one extension point. It also fails a check part declared for a rule the set lacks, and reports a declared check that is not there. A project whose scoped sets disagree in this way sees new errors. The rule-set reader reads a commit through discovery's tree.
- Artefacts take a type their owner declares on a place (COR-056, #1406). A component declares its types in a type registry, `friction.types` in its package metadata, and names one on a place as `type`. A project gives a type on its own place, written `{path, type}` under `friction.places`. Discovery resolves each artefact's type from the places that match its file. Every rule is `backbone:rule`. A project place yields to a component's place that is not root-wide, a root-wide place comes last, and otherwise the more specific place wins. A kind field gates a type, and a tie counts as each type in contention. A rule set's `scope` now takes `{paths, exclude, types}` beside a plain list of paths, which keeps its meaning. A scope on a method rule set is now an error (COR-051 point 2): no shipped method rule set has one. `pkit validate` reports the type findings: the `configuration` member fails a type the project may not give, a bare type taking a declared type's name, and a tie the project can mend. It reports a typed project place that yields. The `packages` member reports what only a component can mend, and the `rule-sets` member fails a type in a scope that no owner declares. `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives each artefact its `type`, `type_place`, `type_conflict` and `ranked_places`, each place its `type`, and every type registry under `types`, at version 1. It prints a `warning:` line for a project place whose `type` the schema refuses. The install plan names a capability's type whose name a bare project type uses. Apart from a scope on a method rule set, nothing that exists changes until a place or a scope uses a type. An older backbone's readers pass over a typed place and a mapping scope, and its validation refuses them.
- A capability may depend on another optionally (COR-030, #1410). An entry in `requires_capabilities` may carry `optional: true`, which the package schema accepts. The dependency then need not be installed: install, both upgrade entry points and `pkit validate` pass it when it is absent, and refuse an installed one outside its range, as for a required dependency. An optional dependency never blocks uninstalling the capability it names, and it does not admit rule-set inheritance (COR-051 point 7). Installing or registering a capability whose version falls outside a range an installed capability declares for it, required or optional, now warns, naming each such dependent and its range, and refuses unless `--force` is given, as upgrading a dependency already does. `pkit capabilities install` and `pkit capabilities register` gain `--force` for it. The lifecycle README states that the backbone-wide `pkit upgrade` moves every installed kit-shipped capability to its source version through its sync.
- The backbone reads where a part of a document lies and a record's points, each in one home (ADR-057 point 2, #1414). `project_kit.document_parts` gives a body's headings outside fenced code, a section by its id or its slug, and the lead; artefact discovery cuts a collection entry's section with it, and the rule-set reader lists a rule-set file's sections with it, so an entry's body and span are unchanged. `project_kit.record_points` reads a decision record's points as COR-058 sets them out: the Decision's extent, its point headings and stubs, its misspelt headings, its retired ids with their successors, its points in list form and what each point stands on, and it holds each stub to its entry in `retired-points`. `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives each artefact a `part_anchors` list, added within version 1: each anchor that names a part of a file, such as an `artefact` anchor on a collection entry, a rule among them, with the file and the span that part resolves to.
- `pkit decisions check-diff` checks a change's numbering against its base (COR-058 point 8). For each decision record the change touches, it fails on a point dropped without being retired, a new id not past every id the branch point holds or retires, a point's text found only under another id, a duplicate id or a refused heading form the change added, a retired id without its stub or a stub without its entry, a new `retired-points` entry whose successor is no live point, and, in a record the change converts from list form, a point whose id differs from its numbered item's. It also fails on a point id or a record id the default branch took since the branch left it. It warns of a point's title under another id with its text changed, a converted point whose id differs from its bold number or label, and a point added to a record in list form or a new record in list form. It reads its base as every comparison does (`--base`, `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`, the default branch), skips a record the project receives by sync, and is a line of its own in a project's check gate (#1417). ([#1545])
- A new core agent, `rule-set-reviewer`, judges one change against the accepted rules that bind on each artefact it alters (COR-057). It reads those rules only from the rule sets, through `pkit rule-sets altered`: the head's rules judge what the head holds, and the base's rules judge what the change removes. It judges only the text the change owns, and only the parts of a rule that no check is there to enforce. Each finding cites its rule by id, the artefact, the line and the passage. Whoever invokes it names the form of its verdict and the mark of each kind of finding. It is advisory: run it by hand on any change. Sync deploys it with the other core agents. `pkit rule-sets reach` and `pkit rule-sets altered` give what it reads: each declared check part now says whether its check is there (`there`, `problem`), and each artefact lists the accepted rules that a pin behind its set holds back (`held`). `reach` also lists an artefact whose rules are all held back (#1385).
- Validation checks a decision record's points, as COR-058 point 7 sets them out (#1416). The decisions member of `pkit validate`, and `pkit decisions validate`, read each record through `project_kit.record_points` and hold each stub to its entry in `retired-points`. They fail on a malformed `retired-points`, a retired id with no stub, a stub whose id is not retired or whose sentence differs from its entry's, a live point on a retired id, a successor the record neither holds nor retires, and a retired id named as its own successor. They warn of a duplicate id, a gap in the ids counting retired ones, and a heading in the Decision that opens with a number or a label in a refused form, such as `### 16 - Title`. Each finding names the file and the id, as `<file>#<id>`, or sits at `<file>:/retired-points` when it is on the field's shape. A synced copy and a superseded record are not checked. Only the new field's shape is read of the front matter, so a project's own fields pass. A record whose points are still a list fails nothing unless it writes `retired-points`.
- A record anchor can name one point of a record, and friction compares only that point (COR-050 points 2, 5 to 7, 9, 12 and 13; #1419). The value is the record's id, `#` and the id the point's heading opens with: `COR-058#4`, `PRJ-002#D1` or `living-docs:DEC-001#3`. The anchor stands on the point's section and on what makes the record bind: its `status`, `supersedes`, `superseded_by` and `title` fields, its preamble, its Summary and the Decision's text outside every point and stub. `pkit friction check`, `check --all` and `debt` read it through one reading of a record's points, so they agree. A changed point flags its dependants, and a change elsewhere in the record flags none. A moved point holds, and so does a renamed record. A point dropped is a dead anchor. An anchor on a point the record retires stands on nothing, so it is flagged once, and its question names where the point's content went. An id two points share makes the anchor ambiguous, reported under `dead-anchor`. A record whose points are still a list holds no point an anchor can name. `pkit friction explain` shows a stale point anchor's diff alone, under its finding and as `diff` in its JSON. `pkit validate`'s `friction` member fails a dead or ambiguous point anchor (`dead-point-anchor`, `ambiguous-point-anchor`) in either mode. It warns of an anchor on a retired point, naming the successors (`retired-point-anchor`). The container schema checks what follows `#` in a record anchor (COR-051 point 3): a value with a `#` and no point id after it fails as a malformed block, where it was a dead anchor before. A whole-record anchor works as before.
- `pkit friction artefacts --json` lists an anchor on one point of a record among an artefact's `part_anchors`, beside an `artefact` anchor on a collection entry (COR-050 point 2, #1552). Its `path` is the record's file, and its `span` the lines the point's section spans, `{first_line, last_line}`. These are exactly the section friction compares, found by the one reading of a record's points. The span does not give what else the point stands on, such as the record's status. An anchor on a retired point is listed with a `null` span, since it is no dead anchor and stands on nothing. A whole-record anchor, a dead anchor and an ambiguous one are not listed. The list is ordered by kind name, then each kind's values as written. A reader skips an entry of a kind it does not read. The key's meaning stays the same, each anchor that names a part, so the document's `schema_version` stays 1.
- `pkit rule-sets altered` says what a change brings under a rule (COR-057 point 2, #1560). Each altered artefact gains `brought_under`: the rules that bind on it at the head and did not bind on it at the base. A new key, `brought_unaltered`, lists each artefact the change brings under a rule without altering it, as `unaltered`: a wider scope, an accepted rule, an updated pin, a typed place, or a move into a set's paths. The human view shows both. The keys are added within `schema_version` 1. The `rule-set-reviewer` agent judges such an artefact in full for each rule the change brings it under, since the change owns the whole of it for that rule. A break of such a rule there withholds the reviewer's approval where the rule's text settles it, whatever brought the artefact under the rule (COR-060). So a change that accepts a rule, updates a pin or widens a scope answers for every document it brings under the rule, or leaves out of the scope what it cannot mend yet. ([#1579])
- An artefact anchor can name one section of a document, or its lead, and friction compares only that part (COR-050 points 2, 5 to 7, 9 and 11 to 13; COR-051 point 3; #1423). A section is named by the artefact, `#` and its heading's slug: `docs/guide.md#installing`. The lead, the body before the first heading, is named by a bare `#`: `docs/guide.md#`. A slug is the heading's title in lower case, every character but letters, digits, spaces and hyphens dropped, each space made a hyphen. The anchor stands on the part's text and on the document's own fields. `pkit friction check`, `check --all` and `debt` read it through one reading, so they agree. A changed section flags its dependants, and a change elsewhere in the document flags none. A moved section holds. A renamed heading leaves a dead anchor, which the change that renamed it owns. A slug two headings share makes the anchor ambiguous, reported under `dead-anchor`. A section or the lead of a collection file, a collection entry or a record is refused, naming the anchor to use, and so is a rule's extension point, such as `RS-CMN-003#labels`. An anchor on a part of its own artefact is a cycle. `pkit friction explain` shows a stale section's diff alone, and `pkit friction artefacts --json` lists a section or lead anchor among `part_anchors` with the lines it spans. `pkit validate`'s `friction` member fails a dead, ambiguous or refused part anchor (`dead-section-anchor`, `ambiguous-section-anchor`, `refused-section-anchor`) in either mode, its artefact gone included. A value with a `#` that names an artefact whole is read whole, so an artefact anchor that resolved before resolves the same way.
- `pkit rule-sets reach --preview` answers where rules would bind if every proposed rule were accepted (ADR-057 point 2, #1607). It is the same walk, with proposed taken as a status at which a rule binds wherever the walk asks: a rule a route reaches, a rule a pin holds back, a rule that offers an extension point and a rule that fills one. A superseded or withdrawn rule binds nothing in a preview either. Sets keep their versions, so the preview assumes each inheritor updates its pin in the change that accepts, and a pin already behind its set stays behind. The document gains a top-level `preview`, and each rule, held rule and conflict gains `previewed`: `true` where it holds only under the preview, always `false` without it. Both are added within `schema_version` 1. A preview can also lose a binding, where a point's fills conflict only under it, so a reader that must know what binds reads the answer without `--preview`. Validation's `rule-sets` member and every gate read the answer without it, so a fill conflict only a preview finds is no validation error. `rule-sets altered` has no preview.
- `pkit capabilities install` refuses a capability whose rule set takes a name one of your project's rule sets already declares (COR-051 point 3, #1373). Rule-set names are unique among a project's rule sets, and a set's name is in each of its rule ids, so the clash could not be undone once installed. Until now only `pkit validate` found it, after the install. The refusal comes before anything is written, `--dry-run` included, and names the set, the capability's file and your project's. Nothing is installed.
