- A changeset can say that a capability needs a version of another capability: `requires_capabilities` maps the capability it depends on to `release`, the version this release writes for it, or to a version of it that has shipped. `pkit release apply` raises the floor of that capability's entry in the dependent's `requires_capabilities`, never lowers it, and states the new floor in the changelog, as it does for a backbone floor. `release check` and `release lint` refuse a declaration the release cannot write. ([#1642])
- A capability may declare one project rule set in its package metadata, `project_rule_set`, and `pkit capabilities install` creates it (COR-017, #1613). The set lands in `rule-sets/` under the internal documentation root. It inherits the capability's method set at that set's major, with the declared scope by the capability's own types. Install creates it once for each install, only where no set of the project's own inherits the method set, directly or not. It never writes over a file, takes a name another set declares, writes through a link, or creates one while a project rule-set file cannot be read. Then it says why in a warning line, and the install goes on. Sync, a later install and an upgrade never write the set again. `pkit capabilities project-rule-set <name> [--json]` reads the same decision for an installed capability, for a capability's migration to read. The package schema learns the key, and the packages check refuses a declaration whose method set the capability does not ship, whose name one of its own sets takes, or whose types it does not declare. `pkit capabilities uninstall` now also refuses while a project rule set's `inherits` names one of the capability's method sets, however the list is written, unless `--force`.
- A changeset can say that a capability runs on the version a release writes for another capability it depends on: `runs_on_capabilities` maps that capability to `release`. `pkit release apply` then widens the upper bound of the dependency's entry to admit that version, up to the next major, and states the new bound in the changelog. A `release` floor raised past the bound widens it the same way. `release plan` and `release apply` refuse a release that would leave a capability's range excluding the version the release writes, or the tree carries, for a capability it depends on, and name both. `release check` and `release lint` refuse a declaration the release cannot write.
- A pull request may correct a capability's range on another capability when the range excludes the version the tree already carries for it, which no changeset can fix and which holds every release. `pkit release check` accepts that correction when it moves only the bound that excludes the version, as far as the version needs: an upper bound up to the next major of it, or a floor down to it. It refuses any other edit to an existing range, which moves only at the release on a changeset's declaration, and the `skip-changeset` label does not waive that. The release's refusal of such a range says how to correct it.
- A migration that runs `pkit` reaches the pkit that runs the upgrade, never your project's pin and never another `pkit` on your `PATH` (ADR-065 point 14). The migration runner puts a `pkit` of its own first on each script's `PATH`, in a fresh folder inside `.pkit/.migration-runner/`, which `.pkit/.gitignore` now ignores, and removes it when the scripts end. Where that folder cannot be made, or files there cannot run, the runner uses the temporary folder. Before the first script it checks that its `pkit` answers with its own version, and stops the upgrade, naming both folders and `TMPDIR`, when neither does. The runner also sets `PKIT_MIGRATION=1` in each script's environment. While it is set, `init`, `sync`, `upgrade`, `pin`, `unpin`, `merge`, `agents adopt`, `agents reconcile --write`, and `capabilities install`, `upgrade`, `register` and `uninstall` refuse before they read or write anything, under `--dry-run` too, and the message names point 14. So a migration that runs one of them stops the upgrade. Reading commands and `config set` run as before. A migration you write calls `pkit` by its bare name, sets no routing variable, and asks only about content, never a recorded version (the lifecycle README, "Script contract"). ([#1662])
- The core rules gain one rule and one clause. Rule 17, on discussing one decision at a time, now asks the agent to say what it recommends with each decision. A new last section, "When a step fails", holds rule 21. An agent retries once a step that failed for a cause outside its own work, such as a locked signing key, a service that is down or the network. It first checks that the step did not take effect. If the step fails again, the agent stops its work and reports what failed. Waiting on something pending is not a retry. No existing rule is renumbered. The rules file and its area's README say that a rule no record or area README holds cites the issue that decided it.
- `pkit friction since <rev> [--head <rev>] [--base <ref>] [--json]` answers whether every change from one commit to a later one, apart from merges of the base, lies inside `revalidated` blocks. Along the later commit's own line of commits, a merge of the base counts only for what differs from git's clean merge of its two parents, and where that merge conflicted, from git's merge taking each side. Any other commit counts for its own diff. A file lies inside only when it is an artefact's file whose two texts are the same once every `revalidated` key is taken out. Each change outside is named with its commit and why. A component that must tell a merge round that only answered again from any other change asks it, and never reads a friction block itself. It reads git and writes nothing, and it does not answer in a shallow clone.
- A link may ask for a revisit of its targets when its source changes (COR-050 point 16). A capability declares a link on one of its friction places, under `revisits`: the source's own field that lists the targets by their anchors' values, and the trigger fields. Nothing is on by default. When a change alters a trigger field of a source, the friction change check asks after each target the link lists at the base and at head. The target changed in the change, or the source carries a `revisited` entry with a new reason, `unchanged` (why it still stands) or `deferred` (the follow-up that revisits it). A target with neither is the new finding `unrevisited`, which enforcing mode fails on, and one that is answered is `revisited`. The answers list, and its `--json` form, give each revisit with the new keys `outcome`, `target`, `links` and `fields`, `answer` `revisited`, a target answered by its change included. The new writer `pkit friction revisit <artefact> --target <anchor> --outcome unchanged|deferred --reason <text>` writes one entry. `pkit validate` fails a dangling or repeated entry, and a link's field that is no list or names no anchor of its source. It reports entries on an artefact whose places declare no link. The package schema's place gains `revisits`, and the container schema's friction block gains `revisited`. Both are additive, so no migration is due. A capability that declares a link needs a backbone that knows the key. ([#1702])
- `pkit friction since` reads a `revisited` list (COR-050 point 16) as it reads a `revalidated` block: a change inside one lies inside. An entry rewritten, added or removed lies inside the list, as a deferral does inside a block. A list removed, emptied or turned to a value that is no list lies outside, as a block removed does, and so does a block turned to a value that is no mapping. Its `--json` keeps schema version 1: the command first ships in this release, so version 1 counts both keys from the start. Counting another key later raises it.
- `pkit friction artefacts --json` gains the key `unplaced`, within schema version 1 (#1724). For each documentation location an installed capability declares, keyed by the capability's name and then the location's, it gives where the location lies and the Markdown files within it that no place and no held folder of that capability holds. Each file says whether `friction.exclude` leaves it out. The files come from the listing the walk reads, so the list agrees with discovery inside a git work tree, outside one, and at a commit with `--at`. Only the capability's own declarations count, so a capability reads what lies in its location outside its places without listing files itself. The human view adds a line for each location that holds such files. The key is added, so no migration is due.
- A capability can ask you a question about a data point it defines, and record your answer (COR-061). It names a command of its own as the point's `question` in its package metadata. `pkit capabilities install`, `register` and `upgrade` end with the questions of the points the capability defines and of the points its contributions reach, and `pkit upgrade` ends with every question, once the transition is complete. Each is asked only where standard input and standard output are both terminals. Without one, nothing is asked or recorded, and each question is printed as the command to run later. `--dry-run` names them, `pkit sync` never asks, and `pkit capabilities show` lists a point's question. `pkit validate` refuses a question that names no command, or one that declares the query contract.
- A link that revisits its targets may name its targets by what stays out (COR-050 point 16). Beside `targets`, the own field that lists them, a capability's link may give `all-but`, the own fields that list the anchors that stay out: every other anchor of the source is then a target, one added later included. A link names exactly one of the two, and the package schema refuses both or neither. The change check asks after such a target as after any other, and its finding says the link takes every anchor the fields do not list. A revisit answer's `links` names only fields that list a target, so a target such a link takes adds none there. `pkit validate` fails an `all-but` field that is no list of texts, and never a value in it that no anchor has, which keeps nothing out. `pkit friction revisit` takes any target such a link takes. The key is additive, so no migration is due. A capability that declares it needs a backbone that knows it.
- A new command, `pkit agents run <agent> --folder <dir> --limit <seconds> [--effort <level>] [--json]`, runs one agent with nobody watching, on the brief read from standard input (ADR-066 points 1, 2 and 10). It is the one way a command starts an agent: no command calls a harness. The backbone starts the runner the project's adapter registers in its `commands:` tree under `run-agent`, and the runner starts the harness. With no adapter's runner, or more than one, the run is refused and the reason names the registered adapters. So is a runner whose script lies outside its adapter's folder, and a run inside a command the backbone runs, whose kill the harness would outlive. The run takes the unconfined form, the one the review command's runs take: it runs in the folder under the person's and the project's settings, and no flag that bypasses permissions reaches it. With `--json` it writes one document, `schema_version` 1, whose `ending` is `finished`, `not-started`, `timed-out`, `refused` or `no-answer`. A caller decides on that ending, never on the exit. The one command runner gains an agent-run policy for it: the request on standard input, a bound of the run's limit and thirty seconds more, and on overrun or an interrupt the runner is asked to stop with `SIGTERM` before it is killed, so it ends the harness it started. `SIGTERM` or `SIGHUP` to the command stops the runner the same way. The CLI README ("Agent runs"), the lifecycle README (the agent-run policy) and the adapters README ("The agent runner") document it.
- A component's folder of held documents (`friction.held`, COR-050 point 1) may now be a glob of folders at one depth, such as `*/revalidations`, and holds every file beneath each folder it names (#1821). A glob stands only before the folder's own name: one in the last segment, or a `**`, is refused as malformed. A held folder now also wins over its own component's places: a place of the component that shares only some files with the folder, such as a glob reaching into it, leaves those files to it, where the folder used to be refused and hold nothing. Two of one component's held folders that share only some files hold each for the component, by the folder declared first, where both used to be refused. A held folder that equals or encloses a documentation root or any place, its own component's included, or shares a file with another component's held folder or a rule-set folder, is still refused, and so are two of one component's folders when one lies within the other. Every package valid before reads the same, so nothing migrates; a component that relies on the new shapes needs this release of the backbone.
- An `artefact` anchor on an id two artefacts share names no one artefact, as one on a point id or a slug two share names no one part, and is reported instead of being read silently as naming the first artefact found (COR-050 points 7 and 12, #1818). It is warned of and fails nothing: both friction checks report it as the new finding kind `shared-id`, naming every artefact with the id and the one the anchor stands on, and enforcing mode passes it. The anchor stands on the first artefact with the id in walk order, as it did. The change check reports it where the change added the anchor, shared an id that was not shared, or changed which artefact the anchor stands on; the whole-repository check reports every one; validation does not report it. Ids no two artefacts share mend it, as does naming a document by its path.
- The run command, `pkit agents run`, gains its confined form (ADR-066 points 3 to 8): `--commit <sha> --rules-of <capability>:<command> --files-to <dir>`, in place of `--folder`. The run works in a fresh clone at the commit that shares no object with your repository, in a folder of the backbone's outside every working tree (`$XDG_STATE_HOME/pkit/agent-runs`, else `~/.local/state/pkit/agent-runs`), under the rules the capability declares for the command's runs. The clone is provisioned, given its workspace and a `pkit` of the run's own, and checked after the run: a change outside the workspace, git's own configuration, hooks and references included, is a breach the document names. The run's files are then copied to `<dir>`, and the clone removed. The document adds `confined`, `commit`, `check`, `files`, `refused_calls` and `excluded`, at `schema_version` 1. Every run, confined or not, is refused inside an agent run, where `PKIT_AGENT_RUN` is set. A new command, `pkit agents rules <capability>:<command>`, shows what a confined run is allowed and denied, and how each deny is held. A capability declares a run's rules in `permissions/runs.yaml`, shaped by the new `.pkit/schemas/permission-runs.schema.json`, and the shared decision core projects them (`projection.py`, `project_run`). No session's model reads that file, so the session catalog and its schema do not change. `pkit permissions overview` and `explain` list each run's rules. The harness requirements gain `unattended-confined-run` (ADR-066 point 13). The CLI, adapters, permissions and lifecycle READMEs document it.
- Each adapter can declare how far its harness meets the harness requirements the core declares, in `harness-support.yaml` in its folder (COR-047 point 2). The new core schema pair `harness-support` shapes it: per requirement a grade (`supported`, `at-cost` or `not-achievable`), the cost of an `at-cost` answer, the harness versions it holds for, and its evidence. A new command, `pkit harness report [--adapter <name>] [--json]`, resolves every requirement for each registered adapter to satisfied, unsatisfied or indeterminate, offline and from files only. It exits 1 while anything is unmet and 3 when a declaration fails its schema. Silence, an answer for no version or for versions it cannot check, and a grade nothing was read to confirm are indeterminate, never satisfied. `pkit validate`'s `schemas` member checks each declaration against its schema, and warns on an answer to a requirement the core does not declare. The adapter-author skill gains the declaration step. The adapters, CLI and schemas READMEs document it.
- The backbone reads answer files in `.pkit/project/friction/answers/`, as COR-062 and ADR-068 decide, and nothing writes one yet. Their schema ships as `.pkit/schemas/backbone/friction-answers.schema.json`, bound by its location. `pkit friction check --all`, `debt` and `explain` read an artefact whose block holds no answer from the answer files: the live answers, each value's point across both homes. Two revalidations made apart are a new finding, `answered-apart`, which is debt, never stale and never failed. The JSON documents gain each artefact's live answers with their points, the folder's path, and the count of files a prune would remove. No place reads a file in the folder, and no anchor stands on an answer file. `pkit validate` checks the answer files. A project with no answer file reads as before.
- The record lookup, `pkit refs lookup`, resolves a capability's decision, written `<capability>:DEC-NNN` with its slug or without, to its file in the capability's `decisions` folder, as it resolves a core, project or architecture record. A capability's script finds a record's file through it, never by looking in a folder itself. The decisions README, "Citing", says how.
- The record lookup, `pkit refs lookup`, resolves a point of a record written as an anchor's value, such as `COR-050#14` or `PRJ-002#D1`. It prints the record's file and the anchor GitHub gives the point's heading, reading the record's points as validation reads them. A retired point gives its stub's heading, and a record whose points are still a list gives its file alone. With `--json`, each answer in its document also gives the file, the anchor of a cited point's or rule's heading, and whether the file arrives as a synced copy. A capability's script finds a record or a rule set through it. The decisions README, "Citing", says how.
- The change check, `pkit friction check`, reads answer files as COR-062 and ADR-068 decide, and nothing writes one yet. The change's answers are the live ones in the answer files its diff adds, judged as a block's are. A file put back writes no answer, and an answer moved word for word out of a block is listed apart, under the new JSON key `moved`. What each answer read is judged against the change's own head, which `PKIT_CHECK_HEAD` names for a run that reads the working tree: HEAD or a parent of it, such as a pull request's head under a host's merge. `--head` keeps its meaning. Enforcing mode fails four new findings: `answer-file-rewritten`, `prune-mixed`, `read-incomplete` and `answers-nothing`. Three new findings fail nothing: `answer-on-what-is-gone`, `answer-file-ignored` for an answer file on an artefact whose block still holds answers, and `answered-apart`. The last, with the JSON key `answered_apart_at_tip`, reports the base tip's revalidations a change's answer did not read. Each listed answer gains `file`, `replaces_apart` and `moved`, and `head` gains `own`. No query inherits `PKIT_CHECK_HEAD`. A project with no answer file reads as before.
- A capability may declare the links its own command writes, so that friction reads each as its words alone (#1844, COR-050 point 5). It names, under `derived-links` on one of its friction places, each form as a regular expression that an inline link's target matches whole. Wherever the change check, `check --all` and `debt` compare the body of a file a place holds, each link of a declared form in running text is set aside: an artefact's content, a section or the lead, a record's content or point, and a placed file's content. So a change that only adds, removes or retargets such links asks nothing of what rests on the file, and a revalidation marked `updated` on it alone is a bump, in a block and in an answer file alike. A word beside a link, and a link of any other form, still count. A comparison reads the forms of its later state at both states, so the change that brings a declaration flags nothing. `pkit rule-sets altered` still lists an artefact changed only in such links. The `packages` check fails a target that does not compile. The key is additive, so no migration is due. A capability that declares it needs a backbone that knows it.
- The privilege catalog gains `text-filter`, the read-only text filters such as `cat`, `grep`, `head`, `tail` and `wc`, with `sort`, `uniq` and `diff` only in a form that writes no file and runs no other program, and `uv-run`, which recognizes `uv run`. Every shipped profile grants `text-filter` to every agent, so a pipe or chain of filters and other granted programs, such as `git log | head -5`, is allowed by the permission hook without a prompt. No shipped profile grants `uv-run`, and a project grants it in its own grants. Only the hook realizes either: `profile activate` and `pkit permissions apply` write no native rule for them. A redirection that writes a file, an expansion or another untrusted construct still defers, and a deny still wins.
- One install command gets the highest release: `uv tool install git+ssh://git@github.com/aleskalfas/project-kit.git@latest` (#2052). The tag `latest` points at the highest release's commit, and the release workflow moves it there whenever a release lands. `pkit init` ends by printing that command, the errors of `.pkit/cli/pkit` that advise installing pkit give it, and the command reference's "Installing pkit on PATH" recommends it. Version tags stay as they are: a project records the exact version it runs, `@v<version>` installs a given release, and `pkit upgrade` picks the highest `v<version>`, skipping `latest`. A git clone that fetched `latest` before must force-fetch it (`git fetch --force origin tag latest`), while `uv tool install` fetches it afresh.
- `pkit refs lookup --json` takes several ids and answers each in one document, `{schema_version, lookups}`, in the order given: where it lies, or why it does not resolve. The exit is 0 either way, since each entry says which. A script that reads several records starts `pkit` once for all of them (#2025). Without `--json` the command takes one id and prints as before.
- The friction writers write a change's answers into answer files of its own, as COR-062 and ADR-068 decide. Where an artefact's block holds no answer, `pkit friction revalidate`, `defer` and `revisit` write into the one answer file in `.pkit/project/friction/answers/` that no commit holds, or add one named for its UTC time and eight random hexadecimal digits. The entry's `read` names every other answer file present on the artefact. Two uncommitted files that each answer one artefact refuse the write, and a revalidation re-states the deferrals it keeps in its own entry. Where the block still holds answers, the writers write there as before, until the move. The new reading command `pkit friction move-plan [--json]` plans that move: each `moved-<hash>.yaml` it would add and each artefact file's new text. The backbone migration `2.0.0/002-move-answers-to-files.sh` writes those bytes. It leaves what the project receives by sync, never writes over an answer file, and writes nothing on a second run. An artefact the project receives by sync, such as a rule of a method rule set, owes it no answer: neither check asks it one or reports it stale, `explain` gives its state as `received`, and the writers refuse it (COR-062 point 9).
- A reviewer now checks every answer no change owes that a change writes, whoever decided it, in every project (COR-064). That is an answer on an artefact a change was not asked about, a revisit entry the change check does not ask for, and a reason for having no anchors. A person who decides what reaches the default branch may also name one agent to decide these answers in a person's place. The backbone configuration gains `friction.delegate`, the agent's name, which counts where the default branch holds it. `pkit validate` fails a name no agent of the project carries, and warns of an agent whose definition does not read COR-064. The delegate never decides an answer it drafted, judges from what the files hold, and refers to a person what only an account settles. COR-050 points 1, 3, 14 and 16 and COR-062 point 7 are refined to match, and so are core rule 20 and the agents README's "Friction writers". With no delegate named, a person decides each such answer.
- `pkit decisions validate` warns of each citation in a record that names a point its record neither holds nor retires, written after the record's id as `#N`, as `point N` or `points N and M`, or as a label alone: `DEC-035 D6` where DEC-035 numbers its points, or `point 9` past a record's last. `pkit refs validate` warns of the same in an agent's or a skill's body, and the `decisions` and `refs` members of `pkit validate` carry both. Each warning names the file and line and fails no command, since the citations are read from prose. The decisions README's "Citing" says which forms are read and how a bare `DEC-NNN` is read.
- `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives each artefact's path anchors with the files each stands on, `path_anchors`, matched by the rule both friction checks use: what `friction.exclude` leaves out and the answer files are not among them, and a dead anchor stands on none. A capability's script reads what such an anchor covers from it, and matches no pattern itself.
- `pkit decisions check-diff` lists, and fails nothing on, each record accepted at the branch point that the change alters or removes: each point the head adds, alters or retires, with its title, each of the record's title, status and supersession fields that changed, and a change to the text its points stand on or to its other text. Whether such an edit changes what the record decides is for whoever accepts it to judge, by the one test the decisions README's "Refining an accepted record" now states. `--head <rev>` reads a commit's records from git objects, and `--json` prints the findings and the list as one document, for a landing command that shows the list.
- `pkit refs rot` also lists each accepted record whose body cites a record that is still proposed, in every id-space, with the lines it cites it on, against the acceptance gate (#2155). A capability's decision is read cited as `[<capability>:DEC-NNN]` from any record, or as a bare `DEC-NNN` inside that capability's own records, unless another capability's name stands just before it. The superseded check reads an agent's or a skill's citations of a capability's decision too. The command still reports and exits 0.
- A new lookup, `pkit refs records-for`, goes from the files a change touches to the accepted records their anchors lead to, the other way from `pkit refs lookup`. It lists apart the records tied to a file directly, by a record's own path anchor or by the artefact at the file, and each artefact that anchors the file, the narrowest first, with the records it rests on. The project's own records come first, and a superseded record is listed by the records that replace it. A new core rule, rule 22, tells an agent to run it before it changes files. The decisions README, "Finding the records a path leads to", says what it reads and what it cannot tell.
- `pkit friction prune [--yes|--dry-run] [--json]` removes each answer file in which no answer is live at HEAD (#2207, COR-062 point 8). It judges liveness from HEAD's full history, as `pkit friction check --all` does. A file holding one live answer stays whole. A file with no live answer is kept, and named with why, where removing it would change one that is live: it cannot be read, its removal brings back an answer it replaced, or a live deferral keeps its point from it. It removes files from the working tree, adds none and stages nothing, so the removal is committed as a change of its own. It asks before it removes, or takes `--yes`, and refuses an uncommitted change in the answer folder and a shallow clone. The whole-repository check's line on answer files names the command, and its `prunable` lists what the command would remove. `pkit friction resolve`, added after 1.149.0, is removed before any release shipped it: it settled conflicts between answers in the artefacts' front-matter blocks, where no answer lives after the move (COR-062 point 11).
- The core's operational rules are now the backbone's loaded rule set `OPS`, in a new area, `.pkit/rule-sets/` (COR-068). Each rule keeps its number and gains a permanent id, a status and an origin: core rule 19 is `backbone:RS-OPS-019`. `.pkit/rules/core.md` keeps its path and is generated from the set, each rule headed `### N. Title (RS-OPS-0NN)`, and arrives with the set by sync as before. The rule-set schema gains an optional `loaded` key, which refuses a scope, an inheritance, checks and extension points. `pkit rule-sets generate` writes a loaded set's file, and `pkit validate` fails one out of step with its set where the set is authored. Its `refs` member warns of a citation such as "core rule 99" of a missing or retired rule. Each rule of `OPS` is anchored, or gives the reason it has none, so the friction checks now run in every project from its first install and count none of them as unanchored. A project's own `project.md` stays a plain file and needs no migration; the rules README says how to convert it.
- `pkit rule-sets reach <path>...` answers for the files given, so a writer can read the rules of its files before it writes them (#2273). The walk is the same, and the paths only narrow its answer to the artefacts at them. A relative path is read from the working directory. Each path is answered with what the state holds there: an artefact, or the entries of a collection file; a file a declared place matches whose front matter does not parse; a file that is no artefact; a folder; no file; or a path outside the repository. None of these is an error. The read view lists each path once, with the rules that bind on each artefact there or why none binds, then the text of each rule that binds, once each, with its set and its status. The entries of one collection file that have the same answer are listed together. The document gains a top-level `paths`, added within `schema_version` 1: `null` when no path is given, else each path in the order given, `{given, path, answer, artefacts}`. Given paths, its `artefacts` holds only the artefacts at them. `--at`, `--preview` and `--json` work with the paths as they do without them.
- The core rules gain rule 24, in "Before changing files": an agent follows the accepted rules that rule sets bind on what its change owns. Before its last commit it runs `pkit rule-sets altered` once and follows each rule it lists as binding, at the head for a file the change adds, edits or brings under a rule and at the base for one it removes; `pkit rule-sets reach` with the files is an optional look before writing. Where `altered` lists none, the rule asks nothing more. These are the rules the rule-set reviewer judges the change against, and the rule adds none of its own. No existing rule is renumbered.
- The core rules gain rule 23, `RS-OPS-023`, under "Before changing files": before making a choice later work will build on, such as a library, an interface or format others use, a convention or a boundary between parts, draft the record that decides it, and let no work rest on the choice until a person accepts it (COR-065 point 3). A change to what an accepted record decides needs a record too, and for a record a project received from elsewhere, the project follows it in its own files or proposes the change where the record is kept. A choice that binds nothing beyond its own change, or that an accepted record already makes, needs no record. An agent now meets the rule at every session start, not only when a person asks for a record. No existing rule is renumbered, and the set `OPS` moves to its next major, as a new accepted rule does.
- Each rules file a session loads may now have a word budget (COR-069, ADR-077). A loaded rule set gives its loaded file's as `budget` in its front matter, beside `loaded: true`; the backbone's set `OPS` gives core.md's, at its size today. Your own `.pkit/rules/project.md`, kept as a plain file, takes its budget from the new configuration key `rule-sets.plain-file-budget`, which nothing sets for you: without it, the file has no budget. A word is a run of characters none of which is Unicode white space. `pkit validate`'s `rule-sets` member prints one line for each rules file, with its count against its budget, in every project. It fails a file past its budget where its owner can shorten it (`rules-file-over-budget`), so never for core.md in a project that receives it, and fails a component's loaded set without a budget where the set is authored (`loaded-set-without-budget`). It warns of the key where you keep no plain rules file (`plain-file-budget-unused`). The rule-set schema refuses `budget` on an ordinary set. `pkit rule-sets generate` prints each file's count against its budget, and `pkit rule-sets altered` lists each budget a change raises, lowers, adds or removes, the raises first, and gains `budgets` in its JSON. Nothing needs migrating: the key and `budget` are optional.
- `pkit workspace remove <path>...` removes files and folders an agent no longer needs from the agent workspace, `.agent-workspace/` at the checkout's root. It reads each path as the shell expanded it and tests every one before it removes any. One refused path refuses the call, which then removes nothing and names each refused path and why. It refuses a path outside the checkout's own folder, the folder itself, `.` and `..`, a path at or under a child of the folder named after a registered capability, where that capability keeps its lasting state, and a nested repository or worktree. A symlink given is removed as a link, and no link is followed. The cross-repository guard runs first, and the command takes no confirmation with the call. `--dry-run` tests every path and removes nothing. It exits 0 when every path is gone, 1 when refused or stopped partway, and 2 on a usage error. It is decided as any `pkit` command: it runs with no prompt wherever a profile grants `kit`, and `read-only` denies it. Core rule 16 now asks agents to remove what they wrote with it, never with `rm`, and only what they wrote; the set `OPS` moves to its next major, since the rule is tightened.
- A capability's operational rules now reach every session where it is installed (COR-070, ADR-076). A capability may ship one loaded rule set in its `rule-sets/` folder; its loaded file is `rules.md` at the root of its tree, written by `pkit rule-sets generate` and checked where the capability is authored, as `.pkit/rules/core.md` is. Its preface names the capability, and its rules are cited `<capability>:RS-<SET>-NNN`. **The capabilities' rules index.** The backbone renders `.pkit/rules/capabilities.md`, one entry per registered capability that ships a loaded set, in the form the adapter declares under its new `loaded_rules:` key, at install, sync, upgrade and each capability's install, register, upgrade and uninstall. Nobody edits it, and sync and upgrade stop on a file at its path that lacks its marker. The new `pkit rule-sets loaded [--json] [--index <adapter>]` lists every layer's loaded file with its owner, and `pkit status` shows the index. **Validation** fails, under `rule-sets`, an index out of step with the registered capabilities, an entry whose file is missing, and a host file such as `CLAUDE.md` without the line that reaches the index (`loaded-rules-undelivered`), and warns of an adapter with no `loaded_rules:`. It also refuses, in any loaded set, a token outside code that opens with the import prefix a registered adapter declares, `@` for Claude Code: **a project's own loaded set holding a bare `@name` outside code now fails validation**; put it in backticks. **What a step newly binds.** Install, sync and upgrade, and a capability's install, register and upgrade, now print, for each loaded set they deliver, core's included, the rules it newly binds, changes or retires. The harness requirements gain `session-loads-rules`, which `pkit harness report` reports indeterminate. Core's rules now say how every layer's rules stand to them, and core rule 9 points to COR-008 for branch naming.
- `pkit validate`'s packages member now warns on a capability alias another name shadows. The dispatcher gives a top-level name to a backbone command first, then to a capability's own name, then to the first capability in manifest order that declares it as an alias. Before, an alias that lost this order reached nothing and nothing said so. The warning is located at the `aliases` entry and names the alias, its capability and what holds the name: the backbone command, the other capability's name, or the capability that declared the same alias first. It reads the same table the dispatcher binds, so the two cannot disagree. It is a warning because `pkit <capability> …` still reaches the capability.
- The `--json` documents of `pkit friction check`, `pkit friction check --all`, `pkit friction debt` and `pkit friction explain` carry `schema_version: 1` at their top level, as `pkit friction artefacts --json` already did. Each is raised when a key a reader relies on changes its meaning or goes, never for a key added, so a reader can refuse a version it does not know instead of misreading the document. A document without the key comes from a backbone that predates it and reads as version 1.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The agent deploy carries an agent's `model:` and `effort:` into the deployed definition under `.claude/agents/`, the keys Claude Code reads from an agent file's front matter. Each is taken from the overlay's `overrides.<agent>.model` / `.effort` when set, else from the agent's front matter; an absent or `inherit` value writes no key, so the harness default applies and a shipped agent that sets neither deploys exactly as before. A value outside the accepted set is not written: the agent still deploys and inherits, and the run prints a `warning` line naming the value.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The agent deploy rebases an agent's storyboards onto their source paths. A source that declares a storyboard by its bare sibling filename (`storyboard.md`) is deployed with that entry rewritten to the storyboard's project-root-relative path, in `storyboards:` and wherever the body cites it as a whole path, so the copy in `.claude/agents/` reads the storyboard from where it lives; an entry already written as a source path deploys unchanged. The adapter README documents it under `deploy-agents.sh`.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The Claude Code adapter ships `undeploy-capability.sh <name>`, which removes one capability's deployed skills and agents from `.claude/`: a skill folder whose `SKILL.md` is the relative symlink `deploy-skills.sh` writes into the capability, and a resolved agent copy carrying `deploy-agents.sh`'s marker for an agent the capability ships, flat or folder form, unless a project agent of that name outranks it. Adopter content and the capability's own subtree are never touched. The script is idempotent and exits 2 on anything but one capability name. The adapters README documents it beside the deploy primitives as the contract every adapter answers.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The Claude Code adapter ships its agent runner, `run-agent.py`, registered in its `package.yaml` under `commands:` as `run-agent` (ADR-066 points 1, 4 and 10). The backbone's `pkit agents run` starts it, and it starts `claude -p --agent <agent>`, with `--effort <level>` where the run sets one, in the run's folder, with the brief on standard input. Those flags are the closed set: it builds no command line with any other, so no flag that bypasses permissions is ever passed. It refuses a run when `claude` is not on `PATH`, when the agent is not deployed under `.claude/agents/`, or when the request carries a key it does not run. At the limit, or asked to stop, it ends the harness and every process still descended from it, a shell command Claude Code started in a session of its own included. It finds them through `ps`, and a process that left the harness's tree before then is not reached. It runs under the system `python3`, standard library only, as the permission hook does. The adapter's README documents it, with the closed set of flags ("The agent runner"). Upgrade the adapter with the backbone that ships `pkit agents run`: without the runner, the command refuses every run.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The agent runner gains its confined form (ADR-066 points 2 to 9). It reads the agent's definition from the run's clone as the deploy resolves it, and refuses a field outside the methodology's shape. It passes only its own settings: the run's allow list and denies as native rules, `dontAsk`, the sandbox on and failing closed with no retry outside it and no network host, and memory off. It starts Claude Code in the run's workspace with a closed set of flags and a scrubbed environment, and ends the run at once where the start report does not match those settings. A confined run is refused outside the Claude Code versions the adapter's author tested it on. That range ships empty, so no confined run starts until the author runs the new probe, `probe-confined-run.py`, and widens it. Every run, confined or not, is refused inside an agent run and sets `PKIT_AGENT_RUN` for the harness. The adapter's README states the form, its flags and what stays open.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The adapter declares how far Claude Code meets each harness requirement, in the new `harness-support.yaml` (COR-047 point 2). It answers `delegated-result-complete` at a cost, for every version: a helper's result arrives whole unless the agent-teams feature is on and the helper was dispatched with a name. It answers `unattended-confined-run` at a cost, for no version yet: the bound is the confined runner's tested range, empty until the probe passes. The README's "Harness support" says the same in prose.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — Every session and every subagent is told that the agent workspace replaces the harness's session scratch directory. A new start hook, `session-start-hook.py`, gives one sentence as context at each session's start and each subagent's start: "The session scratch directory your environment names is replaced by `.agent-workspace/` at the repository root: keep every intermediate file there, never in that directory." The baseline settings register it under `SessionStart` and `SubagentStart`, so the next merge writes both into `.claude/settings.json`. Every agent the deploy writes ends with the same sentence. The permission hook's ask on a write outside the repository is unchanged.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — `CLAUDE.md` now reaches each installed capability's rules (ADR-076). The adapter declares `loaded_rules:` in its package, and its merge keeps `@.pkit/rules/capabilities.md` present directly after `@.pkit/rules/core.md`, inserted wherever it is absent; the next sync inserts it, so no migration is due. A `CLAUDE.md` it creates holds core's include, the index's line and the project's, in that order. The merge now writes through the file, so a linked `CLAUDE.md` keeps its link and the file keeps its mode, and a CRLF file gets CRLF lines. It no longer puts the project's include back on its own, takes an H1 inside fenced code for the host's, or treats an include of core's rules written only in fenced code or a code span as present: it then inserts nothing and reports it. Its answer to the new harness requirement `session-loads-rules` is at a cost, naming the sessions that skip `CLAUDE.md`, and the runner's known gap apart.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The adapter's package.yaml declares a description, which the package schema now requires of every component.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The Claude Code adapter realizes the agent workspace without an allow rule: inside the session's working directory — the worktree root for a subagent in a worktree — Claude Code's own modes already approve reads, accepted edits and sandboxed commands, so nothing is written to `settings.json`; the hook allows the path-scoped `workspace` grant for the file tools on a target in the folder, and `permission-enforcement.yaml` declares the new `path-scoped-allow` dimension as hook-enforced — not a confinement boundary; reach stays `filesystem-confinement`, the sandbox's. A here-document or redirect into the folder is judged like any other shell write. The README states this, and that the harness's own per-session scratch directory lies outside a subagent's working directory, where allow rules did not stop the prompts.
- **doc-examples 1.0.0** — The new doc-examples capability runs the console examples of your pages as tests, so a change in behaviour fails your checks wherever in the code it comes from. Its run, `.pkit/capabilities/doc-examples/scripts/run-examples.py`, is a program you start by its path, never a command of `pkit`, and it needs nothing beyond Python's standard library and `pkit`. It runs each `console` fence in one shell, in a scratch project your own setup step builds outside the repository, and compares what each command prints with the page, through placeholders for what varies. `--update` writes new output into the page for review. `pkit doc-examples pages` lists the pages it reads, by default the documents of living-docs' page type, and `pkit doc-examples validate` joins `pkit validate`. The privilege `doc-examples:run-examples` recognizes a call to the run, and no grant of it ships. The contract a page is written against is the capability's versioned surface from its first release, so that release is a major, 1.0.0.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The capability's project configuration has a schema: in `.pkit/capabilities/living-docs/project/config.yaml`, `spaces` names each documentation space's `entry-point`, and `places` assigns each place to exactly one space, keyed by the place as `friction.places` in the backbone configuration writes it. A place inside a documentation root belongs to that root's space unless assigned; a place outside every root is assigned here (DEC-001 point 1). `pkit validate` checks the file's shape.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The capability ships its first artefacts (DEC-001). Its package metadata declares the two documentation roots as the default places of their spaces and a definitions location, `living-docs/` under the internal root, whose `rule-sets/` folder holds each space's definition as a project rule set. The shared method rule set, `living-docs:LDOC` (`RS-LDOC-001` to `RS-LDOC-006`, the rules DEC-001 point 3 states, each accepted with the decision as its origin), ships in `rule-sets/ldoc.md`. `templates/` holds a space-definition template, inheriting `LDOC`, and the signpost page template; a page names its `reader` and `kind`, whose shape is `schemas/page.schema.json`. A space in the project configuration may name its `definition`. `pkit living-docs validate`, run by `pkit validate` as its `living-docs:spaces` member, checks the places and their assignment, what is never a page, pages' fields, entry points and definitions; reader resolution stays dormant until the readers point ships, and the validator says so.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The capability ships its agent, `living-docs` (DEC-001 points 5, 6 and 8), which the deploy writes with the other agents. It proposes and never applies: it is read-only on the repository, never runs a friction writer, and lands every proposal in the agent workspace, under `.agent-workspace/living-docs/`. It resolves a stale or deferred page's friction by proposing the page change with its evidence (the commits behind the changed anchor, the anchor to re-point or the text to rewrite) as a diff and a pull-request body. It reads a page as its declared reader against `LDOC` and its space's rules and writes a findings record, each finding citing its rule and passage, only when something was found; as a pull-request comment only when asked. On a project with existing documentation it proposes one onboarding plan (each unclassified document's space, splits and rewrites, the anchors each statement needs, which code-to-doc mapping rules become page anchors) behind a single approval gate, and drafts the changes only once it is approved. Its scripted flows are in the sibling `storyboard.md`; the capability README documents the agent and the onboarding path.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — A reference page template joins the signpost template, so a page that describes one surface to its reader — an area, a capability, an adapter — has a format of its own.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The capability provides the `pkit::documentation` role and accepts its two data points (DEC-001 point 7). `pkit::documentation:readers` (version 1, `schemas/readers.schema.json`: entries `{id, description}`) is `union`, with a built-in `user` and `maintainer` always included, and inert policy `fail`; `pkit::documentation:reading-evidence` (version 1, `schemas/reading-evidence.schema.json`: entries keyed `<path>@<commit>#<check>`, the commit by its full name) is `union`, with no default, and inert policy `fallback`. The validator now resolves each page's `reader` against the readers point and fails a page whose reader the point does not hold, naming the readers it does; a readers point that does not resolve is reported once, as "readers unresolved", rather than per page. A new query command, `pkit living-docs fill-doc-check`, contributes to the work-tracking role's `pkit::work-tracking:doc-check` point: one `page-stale` obligation per page the whole-repository friction check reports stale or deferred, and one `code-undocumented` obligation per uncovered surface path, each with the source `friction`. A `code-undocumented` obligation names the code as `path` and no `document`: a page changing never meets it, only a page anchoring the path, after which the command no longer gives it. The contribution is inert when no work-tracking capability is installed.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs validate` checks a page's body against the structure its kind declares (RS-LDOC-004: pages of a kind follow one format). Each kind the capability ships declares its structure once, in the capability's `schemas/page-kinds.yaml`: the sections every page of the kind carries, each a heading level and, where the kind fixes it, its text, in order unless the kind says otherwise. A reference page and a signpost each carry a title, written as `# Title` at the start of a line. While RS-LDOC-004 is accepted, a page that lacks a section, or carries one out of order, is an error naming the page, its kind, the section and the declared structure, so `pkit validate` fails; under any other status no body is checked. A `#` line that is indented, or inside fenced code, a block quote or an HTML comment, is no section; nor is an underlined or HTML heading, an empty one, or a template's unfilled `<placeholder>`. A page may carry other sections. A page whose kind declares no structure, such as a kind your project adds, is reported with its kind and never failed. A declaration that cannot be read is one error, and no body is checked.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — A page can rest on a source outside the repository — a standard, a specification, another project's documentation — by anchoring it by name: `source: [keep-a-changelog]`. living-docs registers the `source` anchor kind. Capture each source once, in `.pkit/capabilities/living-docs/project/sources/<name>.yaml`: its `title`, an optional `url`, and the `version` of its text your pages were last checked against, quoted. Any change to that file is the source's change: when you record a new version, the change check asks every page anchored to the source to answer in the same pull request, and the whole-repository check finds such a page stale until it does. Nothing fetches a source, so a change nobody records is not seen. A name no file captures is a dead anchor. `pkit living-docs resolve-source <name>` prints the file a name resolves to. `pkit validate` checks a captured source's shape under `data`; fails a sources folder named in another case, and anything else in it but a hidden entry, which it leaves alone, or a file not ending in `.yaml`, which it reports; and reports an anchor no file captures, a captured source nothing names, and one carrying `schema_version` or `binds_to`. A rule's origin may cite a captured source. Uninstalling living-docs removes the captured sources with its project tier. It needs the backbone this release ships, the first that runs a resolver a capability registers.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The capability declares its types, and its validator reads each document's type (DEC-001 points 1, 2, 4 and 6, COR-056, #1411). Its type registry, `friction.types`, holds `page`, with `kind` as its kind field and project places admitted, and `template`, which admits none. Both documentation roots give `living-docs:page`, and the definitions location is a new place that gives `living-docs:template`. So a document under a root is a page only where it declares its page kind in `kind`. One that names a reader but no kind is an unclassified document, no longer a page that fails. A page template copied into the definitions location is a template, never a page. A project place outside every root that names no type still holds the pages that declare their kind there. Where places nest, `pkit living-docs validate` takes each document's places in the backbone's order (COR-056 point 3) rather than its own: a longer literal part wins, and at equal length an exact file beats a glob, which beats a folder. So a glob can now win over a folder, where every folder used to rank ahead of every glob. Two project places tie when they rank equal there. The backbone ranks the places of artefacts only, so a file without front matter whose places would give it different spaces, or one of which lies outside every root with no assignment, is reported and counted unclassified. Where every one of its places is such an unassigned place, whichever holds it needs an assignment, which fails. A document of another component's type, of a type of the project's own, or held by another component is its owner's: the validator no longer reads a `reader` or `kind` on it, so carrying one fails nothing. A place another component declares without a type no longer keeps a document from the root around it: one there that declares a page kind is a page, and `pkit validate` reports that the root typed the component's file. A decision record that would otherwise be a page fails. Where a space's definition carries a scope, a place assigned to that space must lie in that scope and in no other space definition's scope, checked on each page it gives the space. Where the definition selects by type, an assigned place outside every root that does not give `living-docs:page` fails. A definition with no scope is held to nothing. `LDOC` declares the parts its checks enforce: RS-LDOC-004's structure and RS-LDOC-003's field, each by `living-docs:validate`. The validator reads the types and the ranked places from `pkit friction artefacts --json`, which the backbone this release ships gives, so the capability requires it. An older backbone's document is one error, and no space is checked. ([#1510])
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The living-docs agent now proposes an anchor on the one point of a record, or the one section or lead of a page, that a statement rests on, in its fixes, reader-reviews and onboarding plans, and validation explains that a source anchor written with `#` names no part.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs new` stamps a page, or a space's definition, paired with the new `docs-author` skill (DEC-001 points 2 and 4; COR-005). `new space <space>` writes the definition under the definitions location's `rule-sets/`, inheriting `living-docs:LDOC` at its current major and scoped to the space's pages by the page type, with the user space's own rule written accepted. It names the file as the space's `definition` in the capability's project configuration, keeping every other byte, and records the definitions location on first use. `new page <file> --space <space> --kind <kind> --reader <id> --title <text>` writes a page from its kind's template, with its reader, its kind and the anchors given. Each runs the validator's own check before and after its write, and puts back what it wrote and refuses when the write brings an error the project did not have. `--dry-run` writes nothing. The validator's report of a space with no definition now names `pkit living-docs new space`, and a definition's scope for a root that is the repository itself is written `**`.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — Where project-management is installed, living-docs contributes the core `rule-set-reviewer` to its merge gate, under the floor `touches-ruled-artefact` (DEC-001 point 9; COR-057). A pull request that alters a document an accepted rule binds on, or brings one under such a rule, now needs the reviewer's `APPROVED` before `done-work` merges it, a page or not. The reviewer runs on the local path only, through `review-pr`. While the contribution is in force, resolving any pull request reads its base and head commits through `pkit rule-sets altered`, so a shallow checkout fails it. software-analysis contributes the same rule, and the reviewer is required once. To keep your gate as it was, list `rule-set-reviewer` under `review.agents.contributed_opt_out` in project-management's configuration, once for each capability that contributes it. living-docs declares project-management as an optional dependency, from project-management's first release with the floor, which ships with this one: living-docs still works without project-management, and install and upgrade hold an installed one to that range. The capability requires the backbone that ships the reviewer, the command and optional dependencies. Requires project-management >=0.55.0.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The capability declares its agent as the fixer of your pages (DEC-001 points 5 and 7). It contributes to the work-tracking role's fixers point, `pkit::work-tracking:fixers`, a static value naming the `living-docs` agent for the type `living-docs:page`, described as "your pages". Only pages of that type are its: a page in a place that gives no type is not. You grant the fixer through project-management, by answering `pkit pm ask-fixers` or running `pkit pm grant-fixer living-docs`. No command that starts granted fixers ships yet, so a grant starts nothing until one does. The agent's body and storyboard say what it takes from such a command and what it returns. In such a run it proposes friction fixes and nothing else. It writes each page's answer as fields, never as a command. It holds with one question a page whose fix needs what only a person knows. It ends with one line per page: proposed, held, or none. The contribution is inert without a work-tracking capability.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The new record living-docs DEC-002 decides how a page shows diagrams, images, console output and callouts. Each is neutral content with no styling, and your presentation decides how it looks. A diagram is a `mermaid` fence in the page by default, and never a fact's home. A screenshot or a recording is anchored at the script that produced it, never at the file. A command and its output sit in a shell-session fence, such as `console`, which your own runner checks, and a callout uses the standard `[!NOTE]` syntax. `LDOC` gains two proposed rules, which bind nothing until a major of the set accepts them. `RS-LDOC-007` succeeds `RS-LDOC-002`: an accepted record or rule, an analysis artefact, a captured source or code the page anchors may be a fact's home, and a diagram never is. `RS-LDOC-008` asks a text alternative of each diagram, image or recording that carries meaning. `pkit living-docs validate` fails a `mermaid` fence without an `accTitle` or an `accDescr` on each page where `RS-LDOC-008` binds. While the rule is proposed it binds on no page, and the summary's new line says so.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — An entry of the `pkit::documentation:readers` point may now carry `paths`, the paths its reader takes through your pages, as living-docs DEC-001 point 7 decides. Each path lists under `pages` the pages the reader reads in order, each named by its Markdown file's path from the repository's root, with an optional `description` of what the reader comes to do. A path names pages, never a pattern of files, so a glob such as `docs/runbooks/**` fits no path. The point stays at version 1, and an entry without paths reads as before. The `living-docs` agent's reader-review takes the paths of a page's reader from the point. Where a rule asks that reader paths stay unbroken, as the user space's own rule does, the review follows each path that passes through the page.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — A space's entry in living-docs' project configuration may now name the space's `audience`, who the space serves in words, as living-docs DEC-001 point 1 decides (#1922). `pkit validate` checks it under `data`, and refuses one that is not text or names nobody. The configuration stays at version 1, and an entry without an audience reads as before. Until the readers point resolves, the `living-docs` agent's reader-review reads a page as the audience its space's entry names. Where the entry names none, it reads a user page as users and a technical page as maintainers. For a space the project adds whose entry names none, it names that entry, and stops.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs validate` reports what a page mentions and its anchors do not cover, where the rule on grounding (RS-LDOC-001) binds: a path to a file the repository holds, a decision record's or a rule's id, and a `pkit` command. `pkit validate` shows one report per page, and the human view lists each mention with an anchor that would cover it. It never fails. A passage that only names a thing is marked with `<!-- unanchored: <reason> -->` right after the mention, and a mark that passes nothing over is reported (DEC-001 point 6).
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs fill-doc-check` gives no answer when `pkit friction check --all --json` carries a `schema_version` other than 1, so the documentation check reports itself unresolved rather than read a document it does not understand, and the `living-docs` agent refuses an `explain`, `debt` or `check --all` document of another version, naming the command and the version, and proposes nothing from it. A document without the key comes from a backbone that predates it and reads as version 1, so an older backbone still works.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs validate` reads the accepted reason for a page having no anchors — the core friction block's `unanchored-because` (COR-050 point 1) — and counts the pages left unanchored in its summary: those without an accepted reason, the onboarding work still to do, and apart from them those accepted with one (DEC-001 point 8, which now names the field). The human view lists both, each accepted page with its reason; an excluded page is in neither, and neither count fails. It reads each page's anchors and reason from `pkit friction artefacts --json`, whose `unanchored_because` comes with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `substrate-map.yaml` gains a `board:` binding arm, so a brownfield adopter can declare that a classification axis is carried by a field on their configured Projects-v2 board. Parameterless by design (`board: true`): the field identity stays a write parameter on the `after_create_issue` hook, and a field name in the map would be a claim about a live board the file cannot self-validate. Admissible on `priority` and `workstream` only — `type` is label-carried by functional dependency (PR-title alignment reads the label), and a board-carried `state` awaits a detector kind that can read a board Status field. `board: true` under a config declaring no board is refused. An optional `default:` may accompany the arm as corroborating metadata, and one with no matching `set-board-field` hook behind it is reported.
Until now the map could not express board carriage at all, and the prerequisite check's remediation told adopters to spell it `unsupported: true` — a declaration the schema defines as the axis having no encoding and every rule needing it degrading. That guidance is withdrawn.
Additive: an axis absent from a present map keeps today's carriage, and every existing map continues to validate unchanged. The exactly-one-binding constraint grows by one `oneOf` branch; the previous branches' explicit mutual-exclusion clauses were redundant under `oneOf` and are removed, with no change to the set of accepted documents. ([#794])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `back-fill` gains a third change kind, `set-axis-label` — the named repair path for a corpus damaged by the carriage bug (DEC-051). It writes a classification axis's value as a LABEL, resolved through the write seam under the adopter's own `remap` (their `P0`, never the kit's `priority:High`); a declared default with no `remap` entry is reported UNRESOLVABLE and skipped rather than written as the raw kit value. Candidacy is scoped by carriage — only an axis the carriage accessor resolves to the adopter's or the kit's labels qualifies, so a `board:`-bound axis stays the existing `set-board-field` kind's job and a `title-prefix` / `derive` / unsupported axis is not writable here at all; `state` is excluded outright as lifecycle-derived. Its intent (the value to seed) is read from a label-carried axis's per-axis `default:` in `substrate-map.yaml` — the declaration point DEC-037 §3 names for a label-bound axis, mirroring how the two hook-driven kinds read theirs from the `after_create_issue` hook. It proposes ONLY for issues carrying no value on the axis, so it never overwrites a value somebody chose and a re-run over a repaired corpus plans nothing. The residual pre-check gate, the confirmation gesture and the re-validate-at-apply read all apply unchanged, and its `--emit-script` guard fails CLOSED on a failed re-read (deliberately not the fail-open shape the milestone and board-field fragments carry). The `--json` plan gains additive `axis` / `axis_value` / `label_value` / `carrier_labels` keys and an `intent_errors` list, at the same `schema_version` — a plan saved by the previous version still applies.
The value written comes from the axis's substrate-map `default:` — the single declaration point a label-carried axis already has — or from `--set AXIS=VALUE` on the invocation, repeatable per axis, for a corpus with no default declared or a repair that wants a different value once. VALUE is the methodology value, not your substrate's label: it resolves through your remap exactly as a declared default does, so the kit never writes a label you do not manage.
A declared default that cannot be used stays silent, because you did not ask that run to touch that axis. An axis named with `--set` is refused BY NAME instead — an unknown axis, a value your remap has no entry for, or an axis carried by something other than a label. Silently ignoring an explicit request is how an operator concludes the repair ran and found nothing to do. ([#818])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `show-pr` gains a `review-history` field (#905): every reviewer verdict posted on the PR, per reviewer, in posting order — including verdicts a later review round superseded and ones marked stale. Each verdict carries its token, timestamp, full body, and whether it is the reviewer's current verdict (the one `--field review` shows). Available as `--field review-history` and as a new `review_history` key in `--json`; existing fields and keys are unchanged. It is built on the same shared verdict recogniser as `review` and the merge gate (a new `all_verdicts` primitive that the latest-per-reviewer selection now reduces), so what counts as a verdict is identical, and the gate's behaviour is unchanged. ([#907])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Filing an issue from a prepared body now links it under the parent named on the body's first line, even without `--parent`, and refuses a `--parent` that names a different issue; the new `link-parent` command links issues that already exist from that same line, one or more by number or every open issue at once, with a dry run that lists what each issue would get, a prompt before it writes, and no second link where one already exists.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Four gaps in the issue and PR verbs are closed (#1049). `close-issue --mode=pr-merge --pr <M>` closes an open Task whose work landed in merged PR M without the PR naming it: the PR is verified merged, the checkbox close-gate runs, the reference is posted as a comment once, the issue closes as completed and the closure cascade runs; without `--pr` the mode is unchanged. `edit-issue --milestone <number|title>` moves an issue to an open milestone, validated as `create-issue --milestone` is, and `--clear-milestone` detaches it; a first line naming the old milestone as the parent follows the move, and an edit that would move the issue in the lifecycle is refused. `open-pr --closes` and the new `edit-pr --closes` repeat, so one PR can close several issues, each with its own `Closes #N` line; `open-pr --body-file` gains a line for any closing issue the file does not name. `reopen-issue` now removes the state label, so a reopened issue reads as backlog (milestone set) or todo instead of staying at done, and it repairs an open issue still labelled done. `edit-pr --force` no longer fails before posting its audit comment.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-pr` gains one uniform reviewer **effort** knob (#1046). A reviewer started headless reads the operator's user settings and so reasons at the operator's own interactive effort, which a review pass rarely needs; the only way to lower it was to lower the operator's sessions with it. Resolution precedence: the new `--effort <level>` flag > the `PKIT_REVIEW_AGENT_EFFORT` env var > the new `review.agents.effort` config key > unset (nothing is passed; the harness default applies). Levels are the harness's own — `low`, `medium`, `high`, `xhigh`, `max`; anything else is a usage error naming its source. Like the timeout, it is one value for every reviewer, not a per-agent map (COR-007); per-agent model and effort policy is #1047's.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The capability accepts a data point for the use cases settled on the default branch, `pkit::work-tracking:use-cases` (DEC-054): entries `{id, title, status, path?}`, `single`, no default, inert `fallback`, with the companion schema `schemas/use-cases.schema.json`. Whatever keeps your use cases fills it — a capability's contribution, or your own filler file — and while nothing does, nothing changes. With a set, batch planning walks the active use cases before it slices the work, names the use cases each planned Feature and Task serves in a `## Use cases` section, and shows at its approval gate the use cases the plan leaves out and the behaviour no use case describes. Over an empty set, a plan whose work touches what users do opens with a Task to author them, and only the issues that touch what users do depend on it; a plan of internal work has none, and its approval gate says why. Revised away, the Task is left out of that checkout's plans for the rest of the day, in the machine's local time, and offered again from the next day: the new command `decline-use-case-task` records that answer in a git-ignored file under `project/instance/`, and shows or clears it. `validate-issue`, `edit-issue` and `create-issue --body-file` warn (`body.use-case-citation`) when a Feature or Task body cites, in that section, a use case the point does not hold — existence only, and a withdrawn use case passes — and say in one notice (`body.use-cases-unchecked`) when the citations could not be checked. A warning: no verb refuses or changes its exit code over it. The point is read through the `outcome` of `pkit connections resolve --json`, which comes with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone. Requires backbone >=2.0.0.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — You can opt out of one reviewer an installed capability contributes to the agent-mode merge gate, without uninstalling the capability or bypassing every gated PR (#148). List `{capability, reviewer, reason}` under `review.agents.contributed_opt_out:` in `project/config.yaml`; the reason is required. That capability's rules for that reviewer leave the resolved reviewer set, so `review-pr` does not invoke it and `done-work` does not require it, while the capability's other contributions, a reviewer you register in `local_registered`, and the same reviewer contributed by another capability all still apply. `pre-check` lists each opt-out with its reason, and `review-pr` prints it next to the reviewers it invokes. An entry naming a capability that is not installed, or a reviewer it does not contribute, is an error: `pre-check` fails on it and `review-pr` and `done-work` refuse until it is fixed or removed.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The closure cascade now says when a Milestone became closeable (#414). After `close-issue` closes an issue — in any mode, and so after every `done-work` merge — the cascade checks each Milestone the issue sits in (its native Milestone field, or its `Milestone: [#<n>](../milestone/<n>)` body ref) as it checks each parent issue. A content-based or `either` Milestone whose every child issue is now closed is reported as eligible to close, with the command that closes it (`pkit pm close-milestone <n>`); a Milestone with an open child is reported as not eligible, and a date-based one as closing on its date. Nothing closes the Milestone: closing it stays your gesture. The check reads the close-trigger and the children through the same reads `close-milestone` uses, so a Milestone reported eligible is one it closes without `--force`. The cascade also no longer reads a deprecated plain `Milestone: #<n>` first line as naming issue `#<n>` as a parent.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A PR that changes only documentation and its changeset no longer draws the code-review panel (#1178). `review.floors.not_code` in the pm `project/config.yaml` lists gitignore-style path patterns that never count as code: a changed path it matches satisfies no diff-property floor, so it never pulls in a `touches-code` reviewer. When the key is absent the list is `.changes/**`, the changesets every surface-changing PR carries. A list you set replaces the default, and `[]` excludes nothing. Every other path is judged as before, so a PR that also changes a `.py` or a schema still requires the panel. `review-pr` and `done-work` read the list the same way and refuse on a malformed one. `review-pr` also skips a required reviewer whose latest verdict was posted after the PR's head commit, reading verdicts the way `done-work`'s gate does. It prints `[<name>] fresh verdict <APPROVED|CHANGES_REQUESTED> — not re-run`, and `--force` re-runs that reviewer. `review-pr` and `done-work` now depend on `pathspec`, which `check-doc-mapping` already uses. ([#1187])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Closing a date-based Milestone now rolls its open children forward (#1175). `close-milestone` moves each open child to the rollforward target — the Milestone's `Rollforward target:` line, else the next-numbered open Milestone of its category, or `--target <M>` — through `edit-issue --milestone`, so the child keeps its lifecycle state, its native Milestone field and first-line `Milestone:` ref both move, and it gets one audit comment naming the old Milestone, the new one and the close. Closed children stay on the closed Milestone; a parent assigned to it moves with its open children. An `either` Milestone rolls forward the same way from its due date on; before it, open children still hold its close, and a content-based close is unchanged. With no candidate target, or more than one, nothing is moved or closed until you name it. The audit line counts the children rolled forward and names the target; when a move fails the command exits 4, and a re-run moves the children still on the closed Milestone without moving any twice. `--dry-run` lists every move.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A new verb, `pkit pm land-work <N>`, lands an issue's pull request in one command (#1203), composing `review-pr` and `done-work`. It replaces three steps run by hand with a wait between them. It pins the PR's head once and waits for the checks on that head, judged as `done-work`'s CI gate judges them: no check reported yet is never green, a green run on an earlier head does not count, and a PR that conflicts with its base, on which no `pull_request` workflow runs, stops at once. It then runs `review-pr` for the reviewers whose verdicts are not fresh for that head, and merges it through `done-work`. The merge is authorised in argv: without `--yes`, off a terminal, it merges nothing, runs `done-work`'s gates as a dry run and stops on a `ready:` line giving the command that merges that head (`--yes --expect-head <sha>`); `--expect-head` stops a run whose head is another. Each step prints one line, and the run stops at the first step that cannot go on, its last line saying why — the push to make, the failed check and its URL, the first line of each blocking finding with its reviewer, the advisories of the approvals. It has one exit code per next action, listed in the README: a needed change (1), nothing changed (2), a head moved or not authorised (3), accepted (4), the wait ran out (5), the review incomplete (6), a re-run completes it (7), ready but not authorised (8). A re-run resumes: green checks on the same head are not waited for, fresh verdicts are not re-run, and a PR that merged meanwhile is completed. No flag skips the checks or the review. The verbs it composes change to support it, a direct run of each behaving as before: `done-work` has `run()`, which says how a run ended — merged, merged with a step after the merge owed, queued, unconfirmed, declined, head moved, refused (and whether a re-run can help), unreadable — and takes the head to land or a merged-only mode, telling a PR lookup gh did not answer from one that found none; `review-pr` has `review()`, which reviews only the head it is handed and keeps only verdicts judged fresh for it; and the CI gate tells the four states of a head's checks apart.
