- **project-management 0.55.0** — A pull request's description lists the answers its change wrote, each updated document with its author's sentence on what changed ([project-management:DEC-055-pr-lists-answers]). `open-pr` writes the change check's list of the revalidations, deferrals and reasons for having no anchors the change wrote — derived at the pushed head with `pkit friction check --json --base <the PR's base> --head`, the base always named — into a section of its own, `## Documentation this change affects`, last before the provenance footer, in plain words for a reader who does not know the methodology — an opening paragraph, then each document with what was decided (updated in this change, still accurate, out of date and fixed later) — every word of an artefact's as the check's human view shows it, each updated document followed by the sentence its author wrote under the description's own `## What changed in each updated document` (one list item per document, the document in a code span) or by a line saying there is none, with each change to the friction places or exclusions the change makes — read through `pkit friction artefacts --json --at` at both ends — as it was and as it is; it never refuses to open over the list, and leaves it out with one warning line when it cannot write it, and warns of an updated document with no sentence. `edit-pr` carries the section across every edit — of two, the latest — and drops any other, saying so, and gives each updated document's sentence as the edited description has it. A section under that heading typed by hand, with no markers, is removed by `open-pr`, `edit-pr` and `land-work`. `land-work` gains a step between the review and the merge, its lines beginning `documents this change affects:`: it derives the list at the pinned head, prints it, and writes it into the description where it is missing or differs. A change with answers merges under the same authorisation as any other: a `--yes` run that writes the list merges in the same run. Above its `ready:` line it tells, as information, what changed in each updated document, which documents are left out of date with each deferral's words, and the friction settings changed. A reviewer other than the change's author, where the project chooses one, checks the answers (DEC-055 point 3). The pm skill's landing text has the agent tell the person those lines and show every finding to judge whole before it asks. `land-work` refuses, with nothing merged: a list it cannot derive, or a base it cannot fetch (exit 2); a word or an author's sentence that reads as a closing reference or holds an HTML comment's delimiter, a list the description cannot hold, front matter the check cannot read in a file the change touches, and — where there is a list — a base whose commits since the head left it changed a file the list's words are on, or the settings it shows (exit 1); a base that moved on elsewhere stops nothing. A write of the description the host answers with an error merges nothing (exit 7). The change check runs unrouted (`PKIT_NO_ROUTE=1`) as the `pkit` first on PATH; where an anchor's resolver must run from a checkout at the head, and only then, it runs again from a temporary checkout of the head, removed afterwards — on a termination signal too — and nothing else of the repository's with it. No reader of a description takes the section as input: it closes no issue, meets no closing or Doc impact requirement and overrides no mapping. The provenance footer's markers, like the section's, now count only as whole lines, so a description that quotes one keeps every line after it. `--doc-impact-from-friction` fills an unwritten `## Doc impact` with one line counting the documents listed there, in place of a bullet per answer. A change that wrote no answers and alters no friction setting lands as before. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release whose change check lists the answers a change wrote.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A reviewer's findings now carry tags that say what each does to the merge (#1405, DEC-028): `[block]` withholds the approval, `[advisory]` withholds and holds nothing (a finding with no tag counts as one), and `[person]` leaves the finding to the person who authorises the merge. `review-pr`'s brief names the three tags, and that they widen no reviewer's remit. `pm-reviewer` writes its severity after the tag, as `[block] (hard-reject)`; a severity written alone by an older copy of it is still read. `land-work` prints each `[person]` finding whole, beside the answers, before it stops ready or asks, and holds a `--yes` run (DEC-055 point 3): it merges only on verdicts carrying them that it found when it started, standing for the head it merges. A verdict posted by the run or while it ran stops it on `ready:`, and the identical command then merges; a verdict for another head stops every `--yes` run until its reviewer reviews the head again. A remote reviewer gets no brief, so if you run one, have its setup write the tags: its untagged findings count as `[advisory]` and never reach the person. ([#1478])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A reviewer contribution can require its reviewer when a change alters a document an accepted rule binds on (#1386, DEC-032): the new floor `floor: touches-ruled-artefact` in a capability's `review-contributions.yaml`, beside `touches-code`. An artefact is altered when the change adds it, removes it, or changes its content, so a change that only writes a revalidation, or a move that keeps the content, alters nothing. A change to the declared places alone fires it too: a page a new or wider place brings in counts as added. The resolver reads the backbone's `pkit rule-sets altered` for every pull request while a contribution names the floor, so a project without one pays nothing. An artefact counts as ruled only through the rules that bind on it, never through rules a pin holds back. Your `review.floors.not_code` list narrows `touches-code` alone, and a page it lists still fires the new floor. Such a reviewer's approval stays fresh after later commits that alter no ruled artefact, read a run of your commits at a time between merges of the base, and after a clean merge of the base (DEC-028). Reading the change needs the base and head commits locally: a missing one is fetched by its object name, and a shallow checkout, a failing command or a backbone without it fails the resolution with its reason. A contribution naming a floor this release does not know now requires its reviewer on every change instead of refusing every pull request, and `pre-check` warns of each such floor. An undeployed reviewer that core ships is named with `pkit sync` as its fix.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The answers list shows each revisit entry in words (#1703, COR-050 point 16). The pull request's `## Documentation this change affects` section and `land-work`'s printed list gave a revisit entry as the bare word "revisited". Each now names the source, the target, the fields that list the target and the trigger fields the change modified, then the outcome and its reason, as the change check's own list does. A target the change revisited by changing it is listed as "updated in this change", for the reviewer. It asks for no sentence and marks its source updated nowhere. The words are read for closing references and comment delimiters, the target, the fields and the trigger fields included. Above its `ready:` line, `land-work` now tells each target of a link a `deferred` revisit entry leaves out of date, with the source that names it and the reason that names its follow-up, and the `ready:` line counts those targets beside the documents left out of date. A backbone whose change check gives no revisit entries gives the same list as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A capability declares its fixers, and a project grants them (#1743, DEC-056 points 1 and 2). The capability accepts a data point, `pkit::work-tracking:fixers`: which agent fixes the artefacts of each type, each entry `{id, agent, description}`, `union`, no default, inert `fail`, with the companion schema `schemas/fixers.schema.json`. A capability gives its entries as a static value. A fixer starts on its own only where the project granted it, in the new grant list `fixers` of `project/config.yaml`, which is empty by default. The new command `ask-fixers` asks one plain question for each agent about the types nobody was asked about. At a terminal each answer is recorded, a yes as a grant and a no as a refusal. With nobody at a terminal nothing is granted, and each question is printed with the command that grants its fixer. `list-fixers`, `grant-fixer` and `revoke-fixer` list the declared fixers with their answers, grant one and revoke one, writing only the `fixers` block. The new validator `project-management:fixers` (`check-fixers`) fails on the project's own entry naming an agent that is not deployed and on a grant list it cannot read. It warns of a grant no entry names, with the command that revokes it. It reports a capability's entry that names a type not its own, an agent it does not ship or has not deployed, or an agent that does more than propose: one that claims paths, lists no tools, or holds a tool that changes existing files or starts agents. The five commands are exempt from the bootstrap gate. Nothing starts a fixer yet, and install and upgrade do not ask the question yet. The point is read through `pkit connections resolve --json` and the type registries through `pkit friction artefacts --json`, which come with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The fixers point's version covers what a fixer run passes and returns (#1792, DEC-056 points 1, 3 and 6): the brief, the answer, the result lines and a fixed list of `none` reasons. A fixer writes each answer as `answer.json`, which the new schema `schemas/fixer-answer.schema.json` holds. The README's Connections section shows the contract for whoever writes a fixer. The command that starts fixers is not shipped yet.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A new command, `pkit pm fix-drift`, starts each fixer you granted on what the default branch left out of date, and gathers its proposals for you (DEC-056 points 3 to 6). It pins the default branch's commit and reads, at it, the whole-repository check, each artefact's type, the fixers point and the grant list. It dispatches only stale artefacts whose fixer you granted, upstream first, within a cap per fixer, and lists every other artefact with its reason. Each fixer runs confined through `pkit agents run`, under the rules the new `permissions/runs.yaml` declares, with one brief that quotes each pull request behind the friction as data. Its result lines and answers are read by the run's contract, which `schemas/fixer-contract.yaml` holds as data. A breach, or a diff that reaches beyond its artefact, sets the fixer's files aside. Proposals wait in the main working tree's agent workspace, under `project-management/drift/`, with an index. `land-work` prints how many wait, and lists a change to the grant list before it stops ready, asks or merges. The fixers point names `ask-fixers` as its question, so install and upgrade ask it where a person can answer. `grant-fixer` and `revoke-fixer` with `--dry-run` check the configuration exists, and `check-fixers` reports a refusal for a type no entry gives the agent. The command needs the backbone this release ships, for the confined run and `pkit agents rules`: upgrade the capability with the backbone and the claude-code adapter.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Four new commands bring the fixers' proposals to you and carry out what you decide (DEC-056 point 6). `pkit pm show-proposals` shows them in one batch, each with its evidence, its diff and its writer's command word for word, and each question a fixer held. `pkit pm apply-proposals` applies what you accept to the change's branch, upstream first: each diff, then its writer, built from the answer's fields. `pkit pm refuse-proposals` remembers a refusal against the artefact's friction, and `pkit pm answer-question` records your answer to a held question for the next run. Each proposal is checked again at the default branch before it is shown or applied, and one that no longer holds is set aside and dispatched again. The pm skill gains the `fix-drift` sub-procedure, and the project manager runs `fix-drift` after each merge into the default branch, in the session that landed it. It then brings you the batch and applies what you accept through an ordinary pull request. `land-work`'s count leaves out a question you answered.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The project manager decides the fixers' proposals in your place where the default branch names it your delegate in `friction.delegate` (COR-064). It reads every word of each proposal, judges it from what the files hold, and accepts, rewords or refuses it without changing its outcome or its diff. The pull request that writes them tells you what it decided, and it tells you what it refused. It never decides an answer it drafted, a question a fixer held, one only an account could settle, a proposal whose type's owner keeps its answers for you, or anything else you decide, such as a merge. It decides nothing where the project chose no reviewer of the answers. DEC-055 point 3 and DEC-056 are refined to match, and so are the `fix-drift` sub-procedure and the README's "Deciding the proposals".
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A clone that set its instance id with `set-instance` now marks the issues it owns (DEC-035, #519). `create-issue` claims each issue it files for the clone, unless it is filed for another person with `--assignee`. `start-work` claims an issue no instance owns once it has set the assignee, and reads the issue again: where a lower-numbered instance claimed in the same instant, it withdraws its claim and stops before the move with exit code 2, naming the branch it cut. `close-issue` releases every owner once it has closed the issue, in each mode, and `done-work` releases through the `close-issue` it runs for each issue its merge closed. Every write goes through the ownership seam on the substrate `project/instance-ownership.yaml` selects (DEC-043): by default an event comment, with the current owner rewritten into the issue's description above the provenance footer, or the `instance:N` label, created on first use. A claim or a release that fails is a warning, and the command's own work stands. A clone with no instance id set writes and prints exactly what it did before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The clash guard of DEC-035 point 6 (#520): with an instance id set, `start-work`, `review-work`, `done-work`, `promote-issue`, `edit-issue` and `move-issue` refuse an issue another instance owns, or one they cannot read, before they write anything. The refusal is bypassable with an audit: `start-work --bypass "<reason>"`, and `--bypass-owner "<reason>"` on the other five, post the reason on the issue as an audit comment first. A command hands its reason to the move it composes, and the comment is posted once. Instance ownership also takes a project's topology of seats (DEC-059) from its caller. In such a project a planner's seat files with no owner, and a claim names its seat and counts only while its clone holds that seat. No command reads the topology for instance ownership yet, so a clone with no instance id still writes and prints exactly what it did before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `pkit pm next-task` takes the next ready Task from the shared pool of DEC-059 point 7 (#2142) and starts it, claimed, through `start-work`. A ready Task is an open Task in Backlog with no owner, not assigned to another account, whose `## Dependencies` section names no open issue by `#N`. In a project with a topology it may also be a Task In Progress whose claim no longer counts. The clone's own Tasks in Backlog go first, then the pool, each the highest in priority first, within the scope of the seat the clone holds. In a project with a topology, a clone with no seat, or a planner's seat, takes nothing. Where another instance claims the Task first, the first claim holds, and the clone takes the next ready Task. It exits 5 when no Task is ready. `start-work` now also stops before the move when its claim finds another instance's claim made after the clash guard passed, so two clones never both start one Task.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A state issue now holds a piece of shared state that changes while agents work (DEC-058, #2138). `scripts/_lib/state_issues.py` is the one module that creates, reads, claims and releases one (ADR-071): it reads the whole comment log, counts a comment only from an author who may write, and keeps the first claim on a free state. The audit-log facility, `scripts/_lib/audit_log.py`, builds and parses every event comment of state issues and of instance ownership. An ownership event's stamp is now `<!-- pkit:audit v=1 ... -->`, naming in a project with a topology the seat, its number and the clone's key. The stamps written before it, `<!-- pkit:instance-owner v=1 ... -->`, still read. The new schemas `audit-log.yaml` and `state-issues.yaml` declare the stamp, the event types and the marker. A clone keeps a key of its own beside its instance id, generated the first time it claims on a state issue. Every command that reads a single issue refuses a state issue, and the board's reads leave one out. No command creates a state issue yet: the commands for seats come later.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `land-work` shows the person who authorises a merge each accepted decision record the change alters or removes, with each point it adds, alters or retires, among the findings to judge, and counts it on the `ready:` line. The list comes from the backbone's `pkit decisions check-diff --json --head`, derived at the pinned head against the pull request's base, and a list that cannot be read stops the run with nothing merged. It stands for the head, so a `--yes` for that head covers it. At a later head an earlier head's authorisation covers, a list other than the one at the head authorised stops the run at `ready:`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A project that runs agents in parallel can now declare a topology of seats in `project/topology.yaml` (DEC-059 points 1 to 3, #2140), whose shape is the new schema `topology.yaml`. `scripts/_lib/topology.py` reads the topology from the default branch, at the commit the backbone resolves it to, and says which commit it read. The new `create-seats` runs on the change that adds seats: it creates each new seat's state issue and writes its number into the file, idempotently. The new `check-topology` checks the file against the default branch's topology, or `--base`: a seat name used twice, more than one organizer seat, a seat with no state issue, a scope naming an undeclared workstream or type, the `label` ownership substrate, a seat deleted or moved rather than retired, and a base that cannot be resolved. The commands that take, release and list seats come later.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A clone now takes, releases and lists the seats of the topology the default branch holds (DEC-059 points 4 to 6, #2141). The new `take-seat` takes the seat named, or the first free one of `--role`, only where it names the agent definition the session follows, given with `--agent` or `PKIT_AGENT`. A clone holds at most one seat, takes up the one it holds, and releases a retired one first. The new `release-seat` releases this clone's seat, or a seat by force with its reason, from the organizer seat's clone or on a person's authorisation. The new `seats` lists each seat with its role and its holder, and names the state issues no seat names. In a project with a topology, every command that writes as an instance now confirms the clone's seat on its state issue first, and refuses where it cannot, and `set-instance` refuses a typed number. `next-task` narrows the shared pool by the scope the topology gives the clone's seat, and refuses a seat it cannot confirm. A project with no topology behaves as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The project-manager agent lists the seats of the project's topology at the start of each session, and takes one with `take-seat` where the session was started for one or its clone holds one already. It then runs its role's loop from the pm skill's new `work-loop` sub-procedure (DEC-059 point 8). A planner slices each intent through batch planning and files the work with no owner into the shared pool. A worker first goes on with what its clone has in flight, then takes the next ready Task with `next-task`, builds it in its own clone, opens its pull request with `open-pr --draft` and `review-work`, and takes the next. No role authorises a merge, or passes an override that is the person's. Batch planning now names the choices the planned Tasks would share before it slices, and where nothing settles one yet, its first plan files only the EPIC, with an outline of the slices, and the foundation's Feature (DEC-060). The slices follow in a second plan once that Feature closes. Its gate now lists which issues go to Backlog on approval, and it schedules them through `promote-issue`, which audits each move, in place of a raw milestone edit. A session that holds no seat works as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `seats` listing now names, last, each Task in Review that a released seat left, with the open pull request from the Task's branch, for a person to land or to hand over to a worker (DEC-059 points 5 and 9). Such a Task's claim stopped counting when its seat was released, by its clone or by force, and no worker's loop takes it up, since the shared pool holds only Tasks in Backlog and In Progress. The Tasks are listed under the seat and the clone whose claim names them. The listing reads the comments of each Task in Review only once some seat's state issue logs a release, and the open pull requests in one listing once a Task is found, and it writes nothing to them. It exits 3 where a Task's comments or the open pull requests could not be read.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A worker's loop now reads its own pull requests in Review each time it starts and after each `review-work`, before it takes the next Task. It mends one that conflicts with the default branch by merging that branch in, never by a rebase or a force-push, and runs the fix round on one whose verdict at its head asks for changes, carrying only the blocking findings. A pull request that a released seat left in Review is the person's to land or hand over: `next-task` never offers it (DEC-059 points 7 and 9). `show-pr` takes a PR's head branch as well as its number, and shows `mergeable`, GitHub's word on whether the PR merges into its base, in its view, its JSON and as `--field mergeable`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `land-work` runs the change check on the merge as it would land, just before it stops ready, asks or merges. It fetches the pull request's base, builds the merge of the pinned head with the base's tip as a commit on no branch, and runs `pkit friction check --json` from a temporary checkout of that merge against the tip, the head named as the change's own head (`PKIT_CHECK_HEAD`). Where the check fails there, the run stops with nothing merged and names each document, so two pull requests that each passed alone can no longer leave a page stale together. In warning mode the findings are printed and stop nothing. A merge that conflicts with the base as fetched stops the run with the merge to make, and a check that cannot be read stops it too. Where the head holds the tip its own check read, that check serves and nothing runs again.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A topology may now declare pools of workers (DEC-061, #2256). Each seat that builds names the pools it serves, and a person puts a Milestone into a pool with a `Pool: <name>` line, through `create-milestone --pool` or the new `set-pool`. Either asks the person's consent and logs it in the Milestone's description. `next-task` then takes only ready Tasks whose native Milestone is an open Milestone of the seat's pools, and takes nothing where the Milestones cannot be read. Each write that brings a Task into a pool, or moves it between pools, asks the person: `create-issue`, `edit-issue` and `promote-issue` with `--milestone`, and an `assign-milestone` hook. Closing a Milestone rolls its open Tasks forward within its pool, and `seats`, `take-seat` and `check-topology` show and check the pools. Every adopter with a topology meets one change of behaviour: code that cannot read the topology in full, a key it does not know among it, now refuses in `next-task`, `start-work`, `take-seat` and `release-seat --force` rather than warn. The order of work (DEC-061 point 9): add pools to a topology only once every clone that may take a seat runs its taking commands from a checkout at or past this release, since older code ignores the pools and takes from the whole Backlog. A project with no pools declared behaves as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A board's Status is now kept as the projection of each issue's state, as DEC-006 decides, where a Projects v2 board is configured and does not carry the state. `move-issue` and every wrapper over it set the card's Status to the issue's new state, the forward cascade sets each parent's, and `create-issue` sets the first Status of the card it adds. A write that fails is one line, and the move stands. The new command `sync-board` writes the Status again from every issue's state, on the cards that differ only, adds a card for each open issue that has none, and names each card whose Status disagreed: the issue's state wins. `pre-check` warns, with the remedy, for each of the five states the board's Status has no option for. Where the board carries the state, nothing changes.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — An issue In Progress that nobody is working on can now move back (DEC-006, #2281). `move-issue <N> --to backlog --reason "<why the work stopped>"`, or `--to todo`, takes it there on the person's authorisation: `--yes`, or a yes at a terminal. It lands in Backlog where the issue is in a milestone, or, for a container, where a child of it is in Backlog, and in Todo otherwise. The other target is refused, naming the right one. The reason is required, posted as the move's audit comment before the label changes, and journaled with the move. Every owner of the issue is released after the move, as a close releases one (DEC-035 point 4). Where this clone cannot release an owner, such as any owner where it takes no part in instance ownership, or a claim another account made, the move is refused before anything is written, naming the owner. A container is refused while a child of it is In Progress or in Review, each such child named, or while its children cannot all be told: an open child in another repository holds it until that child closes. A Task's branch and draft pull request stay as they are. `workflow.yaml` declares the two moves, and the issue lifecycle's definition version rises to 6. Where a board is configured and the state is native, the card's Status follows the move. Where the board carries the state, the move back is refused until #2271.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A Task whose work was not a pull request, such as an audit, an investigation or a decision walked with the user, now closes as completed (DEC-006, #2282). `close-issue <N> --mode=completed --result "<where the result lives>"` closes it on the person's authorisation: `--yes`, or a yes at a terminal. Every checkbox must be ticked, and the gate is not skippable in this mode. The close comment names where the work's result lives, such as an issue, a record or a comment, and a retry posts no second one. GitHub records the Task as completed, its labels are reconciled to done, the move is journaled from where it was as `completed close: <where>`, and the owner is released. Completed work is progress, so the forward cascade then brings each ancestor behind up to In Progress, through `move-issue <N> --to done`, and the closure cascade runs after it. A Task in Todo, Backlog or In Progress closes, and one in Review, which has a pull request open, is refused, naming `done-work`. `move-issue <N> --to done` on an issue closed as completed brings its ancestors level whatever state its label held, Todo or Backlog included, where it moved none before. `workflow.yaml` declares In Progress → Done for a Task and the `manual-completed` closure trigger, and the issue lifecycle's definition version rises to 7. A won't-do close of a Task In Progress is journaled too. `done-work` still moves a Task In Progress at the merge to Review first.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A comment posted through `comment-issue` or `comment-pr` now says which clone posted it (#2272). It ends with a line naming the seat and instance of the clone that runs the verb, such as `🪑 Posted by seat planner, instance 1`, or saying that the clone holds no seat. A hidden `<!-- pkit:posted-by v=1 ... -->` stamp carries the same, with the clone's key. A clone that took a seat confirms it on the seat's state issue first (DEC-059 point 6), and where it cannot, the verb refuses with exit 1 and posts nothing. The pm skill now says that a comment reporting the person's decision names the seat or the session the person gave it in.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The capability ships its operational rules as its loaded rule set `PM`, whose `rules.md` every session loads wherever the capability is installed (COR-070): a pull request that closes issues names each with a closing keyword, first in its body as the PR template puts it; and an issue carries one value on each required axis the project's substrate map carries. Branch naming, and a branch's related issue, stay core's (COR-008).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `open-pr --doc-impact-from-friction` leaves the `## Doc impact` section as it is, and says why, when `pkit friction check --json` carries a `schema_version` other than 1. A document without the key comes from a backbone that predates it and reads as version 1, so an older backbone still works.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability's first usable release: keep actors, use cases, journeys and glossary terms as anchored artefacts under the `analysis/` folder of your internal documentation root (DEC-001). `pkit analysis new actor|term|use-case|journey <slug>` stamps each from its template with a stable id — `ACT-<slug>`, `TERM-<slug>`, and `UC-NNN` / `JRN-NNN` numbered past the working tree and the default branch — writes the anchors the decision asks for (a use case's actor, a journey's use cases from its steps, and any `--path` / `--record`), and records the analysis location the first time. Each artefact kind, and the revalidation record, has a companion schema and a template carrying the friction block inside the `pkit:` container. `pkit analysis validate`, run by `pkit validate` as its `software-analysis:artefacts` member, fails on duplicate ids, missing required parts, a heading that is not the use case's or journey's id and title, an actor or step the stamp would refuse, a use case not anchored to its actor, a journey whose anchors do not match its steps, and malformed revalidation records, reading the working tree alone. `pkit analysis check-numbers --base <ref>`, a line of its own in your check gate beside `pkit friction check`, fails on a use case or journey number the default branch gave another file since your branch left it, and when the base names no commit. The package metadata declares the `analysis` location and the glossary, actors, use cases and journeys as places. The stamp and the checks read the analysis through `pkit friction artefacts`, with `--at` for the default branch, and the stamp records the location through `pkit docs record-location`, which the backbone this release ships adds: upgrade the capability together with the backbone. Requires backbone >=2.0.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Connections (DEC-001 points 7 and 8): the capability provides the `pkit::analysis` role and defines the `pkit::analysis:revalidation-evidence` data point under it — executed results that confirm or refute an artefact at a commit, one entry per result keyed `<artefact>@<commit>#<check>`, the check being the name its filler gives one executed check, opening with the filler's own name (`project` for a project file's), the commit by its full name, an optional `steps` set by the artefact's kind (a use case's steps and variants by number and letter, a journey's use cases by id, none for an actor or a term), `union`, no default, inert policy `fallback` — with its companion schema, `schemas/revalidation-evidence.schema.json`, which reads alone, filled by a capability or by the project's filler file. A revalidation record keeps a copy of each entry it drew on, under `evidence`, whole and in the point's shape, so it shows that evidence whatever the fillers report later. `pkit analysis validate` fails a record copying evidence for an artefact it gives no outcome, since evidence supports an outcome and never replaces one; an entry whose id is not its own `<artefact>@<commit>#<check>`; and a journey's evidence naming a step that is no use case of the analysis, withdrawn ones counting. It warns on a `failed` result under `holds` or a `passed` one under `code-regressed`, and on a copy that differs from what the point now holds under its id; and where it reads the point, it warns on a capability's entry whose check opens with another name, and on an entry whose id is not its own. The capability contributes its actors in force to `pkit::documentation:readers` through a new query command, `pkit analysis fill-readers`: each actor a reader whose id is the actor's id in lower case (`ACT-tester` → `act-tester`) and whose description is its name and needs. The contribution is inert when no capability provides the documentation role. The check reads the evidence point through `pkit connections resolve`, which the backbone this release ships adds: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Authoring and resolving the analysis (DEC-001 points 5, 6 and 9). The `analysis-author` skill walks an actor, a term, a use case, a journey and a revalidation record through `pkit analysis new`: the slug, the area, the actor and the steps, what to anchor, and when an actor or term is stamped `--unanchored-because <why>` instead of anchors. `pkit analysis new revalidation <slug> --change <ref> --trigger <trigger> --outcome <id>=<outcome> … --because <id>=<why> … [--gap "<gap> => <resolution>"]… [--by <who> | --by-agent <name> --confirmed-by <who>]` writes `revalidations/<date>-<slug>.md`, dated in UTC, only when there is something to say — a planned revalidation, one that found a regression or a gap, or one where a person decided an artefact was stale — and refuses a routine one, an outcome without its justification, and any text still holding a placeholder. `pkit analysis validate` reports a regression still open — a record's `code-regressed` artefact not revalidated since — and warns about `unanchored-because` beside anchors; neither fails. The `analysis-resolver` agent, deployed with the other agents, performs the judgment of a revalidation: it proposes each flagged artefact's outcome (holds, analysis-stale, code-regressed, gap-found) with its evidence and hands you the exact commands that record it, running none of them; where it cannot tell a stale analysis from regressed code it asks you, holding only what depends on the answer. `pkit analysis propose <artefact>` is the read-only rule it proposes by: from `pkit friction explain` and the code an artefact quotes from its anchored files, it proposes holds where the code moved, stale or regressed by the quotes you give it with their sources (a quote from a commit checked against its message), leaves the change to be read with the outcome it leans to, or answers ambiguous with the question, and gives the writer commands for each outcome. It needs three things of the backbone this release ships: `pkit friction explain`; the friction writers, `pkit friction revalidate` and `pkit friction defer`, which its commands are; and `pkit friction artefacts --at` with `pkit docs record-location`, which the stamp reads and records the analysis location through. So the capability requires that backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability contributes its use cases to the work-tracking role's `pkit::work-tracking:use-cases` data point (DEC-001 point 8) through a new query command, `pkit analysis fill-use-cases`: every use case the default branch holds, one entry each — its id as the analysis writes it, its title, `active` or `withdrawn` (a withdrawn use case is kept, since its id is never used again) and the path of its document. The command declares `reads: [settled]`: it reads the default branch at the commit the backbone resolves it to and what that commit holds, never the working tree, and asks git nothing itself. It answers the empty list where the default branch has no commit yet, and gives no answer — exit 1, nothing on standard output — when a reading fails or a use case there cannot be read in full, never a shorter list. The contribution is inert while no capability provides the work-tracking role. `reads:` on a command leaf and `pkit friction artefacts --at` come with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A project can remove use cases and journeys that nothing cites, and declare their ids free again: a pilot abandoned before anything cited it, say (DEC-001 point 3). The declaration is `numbering.freed-by` in the capability's new settings file, `.pkit/capabilities/software-analysis/project/config.yaml`, whose shape is `schemas/config.schema.json`. Each entry names the commit that removed the artefacts, by its full id, and lists the ids it frees; only a listed id is freed. `pkit analysis new` gives a listed id again and says so when it is the number it would have followed. `pkit analysis check-numbers` reports no collision for it and lists the ids each entry frees, with a `freed` list in its `--json` document. An id the commit would free that no entry lists stays taken, and both report it. An entry the tools cannot honour refuses the stamp of a use case or journey and fails `pkit analysis check-numbers`, saying why; a clone too shallow to hold the commit's parent, and git older than 2.31, are named as such. The capability's README, "Configuration", says what cites an id and which ids a commit can free. Without the file, nothing changes.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability declares the types of its artefacts (DEC-001 point 2, COR-056, #1409). Its type registry, `friction.types`, holds `actor`, `use-case`, `journey` and `term`, each with a description. None admits a project place, and none names a kind field. Each place names its type: the actors' entries are `software-analysis:actor`, the use cases `software-analysis:use-case`, the journeys `software-analysis:journey` and the glossary's entries `software-analysis:term`. A revalidation record stays held, with no type. `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives each analysis artefact its type, so a rule set's scope can select them by type. While the capability is installed, a project's own bare type cannot take one of the four names. The package metadata uses the type registry and a place's `type`, which the backbone this release ships reads, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability ships its method rule set, `software-analysis:SAN`, in `rule-sets/san.md` (DEC-001 point 1). It takes no scope, as no method rule set does. It holds the format rule, `RS-SAN-001`: an analysis artefact carries its kind's declared structure. Its origin is DEC-001, and it binds once it is accepted. For text of the `sentence` form, its *How* says what one text is, and where a sentence ends. Under `checks`, the set declares what `software-analysis:validate` enforces of the rule: the parts a kind ships, their order, the glossary's head and their forms. The name `SAN` is now taken, so a rule set of your own named `SAN` fails `pkit validate`. Renaming that set renames every rule id in it. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0, and the capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones, which hold no set named `SAN`. The set declares `checks`, which only the rule-set schema of the backbone this release ships reads, so the capability requires that backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Each analysis kind declares its structure once, as data, in `schemas/artefact-kinds.yaml` (DEC-001 point 1, #1363). Its schema, `artefact-kinds.schema.json`, is a strict superset of living-docs' `page-kinds.schema.json`. The file declares the actor, the use case, the journey, the glossary term and the revalidation record, each with the parts the template round chose. Each element has its id, its type, whether it may read `None.`, its form, and its hint and example. Nothing reads the file yet, and a test holds it to its schema. ([#1578])
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate` checks each analysis body against its kind's declared structure (DEC-001 point 1, #1364). It reports a declared part missing, out of order or empty, `None.` in a part that may not read it, and text of the `sentence` form that is not one sentence, in a part or in each of an actor's `needs`. Each message gives the part's hint and example. Only a label the kind declares marks a part, so a label or a section a writer adds passes. A withdrawn artefact is never failed for a part it lacks. A glossary entry is read within the span `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives its section, and the glossary's head above the first entry. An artefact's structure binds at the status of the format rule, RS-SAN-001: while it is proposed, the normal check reports none of it. A revalidation record's binds always. `pkit analysis validate --preview` previews the rule as if it were accepted: each finding is a report whose message opens `preview:`, and the exit status stays the normal check's. `pkit validate` runs the normal check. The check reads each entry's `span` from the artefacts document, which only the backbone this release ships gives, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate` holds each part and front-matter field to the form its kind's structure gives it (DEC-001 point 1, #1365). A use case's *Main path* is numbered steps, no number twice. A use case's and a journey's *Variants* read `None.`, or each opens with a step's number and a letter, such as `2a.`. A journey's *Steps* hold one line for each of its `steps`, `<id> — <title>` with the use case's title, and each hand-over under a step names an earlier step, or a use case its `relies-on` lists. A record's *Outcomes* follow its `outcomes`, in order, and its *Gaps* name each `code-regressed` or `gap-found` artefact with a `**Resolution:**`. A term's `definition` is a phrase with no article first, no full stop last, and no word of its name. A part's list is read as Markdown reads one, and a form that reads a list judges only its items, so a line a writer adds outside them passes. A withdrawn artefact is never failed for a form. An artefact's forms bind at the status of the format rule, RS-SAN-001: while it is proposed, the normal check reports none of them, and `pkit analysis validate --preview` lists each breach. A revalidation record's bind always.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis check-pointers` places on the default branch each commit a planned revalidation record names in `reviewed-against` (DEC-001 point 6, #1448). It reads each source without a `url` whose `version` is a commit's full id, and skips `this change`. A commit is held when the default branch's history reaches it. It is not held when that history does not and the clone holds all of it, a commit missing from a full clone included. It is unknown when a shallow clone's history of the branch stops short. The command fails on a commit the default branch does not hold in a record the change adds, naming the record and the source: a squash merge leaves a branch's commits off it. The same in a record the merge-base already holds is reported and never fails, since a record is never rewritten, and so is an unknown. Commits are placed on the default branch alone, as `pkit repository base` resolves it. The base, `--base`, else `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`, else the default branch, says only which records the change adds: those whose file the merge-base does not hold at its path, so a record the change moves reads as added. It asks no remote, so a partial clone that lacks the merge-base's tree fails rather than fetch it. A tree with no planned record reads no base and says so, and a default branch that resolves nowhere fails it. `--json` lists each source placed under `pointers`. It reads history, so it is a check-gate line of its own beside `pkit analysis check-numbers`, not a member of `pkit validate`. The capability's README, "Commits off the default branch", says how to wire it in.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A project changes each analysis kind's structure in a file of its own, `.pkit/capabilities/software-analysis/project/structures.yaml`, keyed by kind, its shape `schemas/structures.schema.json` bound to it by its path (DEC-001 point 1, #1367). With no rule, it relabels a shipped part and replaces the hint and example of a part or a front-matter field, and the stamp writes the project's hint. Naming a rule of its own, it adds labels and sections `after` a named element, requires a shipped optional part, or closes the kind's list of labels, of an artefact kind only. A group binds once its rule, any rule it fills and the format rule are accepted, on the artefacts `pkit rule-sets reach` says its rule binds on. `pkit analysis validate` refuses a file that drops, loosens or reorders a shipped part, invents a form, adds a kind, names a retired or unknown element or no rule of the project's, or places a part in a cycle. It reports a group whose rule is superseded or withdrawn, and a rule that binds on no artefact of the kind. `pkit analysis new` writes the project's labels, hints and parts, each added part's hint naming its rule. `schemas/artefact-kinds.yaml` may list a kind's `retired` element ids. The check reads `pkit rule-sets reach`, which only the backbone this release ships has, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A use case and a journey carry the anchors DEC-001 point 4 gives them, so a change to an actor or a use case flags every artefact that rests on it (#1507). A use case lists each other actor who takes part in a new front-matter field, `other-actors`, and anchors to each beside its own actor. A journey lists each use case outside it that a step relies on in `relies-on`, which its schema now takes, and anchors to its actor and to the use cases of its `steps` and its `relies-on`. `pkit analysis new` writes both lists from `--other-actor` and `--relies-on`, each repeatable, and the anchors from them. `pkit analysis validate` resolves each to an actor or a use case of the analysis, in force for one in force, refuses a use case's own actor among its other actors and a journey's step among its `relies-on`, requires each actor anchor, and fails a journey whose use-case anchors differ from its `steps` and its `relies-on`. So a journey with a hand-over from a use case outside it can pass the check of its *Steps* once the format rule binds. The templates show both fields.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis check-numbers` keeps a use case's steps and variants append-only (DEC-001 point 3, #1375). Journeys and evidence cite a step by its number and a variant by its step's number and its letter, so the check compares each use case with the same use case, by id, where the branch left its base, and reports a step or a variant renumbered or removed, naming the use case and the number or letter. One added, whatever new number or letter it takes, and a use case new in the change, pass, and so does a step reworded under its own number. It binds where the append-only rule, RS-SAN-002, binds, as `pkit rule-sets reach` reads it, and at its status: accepted, an error; proposed, nothing is compared. The new `--preview` flag compares where `pkit rule-sets reach --preview` says the rule would bind, each finding a report opening `preview:` that never fails. It reads the base `check-numbers` already reads, so a shallow clone that lacks it fails rather than passing. `pkit analysis validate` never runs it. The capability's README, "Steps and variants stay append-only", says what it reports.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Adopting the capability includes choosing each kind's parts, a step never skipped (DEC-001 point 1). The README gains its adoption steps: write a rule set of your own that inherits `SAN`, choose each kind's parts, then write the analysis. The `analysis-author` skill gains a sub-procedure, `choose-templates.md`, that walks you through it one kind at a time: review the kind's shipped parts, fill a candidate with one of your own examples and check it with `pkit analysis validate --preview`, then keep the shipped parts or write your relabels and additions in the capability's `project/structures.yaml`. A shipped part you find wrong is a change you propose to the capability, with `pkit report change-request`. No command, schema, template or check changes.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability declares one project rule set, `ANALYSIS`, and installing it creates the set (COR-017; DEC-001, Implications; #1613). The set selects the analysis by the capability's four types and inherits `software-analysis:SAN` at its major then, so `SAN`'s rules bind from the first artefact. Install creates nothing where a set of the project's own inherits `SAN` already, directly or not. The 0.2.0 upgrade's migration creates the set, on the same conditions, for a project that installed the capability earlier. That adds a set a project may have chosen not to have: remove it if you do not want `SAN` to bind, and nothing creates it again. Where `SAN`'s accepted rules then bind on the analysis, `pkit validate` may fail on artefacts that passed before. The declaration needs the package key and the reading command only the backbone this release ships has, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Where project-management is installed, software-analysis contributes the core `rule-set-reviewer` to its merge gate, under the floor `touches-ruled-artefact` (DEC-001 point 10; COR-057). A pull request that alters an artefact an accepted rule binds on, or brings one under such a rule, now needs the reviewer's `APPROVED` before `done-work` merges it, an analysis artefact or not. The reviewer runs on the local path only, through `review-pr`. While the contribution is in force, resolving any pull request reads its base and head commits through `pkit rule-sets altered`, so a shallow checkout fails it. living-docs contributes the same rule, and the reviewer is required once. To keep your gate as it was, list `rule-set-reviewer` under `review.agents.contributed_opt_out` in project-management's configuration, once for each capability that contributes it. software-analysis declares project-management as an optional dependency, from project-management's first release with the floor, which ships with this one: software-analysis still works without project-management, and install and upgrade hold an installed one to that range. The capability requires the backbone that ships the reviewer, the command and optional dependencies. Requires project-management >=0.55.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `SAN` holds three judgement rules, each proposed, with DEC-001 as its origin (#1577). `RS-SAN-003`: a use case's actor is the one whose goal it is, and each other actor that takes part in it is one of its `other-actors`. `RS-SAN-004`: a use case's goal is one of its actor's needs. `RS-SAN-005`: an analysis artefact's anchors name what its statements rest on, and a use case's anchors also name the decisions that answer it and the architecture views that realise it, as DEC-001 points 4 and 11 give them (#1686). None declares a check: a reviewer, such as the rule-set reviewer, judges each once it is accepted. The *How* of `RS-SAN-003` and of `RS-SAN-005` names what the stamp and the check already refuse whatever any rule's status, and leaves it out. Each rule judges an artefact in force, never a withdrawn one. A proposed rule binds nothing, so no check's result changes, and `SAN` stays at 1.0.0 (COR-051 point 7). The README's method rule set section lists the five rules.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Point 11 of software-analysis DEC-001 says how the analysis and a project's decisions and architecture point at each other, a convention your records and views follow. The analysis always anchors the solution: a use case anchors each record it rests on or that answers it, and each architecture view that realises it. No record and no view anchors the analysis, and a view names an analysis artefact only by its id. What a record serves is worked out from the analysis's anchors for display, and never stored. The use-case template's record anchor holds a new placeholder, `<record it rests on>`, which joins the append-only list of placeholders the templates shipped. The analysis-author skill says so, and the README says it in "How it meets your decisions". No check changes.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability declares the three kinds of requirement that DEC-002 keeps: quality requirements, functional requirements and constraints (#1691). Each has its declared structure, its template rendered from it, its companion schema, and its place under `requirements/`, which gives its type: `software-analysis:quality-requirement`, `software-analysis:functional-requirement` or `software-analysis:constraint`. A requirement holds its `statement`, its `measure` and where it holds, `applies-to`, as front-matter fields, beside `actors` and `refines`. Its body holds *Verified by*, then *Rationale*, or *Imposed by* on a constraint. Its heading shows its kind between its id and its title, as `# REQ-004 · Quality · <title>`. A requirement's id, `REQ-NNN`, is one numbering across the three kinds, and a revalidation record's outcomes and the evidence point's entries may name one. The evidence point's version stays 1. `pkit analysis validate` holds a file in each new place to its kind's schema, and to its kind's structure where RS-SAN-001 binds. A declared front-matter field may now say whether it reads `None.`, as a part does: a requirement's `measure` may, and its `statement` and `applies-to` may not. A field that says neither, as every field of the four earlier kinds, is judged as before. The lists of requirements on use cases and journeys come later. **A clash of types:** while the capability is installed, `pkit validate` refuses a bare type of your own that takes one of the three names (COR-056 point 2). A type of your own named `constraint` is the likeliest. Rename it before you upgrade. **Your rule set:** the set install creates now selects the three types beside the four. A set of yours created before selects none of them, and nothing changes it. Add `software-analysis:quality-requirement`, `software-analysis:functional-requirement` and `software-analysis:constraint` to its scope, so `SAN` binds on your requirements.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis new` stamps a requirement of each of the three kinds DEC-002 keeps: `quality-requirement <slug>`, `functional-requirement <slug>` and `constraint <slug>` (#1692). Each is written as `REQ-NNN-<slug>.md` in its kind's folder under `requirements/`, or an area's folder in it with `--area`. Its heading shows its id, its kind and its title, and its `statement`, `measure` and `applies-to` hold their hints until you write them. A requirement takes the next free `REQ-NNN` across the three kinds, past every number the working tree, the default branch and its history hold in any of their folders. The numbering setting, `numbering.freed-by`, now frees a requirement's id as it frees a use case's. `--actor` and `--refines`, each repeatable, write `actors` and `refines`, and anchor each actor and each requirement refined. A requirement nothing embodies takes `--unanchored-because` instead. The stamp refuses an actor or a requirement the analysis does not hold or has withdrawn, a requirement that refines itself, `--unanchored-because` beside an anchor, and a placeholder left in a value. A requirement's number is now read as a use case's is: `pkit analysis validate` holds a requirement file's name to its id, and `pkit analysis check-numbers` reports a `REQ` number another line of work took, across the three kinds' folders. The `analysis-author` skill gains a sub-procedure for requirements, `requirement.md`, with the test between the three kinds and a use case. Requirements may now be written: use the stamp rather than a file by hand.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `SAN` holds a new judgement rule, `RS-SAN-006`, proposed: a requirement never contradicts an accepted decision record it rests on (#1695). It compares the requirement's `statement`, `measure` and `applies-to` with each accepted record it anchors and rests on, whole or the point its anchor names. The record wins until it is changed. A conflict between two requirements is no break of it, since a decision record holds such a choice. Its origin is DEC-002, and it anchors DEC-002 points 4, 6 and 8. `RS-SAN-005` now asks a requirement for what DEC-002 point 6 gives it: its actors, the requirements it refines, the decisions it rests on or that answer it, the views that realise it, the files that verify it and the code that holds it. Both rules stay proposed and declare no check, so nothing binds yet, and `SAN` stays at 1.0.0 (COR-051 point 7). The README's method rule set section lists the rule.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `SAN` holds a new judgement rule, `RS-SAN-007`, proposed: each goal, minimal guarantee and requirement names its source (#1681). A source is an accepted record the artefact anchors, a requirement's *Rationale* or a constraint's *Imposed by*, code the artefact anchors that already does what the item says, or a person's decision quoted in the artefact, with their name and the date. A requirement's sources cover what its `statement` asks and any value its `measure` sets. Its origin is the maintainer's decision of 17 October. The `analysis-author` skill asks the same of what you write, and the README says so. The rule declares no check and binds nothing while proposed, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0. **The cost, once it is accepted:** accepting it gives `SAN` a new major. Moving your set's pin to that major brings every goal, guarantee and requirement under the rule, not only those a change alters. On that change, each one without a source is a settled break, which withholds the rule-set reviewer's approval (COR-060 point 1). Mend them in that change, or leave a file out of your set's scope until it is mended. COR-060 point 2 gives the other ways out.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate` and `pkit analysis check-numbers` now hold a requirement as they hold a use case (#1693, software-analysis DEC-002). The check fails a requirement whose heading does not show its id, its kind and its title, as `# REQ-004 · Quality · <title>`, and one whose file's name carries no number or another than its id. It fails an id two artefacts share, `REQ-0004` and `REQ-004` read as one id, and a requirement's number is one across the three kinds. It fails an entry of a requirement's `actors` or `refines` that the analysis does not hold, that names the requirement itself, or that is withdrawn while the requirement is in force. A requirement it refines may be of any kind. It also fails a requirement whose anchors within the analysis do not match its `actors` and `refines` exactly, and its message names the anchors to write. A hint left in a requirement's `statement`, `measure` or `applies-to` is refused, as any hint left in is. `pkit analysis check-numbers` counts a requirement's number across the three kinds' folders: two kinds' requirements of one number collide, and a requirement moved to another kind's folder keeps its number and collides with nothing. `SAN`'s declared part of the format rule names the three kinds among those whose missing title heading the heading check holds. That part is no content of the rule, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate` fails a Markdown file under `requirements/` that lies outside the three kinds' folders (#1724, software-analysis DEC-002 point 3). That folder is no place, so such a file is a requirement of no kind: the check held it to nothing and the stamp counted no number it holds. Move it into its kind's folder, or leave it out with `friction.exclude`, and the check no longer reports it. The check reads these files from the new `unplaced` key of `pkit friction artefacts --json`, which the backbone this release ships adds, so the capability requires that backbone. A backbone without the key fails the check with a finding that says it could not look.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A use case and a journey now list the requirements they must meet, in an optional `requirements` field, and `pkit analysis met-by` lists what meets each requirement (#1694, software-analysis DEC-002 point 7). A journey lists only the requirements that bind its whole path. `pkit analysis new use-case` and `new journey` take `--requirement <REQ-id>`, repeatable, write the list and anchor each requirement, and refuse one the analysis does not hold or has withdrawn. `pkit analysis validate` fails a listed requirement that is no requirement of the analysis, or that is withdrawn while the use case or the journey is in force. It also fails requirement anchors that do not match the list exactly, a part anchor on a requirement included. So a changed requirement flags every use case and journey that lists it. `pkit analysis met-by [<REQ-id>] [--json]` gives each requirement, or the one named, with the use cases and journeys in force that list it. It is computed from the working tree each time, and nothing is written on the requirement. A file it cannot read, or whose id or list it cannot tell, is no answer. The field is optional, so a use case or journey written before needs a change only where it anchors a requirement by hand: list that requirement in `requirements`, or drop the anchor. A new use case or journey is stamped with `requirements: []` when it lists none. `SAN`'s `RS-SAN-005` names the requirements a use case and a journey list among what each rests on, and leaves their anchors to the check. It stays proposed, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A change to a requirement's obligation now asks for a revisit of what it anchors outside the analysis (#1659, software-analysis DEC-002 point 4). Each kind of requirement's place declares the core's link (COR-050 point 16): a change to `statement`, `measure` or `applies-to` asks the same change to change each record, view, test and file of code the requirement anchors, or to record on the requirement why it still stands (`unchanged`) or which open follow-up revisits it (`deferred`), with `pkit friction revisit`. The anchors its `actors` and `refines` list stay out. The friction change check reports each target with neither, and fails it where the project enforces friction. A change to a body part, a change of kind or a withdrawal asks for none. The link names the fields that list what stays out, which needs the backbone this release ships. The README, the templates and the `analysis-author` skill say what such a change asks.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The capability declares the `analysis-resolver` the fixer of each of its seven artefact types, in the work-tracking role's point `pkit::work-tracking:fixers`, as a static value (DEC-001 points 5 and 8; project-management DEC-056). Where project-management is installed, `pkit pm ask-fixers` asks you once whether the agent may keep your analysis true on its own, and `pkit pm list-fixers` lists its types. Nothing starts it yet: project-management's command that starts fixers is still to come, and it will start only a fixer you granted. The agent's body and storyboard say what such a run takes and returns. The run takes the command's brief. For each artefact it proposes an answer for, it writes the answer as a JSON file of fields, the diff of any edit and the evidence, and never a command. It ends with one result line per artefact: proposed, held with its question, or none with its reason. It never asks, since nobody watches the run, never proposes a deferral, and never runs a writer. Where the run cannot start `pkit analysis propose`, it judges nothing. The optional dependency on project-management starts from its first release with the fixers point.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — An analysis is built from its concepts up (#1784). DEC-001 point 12 gives the order. First come the concepts and how they relate, in the glossary, and then the actors. The requirements that bind the whole system follow. Then come the use cases, area by area, each area after the areas it builds on, and the journeys come last. An artefact uses only what the order puts before it. One that needs something there, and finds it missing, goes back and writes it there first. The order holds for a new analysis, for an area added later and for a brownfield project's candidates (point 9). The `analysis-author` skill walks it in a new sub-procedure, `build-analysis.md`, which says when each step is done, and the README's "Adopter setup" says so. `SAN` holds a new judgement rule, `RS-SAN-008`, proposed: an analysis artefact uses a domain concept only where a term of the glossary defines it, and names it by that term's name, and no two terms define one concept. A term's definition is judged too. The rule declares no check and binds nothing while proposed, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0. **The cost, once it is accepted:** accepting it gives `SAN` a new major. Moving your set's pin to that major brings every artefact of your analysis under the rule, not only those a change alters. On that change, each use of a concept of your domain, in the rule's sense, that the glossary does not define is a break: settled where the rule's text settles it, which withholds the rule-set reviewer's approval (COR-060 point 1), and otherwise a passage for a person. Define the terms in that change, or leave a file out of your set's scope until it is mended, such as one area at a time. COR-060 point 2 gives the other ways out.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — DEC-001 decides how an analysis is built from what is already true, and how a project with several components keeps one analysis per component (#1809). The capability reads none of it yet: a later release builds it, and every part stays optional for a project. **Preconditions (point 1).** Each precondition names its producers, the use cases that make it true, such as `**From UC-003:**`. A producer is another use case of the same analysis, or the use case itself. What the environment gives alone is an assumption, stated once. A use case's optional `after` lists the producers as data, and draws no anchor. **One analysis per component (points 2, 3, 4 and 8).** A component may keep an analysis of its own, where it is developed, and never ships it. An analysis builds only on the analyses of the components its component depends on. Its glossary's head lists the terms and actors it takes from them, by name with a link, and its artefacts anchor the records behind them. Only the actors a use case, a journey or a requirement names may be another analysis's. Journeys and the chain of preconditions stay within one analysis. Ids are unique across a repository's analyses, with one numbering of each numbered kind, and DEC-002 point 2 numbers requirements the same way. An area's folder may open with a number, which gives the order the areas build in (point 12). `SAN` holds two new judgement rules, proposed: `RS-SAN-009`, the rule of logical consequence, with the check a script can hold and its seven limits, and `RS-SAN-010`, which lets an analysis name only the analyses its component depends on. `RS-SAN-003`, `RS-SAN-004`, `RS-SAN-005` and `RS-SAN-008` read an actor or a term of another analysis. Proposed rules bind nothing, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The analysis's diagrams are decided, and `pkit analysis diagram` prints three of them (#1804, software-analysis DEC-003). The new record draws four diagrams from the analysis's front matter, each for one analysis: a use-case diagram, a journey's flow, a requirement map, and the chain of use cases from `after`. It decides that a diagram is never a fact's home, and that a diagram written in an artefact shows only what that artefact, or an analysis artefact it anchors, states. A generated overview of an analysis's diagrams, and two proposed rules of `SAN` with this record as their origin, come in later changes. `pkit analysis diagram [<subject>] [--json]` prints the use-case diagram, each journey's flow and the requirement map as Mermaid fences, each with its `accTitle` and `accDescr` and no styling. The subject is `use-cases`, `journeys`, `requirements`, a journey's id or a requirement's id. Only artefacts in force are drawn, the same analysis prints the same bytes, and a file it cannot read is no answer. It is computed from the working tree each time, and writes nothing. The chain's query comes with the build that reads `after`. The record refines software-analysis DEC-001 points 1, 2 and 11.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The domain model is decided: an analysis may keep a few views of its concepts beside the glossary, written by hand (#1814, software-analysis DEC-004). The capability reads none of it yet: a later release builds the kind, and a domain model stays optional for a project. **The view kind.** A view lies in `domain-model/`, one for each group of related concepts. Its file's name opens with its number, such as `010-orders-and-payments.md`, and its id is `VIEW-orders-and-payments`. It opens with what its group covers and the group's class diagram. Then it gives one section to each concept its `explains` lists, headed by its term's name. A section explains the concept, then gives its attributes, relations, states and what is always true of it. **Anchors and order.** A view anchors each term it explains and the records it rests on, never code. A view uses only the views of its analysis with lower numbers. The glossary stays as it is, and a term a view explains links to its section. DEC-001 points 1, 2, 3, 4 and 12 name what the record adds. **Rules.** `SAN`'s `RS-SAN-005` and `RS-SAN-008` read views, and `RS-SAN-008` reads a Mermaid diagram's labels as statements. A new rule, `RS-SAN-011`, holds the order of the views. Proposed rules bind nothing, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A repository may keep one analysis for each component, as DEC-001 point 2 decides. List them in the capability's settings file as `analyses`, each with its component, its folder and the analyses it builds on. Each lies in a folder named after its component, with the layout of one analysis. The places and the records' folder are declared one folder down too, so they match both layouts. A project that lists nothing keeps its one analysis at the top, and needs no change. `pkit analysis new` takes `--analysis <component>` to write in a listed analysis, and `pkit analysis diagram` takes it to draw one. Ids stay unique across the analyses, and each numbered kind has one numbering across them. `pkit analysis validate` refuses a file no listed analysis holds, an id two analyses share, and a field naming another analysis's artefact. An actor of an analysis it builds on is the exception, named with it, such as `core:ACT-developer`, where the glossary's head lists it. The head lists what an analysis takes from each one it builds on, as `builds-on` data, and the check holds its text to that data where the format rule binds. The list is held to what a capability's package metadata declares, and the analysis of `core` is the backbone's. A head whose links name another version than that floor is warned of. The readers point gives each actor the needs every analysis states for it. It needs the backbone that lets a held folder be a glob of folders, which this release ships.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — An analysis may keep a domain model, as DEC-004 decides: a few views beside the glossary, in a folder `domain-model/`, one for each group of related concepts. `pkit analysis new view <slug> --number <digits> --explains <TERM-id>…` writes `<number>-<slug>.md`, its id `VIEW-<slug>`, its number in the project's own form and no part of the id. The view lists the terms it explains in `explains`, anchors each and no code, and holds one section for each, headed by the term's name. The stamp also writes each term's link to its section, on a line under the term's heading in the glossary. Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` holds a view's file's name to its id, its terms to its own analysis and to one view in force each, its term anchors to `explains`, no path anchor, and each term's link to the view that explains it. Where the format rule binds, it holds each section's heading to its term's name and each section's parts, a new repeating section of the kinds' declared structure. A project's own structures add labels to a view, never a heading. The kind is optional: a project with no `domain-model/` folder fails nothing. A set of your own created before this release selects no `software-analysis:domain-model-view`, so add it to its scope for `SAN` to bind on your views. While the capability is installed, a bare type of your own named `domain-model-view` clashes with the capability's, and `pkit validate` refuses it. The `analysis-author` skill walks a view, and its build order writes the views after the glossary. The `analysis-resolver` is the fixer of the new type too.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A use case may name what makes each precondition true, as DEC-001 point 1 decides. Each line of its *Preconditions* opens with its producers, such as `**From UC-003:**`, and its optional `after` lists them as data, one entry for each line, such as `after: [[UC-003], [UC-059, environment]]`. The schema holds that shape always, and an entry never names `environment` alone. `pkit analysis validate` checks a use case in force that writes `after` where RS-SAN-009 binds, at its status. Each producer is a use case in force of its own analysis, the field matches the words, and in a numbered area each precondition has a producer in no later area. The rule is proposed, so only `--preview` reports it, and `SAN` declares the check under its `checks`. A use case without `after` fails nothing. The stamp's `--area` takes a numbered area, such as `010-develop`, whose number is no part of the id. `pkit analysis diagram chain` draws the chain of use cases from `after` (DEC-003 point 5). It groups the use cases by area, in the order of the areas' numbers, and draws a producer in a later area dotted. It leaves out a solid arrow that other solid arrows imply, a loop counting as one node. A closed list of labels no longer reports a precondition's opening as a label.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A domain-model view heads each concept's section with its term's name in sentence case, as DEC-004 point 2 decides: its first letter capitalized, the rest as the name writes it, so `decision record` heads `## Decision record`. A name whose first word holds a capital already, such as `iPhone app`, stands as it is. `pkit analysis new view` writes the headings so, and where the format rule binds, `pkit analysis validate` refuses a heading in lower case and names the heading to write. A heading's slug is in lower case, so each term's link to its section is unchanged. The template's hint and the `analysis-author` skill ask for each concept's box in a diagram to be labelled the same way. The term's name itself stays in lower case in the glossary and in prose, unless it is a proper name.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — An analysis's terms are cross-linked, and one name means one concept, as DEC-004 point 7 and DEC-001 points 1 and 2 decide (#1841). **The links.** In an analysis that keeps a domain model, one whose `domain-model/` folder holds a view, `pkit analysis link` opens each view section with `Defined in [<TERM-id>](<glossary>#<anchor>).`. It links the first mention of each term in each section to the term's glossary entry, in a view's opening and its sections and in each glossary entry. A term is never linked in its own section or entry, and front matter and the glossary's head take no link. A mention is a term's name or one of its declared `noun-forms`, whole, the longest first. Code, headings, bold labels, comments and links are skipped. A phrase a term lists in `other-senses` never links it, and `<!-- pkit:not-the-term -->` right before a mention marks it as no mention. A term the glossary's head lists from an analysis the settings list links to its entry there. Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` reports a link missing, extra or stale and names the command, and `pkit analysis new view` writes each section's link to its term. An analysis without a view sees no change. **One name, one concept.** Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` refuses two terms in force of one name, a noun form counted and case ignored, and a name another term replaces. It refuses a term that takes a name of an analysis it builds on, listed by its head or not, and two analyses it builds on that share a name. Give each concept a qualified name, such as `business process` and `system process`. The term schema gains `noun-forms` and `other-senses`. A new rule, `RS-SAN-012`, proposed, holds what validation cannot read, and takes over `RS-SAN-008`'s item on a listed term's name. Proposed rules bind nothing, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A domain-model view explains purpose first, as DEC-004 points 2 and 3 now decide (#1845). Its opening states the problem its concepts solve together, and names them as the parts of the answer. Each concept's section opens, below its link to its term, with a new part, `**Why:**`: what the concept is for, in one paragraph of plain words, saying again a reason the Context or the Rationale of a record the view anchors gives. It names no actor, and reads `None.` where no record gives a reason. The explanation follows below it, after a blank line, with no label. `pkit analysis new view` writes the part with its hint, and where the format rule binds, `pkit analysis validate` holds it like the section's other parts. A label listed right before an opening now holds its paragraph alone, so the check tells the two apart. A project's own structure may not place an addition before a section's explanation. The proposed rule `RS-SAN-005` names the records a view's reasons come from among those it anchors, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0.
