- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A domain-model view shows how its concepts move together, as DEC-004 points 2 and 3 now decide (#1847). After the class diagram, its opening holds a flow: a Mermaid flowchart of the events and the state changes across the group's concepts, with an `accTitle`, an `accDescr` and no styling. It names no actor and no use case, and shows only what the view's sections state. The opening's hint, which `pkit analysis new view` writes, asks for it, and `analysis-author`'s view walkthrough shows one beside its class diagram. No check reads a diagram, so nothing requires the flow.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Each analysis keeps a generated overview, `overview/README.md` in its folder, as DEC-003 point 8 decides: the reader's entry point. It shows the glossary and each domain-model view with the first paragraph of its opening, the actors, the requirements and their map, the areas in the order of their numbers with each one's goal and use cases, the chain of use cases, the use-case diagram, and each journey with its flow, every artefact in force linked. `pkit analysis overview` writes it, and the stamp rewrites the overview of the analysis it writes in. It carries a fingerprint of the data it reads, and `pkit analysis validate` fails a stale one, naming the command. A missing overview is a warning, so an upgrade fails nothing. Each area's goal is one line in the settings file's new `areas`, in an entry of `analyses` or at the file's top. The check warns of an area of use cases in force without a goal, and fails a goal that names no area. The new setting `overview: false` keeps no overview. The capability declares the overview's folder as held, at the location's top and one folder down.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A use case shows where the system falls short of an accepted record. Below each step or variant the system does not carry out yet stands a box, a `> [!WARNING]` callout whose text opens with `**Not built yet:**`, naming the record and the issue, and the use case's new optional `shortfalls` lists each one as data: its step or variant, its issue and its record. `pkit analysis validate` holds each box to an entry and each entry to a box, whatever any rule's status, and requires each entry's record among the use case's record anchors, and accepted, found through `pkit refs lookup`, which the backbone this release ships resolves a capability's decision with: upgrade the capability together with the backbone. The overview ends with a "Not built yet" section listing every open shortfall. Its fingerprint reads the entries, and its version is 3, so an overview written before is a warning until `pkit analysis overview` writes it anew. DEC-001 points 1, 5 and 9 and DEC-003 point 8 are refined in place, and SAN gains RS-SAN-013, proposed, which judges that each box, its step and its record agree.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The analysis links its citations of records, points and rules, as the new point 13 of DEC-001 decides (#1865). In every analysis, `pkit analysis link` links the first citation of each record, point and rule in each section of an artefact in force to what it cites, as `pkit refs lookup --json` finds it: a record's file, a point's heading by GitHub's anchor, a rule's heading. The forms are listed in the README's "Linking citations", from `COR-050` and `COR-050 point 14` to `points 6 and 9`, `PRJ-002 D1 and D4`, `software-analysis DEC-001 point 2` and `RS-WRITE-005`. A `DEC-NNN` names the capability a list carries, else the word before it, else the analysis's own, and otherwise takes no link and is warned of. A section of a use case, an actor, a journey or a requirement is each of its parts. Code, headings, labels and other links are skipped, a citation is no mention of a term, and `<!-- pkit:not-a-citation -->` marks one citation as none. Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` reports a link missing, extra or stale and names the command. It fails into a file the project writes, and warns into one it receives as a synced copy, such as a core record. Run `pkit analysis link` once after you take this release, and again after a version transition that rewords a heading you cite. The examples the hints ship cite a project's own record, such as `PRJ-003`, in place of a bare `DEC-003`, which names no capability.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The new command `pkit analysis renumber <id> [--to <id>] [--base <ref>] [--dry-run | --yes]` gives a use case, a journey or a requirement your branch numbered a free number before your change reaches the default branch, with every reference to it (DEC-001 point 3). It renames the artefact's file, rewrites its `id` and its heading, and rewrites each mention of the id in the repository's analyses: front matter, anchors and deferrals, prose, links to the file or its heading, revalidation records, and a mention written with its analysis's name, such as `core:UC-021`. Each overview that names the id is drawn anew. Without `--to`, the new number is the next free one, as the stamp would give it. The command refuses an artefact the default branch holds, a number your working tree holds, and one not past every number the default branch holds or its history gave. It shows the files it would change first: `--dry-run` writes nothing, `--yes` writes, a terminal is asked, and off a terminal it refuses. Each collision `pkit analysis check-numbers` reports now names the command, with the base where it is not the default branch.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The new record software-analysis DEC-005 decides how a reader finds each function in an analysis (#2106), and partially supersedes DEC-001's optional grouping of use cases by area. Every use case lies in the functional area of the function it serves, cut by what the use cases do and never by how many there are. A journey tells each path one actor takes through several of its own use cases, and each concept leads back to what is built on it. **The skill.** The `analysis-author` skill plans the areas before the first use case, writes each use case with `--area`, and writes a journey for each path as part of building. DEC-001 point 12, DEC-002 point 3 and DEC-003 point 8 name what the record adds. **The rule.** `SAN` gains `RS-SAN-014`, proposed, which judges where each use case lies and that a journey tells each path. Proposed rules bind nothing, so `SAN` stays at 1.0.0. The check of a use case in no area, and each area's journeys in the overview, are not built yet.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A reader who starts from a concept now finds what is built on it, as DEC-004 point 7 and DEC-005 point 3 decide (#2104). In an analysis that keeps a domain model, `pkit analysis link` ends each view in force with a section `## What is built on these concepts`. Under each concept the view explains, it lists each use case, journey and requirement in force of the view's analysis whose body links the concept's term, by its id and title, or `None.`. The stamp writes the section on a new view. Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` reports the section missing or stale and names the command, so an analysis with a view fails validation until you run `pkit analysis link` once. The structure check reads the section as no part of the view. The views' places declare the form of its links, so a change that only retargets them asks no answer. The overview's areas gain "Its concepts": each view of the analysis that explains a term one of the area's use cases links, read from those links. The overview's fingerprint reads them, and its version is 5, so an overview written before is a warning until `pkit analysis overview` writes it anew. Run `pkit analysis link` before `pkit analysis overview`. The proposed rules RS-SAN-005, RS-SAN-008 and RS-SAN-011 read a view's closing section as no statement. DEC-001 points 1 and 11, DEC-003 point 8 and DEC-004 points 2 and 7 are refined in place.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Every mention of an analysis artefact by its id now links to it, as DEC-001 point 14 decides (#2109). In every analysis, a domain model or none, `pkit analysis link` links the first mention of each artefact's id in each section: a use case, a journey, a requirement, an actor or a view to its file, and a term to its glossary entry, by a path from the file. A bold label is read, so a journey's seams and a precondition's producers link their ids inside the bold, and each step line of a journey links its use case's id, as the stamp writes it on a new journey. An id alone names an artefact of its own analysis, in force or withdrawn, and `core:UC-021` one of an analysis yours builds on. Code, a goal, an actor's first sentence and an artefact's own id stay plain. Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` reports such a link missing, extra or stale and names the command, so each analysis that names an id fails validation until you run `pkit analysis link` once. The forms of a journey's steps and seams and of a precondition's producers, and `pkit analysis check-numbers`, read a linked id as the id. Each place declares the links' form, so a change that only moves them asks no answer. The shipped example of a use case's other actors names its id plain. DEC-001 and DEC-004 point 7 are refined in place.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis propose` refuses, with exit 1 and nothing proposed, an explanation whose `schema_version` is not 1, naming the version `pkit friction explain` answered. An explanation without the key comes from a backbone that predates it and reads as version 1, so an older backbone still works.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The `software-engineer` agent gains "Friction answers": when its change makes an anchored artefact owe an answer, it may give it with `pkit friction revalidate` or `pkit friction defer`, without asking first, and only for what its own change owes — the answers the change check asks of it, and the revalidation of a combined state after a conflict it resolved. It passes `--yes` under that rule alone, and writes a justification it can defend from the diff. A reviewer other than it, where the project chose one, checks every `unchanged` and `deferred` reason against the change. Each deferral it writes names an open follow-up issue, and for each artefact it revalidates as `updated` it says what changed in it, in one sentence, under the pull request's `## What changed in each updated document`. It says in its hand-off that the change wrote answers, and never composes the list of them. Any other answer, and any reason for having no anchors, it names in its hand-off for the person. The rule is the backbone's this release ships (COR-050 point 3; the agents README, "Friction writers"), so the capability requires that backbone. Requires backbone >=2.0.0.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — `docs-reviewer` checks the answers a change wrote on its documents, as a reviewer other than their writer, the role COR-050 point 3 describes and software-engineering DEC-002 gives it wherever it reviews: its docs-match-behaviour lens applied to the change's own claims about its documents. It reads them from the change check's own list (`pkit friction check --base <base> --head <head>`, "Answers written in this change"), never from the author's account, and raises a `[block]` finding in its verdict on each that does not hold: an `unchanged` reason the change does not bear out; a deferral the change writes or rewords whose reason does not hold, or that names no open follow-up issue; and a document revalidated as `updated` with no sentence on what changed under the pull request's `## What changed in each updated document`, or with one that does not match the diff. A deferral the change keeps as it was is not judged again. An answer on an artefact the change check asked nothing about, and a reason for having no anchors, are a person's decision that no reviewer can confirm, so it names each in an `[advisory]` finding, and still checks the sentence of such an answer revalidated as `updated`. A project with friction dormant has nothing to check. The check reads the change check's list of answers, which the backbone this release ships gives, so the capability requires that backbone.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — `docs-reviewer` checks a change's revisits of the targets of a link, as it checks revalidations and deferrals (COR-050 points 3 and 16). It reads them from the change check's own list, and raises a `[block]` finding on each that does not hold: a target the change revisited by changing it that still contradicts the fields the change modified on its source, an `unchanged` revisit entry whose reason does not hold against the change, and a `deferred` one whose reason does not hold or that names no open follow-up issue. A changed target that contradicts nothing but leaves part of the change unmet is an `[advisory]`. A target revisited by changing it needs no sentence on what changed. A revisit entry the list flags as not asked for by the change is a person's decision, named in an `[advisory]` finding, never blocked. software-engineering DEC-002 D1 and the README say so. The `software-engineer` agent may write the revisit entries its own change owes with `pkit friction revisit`, each with a reason new to the change and each `deferred` one naming an open follow-up issue, and names any other entry for the person to decide. The writer and the list's revisits are the backbone's this release ships, so the capability requires that backbone.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The new record DEC-004 has the software-engineer, when it builds a walking skeleton, write each convention that building it settles into the project's conventions corpus, in the skeleton's change (project-management DEC-060). A person accepts the entries once, with that change, before the slices are planned, so every slice reads the conventions as the project's rules. Where the corpus offers no single location the project owns, as the ownership test and the other agents' `owns` decide, the agent writes none and says so. The change merges only once the entries are accepted, so where the agent can reach no person, it hands back the entries and its question, and the change waits in review. The software-engineer's definition and the README say what it writes, and DEC-001 points to the new record. Requires project-management >=0.55.0.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The capability ships its operational rules as its loaded rule set `SWE`, whose `rules.md` every session loads wherever the capability is installed (COR-070): while building, run the tests of what your change changes and the checks the project names as its fast checks; where the project runs its full suite in a gate on each change, you need not run it yourself, and where it runs it nowhere, run it once or say you could not.

### Changed
- `pkit sync` now works at the release your project records, and moves no version (COR-059, ADR-065). It copies that release's content, regenerates what is derived from your project's own declarations, and runs merge delivery in its tier that asks nothing. It writes no version, no manifest and no seed path, and runs no migration. `pkit upgrade` is the one command that moves a project to a new release, and every migration runs inside it. Sync copies a capability only at the version the project records: one the running pkit ships newer is reported pending `pkit upgrade`, and one it ships older is skipped, with no override. `pkit upgrade` copies through the same step, runs each capability's migrations after its copy, and creates each missing seed path (the agents overlay, an area's or a capability's `project/` folder, an adapter's project settings), never over one that exists. `pkit agents reconcile --write` creates a missing agents overlay from the seed. **Pipelines and every project's first sync.** Sync now stops, writing nothing, when the running pkit is not the release the project records, when the last upgrade is incomplete, or when the pin is newer than the running pkit, and its message names the remedy. No flag overrides the stop. A project upgraded before this release has no recorded completion, so its first `pkit sync` under this release stops and names `pkit upgrade`. A project that pins no version, run by a pkit installed fresh in each pipeline run, meets the stop at every release until it pins: commit a pin (`pkit pin`, or the pin `pkit upgrade` writes by default), or install the project's version of pkit in the pipeline. `pkit sync --force` is accepted for this release and ignored, with a notice, and a later release removes it. This release's migration names each file of your project that passes it. The guard replaces #1452's refusal, which no release shipped. **Before you upgrade to this major release,** widen the `requires_backbone` range of any component of your own (an incubated or externally sourced capability, or one the methodology no longer ships) that stops below 2.0.0, as `pkit new capability` scaffolds it: `pkit upgrade` refuses a component whose range excludes its target, before it writes anything.
- `pkit upgrade` runs every migration inside the transition, in COR-010's order (ADR-065 point 4). It copies the release, renders `.pkit/.gitignore`, runs the backbone's migrations, then each adapter's, then each capability's, dependencies first, and only then creates the missing seed paths and regenerates merge, deploy, the ignore file, the workspace and provisioning. An adapter's migrations are read from the release's bundle, so they run in a project for the first time, and a project's copy of an adapter now carries every file pkit reads there, `permission-enforcement.yaml` among them. **New stops, before anything is written.** A capability's new version that ships a skill or agent your project's own artefact already names is asked about at a terminal (override, skip or inspect), and a skip is recorded in the capability's manifest before its copy. Without a terminal the upgrade stops and names a re-run at a terminal or a rename. An adapter or a capability this pkit ships older than your project records stops the upgrade, with no override. **The dependency gates** read the versions the upgrade installs: a capability the upgrade moves whose dependency is missing or out of range, or whose mandatory process connection the upgrade would leave unmet, stops it, and a moved dependency that leaves a capability of your own out of range, or with a mandatory process connection unmet, warns and proceeds only under the new `pkit upgrade --force`, which overrides nothing else. The report lists each allow entry and top-level key the settings merge adds to `.claude/settings.json`, and the dry run lists each component's migrations and each collision it would ask about. `pkit capabilities upgrade` becomes a transition of one capability: it checks the new version's `requires_backbone`, records a skip before its copy, and runs the capability's migrations after its copy, then deploys and renders `.pkit/.gitignore`. ([#1574])
- A pinned project's `pkit upgrade` finishes its own pin first. Run under the pin's code, it hands over to a newer latest release as before, and that release's upgrade finishes any interrupted transition on the way. With no newer release, at the latest release, ahead of it or offline, it now runs the transition to its pin itself, so content behind the pin or an interrupted transition to it is finished. Content ahead of the pin, with no newer release to hand over to, is refused before anything is written, and offline the message names reconnecting. A pinned dry run now runs the newer release's upgrade with `--dry-run`, and `--force` passes to it, as it does from the new `pkit pin <version> --force`. A pinned project whose installed pkit is its pin now advances the same way, without a terminal too, and its upgrade no longer updates the pkit tool. The order lives in the pin's own code, so a project pinned below this release gets it on its next raise past this release. **`pkit upgrade` refuses under the router's fallback.** Where the router cannot fetch the pin and runs your installed pkit instead, `pkit upgrade` stops before it writes anything, whether that pkit is older or newer than the pin, where it used to raise the pin to the installed version. Its message names reconnecting, the pin run through `uvx`, or the pin's version installed from the HTTPS URL. To move on purpose, run `pkit unpin`, then `pkit upgrade`. A pin that is no version is no longer turned into a `uvx` or install command in that message, or in sync's. ([#1575])
- `pkit capabilities install` and `pkit capabilities upgrade` now stop wherever `pkit sync` stops. Each copies the capability the running pkit ships, so each stops, before it reads or writes anything, when the running pkit is not the release the project records, when the last upgrade is incomplete, when the manifest is missing or its version is no version, or when the pin is newer than the running pkit. Each stops under `--dry-run` too, and `install` under `--plan` too. Nothing overrides the stop, `--force` included, and its message names sync's remedies for the state it finds, with the command as you would run it again. A project upgraded before this release has no recorded completion, so both stop and name `pkit upgrade` until its first upgrade under this release. With a pkit newer than the project, pin the project or upgrade it before you install or upgrade a capability. A project that pins no version, run by a pkit that moves to each new release, meets the stop at every release until it pins. `pkit capabilities register` copies nothing and runs as before. **`pkit capabilities upgrade` refuses a capability the running pkit ships older than the project records,** where it used to copy it and record the older version. Nothing overrides the refusal. Its message names both versions, and `pkit upgrade` run by a pkit that ships the recorded version or newer. ([#1580])
- Your project's answers to friction move out of the artefacts' blocks into answer files, once, as COR-062 point 11 decides. **The upgrade to 2.0.0 makes the move:** the backbone migration `2.0.0/002-move-answers-to-files.sh` takes each artefact's `revalidated` block and `revisited` list out of its front matter into a `moved-<hash>.yaml` file in `.pkit/project/friction/answers/`, every other byte kept and each value keeping its revalidation point. It leaves what your project receives by sync, writes over no answer file, and writes nothing when run again. The change check lists each moved answer apart, and asks nobody to accept it. **From then on an answer left in a block fails `pkit validate`**, naming the move. Where no answer file exists, one error says your answers have not moved. In a file your project receives by sync it is only reported. **A branch open at the move** that answered in a block conflicts with it, or merges cleanly where the block held no answer before. It catches up by merging the default branch in and running the move again from the project's root, `ROOT=. bash .pkit/migrations/backbone/2.0.0/002-move-answers-to-files.sh`, which takes its answer out into a new moved file. **`pkit friction since`** now answers whether every change since a commit lies in answer files the change adds, which the base where the head left it does not hold. Its `--json` document goes to `schema_version` 2, and a reader of version 1 refuses it. An answer written in a block lies outside.
- The friction readers and writers now read and write your answers in answer files alone, as COR-062 point 1 decides. **`pkit friction check`, `check --all`, `debt` and `explain`** read an artefact's answers from `.pkit/project/friction/answers/` only. An answer left in an artefact's block is read only in history, for the point its value keeps. At the head it answers nothing, and `pkit validate` fails it, naming the move. Run over a project whose answers have not moved, these commands refuse to run and say so once, naming the move, instead of reporting each answer as missing (COR-062 point 11). **`pkit friction revalidate`, `defer` and `revisit`** always write an answer file of the change's own, and refuse an artefact whose block still holds an answer, naming the move. `record-status` still writes the block's `last-check`. The change check's `answer-file-ignored` finding goes, since no block answer is read beside an answer file any more. **A move whose history starts again**, such as an entry that becomes a document of its own: the change check no longer reads a value its old location carried as written back, nor reports the base's tip's answers on that location as answered apart (COR-062 point 10).
- `pkit agents` now models an overlay category referenced only through `reads.patterns` as an OPTIONAL read (ADR-052): its absence no longer skips the agent. An agent whose only undefined categories are optional now reports as deployable, and the undefined optional categories surface in a dedicated `Optional` footer state ("agents deploy without them") rather than as a skip cause. `reconcile` gains a sixth state for a missing optional (patterns-only) category — appending a commented stub framed "the agent already deploys without this" instead of an action-needed blocker. The agent-overlay status gains an `optional` field with matching `agent_category_roles()` / `optional_categories()` helpers, and `deployable` now counts only undefined HARD categories as blocking. ([#773])
- `pkit init` now tells you where it will install before it does, and asks first when that target isn't your current folder (#780). Every run announces the resolved target and why it was picked, plus any existing project-kit install it finds between your current directory and that target. When the target is a parent directory (a git repository root above you) or a fresh non-git folder, init asks for confirmation on an interactive terminal and refuses on a non-interactive one (an absent or closed stdin counts as non-interactive) — a piped `yes |` no longer silently installs somewhere you did not expect. Declining the prompt exits non-zero, so a chained `pkit init && …` stops. If the target already has a project-kit install, init refuses (non-zero) and points you at `pkit sync` to refresh it; a leftover `.pkit` there that is not an install is refused too, naming what is wrong and asking you to move it aside — both before any prompt. Every refusal's suggested remedy is one the same command accepts, run from where you stand: a redirect to `pkit sync` reads `cd <project> && pkit sync` when you are outside that project (for example after `--root` named it), and an already-adopted repository git will not verify or open is pointed at the git fix, then `pkit sync`. A new `--here` installs into the current directory (refused when it is a subfolder of a git worktree, where a `.pkit/` there would be unreachable, or sits inside an existing project-kit project, where it would nest a second install), and `--yes` accepts the offer to install in a fresh non-git current folder non-interactively; `--dry-run` previews without prompting, but every refusal still applies.
- `pkit init` now validates the target it resolves instead of trusting a bare `.git` on sight (#787). A workspace folder carrying a vestigial `.git` (a structurally hollow one) is no longer offered as an install root and never mislabelled a "git repository" — init falls through to offering an install in your current directory; a real-looking repository git cannot open is refused as broken, with repair guidance rather than an ownership fix. A structurally-real repository that git refuses to verify (dubious ownership / safe.directory, routine in Docker/CI/ sudo trees) is now detected and guided — init refuses, names the `safe.directory` remedy and the explicit-target escape rather than dropping a shadowed `.pkit/` inside it. From a subfolder of an already-adopted project, init refuses and redirects to `pkit sync`. A new `--root <path>` installs at an explicit target non-interactively — the sanctioned way to install at a resolved parent (e.g. a git repository root) in CI; a bare `--yes` no longer installs anywhere other than your current directory, so an unattended run can never install somewhere you are not standing. `--root` must point at an existing directory — a typo fails cleanly at the CLI boundary instead of materialising a tree — and refuses a target that is a subfolder of a git worktree or sits inside an existing install, where the nested `.pkit/` would be unreachable; at a root git will not vouch for, it warns and installs. The shared root resolver (`find_target_root`) likewise skips a vestigial `.git`, and still resolves a `.pkit/`-marked project with no git at all. ([#791])
- Container validation now reads the active wiring (#1030). `pkit validate` resolves the project's wiring once per run and shares it across the `connections`, `versions`, `friction` and `rule-sets` members; the container check inside an artefact or a rule reads from it which roles have an active provider and at which version each of their data points stands. A role block whose role has an active provider is no longer reported as orphaned — only a role no installed capability answers is. A point block whose `schema_version` differs from the active provider's point, or which names no data point that provider defines, is reported as inert and its body left unvalidated. A compatible point block's body is now validated by the provider's point schema, and its errors fail `pkit validate` like any malformed block; a point schema that cannot be applied is reported. A bare role word that two active roles share is an error until the key is written in its qualified form.
- Package metadata is strict on unknown keys. A key the package schema does not know, anywhere in a component's package.yaml, is now an error rather than a warning: it fails the packages pass of pkit validate and refuses pkit capabilities register, located at the key and naming the nearest known key ("unknown key 'foootprint'; did you mean 'footprint'?"). The fields COR-017 lists, schema_version, description and requires_backbone, are required of every component, adapters included, and every part of a role name or point address in the connections block must be a word, [a-z][a-z0-9-]*, the pattern the configuration file's selection keys already use. Every kit-shipped package complies and arrives with the schema in the same sync, so a project with only kit-shipped components sees no change. A package you wrote yourself, such as an incubated capability, that carries a misspelt or extra key now fails: rename the key to the one suggested or delete it, and add any required field it lacks. A tree not yet synced keeps its own permissive schema, where an unknown key still only warns. The package file's schema_version is unchanged and no migration runs. The lifecycle README records the decision, under "Strict on unknown keys".
- The commands a component registers are looked up and run through one runner, `project_kit.command_runner` (ADR-057 point 5): one walk of the `commands:` tree that the dispatcher, package validation, the validator registry and the process engine's predicate runner all read, and one bounded run with a policy per kind — the predicate policy (the subject and `--json`, the environment unchanged, anything but an answered JSON object indeterminate) and the query policy of `pkit validate`'s capability validators (the declaration required, the offline marker set, the answer validated as a findings document). The time bound is one named backbone constant, thirty seconds, now documented with the runner contract in the lifecycle README ("How a registered command is run"). Predicates now run in their own process group, and a predicate that overruns has its whole group killed — the interpreter a `uv run --script` shebang starts as a grandchild used to keep running past the bound; an interrupt of `pkit` kills the group too, for predicates and validators alike, and a predicate whose output cannot be decoded as UTF-8 is indeterminate instead of a crash. `dispatcher.resolve_capability_script` resolves a command reference by its path through the tree, so a nested leaf (`"create page"`) resolves as well as a top-level one.
- The `pkit validate` command is the project's one check of its own state against the invariants its records own (COR-055; ADR-058). A validator registry (`project_kit.validators`) replaces the hand-wired passes: each functionality registers a name, an order and a callable answering summary lines and findings, each finding with a severity — `error` fails the command, `warning`, `info` and `report` print. The backbone's twelve members run in dependence order — `manifests`, `schemas`, `configuration`, `packages`, `connections`, `versions`, `decisions`, `friction`, `rule-sets`, `refs`, `process`, `data` — and the five focused commands (`schemas validate`, `decisions validate`, `refs validate`, `data validate <path>`, `process validate <address>`) stay, `schemas validate` and `data validate` now phrasing an unknown key through the shared renderer (one line per key, with the nearest known key). A capability registers its own validators in package metadata: a `validators:` entry names a `commands:` leaf by `command` (optional `order`), the leaf carries `query-contract: true` — the literal of ADR-057's query-contract declaration, now specified — and the umbrella runs it addressed `<capability>:<name>`, from the project root with the one argument `--json`, the offline marker set (`PKIT_OFFLINE=1`, `UV_OFFLINE=1`), in its own process group, bounded like a predicate, reading one JSON findings document from stdout; no answer — a leaf without the declaration, an abnormal exit, a timeout, a half-formed document — is an error finding. The package schema requires the declaration on a validator's command, and package validation checks the reference like every other command reference. One renderer prints every member under its heading and one summary closes the report. `--only NAME` and `--skip NAME` address members; `--no-refs` stays as `--skip refs`. The `refs` member reports drift between an artifact's front matter and its body as a warning, and as an error a path owned twice or by nobody, a hook no provider answers, colliding providers, a missing storyboard, a citation that does not resolve or a sub-procedure that does not exist; it reads a bracketed `reads.patterns` declaration the way the deploy does, so four false positives on the shipped agents are gone; `pkit refs validate` keeps failing on any finding. The `data` member checks every file a `pkit_schema:` field or a `binds_to:` glob claims, in the repository outside `.pkit/` and in every project-owned `project/` folder under it, skipping what nothing claims (an unreadable unclaimed file included); the `process` member checks that every declared definition resolves. The check aggregator calls `pkit validate` once in place of `schemas validate` and `decisions validate`; the diff-scoped checks stay their own lines.
- Roles and points of a connection can be cited as typed tokens: [<publisher>::<role>] names a role and [<publisher>::<role>:<point>] a point under it (COR-019, refined per COR-053). pkit refs validate resolves every such token in an agent's or skill's body against what installed capabilities declare, read from the run's one wiring, and reports one naming no declared role or point like an unresolved record citation; a bracketed token carrying :: that is not an address is reported as malformed, on its text alone. pkit refs lookup accepts an address and prints where it is declared. The shared schema library gains refs.schema.json#/$defs/address_token for the shape in data; the reference_token fragment is unchanged. The decisions README gains a Citing section listing each citation form.
- A capability's connections are readable before it is installed, and what an install or an uninstall would change in the wiring is shown before it happens (COR-053 point 8). pkit capabilities show <name> lists, from package metadata alone, the roles a capability provides, the points it accepts and offers, its extensions and what would connect here, for installed capabilities and for those in the local catalogue: capabilities authored in the repository and those that ship with the running pkit. pkit capabilities install <name> --plan lists the connections the install would make and break, the role conflicts it would open with the provider-selection command that resolves each, and what the capability needs; pkit capabilities uninstall <name> --plan lists the fillers lost, the processes and other counterparts left without a provider, the artefacts whose role blocks would be orphaned and the selections left naming it. Both plans are computed by the wiring resolver over the installed set plus or minus the capability, so the operation then changes the wiring by exactly the plan; both write nothing, and --json prints the machine form of show and the plans. pkit status suggests, for an unfilled data point, a role nobody provides or an upstream not installed, the capabilities of the local catalogue that would answer it, reading only what is on disk; a suggestion is never an action.
- `pkit connections resolve <address> [--json]` prints one data point as it resolves — its value, or each entry of a union or additive point with its origin, how it resolved or why not, and every filler considered. The JSON document is stable: it is how a capability's own script reads a point it defines without importing the backbone. Read-only; exit 1 when the point does not resolve or nothing defines it.
- Process connections by role (COR-053 points 2, 4, 5 and 6). A process definition's depends_on entry may name its upstream by role, <publisher>::<role>:<point>, reaching the process the role's active provider offers; it may state the upstream interface version it targets and carry a mandatory mark with a reason, and a process interface may carry an integer version. All four are additive: every existing definition validates unchanged. The capability's depends-on list in package metadata is generated from those entries by the new pkit capabilities refresh <name>, marked generated: true; pkit validate fails a stale copy and names the refresh as the fix, and refresh refuses a kit-shipped capability in an adopting project. Installing, registering or upgrading a capability whose mandatory upstream is missing or at another interface version is refused, the mark's reason shown; upgrading or uninstalling the capability such a mark depends on warns, naming each counterpart, and proceeds only under --force. pkit process health evaluates a hand-off contract on a role-addressed entry by reading the active provider from the wiring resolver, and reports an address no provider offers as indeterminate. The runtime engine and every gate still never read depends_on, and a test pins it.
- The pkit process couple stamp writes the connection forms COR-053 added. --upstream takes the role form <publisher>::<role>:<point> beside <capability>:<process-id>, resolved through the wiring as pkit process health resolves it; --version <n> writes the upstream interface version the entry targets and --mandatory <reason> the mandatory mark, each only when given. The stamp writes the definition only: when the capability's generated depends-on list is left stale it ends by naming pkit capabilities refresh <capability>, under --dry-run too, and never writes package metadata itself. A re-run with a different version or mark on a declared edge refuses, as a different why already did. The pkit validate packages pass now reports an offered process point whose schema_version differs from its definition's interface.version, naming both values and both files, and an offered process point that names no definition of its capability. The process skill's couple walkthrough asks for the role form, the version and the mark, and ends with the refresh step.
- Process journal logging is opt-in and off by default (COR-033 point 7). The process engine keeps its per-subject journal only when `.pkit/project/config.yaml` sets `process.journal.enabled: true`; with it unset the engine writes and reads no journal, and every verdict — position, gates, invariants, the live blocked overlay, whether a move is allowed — is unchanged, because none of them ever read the journal. Only the audit goes: no move or wait entries, and no `since` on a wait. `pkit process status` says "journal logging is not enabled for this project" instead of an empty history, and its `--json` gains `journal_logging: {enabled, committed}` beside `journal`.
When logging is on, `process.journal.committed` chooses between committing the journals (`true`) and keeping them per clone (`false`, the default). The rendered `.pkit/.gitignore` follows it: the backbone now declares the journal pattern `.pkit/capabilities/*/project/process/**/*.journal.jsonl` itself (the engine owns the path) and drops it only for committed journals. `pkit config set` re-renders `.pkit/.gitignore` at once when the new value changes it. `pkit validate` checks both keys (booleans, unknown keys refused with the nearest known one).
Upgrading to this release keeps behaviour: the upgrade runs the backbone migration `001-keep-process-journal-logging.sh`, which records `enabled: true` for a project it finds keeping journals, with `committed` following whether those journals are tracked by git, and writes nothing otherwise. Upgrade the project-management capability together with the backbone: an older version still declares its own journal ignore line; while journals are committed the render leaves that entry out and names it in a comment, and `pkit validate` warns until the capability is upgraded. Documented in the process README ("The journal — optional audit") and the CLI reference's configuration table.
- Uninstalling a capability whose subtree stays on disk — an incubated one, or any capability in the methodology's source repository — now removes its deployed skills and agents through each installed adapter's undeploy primitive, `undeploy-capability.sh <name>`, found by name as the deploy primitives are. The lifecycle no longer reaches into a harness's layout itself. An adapter that ships no undeploy primitive is named in a `warning` line, since its harness still carries the capability, and the uninstall result lists it. The undeploy runs before the registry entry is removed, so a failing adapter leaves the capability registered and the uninstall re-runnable.
- A reviewer's read-only claim is stated as a property of the reviewer role: it governs the work under review and the repository it lives in, not "writes nothing anywhere", and a reviewer that can execute keeps every working file of a review — a dumped diff, a script, a reproduction (even one that executes a payload to prove a defect), captured output — in the agent workspace, leaving no file of its own outside it. The agents README gains a "Reviewers" paragraph with the rule; the core `methodology-reviewer` and `convention-compliance-reviewer` carry the shared `## What read-only covers` section and scope their read-only claims; the `agent-author` skill and the `pkit new agent` template point a new reviewer's author at the section. The core `methodology-reviewer` and `convention-compliance-reviewer` gain the `Write` tool, scoped by their bodies to the agent workspace, so a review's working files land there without a prompt instead of through a shell redirect, which the permission hook judges like any other shell write.
- A change to `friction.exclude` is read as a change to the path anchors whose files it moves, and both friction checks agree on an artefact under an excluded path. Each state is read under the exclusions it declared: the change check (`pkit friction check`) reads its base under the base's, and the whole-repository check (`pkit friction check --all`), with `friction debt` and `friction explain`, reads an artefact's revalidation point under the point's, never every state under HEAD's. Narrowing `friction.exclude` over files an anchor did not stand on is friction the covered artefacts answer in the pull request, and stale debt dated from that commit once merged; widening it is friction where a file it takes from an anchor changed while the anchor stood on it, dated from that change, and is otherwise a new, non-failing `left-out` finding in both checks. So a pull request that only edits the configuration can now fail an enforcing check. A widening no longer erases stale debt, and a narrowing no longer dates it from changes made while the files were left out. An artefact a narrowing lets back in must revalidate in that change, and is stale from that commit otherwise; an artefact under an excluded path owes no answer, though a new `at` on it is judged like any other. A path anchor an exclusion leaves matching only excluded files is dead and says why, `excluded since this diff` or `excluded since <commit>`, and `friction explain` lists the point's files under the point's exclusions and the commits behind each exclusion change. A state whose `friction.exclude` does not read is reported as `unreadable` and read as the state it is compared with. `pkit friction artefacts --json` gives each held file an `excluded` key.
- `pkit agents adopt` asks before it records an overlay category over a folder that already holds documents (COR-049 point 5): recording it puts those documents within reach of every agent that references the category. At a terminal it asks once for the run, listing each `category = path  (reached by <agents>)`; `--yes` consents without a prompt. **Behaviour change for non-interactive callers:** a run off a terminal without `--yes` that would record over such a folder now refuses — exit 1, nothing written, no folder created, no deploy — and names the command with `--yes`; before, it recorded silently. A folder `adopt` creates, or one that holds nothing, is recorded as before, with no prompt. **Output:** `adopt` prints one `recorded <category> = <path>  (<file>)` notice per category, each followed by the root the path was derived from and where to change it, in place of the `wired N overlay categor(ies):` block; `pkit agents reconcile` shows each entry it would record as `category = path  (reached by <agents>)`, and its consent is as before — the default shows, `--write` is the confirmation. **For a component's own commands:** the rule they inherit changed (COR-049 point 5) — recording a location in the component's own configuration takes no confirmation, and the command says what it recorded, which root it was derived from and where to change it; recording an overlay category over documents already there asks. No migration.
- A data point's command filler may declare what it reads beyond the working tree (COR-052 point 6): `reads:` on its `commands:` leaf, `history` (the current history, read at HEAD) and/or `settled` (the default branch as the backbone resolves it, never a base named for one run). The package schema takes the key as an optional, non-empty list of distinct values, and the `packages` member of `pkit validate` refuses it on a leaf without `query-contract: true`. For each such filler that was asked, the `connections` member of `pkit validate` adds one line naming what it read and at which commit (`<address>: <capability> (command '<ref>') reads history (HEAD <sha12>[, shallow clone])`, `... reads the default branch (<ref> at <sha12>)`), `pkit status` appends the same to the filler's line, and `pkit connections resolve --json` carries it as each filler's `reads`. A filler that declares `settled` is not started while the default branch resolves to no commit for any reason but having none yet: it is inert, with the fetch and the declaration as the fixes, never a base. The query policy removes `PKIT_CHECK_BASE` from the environment of every validator and every command filler it starts. `pkit repository base --json` gains `default_branch.unborn`, true only when the branch has no commit yet (no remote, and HEAD is that branch before its first commit); its `schema_version` stays 1.
**`pkit validate` and what a clone has fetched.** A command filler may declare that it reads beyond the working tree: `reads:` on its `commands:` entry, `history` and/or `settled`. `pkit validate`, `pkit status` and `pkit connections resolve` name what each such filler read and at which commit. `PKIT_CHECK_BASE` no longer reaches a filler or a validator — a project's own included — so their answers are the same whatever base a pipeline names. Nothing else changes in an installed project until a capability ships a filler that declares `settled`. From then on, in a repository with a remote, the default branch's remote-tracking copy must be present wherever the point is resolved — `pkit validate`, `pkit status`, `pkit connections resolve`: a pipeline job that checks out a single ref fetches it first (`git fetch origin <default-branch>`). Where it is missing the filler is inert, with the fetch as the fix — a warning under a `fallback` point, an error under a `fail` point — and naming a base does not fix it. That includes a repository with a remote whose default branch has not been pushed yet: the filler is inert there until the first push. A filler that declares `history` gives no answer where a shallow clone stops short of what it reads (`git fetch --unshallow`).
- COR-055 (accepted) decides what validation is: the project's one check of its own state against the invariants its records own, which the backbone's functionalities and installed components join by registering validators — a component by naming a command it registers. Every finding carries one severity from a shared set, and exactly one fails validation: a broken invariant, or a validator that gave no answer. COR-052 point 6 is refined to match: a validator is a query with no subject, as a filler is; and a data point whose every use is advisory may declare `fallback` — a use is advisory when nothing refuses on the point's value, nothing fails on it, and no finding is drawn from an entry's absence unless the point resolved with no filler inert. A point read only for findings that do not fail validation, which had to declare `fail`, may now fall back. The records state behaviour that has shipped: no command changes. ([#1282])
- `pkit new decision adr <slug>` derives where the record goes from the documentation roots when the overlay has no `adr-records` key (COR-049 points 3 to 5, #1264), where it used to refuse. The location is the internal root plus `architecture/decisions` — `docs/architecture/decisions` with the default root — and is recorded in `.pkit/agents/project/overlay.yaml` before the first record is stamped there, so a later change of root moves nothing. The command says what it recorded, the root it was derived from and where to change it, and that `pkit sync` deploys the agents that now reach the folder. A folder that is absent or empty is created and recorded without asking. A folder that already holds documents takes consent: a terminal is asked once, and the new `--yes` consents without a prompt. A non-interactive caller — a script, CI, an agent — that runs the command without `--yes` over such a folder is refused, exit 1, with nothing recorded and nothing stamped, and is named the command to run with `--yes`. An explicit `adr-records` value still wins, and a location inside `.pkit/`, explicit or derived, is still refused.
- `pkit friction check` and `check --all` agree on histories that edit and put back: an anchor has changed when what it stands on differs between two states — the base and the head, or the revalidation point and HEAD — so an edit put back is no change however the branch lands, and a file's mode counts. With several answering states an anchor is judged as a whole: it has changed where it differs from what it stood on at every one of them, each state read under its own `friction.exclude`. The revalidation point is the commit where the artefact first carried the `at` it carries, in whatever spelling YAML's timestamp grammar allows: a revert of a revalidation is not one, restating the stamp is not one, and a deferral put back keeps its first point, so none of them erases debt nobody answered. The change check reads an artefact's file history behind the base when the change alters its `at` or brings back a deferral, and judges a written-back `at` against that value's point: in enforcing mode, reverting a revalidation now fails where it takes back answers to debt that is still there, until someone revalidates or defers. Stale debt is dated from the first change not put back. In a shallow clone a value first carried inside it is read as first carried there and marked `cut`. `check --all`, `debt`, `explain` and `record-status` follow, so verdicts move for some histories: an edit and its revert is no longer debt, a reverted revalidation brings its debt back, and points and origins may move — a project running the after-merge status job may see many `last-check` blocks rewritten on its first run. Additive output: the answers list marks an `at` put back, or a deferral put back that covers nothing, as `written-back`, flags every deferral put back `put_back` (one that covers its anchor `stands`), and lists `kept` on a written-back revalidation; `check --json` gains `history` (`shallow`, `cut`); each `artefacts` entry of `check --all --json`, and the `explain --json` document, gain `revalidation_points`, and the former also `cut`; every `schema_version` stays 1.
- `pkit friction check` asks for a revalidation when a collection entry becomes a document of its own, as it does for any other move (#1359, COR-050 point 3). The check pairs the document with the entry it was, where the document's own `id` is the entry's key. Before, the entry read as removed and the document as new, and a new artefact counts as revalidated, so the move asked nothing. A document that becomes an entry is paired the same way. Such an artefact starts its history at the move, as `pkit friction check --all` reads it. Its content is compared as any other's, so a document that now carries its `id` as a field answers `updated`. A removed entry beside a new document with another id stays a removal and a new artefact.
- `pkit friction check`'s closing list of the answers a change wrote now says what each answer answers. Its heading states the question once: these documents rely on something this change modifies, and each answer says whether the document is still true. Each line names the document, the anchors the change modified that it relies on, and the outcome in plain words, with its words still quoted in full. `docs/guide.md relies on src/cli/**, which this change modifies. Still accurate: "…"` replaces `docs/guide.md  unchanged — "…"`. The outcomes read "updated in this change", "still accurate", "out of date, fixed later", "not tied to anything" and "no decision recorded". Each flag reads in plain words, in its own parentheses, and each deferral a revalidation kept follows on a line of its own. `--json` gains `changed` on each entry of `answers`: the anchors the change modified that the answer is about, as `{kind, value}`. `schema_version` stays 1.
- `pkit upgrade` now records where it is going before it changes anything and that it arrived only after its last migration, so an upgrade that stops on the way is finished by the next one instead of being skipped. Every project's first upgrade under this release runs each backbone migration once, because no project has recorded which of them already ran: the report and `--dry-run` say so and list them, and it does not happen again. Each migration checks for itself whether its change is needed, but three restore a default the project removed after they first ran: the architect's overlay categories in `.pkit/agents/project/overlay.yaml`, process journal logging in `.pkit/project/config.yaml` where old journals are still on disk, and the untracking of a runtime-local file declared ignored that the project put back into git. `pkit init` records the same two states, `pkit status` shows an upgrade left unfinished and each component an upgrade would move, and `pkit upgrade` writes a missing or corrupt `.pkit/manifest.yaml` at its version, which `pkit pin`, `pkit validate` and `pkit status` now name for it. ([#1568])
- A capability's manifest now sits under its project folder, `.pkit/capabilities/<name>/project/manifest.yaml`. No sync or upgrade deletes or overwrites it there, and the capability's entry in `.pkit/manifest.yaml` names that path. A manifest an older release left at the capability's root moves there the next time pkit writes it. No migration runs for it. The skills and agents you chose to skip on a naming collision now stay skipped across `pkit sync` and `pkit upgrade`. Until now they were read from a file nothing wrote, so every refresh copied them back. A restamp now keeps the rest of the manifest's `backend_state`. The bundle no longer ships project-kit's own manifest of project-management (ADR-065 point 2). ([#1566])
- The decisions README writes a record's points as headings that keep their numbers (#1422, COR-058). Its schema shows each point as a heading, `### 5. Title` or `### D1 — Title`, the optional `retired-points` field, and a stub for each retired point. A numbered list stays allowed inside a point or another sub-section. Its refining rule has a new point take the next unused number, never renumbers one, and keeps the id of a point no other point continues in `retired-points`, with a stub. It reads the entry and the stub as data, not residue. Its citing section names a point two ways, "COR-050 point 11" in prose and `COR-050#11` as an anchor's value. `pkit new decision` stamps a first point heading, `### 1. Point title`, under `## Decision`, so a new record starts with its points as headings. COR-025's illustrative ADR block shows the `retired-points` field.
- The core records whose points were a numbered list, or paragraphs opening with a bold number or label, write each point as a heading carrying its number or label, word for word (#1421, COR-058): COR-028, COR-029, COR-031, COR-033, COR-039, COR-043, COR-046, COR-048 to COR-057. A page can now anchor to one point of these records, such as `COR-050#11`, and a change to another point of the record does not ask for the page to be revalidated (COR-050 point 5). No point's text, number or citation changes, so every citation of a point still names it. The sentence after the last point of COR-028, COR-031 and COR-039 moves into the Decision's opening, with the same words. Where text cited a record by line number, it now cites the point or section that line held, so the citation survives the conversion. The `pkit permissions` messages, the permission hook's reason for refusing a negation glob in a domain-scoped grant, and the confinement toolkit's notes cite ADR-004's alternative "Relabel `network-egress` as natively enforced…" instead of "ADR-004 §61".
- The recommended install of pkit names a tag on a release's commit. `pkit init` ends by printing a tagged install, where it printed the URL with no tag. The CLI reference's "Installing pkit on PATH" and the README recommend a tagged install, and so do the errors of `.pkit/cli/pkit` that advise installing pkit. Only a tagged install ties pkit's version to one content, so the version a project records names the content it holds. The URL with no tag installs main's head: that is the development install, which no version vouches for. It still works, and nothing checks whether an install is tagged. ([#1582])
- A whole record anchor stands on the record's content, read as an artefact's content is, and the cycle check and the upstream-first order follow record anchors (COR-050 points 5, 11 and 12; #1647). The content is the record's body as text and its own fields compared parsed, never anything inside the `pkit` container. It is read so whether or not a place holds the record. Where the front matter does not parse to a mapping, it is the whole file, line endings aside. So `pkit friction check`, `check --all`, `debt` and `explain` flag fewer anchors. A revalidation of a record a place holds no longer flags a whole anchor on it, nor does a container a sync brings into a record, nor a change to the record file's mode. A commit that wrote only the container no longer dates the anchor's debt, and `explain` lists none. In the other direction, `pkit validate`'s `friction` member fails a cycle through a record anchor, whole or on a point, on a record whose file is an artefact, where it found none before. A record's anchor on itself or on one of its own points is such a cycle. The finding sits under `anchors.record` where a record anchor leads from the cycle's first member. Removing or re-pointing one anchor of the cycle mends it. Both checks report a record before what anchors it. A path anchor, and an anchor of a registered kind, name files and still close no cycle. ([#1660])
- A path anchor, and an anchor of a registered kind, stand on the content and mode of a file a place holds at either state compared (COR-050 point 5; #1661). The content is read as an artefact's is: the body as text and the front matter's fields compared parsed, with every `pkit` container left out where one may sit, the front matter's own, an entry's or a rule's. A file with no front matter there has its body and no fields. Any other file is compared as stored, as before. So `pkit friction check`, `check --all`, `debt` and `explain` flag fewer anchors. A revalidation, a deferral or a status write in a document, a collection entry or a rule no longer flags a path anchor on its file, and the cascade stops at an `unchanged` revalidation whatever the kind of anchor. Reformatting a placed file's front matter, or giving a placed page a front matter that holds only a container, flags nothing either. A commit that wrote only a placed file's containers no longer dates a path anchor's debt, and `explain` lists none. The whole-repository check reads each state's places from that state's own commit. ([#1707])
- A project's rule sets stay found when its internal documentation root changes (COR-051 point 6). `pkit config set docs.internal <path>` now also adds the old root's `rule-sets/` folder to `friction.places` where a rule-set file there would go unfound under the new root, in the same write and under the one consent, and prints `recorded project rule sets = …` with how to move the sets; where the entry cannot be added without rewriting another line of the file, it writes nothing and names the entry to add by hand. `pkit validate` gains the `unfound-rule-set` error on a rule-set file left behind: a Markdown file in or beneath a `rule-sets` folder, outside the method folders, whose front matter names a set (`rule-set:`) while no place finds it. A project that changed its root by hand, or keeps a `rule-sets` example whose front matter has a `rule-set:` key, fails `pkit validate` until it declares the folder, moves the file, or removes the key. A configuration write that adds items at the end of a block list now keeps that list's lines and comments. No migration runs. ([#1665])
- The permission decision core reads a redirection that writes no file as harmless (ADR-025 point 2): an fd duplicated onto stdout or stderr or closed, such as `2>&1`, `>&2` or `2>&-`, and output discarded to `/dev/null`, such as `>/dev/null`, `2>/dev/null` or `&>/dev/null`. It removes such a word before it reads the command, so where `git` and `gh` are granted, `git status 2>&1 | gh pr list` and `cd src && git log >/dev/null` are allowed unasked, and a deny binds on a program behind one, as on `2>/dev/null rm -rf ~`. Each form counts only as a whole word, with fd numbers of one digit, `1`, `2` or `-` after `>&` or `<&`, the target exactly `/dev/null`, unquoted, and `&>` or `&>>` only last in its segment. Every lookalike, such as `2>&1t`, `12>&1`, `>&3`, `>&1x` or `{fd}>/dev/null`, a segment of redirections alone, and every other redirection, such as `> out.txt`, `<`, `>|`, `<>`, a here-string or a heredoc, still defers to the harness, and a strict posture denies it. An assignment to zsh's `NULLCMD` or `READNULLCMD`, which name the program a command of redirections alone runs, now keeps every grant from allowing, as one to `PATH` does.
- A lookup of a point another record overturns in part says so. pkit refs lookup X#N prints, after the point's place, the record's top line, which reads "Partially superseded by ...", since the point stays as it stood and a reader sent to it would never see that line (COR-058 point 5). With --json, each answer gains partial_supersession, that line for a point of such a record and null otherwise; paths is unchanged, and schema_version stays 1. The line names the overturning record, not whether it overturns this point.
- The core rules every session loads are short again: each says what it requires, in a sentence or two, and points to the record, README or issue its reason lives in, and the detail those already hold leaves `core.md`. Rule 2 now says what COR-066 decides: a person accepts a proposal within the change that proposes it, an agent writes `accepted` only once that person has, the change does not land until then, and the person sees what the change makes accepted. The preface says where a rule's reason lives, in place of the section of umbrella references. No other rule's meaning changes, and no rule is renumbered. The set `OPS` moves to its next major, since rule 2 is tightened. No migration: `core.md` keeps its path, and a project receives it by sync as before.
- `pkit schemas show` and schema loading errors now name a namespace's owner as the core schemas area or its capability, instead of calling the core area a capability (#882).
- The project-kit architecture decisions moved from docs/architecture/decisions/ to tech-docs/architecture/decisions/ (ADR-055); relative links in shipped READMEs, records and scripts now point at the new location. Adopter defaults are unchanged.
- Friction discovery now reads a capability's friction.places in the package schema's shape, {path, location?}: with location, the path lies inside that docs.locations entry under its documentation root (internal, or user with root: user); without one it is repository-relative. A capability place in any other shape, plain text included, or one leaving the repository (through a link included) is no longer skipped silently but is a friction finding of pkit validate.
- A capability's documentation locations are read one way everywhere, in the package schema's shape: each docs.locations entry is {path, root?}, under the internal root, or the user root with root: user, and a location recorded for it in .pkit/capabilities/<name>/project/docs-locations.yaml wins over the declared one. The documentation-roots readers (capability_location, capability_subpaths) and friction discovery share that reading, so a capability place now lies inside its recorded location when one is recorded, the change check's base commit included. A capability's friction.surface is resolved repository-relative, as the package schema states, instead of under its documentation locations; a surface that is not a list, or an entry that is not a path, is a malformed-surface finding of pkit validate rather than skipped silently.
- The header of the rendered `.pkit/.gitignore` now names everything the file is rendered from: the backbone's own declarations, each installed component's `runtime_ignore:` declaration, and the project's configuration in `.pkit/project/config.yaml`. It also names `pkit config set` beside `pkit install` and `pkit sync` as a command that re-renders it. Only the comment lines change; the ignore patterns are the same, and the next sync rewrites the header.
- `pkit friction revalidate` refuses a `--because`, and `pkit friction defer` a `--reason`, that still holds a placeholder: words in angle brackets, as a command shown for a person to run writes what they supply — `friction explain` shows `--because "<why the content still holds>"` and `--reason "<why it can wait>"`. Run as shown, the command would have written the placeholder as the justification, a marker bump with nothing behind it. Code in brackets is not a placeholder: a bracket with no space inside, or opening in capitals (`Vec<u8>`, `Map<String, int>`).
- `pkit repository base` resolves the default branch once per run and reuses that resolution for the base and for the warnings it prints. It reads a branch's references in one `git for-each-ref` and where HEAD left the base in one `git merge-base`, so a reading starts three git processes where it started thirteen (#1221). The friction change check, `pkit release check` and `pkit migrations check-diff` also resolve their base once per run. The reading's document, its warnings and every refusal are unchanged. ([#1225])
- `adr` is a reserved capability name, as `core` and `project` are: `pkit new capability`, `pkit capabilities install` and `pkit capabilities register` refuse it, giving the reason (#1289). `adr` is the namespace of the project's architecture decision records, which `pkit new decision` reads before any capability name, so a capability named `adr` could have no decision records stamped. The refusal of `core` gives every place the name collides: the namespace of the core decision records and agents, beside the core schemas area. A project that already has a capability named `adr` keeps it registered, but sees `pkit validate` fail with an error at that capability's `component.name` that names the rename: `pkit capabilities uninstall adr` (which keeps an authored capability's files), rename the directory and its `component.name`, and `pkit capabilities register <new-name>`; one a sync restores is its author's to rename, and is uninstalled until it ships under another name. Until it is renamed, `pkit capabilities register adr` and `pkit capabilities refresh adr` refuse it as well. ([#1291])
- `backbone` is a reserved capability name, as `core`, `project` and `adr` are: `pkit new capability`, `pkit capabilities install` and `pkit capabilities register` refuse it, giving the reason (#1292). `backbone` is the name the backbone carries where a component's carry the component's name: the component of the backbone's changesets, the owner of its validators, the component its rule sets are cited with, and the component its documentation locations are recorded under, so a capability named `backbone` would have its changesets, validators, rule sets and documentation locations read as the backbone's. A project that already has a capability named `backbone` keeps it registered, but sees `pkit validate` fail with an error at that capability's `component.name` that names the rename: `pkit capabilities uninstall backbone` (which keeps an authored capability's files), rename the directory and its `component.name`, and `pkit capabilities register <new-name>`; one a sync restores is its author's to rename, and is uninstalled until it ships under another name. Until it is renamed, `pkit capabilities register backbone` and `pkit capabilities refresh backbone` refuse it as well. ([#1299])
- A component's name can no longer collide with a name the backbone reads beside it (#1300). `backbone` is a reserved adapter name: `pkit new adapter` refuses it, giving the reason, and `pkit init` refuses, before writing anything, a methodology source that ships an adapter under it, since the backbone's changesets and validators are read under that name and both read adapters too. `core`, `project` and `adr` stay reserved for capabilities alone and are free for an adapter. `pkit new capability` also refuses the name of a backbone command (`validate`, `status`, `sync`, … — read from the dispatcher when it runs), whose top-level name every capability yields to; `pkit capabilities install` and `register` do not, since the backbone gains commands with its releases. A project that already has an adapter registered as `backbone` keeps it registered, but sees `pkit validate` fail with an error at that adapter's `component.name` naming the rename: its directory, its `component.name` and its entry in `.pkit/manifest.yaml`. A project with a capability that ships a `commands:` block under a backbone command's name — its `pkit <capability> …` namespace runs the backbone command instead, and an upgrade that adds the command makes it so — sees `pkit validate` fail with an error at that capability's `component.name` naming the rename: `pkit capabilities uninstall <name>` (which keeps an authored capability's files), rename the directory and its `component.name`, and `pkit capabilities register <new-name>`; one a sync restores is its author's to rename. A capability with no `commands:` block is not reported. ([#1305])
- `project` is a reserved capability name, as `core` is: `pkit new capability`, `pkit capabilities install` and `pkit capabilities register` refuse it, giving the reason (#1269). `project` is the name the project's own entries carry where a capability's carry the capability's name — the namespace of the project's decision records and agents, and the opening name of the project's checks in an evidence point — so a capability named `project` would be indistinguishable from the project itself. A project that already has a capability named `project` keeps it registered, but sees `pkit validate` fail with an error at that capability's `component.name` that names the rename: `pkit capabilities uninstall project` (which keeps an authored capability's files), rename the directory and its `component.name`, and `pkit capabilities register <new-name>`. Until it is renamed, `pkit capabilities register project` and `pkit capabilities refresh project` refuse it as well. `pkit validate` reports a capability registered under `core` the same way. ([#1288])
- The process shape contract's journal entry has one place for why a move was taken: `reason`, which holds what the caller gives with `pkit process move --reason` — the justification for overriding a gate a binding lets its caller bypass, another subject's move that caused this one, and so on (#1232). The `bypass_reason` field, which nothing wrote, is gone from the contract; an entry that still carries it validates, as the entry admits fields it does not declare. The engine is unchanged.
- An adapter and a capability can no longer share a name (#1306). The backbone reads a component by its name alone where it reads both kinds: a changeset names its component and the release keys every `package.yaml` under `.pkit/` by name, so a release would move only one of the two; every registered component's validators are owned by its name, so the two would share one owner; and the wiring resolver reads the component registry by name, so one of the two would be read under the other's kind. The second of the two is refused, giving that reason: `pkit new adapter` refuses a name a capability holds, and `pkit new capability`, `pkit capabilities install` and `register` a name an adapter holds — a component holds its name when `.pkit/manifest.yaml` registers it or its directory exists under `.pkit/adapters/` or `.pkit/capabilities/` — and `pkit init` refuses, before writing anything, a methodology source that ships an adapter and a capability of one name. A project that already has an adapter and a capability registered under one name keeps both registered, and no migration runs, but sees `pkit validate` fail with an error at each one's `component.name` naming its rename: for the capability, `pkit capabilities uninstall <name>` (which keeps an authored capability's files), rename the directory and its `component.name`, and `pkit capabilities register <new-name>`, or, for one a sync restores, its author's rename; for the adapter, its directory, its `component.name` and its entry in `.pkit/manifest.yaml`, or the methodology source's rename for one a sync restores. Renaming either clears both. ([#1310])
- Changing the internal root with `pkit config set docs.internal` now names each recorded documentation location that the old internal root held and the new one does not, such as the overlay's `adr-records` on a fresh project (#2084). Each stays where it is (COR-049 point 6), and the command prints two lines for it: `kept <location> = <path>  (<file>)`, then where it was recorded and how to move it. Nothing else changes, and no migration.
- After you upgrade a project to this release, commit the upgrade before you run `pkit validate`. Until it is committed, the friction check inside validation reads the last commit, where your answers have not moved into answer files yet.
- The upgrade to 2.0.0 also rewrites your project's own documents, since it moves their answers into answer files, so `git checkout <ref> -- .pkit/` no longer undoes it. Commit the upgrade on its own, and roll it back by reverting that commit, which brings back every file it changed, inside `.pkit/` and outside.
- Where `pkit sync`, `pkit upgrade` and `pkit pin` name a way to roll a project back, they now name reverting the commit of each upgrade, `git revert <commit>`, in place of `git checkout <ref> -- .pkit/`. The revert also restores the files a migration edited outside `.pkit/`, which the checkout left as the upgrade wrote them. The guard's stop over an incomplete upgrade adds that an upgrade not yet committed is undone over every file `git status` lists.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The agent deploy resolver now treats `reads.patterns` as an OPTIONAL read channel (ADR-052): a category referenced only there resolves to the adopter's path when defined, and — when undefined — drops the item and still deploys the agent, rather than failing the whole deploy. `reads.paths` / `reads.records` and the list keys (`owns` / `needs` / `answers`) stay HARD (an undefined placeholder still skips the agent). A category referenced both optionally and hard is hard. Tolerance is inferred structurally from the reference site; no category is named in resolver code. ([#773])
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The permission hook no longer asks on a Bash command that may hide a form of a guardrail where the session runs in auto mode or bypass mode, the modes in which the person chose not to be asked. There it defers, and the harness's own mode decides. The hook reads the mode from the payload's `permission_mode`, `auto` or `bypassPermissions`. In every other mode, and where the payload names no mode or one the hook does not know, it asks as before. Its allow and its deny are unchanged, and it still asks on an Edit or Write to an absolute path outside the repository in every mode.
- **demo-recording 0.2.0** — DEC-002 and DEC-004 write each of their points as a heading carrying its number, word for word (#1421, COR-058). A page can now anchor to one point of them, such as `demo-recording:DEC-002#4`, and a change to another point of the record does not ask for the page to be revalidated. No point's text, number or citation changes. DEC-002 gains point 4, "One-directional knowledge flow": the sentence after its point 3, that Layer 1 knows nothing of Layers 2 and 3 and Layer 3 depends on both, is now a point of its own, with the same words.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — A document another capability holds in a folder of held documents — an analysis capability's review log, say — is claimed by that capability (DEC-001 point 1; COR-050 point 1): `pkit living-docs validate` counts it "of another component" in its summary rather than as an unclassified document, and refuses a page's `reader` or `kind` written into it.
