It reads the held documents and their owners from the `held` and `held_files` lists of `pkit friction artefacts --json`, which the backbone this release ships adds, so the capability requires that backbone. Requires backbone >=2.0.0.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The `living-docs` agent states the recorded rule for friction writers: it names `pkit friction revalidate` and `pkit friction defer` in the one paragraph that points at the agents README's four conditions for running one, two of which never hold for it — it has no tool for asking the person, and it reviews pages — so in every session it names the command that gives the answer, without `--yes`, and runs none, and points at the agents README for who may run it. Its storyboard and the capability README say the same. What it does is unchanged. The rule is the backbone's this release ships (COR-050 point 3), so the capability requires that backbone.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `LDOC` binds on a page only through a rule set of the project's own whose scope selects the page, such as its space's definition (living-docs DEC-001 points 2 and 3, COR-051 point 7). The validator checks a page's structure only where `RS-LDOC-004` binds on it, and its reader only where `RS-LDOC-003` binds, reading where they bind from `pkit rule-sets reach --json`. On any other page it reads neither, and `schemas/page.schema.json` requires a page's `kind` alone. The space-definition template carries a scope. A definition with no scope is reported with the scope to give it, and so are the pages of a space that its definition's scope leaves out. Act on upgrade: a definition written from the template before this release has no scope, so neither check runs on its space's pages until you give it the scope `pkit living-docs validate` names. The `living-docs` agent judges a page against the rules that bind on it. `pkit rule-sets reach` comes with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — DEC-001 writes each of its points as a heading carrying its number, word for word (#1421, COR-058). A page can now anchor to one point of it, such as `living-docs:DEC-001#3`, and a change to another point of the record does not ask for the page to be revalidated. No point's text, number or citation changes, so a rule's origin and the README still name the same points. The shared rule set LDOC now anchors each rule to the point of DEC-001 that states it, `living-docs:DEC-001#3`, and RS-LDOC-003 and RS-LDOC-004 also to `#4`, which gives the page's `reader` and `kind` fields.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — A page may now give another page's fact as a summary that links to that page and anchors to the section stating it, a fact repeated within one page is left to a person while two statements of a fact that disagree break the rule, and only what a page anchors grounds what it says.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — A page that anchors a decision record may state a fact the record decides with no link to another page, and any words stating another page's fact, however short, need a link and an anchor, while naming a thing needs neither.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — On a project whose two documentation roots are one folder, as both are by default, `pkit living-docs new space`, and `new page` for the user or technical space, first offer to set the internal root apart, as living-docs DEC-001 point 2 decides (#2084). The internal root goes to `tech-docs`, or to the folder `--internal-root <path>` names. A folder that lies in the user root, or holds it, is refused. The write is `pkit config set docs.internal`, which asks once at a terminal. Off a terminal it needs the stamp's new `--yes`, and without it the stamp refuses and writes nothing. A dry run says it would set the root first. So a fresh project creates its spaces with no layout step before them, whatever order it installed its capabilities in. The validator's report on roots that are one folder now names the stamp and the folder it recommends. No migration.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The spaces validator reads your documentation roots, the declared places, the files each matches and their front matter through `pkit friction artefacts`, the core's one discovery, instead of reading the declarations and matching paths itself — so it can no longer disagree with the core about which files a place holds. Its findings are the same except where the core's reading now wins: a synced copy a place matches, which the core already refuses under `friction`, is no longer also read as a page; a file the core's rule-set location rule claims is a rule-set file rather than a page; a `friction.places` written as a single string counts as one place; a git-ignored entry point reads "lies in no place of any space" rather than "is not a Markdown document of the working tree"; and a YAML date in `reader` or `kind` reaches the schema as its written text. When the core gives no reading of the places, the validator answers one error and checks no space.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The `living-docs` agent reads each commit behind a changed anchor limited to that commit's own `paths` in `pkit friction explain --json` — what the check read as the anchor's change, the page's own file never among them — instead of re-matching the anchor, and never takes the anchor's `files` for it, which are the revalidation point's and HEAD's view and miss a file that came and went between them. It reads a dead path anchor's commits as where the anchor's files went — their removal or rename away — to see what the anchor should point at now. Both keys come with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `fill-doc-check`, this capability's filler of the documentation-check point, declares that it reads history (`reads: [history]` on its `commands:` entry), so `pkit validate` and `pkit status` name the commit it read HEAD at. What it answers is unchanged: nothing owed in a repository with no commit, and no answer where a page's friction lies beyond a shallow clone's history. It needs the backbone this release ships, since an older one refuses the `reads` key.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The `living-docs` agent ends a fixer run in the exact forms of project-management's run contract (DEC-056 point 3), so the command that starts fixers reads every line it writes. Its `none` line carries one of the contract's reasons before the reason in words: `not-mine` for a location that is no page, `not-stale` for a page `explain` reports current or unresolved, `refused` for a call the run refused, `out-of-step` for a friction document of a version it does not read, `upstream` for a page that waits for its upstream, and `failed`. A brief that says a command started it as a fixer starts a fixer run, whatever words name the type. Its storyboard shows the result lines in a fenced block of their own, never behind a quote's `>`, which the contract would not count.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The README no longer tells you to settle two branches' revalidations of one page with `pkit friction resolve`. Each change writes its answers in answer files of its own, so the branches never conflict over them, and the page is debt until the next revalidation that reads both.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-pr`'s per-agent reviewer timeout is now configurable and its default is raised from 300s to 1200s (#766). The default is a generous ceiling, not a typical wait: reviewer agent runs are slow and variable (observed 300s to >600s on the same reviewer), so 1200s is meant to be hit only by a genuinely hung agent. The old hardcoded 300s silently killed heavier reviewers out of the box (e.g. `code-reviewer` needs ~323s), and an interim 600s still timed `code-reviewer` out on a real panel review. The timeout is a single uniform knob applied to every reviewer — deliberately not a per-agent map (COR-007). Resolution precedence: the new `--timeout <seconds>` flag > the `PKIT_REVIEW_AGENT_TIMEOUT` env var > the default 1200. A non-integer, zero, or negative value is a usage error (fail fast) rather than a silent fall-back. ([#768])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The capability's default local-path reviewer agent is renamed `reviewer` → `pm-reviewer` (#770). The single-token name collided with the English role-noun "reviewer" that saturates this capability (required reviewers, reviewer-contribution rules, per-reviewer overrides), making the registered agent name indistinguishable from the role. It now deploys at `.claude/agents/pm-reviewer.md` and is registered as `local_registered: - name: pm-reviewer`. The verdict-line GRAMMAR is unchanged (`Reviewer agent (local, <name>): <VERDICT>`); only the bound `<name>` moves. A capability-tier migration (0.55.0) rewrites an adopter's `local_registered` entry from `reviewer` to `pm-reviewer` — but ONLY when it is the shipped default, keyed on the deployed agent's provenance so an adopter's OWN agent named `reviewer` is left untouched — and removes the stale kit-deployed `reviewer.md`. Any OPEN PR carrying a `Reviewer agent (local, reviewer):` verdict goes stale after the rename (the gate now matches `pm-reviewer`) and self-heals on the next `review-pr` run. ([#770])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The guidance that told adopters to spell board carriage `unsupported: true` is withdrawn at its source, and the onboarding ceremony that produced that shape now drafts `board: true`.
`pre-check`'s cross-substrate conflict message — the one place the withdrawn instruction was actually shown to an adopter — now names `board: true` as the board-backed exit, in both its live remediation and its docstring. The `state` arm of that message is worded separately and deliberately: `board:` is admissible on `priority` and `workstream` only, so a `state` adopter is told to remove the competing binding rather than to write a map `pkit schemas validate` would reject.
`adopt-existing` drafts `board: true` for a `priority`- or `workstream`-shaped field on the configured Projects-v2 board. It previously detected such a field and then drafted `unsupported: true` over it, because the map had no way to name the board — which is where the reported adopter's broken map came from. The field name and its options stay in the audit report as context (the `after_create_issue` hook needs them; the map deliberately does not carry them), and label bindings still win when both a label set and a board field are observed — with the overlap now reported, since that pair is the conflict `pre-check` refuses.
`pre-check` gains three checks for the arm. `board: true` under a config declaring no board is **refused** — the pair is unsatisfiable, and that refusal is deliberately hard. A `default:` on a `board:` arm with no `set-board-field` hook behind it **warns**, bounded by what a hook can actually be matched to: a hook declares an opaque `field_id` and never an axis, so an existing hook yields "unverified" rather than a false all-clear. And a board-declarable axis absent from a *present* map under a configured board is **reported** — carriage for that axis is unchanged and still comes from the flag; the note says so and gives the one-line edit that makes it explicit, which is how the cleanliness is bought without changing anyone's configuration meaning.
A warn-on-detect capability migration reaches adopters who already followed the old advice: it reports the one-line change where a configured board, an axis marked `unsupported: true`, and a `set-board-field` hook all coincide. It never edits `substrate-map.yaml` — the file is adopter-owned hand-authored intent, and the three-way signature is a heuristic, not a proof.
The upgrade-time retraction also now points at the repair. Its detection previously required a `set-board-field` hook, so it matched only adopters who followed the withdrawn `unsupported: true` guidance — and missed the adopters with a `label:` binding under a configured board, whose already-filed issues carry a value on NEITHER substrate. That population had the damaged corpus and saw nothing on upgrade. The migration now reports that shape too and names `pkit pm back-fill`, while stating plainly that it has not looked at your issues: answering whether a corpus is affected means reading them, and a migration makes no network calls. Still warn-only; still edits nothing. ([#794])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The write verbs, the workstream mutators and `bootstrap` now ask one accessor where a classification axis lives, instead of each deciding for itself from the board flag (#712, from report #708). Where your `substrate-map.yaml` binds an axis, that binding governs it and `has_projects_v2_board` is not consulted for that axis; where the map says nothing, the flag governs exactly as before, so a configuration with no bindings behaves identically.
`create-issue` resolves each axis's substrate BEFORE deciding board-versus-label. Previously `priority` and `workstream` were considered for labelling only when no board was configured — a decision taken before the seam was consulted — so a board adopter whose map bound `priority` to their own labels got no label written and no board field either: the value landed on neither substrate, silently. The `type` axis already resolved in the correct order in the same function. The `--workstream` requirement follows the same answer, so it now fires for a label-bound workstream under a configured board (where the label really is written) and stays silent where nothing would be.
`set-field` routes each axis three ways instead of crossing the flag with the map. An axis the map binds to labels is now WRITTEN as that label rather than refused as a two-substrate conflict — the state DEC-051 makes legal. Two honesty fixes ride along: a value with no `remap` entry is a refusal with a non-zero exit rather than `ok`-with-no-change (it landed nowhere), and an axis served by a substrate `set-field` does not write — a title prefix, a derive predicate — is refused by name instead of being handed to the label planner, which would have applied the title PREFIX as a label the tracker does not have. An axis you declared `unsupported` is still a note, not a refusal: that is degradation by your own declaration.
`move-issue` asks the same question for `state`, so a configured board no longer suppresses a label write the map asked for, and the forward cascade uses the same answer as the move itself instead of assuming labels. When replacing a value on a label-bound axis, `set-field` and `move-issue` now find the stale label through the map's own vocabulary — a prefix-only search never matched the adopter's own label name, so the new value was added and the old one left in place, two values on a single-valued axis.
The five workstream mutators no longer create, rename or delete kit `workstream:*` labels unless the kit's labels are actually that axis's substrate. A naive flag-for-accessor swap would have turned kit-label mutation ON for a board adopter with a label-bound workstream: `add` would create an unmanaged label, and `remove` / `merge` / `split` / `rename` would delete or rename a name the kit never owned. The declared workstream vocabulary is still updated and the suppressed label half now says which substrate carries the axis instead of skipping silently.
`bootstrap` refuses to provision a kit label palette for an axis the kit's labels do not carry, and that refusal is now enforced rather than assumed. Its comment claimed the map-blind construction was "greenfield by construction"; nothing stopped a run in a repo carrying a `substrate-map.yaml`, and it created the full kit palette for every axis. A present map now suppresses every axis palette (adopting an existing repo's labels is `adopt-existing`'s job), each reported in the plan with the substrate that carries the axis instead.
`self-test` no longer seeds a `priority:Low` label on a board adopter — it resolved that axis unconditionally, outside the guard that wrapped `state`, so the throwaway issue was created with a label `bootstrap` deliberately never made and `gh issue create` failed on a correctly-configured project. `check-mesh` compares kit label sets across peers only where the kit's labels are actually that axis's substrate, asked of the same accessor. A greenfield project WITH a board previously skipped the comparison by reading the flag inline; a map-presence test alone would have resumed comparing `priority:*` sets that neither peer uses, turning a leftover label from before the board into a recurring drift warning nobody can act on.
A guard test keeps the invariant from eroding: exactly one site may read `has_projects_v2_board` to decide carriage, and every other read of the flag — board identity, board membership — is named with the job it does. ([#794])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A configured board plus a `label:` binding on the same axis is no longer a hard prerequisite failure. It reports a warning instead, and the run passes.
The refusal was right when it shipped: nothing decided which declaration won, so the writer honoured the board flag and wrote no label, nothing wrote the board field, and the reader looked for a label that never existed. The axis ended up set on neither substrate and the capability's own verbs could not satisfy its own gate.
A binding now governs the axis it names, writers and readers ask one accessor, and the value lands on your own labels exactly as your map asked — so keeping the refusal would block the configuration the rule sanctions. What survives is worth saying once, which is what the warning says: the board's field for that axis goes unused, because nothing writes it and no gate reads it.
This is the only softening. The genuinely unsatisfiable pair — `board: true` on an axis while no board is configured — stays a hard refusal in its own check. ([#794])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The capability provides the pkit::work-tracking role and defines the pkit::work-tracking:doc-check data point (DEC-053): additive, inert policy fail, companion schema doc-check.schema.json. Its code-to-doc mapping is the always-included default filler — the new query fill-doc-check prints it, one obligation per rule. check-doc-mapping reads the resolved point through `pkit connections resolve` and applies it to the diff: without a documentation capability it prints and exits exactly as before; an obligation another source contributes is met only by its page changed in the diff, never by a `## Doc impact` line; an unresolved point fails the check, naming the filler and the fix. Each contributed source has its own setting, doc_check.sources.<source> (advisory by default); the mapping keeps code_path_to_doc_mapping.enforce.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `open-pr --doc-impact-from-friction` (opt-in) runs `pkit friction check --json` against origin/<base> and renders the answers the changed pages carry — updated, unchanged, deferred, new — as the `## Doc impact` bullets, filling only an unwritten section (the template's placeholder, empty, or absent). A page still carrying friction is named on stderr. Rendering only: the section meets no documentation obligation (DEC-053).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `pkit pm history` and `history --check-drift` follow the project's journal setting (`process.journal`, off by default). With journal logging off, `history` reports "journal logging is not enabled for this project", names the tracker (the timeline plus pkit's audit comments) as the audit trail, and gives the command that turns logging on, instead of an empty history; `--check-drift` is skipped with the same reason and exits 0 rather than reporting a clean result. With logging on they behave as before.
DEC-049 now says where the canonical audit trail lives in each mode — the engine journal where one is kept, the tracker otherwise — and what `history` and `--check-drift` guarantee with committed, clone-local and no journals. The capability no longer declares the process-journal ignore pattern in its `package.yaml`: the backbone owns it and follows the setting. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it requires the backbone release that makes process journal logging opt-in, which takes the ignore line over. On that backbone an older version of this capability still declares the line; while journals are committed the render leaves that entry out and `pkit validate` warns until the capability is upgraded. The README gains a "Lifecycle history" section.
With journal logging off, `move-issue --bypass` keys its audit comment on the issue's count of state-label events on the GitHub timeline instead of the journal length, which no longer grows. A retry of a failed attempt still posts nothing new, and the same bypass made again later posts its own comment — one audit comment per audited mutation in both modes. With logging on the key is unchanged. `move-issue`'s warning for a move the engine did not record now says what that costs in each mode.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `pm-reviewer` agent states its read-only claim precisely: it never changes the pull request, a tracked file, the repository or the issue tracker, which is not "writes nothing anywhere". Its body gains the `## What read-only covers` section every reviewer that can execute carries: the working files a review needs — a dumped diff, a script, a reproduction, captured output — go in the agent workspace, never loose in the repository, where an author's `git add` would commit them, nor in `/tmp/`, and a review leaves no file of its own outside the workspace. The `pm-reviewer` agent gain the `Write` tool, scoped by their bodies to the agent workspace, so a review's working files land there without a prompt instead of through a shell redirect, which the permission hook judges like any other shell write.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The default branch is the backbone's `repository.default-branch` (COR-054, #1128), read through `pkit repository base --json`: start-work cuts from the commit the backbone resolves the base to, create-draft counts commits beyond it, open-pr's `--doc-impact-from-friction` runs the change check with no `--base` for the default branch, and `check-doc-mapping` diffs from where the branch left the base the backbone names (`$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`, else the default branch) instead of a fixed `origin/main` and a merge-base of its own. An integration branch resolves the same way. When the backbone cannot answer, the verbs that act on the branch refuse, naming the cause, rather than cut from `main`; `check-doc-mapping` refuses a base that cannot be compared with the backbone's reason and fix (exit 1). The capability's own `default_branch` is retired: no longer required, and the 0.55.0 upgrade migration carries a value other than `main` over to the backbone's key and removes it.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A reviewer's verdict stays valid until the author changes something that reviewer checks, instead of going stale on every commit (#1179). `review-pr` records the PR's head and its base branch's head before it invokes each reviewer, names the head in the reviewer's brief, and stamps both into the verdict marker (`<!-- pkit-verdict sha=<commit> base=<commit> -->`); a head that moved during the review is reported, the verdict is posted against the head the reviewer saw, and the native review is skipped. Only each reviewer's latest verdict is judged: an approval its later rejection superseded never counts again. The author's changes since the reviewed head are read from the local repository: a clean merge of the base branch counts for nothing, an edit made inside a merge or a resolved conflict counts, and any other commit counts for its whole diff. An `APPROVED` from a reviewer whose rules are all diff floors (the code-review panel's `code-reviewer` and `security-reviewer`) stands while those changes reach none of its floors, read through `review.floors.not_code`; every other approval — the baseline's, and that of a reviewer with any classification rule, `docs-reviewer` included — and every `CHANGES_REQUESTED` goes stale on any change. A verdict whose recorded base is outside the base branch's history — a retargeted PR — is stale. A commit missing from the checkout is fetched from origin first; when the answer still cannot be read — the commit cannot be fetched, the checkout is shallow, the reviewed head was rebased away, git is older than 2.38 — the verdict is stale and the reason says which. A verdict naming no head keeps the commit-time rule. `done-work`'s gate, `review-pr`'s skip and `show-pr --field review` apply this one rule; a `done-work` refusal and `show-pr` name each stale verdict's reason, `review-pr` names it for every reviewer it re-runs, and `show-pr --json` carries it as `freshness`. A `done-work` refusal blocked by a fresh `CHANGES_REQUESTED` names `review-pr <N> --force`, and `done-work` merges only the head its gate checked (`--match-head-commit`). A semantic conflict introduced by a clean merge of the base is not re-reviewed; CI is the backstop.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Where a pull request's base branch merges through a merge queue, `done-work` and `merge-pr` enqueue instead of merging (#1011): `gh pr merge <N> --auto --match-head-commit <head>`, pinned to the head their gates checked, after every gate has passed. The queue runs the base's required checks on the merge it is about to make and merges once they pass, so those checks answer for the true base. The verbs read the queue before any gate and again just before the merge, print the pull request's place in it and wait for the merge — as long as the queue estimates plus 2 minutes, at most 30, or `--wait-minutes`; what follows the merge (`done-work`'s move to Done, close, cascade and clean-up; `merge-pr`'s after-merge hooks and clean-up) runs once GitHub reports the pull request merged, never on a merge command's success alone. When the wait ends first, or `--no-wait` returns at once, the run exits 4 — accepted, a re-run completes it — and the same verb run again once the pull request has merged completes it; `done-work` finds the merged pull request from the issue as well as the branch, so any clone completes it, and refuses only an issue reopened since the merge. `merge-pr` run again fires its after-merge hooks once: the clone records that they fired, so a further run from it does nothing and says so, and a pull request someone else merged without a queue is still refused. A merge `gh` accepted that GitHub cannot then be read to confirm also exits 4, saying so rather than calling the pull request queued, and a re-run completes it. A pull request that leaves the queue unmerged, or that is pushed to after the enqueue and so taken out of the queue, exits 3. The queue is the only path to such a base: `--admin` and `--bypass-ci` are refused, and so are a queue whose merge method is not squash, a repository whose default squash-commit title and message are not the pull request title and body, and a head the queue already dropped unless `--force` is given. The local branch is deleted after a merge only when everything on it merged, and `merge-pr`'s direct merge is now pinned to the head its gates read. A base without a queue merges directly, as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Every title rule `titles.yaml` declares now runs (#803); before, only each surface's regex did, and three rules declared as refusals were never checked. A title with a Conventional Commits prefix after its bracket (`[Task] fix: …`, `[EPIC] feat(x): …`) is now refused by `create-issue`, `edit-issue --title` and `validate-issue --phase create`; at a lifecycle transition `validate-issue` reports it as a warning, so an issue filed with one still moves. New warnings, which never block: a bare lowercase `scope:` prefix right after the bracket (`[Bug] project-management: …`), which reads like a commit scope but is carried by inbound reports and component-scoped titles, so filing goes on (a colon after a territory named in words is not flagged); a Task title, under any kind-driven prefix, of fewer than 30 characters after the prefix (EPIC, Feature and Umbrella titles name a territory and have no length floor); a PR summary that starts with a capital, ends with a period or runs past 72 characters (`validate-pr`); and a Milestone title with no word in it (`create-milestone`). Each validation in `titles.yaml` now names the check that runs it (`check`) and its finding label (`name`), which `titles.schema.json` requires, so a rule nothing enforces cannot be declared. The title descriptions and examples now state the house style per type: EPIC, Feature and Umbrella titles name a territory, optionally followed by a colon or a dash and a short specifier; Task titles say what is true once the work is done, or the work in the imperative for a decision, a document or an exploration. DEC-011 states the same rules.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Reading where an issue stands fetches the issue once instead of five times. The issue lifecycle's five states now all detect with one classifier, the new `detect-state` command, under the process shape's `classified` mode: it reads the issue once and answers which lifecycle state it is in, by the same inference as before, or `null` with the inferred value in its reason when that value is not a lifecycle state (a derived `open` / `blocked`, a stray state label). So `pkit process status` for an issue starts one detection script instead of five, and every lifecycle verb that reads an issue's position — `start-work`, `review-work`, `move-issue`, `done-work`, `close-issue` — makes four fewer `gh issue view` calls per reading. When the issue cannot be read, the predicates that read it now pass `gh`'s own words on standard error, so `status` and a refused move show why. The definition's `version` rises to 5; `schema_version` stays 4. The five per-state commands (`detect-todo` … `detect-done`) stay registered, for direct use; the shipped lifecycle no longer runs them. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: under a `pkit` older than the `classified` mode the engine cannot tell where any issue is, and the lifecycle verbs move issues on their own local inference and warn that the engine did not record the move.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — An issue's parent is read one way everywhere. The containment seam's new `resolve_parent` returns an issue's parent — the native one wherever the tracker reports it, else the issue its first line names — with how the two records stand, and the forward cascade, `close-issue`'s closure cascade, the close gate's membership step and `show-tree` all read it. The forward cascade follows a first line its type does not allow where the native parent names the same issue, with a note, and still stops where the two disagree or such a line is the only record; where the record could not be read and the line is not an allowed form, it says both. The closure cascade checks every parent the close gate counts the closed issue under: a natively linked child's native parent, where it reported no parent; the parent a first line in a form the type does not allow names, with a warning, where it skipped the check; and both parents where the two records disagree, with the `set-field` line that settles them. A native parent in another repository is named and not checked. A first line naming the issue itself names no parent: no child set holds the issue under itself, and every reader says so rather than taking the issue for its own parent. `show-tree`'s `parent_number` is the native parent wherever one was seen, a child listed under a first-line parent other than its native one is marked `[native parent #<P>]`, a first line not in an allowed form is marked, and the JSON gains `parent_resolution`, whose `native_parent_known` says whether a `native_parent` of `null` means none or only none seen (a parent in another repository, outside `--state` or `--limit`, behind an unreadable panel), and whose `first_line_names_itself` marks a self-naming line. The close gate reads a first line as the cascades do: `Epic:#5` (no space) now names #5 and holds it open, and the deprecated `Milestone: #3` no longer names issue #3 — so an EPIC whose first line is `Milestone: #9`, as `set-field --parent 9` writes on one, no longer counts toward issue #9's close gate: it never was issue #9's child, the old reader misread a milestone's number for an issue's. `cascade-membership` reads the candidate's REST record in place of `gh issue view` and adds `parent_kind` to its detail; that read refuses a pull-request number and an issue whose read was redirected to another, so such a candidate now holds the fold indeterminate rather than counting as read — the safe direction. A Feature may name an Umbrella (`Umbrella: #<N>`) and is walked through it to the EPIC, and `create-issue` and `set-field --parent` label the first line with the parent's own type, so a Task set under an Umbrella reads `Umbrella: #<N>`. `set-field --parent` replaces a first line only when it is a parent-ref and nothing else; a first line that names an issue and says more (`Feature: #12 — auth`, a sentence opening `Note: #45 …`) is kept, the new parent-ref is written above it, and the plan line says which was done. For an EPIC, whose container is a milestone, `--parent N` names milestone N: the warning says the first line names milestone N, not issue #N, and where an EPIC has a native parent the line that would settle it says its container is a milestone instead of offering a rewrite that names nothing. `create-issue` refuses `--parent` beside a first line naming another issue in any form, a loose one under `hierarchy: advisory` included. `link-parent` reports a first line in a form the type does not allow as naming its parent, non-conforming (a new outcome, `parent line not an allowed form`), not as no parent line, and follows each conflict with the seam's line: the native parent wins. A move to done, and the journal entries for a close, read one origin: an issue closed with neither a state label nor a milestone is told apart by its close reason, so one closed as completed brings its ancestors level and its close is not journaled from a guessed Todo; one with only a milestone reads Backlog, inferred, for both.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A native sub-issue that lives in another repository is now read as that issue, not as this repository's issue of the same number (#1308). The close gate took such a child for the local issue numbered like it and folded that issue's state — or a pull request's. It now holds its container like any other child, its state read in its own repository by open/closed alone (nothing is written there): closed counts as done, open holds, and one the container's sub-issue list names but that cannot be read leaves the container held. A sub-issue GitHub leaves out of that list — one in a repository the reader cannot see — is not seen at all, and the container can close over it. So a container may now hold where it closed — its child elsewhere is open while the local issue of that number was closed — or close where it held — its child elsewhere is closed while that local issue was open. Each listed sub-issue is placed by comparing the repository it names with this repository's own name: one naming this repository is this repository's issue, whatever else its entry lacks. A repository name or an issue number spelled other than GitHub spells one is unreadable, and holds the container. The subject id `owner/repo#<n>` is new: `cascade-members` lists such a child under it, so `pkit process cascade` reasons name it (`member 'owner/repo#<n>'`), and `detect-state` and `cascade-membership` accept it as their subject. `show-tree` shows such a child as a leaf under its parent, `owner/repo#<n>` marked `[in another repository]`, with no state, its JSON gains `children_in_other_repositories` (`ref`, `repository`, `number`) beside `children`, and `--refresh-children-views` refreshes a parent whose only children are elsewhere, as `create-issue` and `link-parent` already did. The textual children view writes such a child as `owner/repo#<n>` in a code span, which GitHub does not link, so the view leaves nothing on that issue's timeline. `close-issue <N> --mode=cascade-eligibility-close` now says what holds a container, from its own read of the children and the engine's word for a read only the engine made (a members list or a candidate's membership it could not read): open children, each named — one elsewhere with its repository — with what releases the container, for a child elsewhere its closing there or the removal of its link in this repository by the command the refusal prints (`gh api -X DELETE repos/{owner}/{repo}/issues/<N>/sub_issue -F sub_issue_id=<id>`, filled in, never run by pm); children that could not be read, each with the read that failed — one elsewhere with its two ways out, made readable or unlinked, any other with the advice to re-run once `gh` is reachable, which appears only then; both, each block with its own remedy; and, where it finds neither, the engine's reason under a header that claims no cause.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The capability's records whose points were a numbered list, or paragraphs opening with a bold number or label, write each point as a heading carrying its number or label, word for word (#1421, COR-058): DEC-033, DEC-035, DEC-036, DEC-038 to DEC-041, DEC-043 to DEC-051 and DEC-053 to DEC-055. A page can now anchor to one point of these records, such as `project-management:DEC-055#3`, and a change to another point of the record does not ask for the page to be revalidated. No point's text, number or citation changes. DEC-049 gains point 4, "Format + single-poster rules": the paragraph after its point 3, one audit comment per audited mutation in one canonical format, is now a point of its own, with the same words. The text after the last point of DEC-035, DEC-040, DEC-050 and DEC-051 sits under a heading of its own, and DEC-039's moves into the Decision's opening. DEC-029 cited DEC-008's Implications by line number, and now names the bullet that line held.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `touches-ruled-artefact` floor counts the documents a change brings under a rule, as well as those it alters (DEC-032, #1560). A pull request that widens a rule set's scope, accepts a rule, raises a pin behind its set or types a place requires the floor's reviewer for each document it brings under a rule, though it edits none of them. Such a push after a review stales that reviewer's approval too (DEC-028). A change that only takes documents out from under a rule fires nothing. The floor reads these documents from `pkit rule-sets altered`'s `brought_unaltered`, so it needs the backbone this release ships. An answer without that key fails the resolution with a reason that names the backbone to upgrade to. ([#1579])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-pr` runs the reviewers a pull request requires at the same time, not one after another (#1594). A run, and the review step of `land-work`, now waits for the slowest reviewer instead of the sum of all of them. Each reviewer is shown the head read before they start. Once they end, the head is read again and each verdict is posted against the head its reviewer saw, in the order of the required set. Each reviewer's lines print together, in that order. A reviewer that fails or times out does not stop the others. With a pinned head, as `land-work` calls it, a head that moved during the reviews stops the run as before, and the verdicts they made are posted. A new setting caps how many reviewers run at once: the `--parallel <n>` flag, then the `PKIT_REVIEW_PARALLEL` env var, then the default, the whole set at once. `1` runs them in turn. An adopter who edited `_invoke_agent` for another harness re-applies the edit to its new form: it takes a `say` writer for the lines it reports, and runs on a thread of its own.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `land-work --yes --expect-head <sha>` lands a later head that the authorisation for `<sha>` covers: one where every change since lies inside `revalidated` blocks, a clean merge of the base counting as none, as the backbone's `pkit friction since` answers it. That is a merge round that only wrote answers again. The run goes on at the later head, its checks, review and merge gates as for any head. Its `head:` and `merge:` lines say which head it merged and why the authorisation covered it. It stops at `ready:` on that head where it finds a finding to judge or an answer the change check did not ask for, since each needs the person. Any other later head, or one the backbone cannot answer for, still stops the run (exit 3), now naming the changes outside the blocks (DEC-055 point 3). Approved pull requests whose answers cover the same documents merge one after another without a new authorisation after each merge round.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `land-work`'s authorisation for one head also covers a later head whose changes since lie inside `revisited` lists, which hold the entries a link asks of its source (COR-050 point 16), as well as inside `revalidated` blocks, a clean merge of the base counting as none (DEC-055 point 3). A merge round that only wrote a source's revisit entries again needs no new authorisation. An entry rewritten, added or removed lies inside its list, as a deferral does inside a block, and a list removed whole or emptied is a change outside, as a block removed is. The `head:` and `merge:` lines, the stop at a head the authorisation does not cover, and the `--expect-head` help say so.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `land-work`'s authorisation now names the head it merges (DEC-055 point 3, #1760). `land-work --yes` now needs `--expect-head <sha>`. Without it the run is refused (exit 2) before anything is read, waited for or reviewed, since a `--yes` that names no head would merge whatever head the run found. Run `land-work <N>` without `--yes`: its `ready:` line names the head and the command that merges it. `done-work` and `merge-pr` with no `--yes` and no terminal to ask at used to merge unasked. They now run their gates and refuse (exit 2), naming the head, with nothing posted, moved or merged. Pass `--yes` on the person's authorisation. Their `--yes` names no head: it merges the head the gates read when the run starts. At a terminal, their question now names the PR and the head the merge is pinned to. Completing a PR that has merged already merges nothing, so it still goes on without `--yes`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Landing and the review floors read a change's answers through the backbone, so they need no change when the answers move into answer files of their own (COR-062, #1872). An answer file never counts as code for the `touches-code` floor, whatever `review.floors.not_code` says: a change that only answers draws no code review, and a push that only answers keeps a code-floor approval standing (DEC-032 D1). The folder is read from the backbone's `pkit friction check --all --json`, once for a run and only where the paths the list leaves touch code. `land-work`'s firm stop on a base that moved on reads the file the change check names for each listed answer, an answer file or the artefact's own. Beside the list, and never in the description, `land-work` prints each revalidation on the base's tip that the change's answer did not read, which the merge would leave answered apart, and its `ready:` line counts them. They stop nothing (DEC-055 point 3). With every answer still in a block, each command gives the results it gave before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The workstream commands do what DEC-018 decides for each of them. `add-workstream` and `edit-workstream` refuse a name, a description or a deprecation reason outside DEC-018's rules, and `rename-workstream` and `split-workstream` refuse a slug with consecutive hyphens or over 40 characters. Where a board carries the workstream axis, `add-workstream`, `rename-workstream`, `merge-workstream` and `split-workstream` keep the board's `Workstream` field in step with `workstreams.yaml`. `rename-workstream` checks the old and the new label first, and refuses where both exist. `merge-workstream` now rewrites the typed references to each loser, in the tracked files and in issue, pull-request and comment bodies, by default: `--skip-references` leaves them. It asks before each file and body, refuses up front where no shell can ask unless `--yes` is given, and leaves the `.pkit/` tree and the changelog. `split-workstream` asks of each issue which new workstream it moves to, takes `--assign <issue>=<slug>` and `--default`, and refuses before any change while an issue has no answer and no shell can ask. Its `--include-github-bodies` opts into rewriting the references. `remove-workstream` refuses while any issue carries the workstream, whatever carries the axis, and with `--skip-label` too. Each command changes the label or the board before the file, so a re-run after a failure finishes it.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `land-work`'s cover of a later head reads version 2 of the backbone's `pkit friction since`, as DEC-055 point 3 decides. An authorisation for one head covers a later one where every change since lies in answer files the change adds, a clean merge of the base counting as none. An answer written in an artefact's block, or an answer file removed or rewritten, lies outside, so the run stops there. A backbone that answers version 1 gives no answer here, which is never read as covered.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Each axis is put on the board in the substrate map, as DEC-062 decides. `has_projects_v2_board` now says only that a project has a Projects v2 board: it names which board and puts every issue on it, and carries no axis. An axis lives on the board only where `substrate-map.yaml` binds it with `board: true`. With no map, every axis reads the kit's own labels, board or no board, and in a present map an axis left out or marked `unsupported` degrades, board or no board. The map gains a `kit-label: true` arm on every axis, which reads, writes and checks the axis on the kit's own labels as with no map, so a project can move one axis to its board and keep the others.
`board: true` is now admissible on `state`. There the board's Status is the source of an open issue's position, and a closed issue is Done whatever its card says. The Status is read as DEC-051 point 6 says: a board that cannot be read, no card, no Status, and a Status naming no open state are four outcomes, each with its own message, and none falls back to the labels or the milestone. `move-issue` refuses a move the Status does not place, and writes the Status as the move, the forward cascade each ancestor's, and `create-issue` sets the first one. `next-task` skips a Task it cannot place, with a warning, and takes nothing where the board cannot be read. `start-work`, `review-work`, `seats` and the lifecycle's classifier read the Status too. Where a present map leaves the state out, no board Status is written, and `sync-board` refuses, saying why.
The configuration's schema moves to version 2. The upgrade's migration writes it in every project at version 1. Where the flag is set, it keeps priority and workstream on the board through the map, keeping each `default:`, writes a map where there is none, and leaves state out, so state is read as before. Its report names each binding it wrote, and each comment of the map that speaks of the board. `pre-check` no longer reports a label binding under a board as a double claim, nor nudges an omitted axis towards `board: true`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The project-manager agent's rule to dispatch helpers without a name is now recorded in its own decision record as standing discipline, tied to the harness requirement it protects (#854).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `project-manager` and `pm-reviewer` agents are told to keep their intermediate files — drafts, scripts, captured output, notes — in the agent workspace, `.agent-workspace/` at the repository root, and nowhere else outside the repository, writing them with the file tools: the workspace is granted to every agent for those, while a shell redirect into it is judged like any other shell write. DEC-045 cites the cross-repository rule by meaning and follows its renumbering to rule 19.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The pm skill states how reviewer findings become work (#1180). `create-issue` scopes a follow-up a reviewer produced: it gets a Milestone only when it blocks an acceptance criterion of that Milestone's work or is a correctness defect in a check that already enforces something, and otherwise is filed without one, or on the rolling maintenance Milestone, and waits. `batch-plan` treats two Tasks whose implementation notes name overlapping source files as one module: the later names the earlier in its `## Dependencies` section and is dispatched once the earlier has merged, while Tasks in different modules run in parallel. `transition-state` gains a review step: it runs `review-pr` once every check on the PR's head commit has passed (or when the PR has no checks), hands a PR with a failing check back to the builder, and sends only the findings the reviewer marks blocking into a fix round, each advisory being answered in the PR body or filed as a follow-up. The project-manager agent and the pm skill's routing table send a request for verdicts to that step. The capability README points at the procedure that states each rule. ([#1186])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work` and `merge-pr` land a PR through the backbone's merge mechanic, `pkit pull-request`, the one `pkit release merge` uses too: the merge-queue reading, the merge or the enqueue, the wait and the dequeue are the backbone's, run in the environment the `gh:` block pins, and the verbs keep their gates, refusals, output and exit codes. Each call to it is bounded and, past its bound, stopped with what it started; a merge or an enqueue it gives no answer to is never taken for a failed one — the PR is read again, and when GitHub cannot say either the run exits 4 with what follows the merge owed. Upgrade it together with the backbone: against one that predates the command the verbs cannot read how the base merges, say which `pkit` ran, and merge nothing. ([#1207])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `pm-reviewer` reads a pull request's diff from the local checkout, `git diff <base>...<head>`, when `gh pr diff` refuses it for changing more than 300 files, and requests changes for a diff it cannot read only when neither source yields it — however it was started (#1190). Its body also describes the verdict marker as it is posted, naming the reviewed head and base, and tells a reader to match its `<!-- pkit-verdict` prefix rather than the bare string. ([#1205])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The use-case point's contract for its filler (DEC-054 point 2) states its one exception: an id, once settled, names the same use case for good, unless the project declares that nothing cites it. Such a use case may then leave the value, and its id may later name a different use case. Anything that keeps an id is citing it. The point's description, its companion schema and the README say so. The point stays at version 1: a data point's counterparts are its contributors, and loosening an obligation on fillers breaks no existing filler (COR-053 point 5).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The pull request's `## Documentation this change affects` section and `land-work`'s printed list read as the change check's own list does. They state the question once: these documents rely on something this change modifies, and each answer says whether the document is still true. Each entry names the document, the anchors the change modified that it relies on, and the outcome in plain words: "updated in this change", "still accurate" or "out of date, fixed later", with its words quoted in full. Every flag the change check puts on an answer now shows, a deferral or an `at` written back and a deferral put back included. A backbone whose check names no changed anchors still gives a list, without them. A list written before this release differs from the one `land-work` derives, so `land-work` writes it again.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The help for `review-pr --no-native` names the section of the capability's decision that describes native review delivery. ([#1492])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-pr` starts its reviewers through the backbone's run command, `pkit agents run`, and calls no harness itself (ADR-066 point 10). It asks through the capability's one module for it, `scripts/_lib/agent_runs.py`. A reviewer runs as before: in your checkout, under your settings and the project's, with `--effort` where one is set, under the same timeout. What changes around it: where no registered adapter registers an agent runner, no reviewer runs, and each reviewer's line says why (`[<name>] invocation refused: …`), as it did when `claude` was not on `PATH`. A project with another harness answers through its own adapter's runner, not by editing `review-pr`. At the timeout the runner ends the harness and the processes still descended from it, as the adapters README's "The agent runner" says. A timeout above 604800 seconds (a week) is now a usage error. The run command comes with the backbone this release ships, so the capability needs it: upgrade the capability with the backbone and the claude-code adapter.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Every listing of issues in project-management now runs through one read, which leaves state issues out by their marker, as DEC-058 point 3 decides (#2139). An issue whose description holds the line `<!-- pkit:state-issue … -->`, outside a code block, is then no child, no parent and no carrier of a workstream. It is in no sample of pre-check or adopt-existing, and nothing back-fill, link-parent or a migration's label-delete acts on. No state issue exists until the state-issue primitive is built, so every issue of work reads as before. A listing that reached its limit is judged by every issue it returned, state issues included. The check a label-delete makes that no issue uses the label reads a page of 100 issues instead of one, so that a state issue on it hides no issue of work.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A new state issue now has its conversation locked before its first comment, so GitHub lets fewer accounts comment on it, such as those with write access (#2149). A member who takes or releases a seat may need write access. A lock that fails is named, for a person to lock that state issue on GitHub, and stops nothing. `create-seats` finishes an issue left half done only where an account that may write opened it. It leaves alone one opened by an account that may not write, or by one GitHub no longer has, names it on standard error, and gives the seat a state issue of its own. Neither change makes a state issue a security boundary (DEC-058 point 5). A state issue created before this version stays unlocked: lock it on GitHub if you want it locked.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The revalidation records' folder is declared as the capability's folder of held documents (`friction.held`, DEC-001 point 2; COR-050 point 1), so a record under the internal root is no longer an unanchored artefact in the friction measures, nor a document a documentation capability counts as unclassified. `pkit analysis validate` reads the records as the files that folder's declaration holds in `pkit friction artefacts --json`, instead of listing the folder: a draft git ignores is no longer checked, and a record in a sub-folder of `revalidations/`, which the check used to skip, is now checked like the rest. When the backbone skips the folder, the check's summary says it holds nothing and `pkit validate` says why. The document is the backbone's this release ships, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The `analysis-resolver` agent states the recorded rule for friction writers: it names `pkit friction revalidate` and `pkit friction defer` in the one paragraph that points at the agents README's four conditions for running one, the second of which never holds for it — it has no tool for asking the person — so in every session it hands the commands over, without `--yes`, runs none, and points at the agents README for who may run them. `pkit friction record-status` is the after-merge job's, and the record stamp and the defect's reference stay the person's. Its storyboard and the capability README say the same, and the `analysis-author` skill says a reason for having no anchors is shown to the person word for word and stamped only once they accept it. What the agent does is unchanged. The rule is the backbone's this release ships (COR-050 point 3), so the capability requires that backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The `analysis-resolver` agent no longer says that every answer an artefact carries is a person's decision. It makes no change of its own, so no answer it proposes is one its own change owes: the agent making a change may write an answer that change owes, checked by a reviewer where the project chose one, and a person decides every other, and every reason for having no anchors (COR-050 point 3). It still runs no writer and hands the commands over. For an artefact whose outcome is recorded as `updated`, its proposal now drafts the one sentence on what changed in it, which whoever records it gives with the change. Its storyboard and the capability README say the same.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — DEC-001 writes each of its points as a heading carrying its number, word for word (#1421, COR-058). A page can now anchor to one point of it, such as `software-analysis:DEC-001#5`, and a change to another point of the record does not ask for the page to be revalidated. No point's text, number or citation changes.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Each actor is a file of its own, `use-case-model/actors/ACT-<slug>.md`, so its needs read beside its description (DEC-001 points 2 and 3, #1346). The needs stay in the front matter, where the schema checks them and the readers filler reads them. The actor's schema gains an `id`. `pkit analysis new actor` stamps the file from the new `actor.md` template, headed `# ACT-<slug> — <name>`, and `pkit analysis validate` checks that heading as it checks a use case's. The readers filler gives no answer when any one actor file cannot be read. The actors' place in the package metadata is now the folder, and the old collection template, `actors.md`, is gone, with a migration that does nothing and says why. Glossary terms stay in their collection file. This breaks a project that keeps its actors in `use-case-model/actors.md`: that file is no longer a place, and its actors must move by hand, each with its revalidation. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0, and the capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones, whose actors move in the same change.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The revalidation record's schema gains `reviewed-against`, the sources of the design a planned record reviewed (DEC-001 point 6, #1363). It is required when `trigger` is `planned`, and refused otherwise. Each source has a `title`, a `version` and, outside this repository, an absolute `url`. Without a `url`, the version is a commit's full id or `this change`. With one, it is the time the text was read or a commit's full id. This breaks a planned record without the field: `pkit analysis validate` fails it, and `pkit analysis new` refuses to stamp a planned record until it takes the sources. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0, and the capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones, which hold no planned record. ([#1578])
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A front-matter field the analysis stamp leaves you to fill now holds its declared hint as its placeholder, as each body part does (DEC-001 point 1). `pkit analysis new actor` writes `needs` with one item, the hint, and `pkit analysis new term` writes `definition` the same way. The hint is the marked comment a part holds, `<!-- pkit:hint … -->`, with the field's example, and a YAML parse reads it as text. Each template's front matter shows the same hint. `pkit analysis validate` refuses a hint left in a field whatever the format rule's status, and repeats the hint. A field that still holds an earlier `<…>` placeholder stays caught. A `--gap` written without its ids, whose text holds a colon, is now refused for its form, naming what is no id.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis new` renders each body from its kind's declared structure, `schemas/artefact-kinds.yaml`, and no longer copies template lines (DEC-001 point 1, #1366). Every part is written, in its order, each holding its hint as its placeholder: a one-line comment `<!-- pkit:hint … -->` with the part's example. Each template's body is the same rendering, and its front matter stays written by hand. A use case's and a journey's parts are now those the structure declares, so the old *Starts when*, *Done when*, *Starts* and *Seams to watch* are gone. A journey's step lines under *Steps* are written for each of its `steps`. `pkit analysis validate` refuses a hint left in a body, outside code spans and fenced code, whatever the format rule's status, and repeats the hint. Every placeholder a template shipped before is still caught while unfilled. The revalidation record's stamp writes no hints. Its `--gap` now names the artefacts each gap affects, `--gap "<ids>: <gap> => <resolution>"`, written as the gap's bold label with a `**Resolution:**`, and the stamp refuses what the form `gaps-match-outcomes` refuses. It writes `None.` where it wrote `None found.`. A planned record takes `--reviewed-against` once for each source, `"<title> @ <version>"` or `"<title> @ <version> @ <link>"`, and only a planned record takes it. A commit the default branch does not hold is written with a warning that `pkit analysis check-pointers` will fail the change. `--change` names a range of commits on the default branch. A time written unquoted in a record's front matter gets a message that says to quote it. This breaks a `--gap` written without its ids, and a command or script that expects the old parts. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0, and the capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate --preview` places a project's group whose rule, or a rule it fills, is proposed where the rule would bind if every proposed rule were accepted, as `pkit rule-sets reach --preview` reads it (DEC-001 point 1, #1607). What binds still comes from `pkit rule-sets reach` without the preview, and each body is held a second time with the preview's groups added. Only what that adds is a report opening `preview:`, so the findings and the exit stay the normal check's. A proposed rule that would bind on no artefact of its kind even under the preview is a preview's report, and so is a preview reading that cannot be read. The check no longer reports such a group as not previewed, and on an artefact it reads a fill's chain from the backbone alone. So a group whose accepted rule binds on no artefact of its kind, because a rule it fills is proposed, is reported as binding on none, the report naming that cause. The preview reads `pkit rule-sets reach --preview`, which only the backbone this release ships has, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A journey anchors to its actor, as DEC-001 point 4 asks, so a changed actor flags its journeys as well as its use cases (#1507). The stamp writes the anchor, and `pkit analysis validate` requires it. This breaks a journey stamped before the change, which has no anchor to its actor: the check now fails it, withdrawn ones included, until its actor is added to its artefact anchors. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0, and the capability has never been released. Only a clone that tracks project-kit's default branch can hold such a journey, and project-kit's own analysis holds none yet.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `SAN` binds only through a rule set of the project's own that inherits it (DEC-001 point 1, #1475). `pkit analysis validate` holds an artefact's body, and a glossary's head, to its structure only where `pkit rule-sets reach` says the format rule, `RS-SAN-001`, binds, and `--preview` only where `pkit rule-sets reach --preview` says it would. An artefact no such set selects is not checked. The summary counts the artefacts no rule of `SAN` binds on. Where it binds on none of the analysis, the check reports it while no rule of `SAN` is accepted, and warns once one is, and neither fails `pkit validate`. A revalidation record is a held document, which no rule binds on, so the check holds its body to the record's structure always, whatever any rule's status, citing DEC-001 and no longer the format rule. `RS-SAN-001` covers the analysis artefacts only. `project/structures.yaml` refuses a `tighten` on the record's kind, saying why. A record that breaks its structure, or a file that tightens the record's kind, now fails a check that passed before. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0, and the capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones, which hold no record and no such file. Installing creates no project set yet, so a project writes its own, as the README's "Where `SAN` binds" shows.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The `sentence` form reads a sentence's end as the maintainer chose on 17 October (#1370). A `.`, `?` or `!` ends a sentence only where a space and a capital letter follow it, and the text's end ends one. So "e.g. for" and "3. října" end none, and a text in a script without capital letters is never split. Any white space counts as a space, a character a reader cannot see as nothing, and a mark inside a fenced block ends none. A body part of the form, such as a use case's *Goal*, is one text and holds no Markdown list, not even inside a block quote: `pkit analysis validate` fails on a list there, at its first item, where it held each item to one sentence before. An actor's `needs` stays one text per item. `SAN` holds a second rule, `RS-SAN-002`: a use case's steps and variants keep their numbers and letters (DEC-001 point 3). The check of a step or a variant renumbered or removed, in `pkit analysis check-numbers`, binds where that rule binds and at its status, no longer the format rule's, and `SAN` declares it under the rule's `checks`. Its message for a step or a variant removed says to put it back under its number or letter even if it no longer happens, as RS-SAN-002 says, no longer to mark it withdrawn, and for one renumbered, that a new one takes a number or a letter after every one its use case or step holds. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0: a goal written as a list, which passed before, now fails where the format rule binds. The capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones, whose analysis holds no use case and no journey.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Both rules of `SAN` are accepted, as the maintainer decided on 17 October (#1370): the format rule, `RS-SAN-001`, with its sentence end and its refusal of a list in a part of the `sentence` form, and the append-only rule, `RS-SAN-002`. Accepting them takes `SAN` to 1.0.0 (COR-051 point 7). Where a set of the project's own that inherits `SAN@1` selects an artefact, `pkit analysis validate`, and so `pkit validate`, fails on a body or a front-matter field that departs from its kind's structure, and `pkit analysis check-numbers` fails on a step or a variant renumbered or removed. A set that still pins `SAN@0` binds neither rule, and `pkit validate` fails on its pin, until it is re-pinned to `SAN@1` (COR-051 point 7). Where no rule of `SAN` binds on any of the analysis, the check now warns, where it reported before, and the warning does not fail `pkit validate`. The README's example set pins `SAN@1`. The segment is `minor`, as semver allows a breaking change before 1.0.0: the capability is installed only in project-kit's own clones, whose set re-pins to `SAN@1` in the same change and whose analysis breaks neither rule.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The overview of each analysis lists each domain-model view by its number and title, and reads front matter only again: it no longer quotes a view's opening. Under each actor it lists the use cases the actor is the main actor of and those it takes part in, each linked. In an analysis that builds on another, each actor of that analysis the glossary's head lists, or a use case names, follows the analysis's own actors, by its id with that analysis and the name the head gives it. The fingerprint reads the head's names, and its version is 2, so an overview written before is a warning until `pkit analysis overview` writes it anew. DEC-001 point 1 and DEC-003 point 8 are refined in place.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — An analysis's overview is written for a newcomer first, someone new to the analysis joining work on the component, who reads it from top to bottom, and it reads in two parts. The story opens with what the component is for: the title of the accepted decision record that the analysis's new optional `purpose` setting names, linked, or a line that says no record states it yet. Then two sentences say what the analysis is and what it builds on, such as "This analysis describes what core does: who uses it, what they do with it, and what it must meet. It builds on no other analysis." The areas follow in their order, each with its goal, the areas it builds on, read from its use cases' `after`, its requirements, read from their folder, and its use cases. An area with a goal and no use case yet shows as not written yet, so such a goal no longer fails the check. Reference follows, under its own heading. One table there shows who takes part, with each actor's new optional `summary`, the first sentence of its opening, which the check holds equal to it where the format rule binds. The concepts, the requirements in no area, the journeys, each with its flow, and the open shortfalls follow it. The overview no longer shows the requirement map, the chain of use cases or the use-case diagram. Reference ends with one line that names `pkit analysis diagram`, which prints them, with its subjects. `pkit analysis validate` fails a `purpose` that names no record, one that is not accepted, or another capability's decision, and warns of an area of requirements without a goal. An area's goal is one phrase that starts with its verb, a summary goal over the area's use cases, such as `010-ordering: Place an order and pay for it`, as the README, the authoring skill and that warning say. The overview's fingerprint reads other data of the analysis, and its version is 4, so an overview written before is a warning until `pkit analysis overview` writes it anew. DEC-001 point 1, DEC-002 point 3 and DEC-003 are refined in place.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The term links `pkit analysis link` writes no longer flag what rests on the documents that hold them (#1844, software-analysis DEC-004 point 7). The places of the glossary and of the domain-model views declare the links' form, a link to a glossary entry, as the core's derived links (COR-050 point 5). So a change that only adds, removes or retargets those links asks no revalidation of the views and pages that rest on an entry or a section. A word changed beside a link still does. A revalidation whose only edit is links is `unchanged`. The declaration needs the backbone this release ships. The README says what a moved link asks.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis propose` answers a regression as DEC-001 point 5 decides. A regressed step that an accepted record of its use case decides takes its `Not built yet` box and its `shortfalls` entry, naming the record and the defect's issue, and ends `updated`. Which record decides which step is the resolving agent's reading: on a use case that anchors an accepted record, a regression's answer gives no command, and its new `which` field names those records, until the agent passes `--box <step> --decided-by <record>` or `--no-box`. A box on an artefact that is no use case, or on a record that is not one of the use case's accepted record anchors, is refused with exit 2. The `analysis-resolver` agent and its storyboard script both regressions.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The terms are linked where an actor, a use case, a journey or a requirement mentions them, as DEC-004 point 7 decides (#1895). In an analysis that keeps a domain model, `pkit analysis link` links the first mention of each term in each part of their bodies to the term's glossary entry, by a path from the file. A part opens at each heading and each label its kind declares, a label your own structures add or relabel included, whatever any rule's status. Front matter takes no link. Nor do a use case's or a journey's goal, a journey's step lines and an actor's first sentence, which say a need, a title and the summary again. An actor's name, of the analysis or listed by its glossary's head, holds no mention of a term only part of it, in every kind. Whatever any rule's status, `pkit analysis validate` reports such a link missing, extra or stale, and names the command. So an analysis with a view fails validation until you run `pkit analysis link` once. The places of the actors, the use cases, the journeys and the requirements declare the links' form, so a change that only moves links asks no answer of what rests on them.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The citation links `pkit analysis link` writes no longer flag what rests on the documents that hold them (#2064, software-analysis DEC-001 point 13). Each place of the analysis declares their form beside the term links' form, as the core's derived links (COR-050 point 5): a path to a file named by a record's id, or to a heading named by a rule's id. So a change that only adds, removes or re-points those links asks no answer, and `updated` on it alone is a bump. A word changed beside a link still asks. A link of that form written by hand is set aside too, whatever its words. The link of a citation in brackets, such as `[software-analysis:DEC-001]`, stays content, since the core reads no link whose words hold a bracket. The README's "Linking citations" says what a moved link asks.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis new actor|term --unanchored-because <why>` writes the reason inside the artefact's friction block, `pkit.friction.unanchored-because` — the core's key (COR-050 point 1; DEC-001 point 9) — instead of among the entry's own fields, where the core never read it: the core's unanchored measure now lists an accepted actor or term apart, with its reason, and no longer counts it. The actor and term schemas no longer admit the field among the entry's own fields, and `pkit analysis validate` drops its warning about the reason beside anchors in favour of the core's validation finding (`unanchored-beside-anchors`); it still fails a placeholder the skill's commands shipped left in the reason, now read from the block through `pkit friction artefacts --json`. The capability is pre-release, so an entry written by an earlier stamp is not migrated: move its `unanchored-because` into its friction block by hand. The key comes with the backbone this release ships: upgrade the capability together with the backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis check-numbers` compares with the base the backbone names (COR-054, #1128) — `--base`, else `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`, else the project's default branch, `repository.default-branch` — where it used to fall back to a fixed `origin/main`, and `pkit analysis new` numbers past the default branch and any `--base` it is given, never past `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE`'s. Both read what is settled through `pkit repository base --json` and no longer resolve a base or compute a merge-base themselves. A base that cannot be compared, or a default branch that resolves nowhere, refuses them with the backbone's reason and fix; the stamp no longer numbers from the working tree alone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis new` asks the backbone what is settled once per stamp: one `pkit repository base --base=<ref>` names both the default branch and the base given on the command line. A stamp given `--base` therefore starts one `pkit` process fewer (#1221). It numbers exactly as before. ([#1225])
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The text `pkit analysis new` writes from software-analysis's templates holds no semicolon and no sentence over 25 words. The actors and glossary files open with one sentence that says what they hold, followed by short sentences on ids and names. A use case's line under *Variants* is two sentences, and the revalidation record template's outcome placeholder reads without a semicolon. Every placeholder a template ever shipped is still caught while it is left unfilled.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A journey is one actor's path across use cases, and the check and the stamp now hold it. `pkit analysis validate` fails a journey in force whose step is a use case of another actor, and its message names the step and both actors. To clear it, name a use case of the journey's actor in the step's place, or correct the use case's actor. `pkit analysis new journey` refuses the same journey, writing nothing. A withdrawn journey is history, so its steps are not compared.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The 0.2.0 upgrade's migration that creates the `ANALYSIS` rule set reads its answer from the pkit that runs the upgrade, the one the migration runner puts first on the script's `PATH` (ADR-065 point 14). Neither your project's pin nor another `pkit` on your `PATH` answers in its place. Where the answer cannot be read, the migration creates nothing and warns that the command failed, or that the script was run by hand under another pkit. It needs the migration runner only the backbone this release ships has. ([#1662])
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The `analysis-resolver` ends a fixer run in the exact forms of project-management's run contract (DEC-056 point 3), so the command that starts fixers reads every line it writes. Its `none` line carries one of the contract's reasons before the reason in words, such as `not-mine` for an artefact that is not the analysis's, and `refused` where the run refuses `pkit analysis propose` or it cannot start there. In a run it takes the pull-request text the command's brief quotes as evidence of intent: it passes the words that say whether a change was meant to `pkit analysis propose` with `--intended-from` and the pull request's URL, which shows the quote unverified. Its storyboard shows the result lines in a fenced block of their own, never behind a quote's `>`, which the contract would not count.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The check's open regression reads an artefact's revalidations through the backbone, never from the artefact's file, so it needs no change when the answers move into answer files of their own (COR-062, #1872). It reads the `at` of each live revalidation from `pkit friction explain --json` at HEAD, whether the backbone read it from the artefact's block or from an answer file. A revalidation therefore counts once it is committed, where the check read the working tree before. An artefact the backbone cannot explain is still reported open, with why.
