- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — After you upgrade a project that keeps an analysis, run `pkit analysis link` and then `pkit analysis overview`. This version links each citation of a record, a point or a rule, and each mention of an artefact's id. Until `pkit analysis link` writes those links, `pkit analysis validate` fails on an analysis holding such a citation or mention. Then `pkit analysis overview` writes each overview anew, as validation reports one that is missing or out of date.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The four agents (`software-engineer` and the `code-reviewer` / `security-reviewer` / `docs-reviewer` panel) now declare their project conventions corpus read as `reads.patterns: [<project-conventions>]` — the angle-bracket form ADR-013 D1 always specified — instead of a bare literal. Combined with the resolver's new optional-read behaviour (ADR-052), the adopter's resolved `project-conventions` path now actually reaches each deployed agent when defined, and `pkit agents reconcile` surfaces the category (as OPTIONAL — the agents already deploy without it). Undefined, the agents still deploy and act as careful generalists. ([#773])
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The code-review panel (`code-reviewer`, `security-reviewer`, `docs-reviewer`) states its read-only claim precisely: it governs the work under review and the repository it lives in, not "writes nothing anywhere". Each body gains the `## What read-only covers` section every reviewer that can execute carries: the working files a review needs — a dumped diff, a script, a reproduction (even one that executes a payload to prove a defect), captured output — go in the agent workspace, never loose in the repository, where an author's `git add` would commit them, nor in `/tmp/`, and a review leaves no file of its own outside the workspace. The descriptions say "Read-only on what it reviews". The three panel reviewers gain the `Write` tool, scoped by their bodies to the agent workspace, so a review's working files land there without a prompt instead of through a shell redirect, which the permission hook judges like any other shell write.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — DEC-001 and DEC-002 write each of their points as a heading carrying its number, word for word (#1421, COR-058). A page can now anchor to one point of them, such as `software-engineering:DEC-002#3`, and a change to another point of the record does not ask for the page to be revalidated. No point's text, number or citation changes. DEC-002's paragraph on the pm-side companion decisions, after its point 7, sits under a heading of its own, with the same words.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The docs reviewer now checks every answer no change owes that a pull request writes, whoever decided it, and blocks where one does not hold (COR-064 point 4). That is an answer on a document the change check did not ask about, a revisit entry it did not ask for, and a reason for having no anchors. So a pull request can now be blocked on such an answer a person accepted word for word, where the answer does not hold. The person may skip that review on record. The reviewer still names each such answer in an advisory finding, since it cannot tell whether a person or the delegate a person named decided it. The software engineer names such an answer in its hand-off for the person, or for that delegate, to decide. DEC-002 point 1 is refined to match.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The docs reviewer now checks each revalidation that the change check flags as replacing revalidations answered apart. Its `unchanged` reason, or the `updated` document, must hold against what changed since each replaced revalidation, or the review blocks (COR-062 point 7). So a pull request can now be blocked on a revalidation that judges only one of two lines of work. An `answered-apart` finding of the change check is debt that no change owes, and the reviewer never blocks on it. The capability's README lists the new block.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The `software-engineer` agent and the code-review panel (`code-reviewer`, `security-reviewer`, `docs-reviewer`) are told to keep their intermediate files — drafts, scripts, captured output, notes — in the agent workspace, `.agent-workspace/` at the repository root, and nowhere else outside the repository, writing them with the file tools: the workspace is granted to every agent for those, while a shell redirect into it is judged like any other shell write.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The `software-engineer` agent fixes the review findings their reviewer tagged blocking and records each advisory in the PR body with how it was answered, rather than widening the fix to take it in (#1180). ([#1186])
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The code-review panel (`code-reviewer`, `security-reviewer`, `docs-reviewer`) reads a pull request's diff from the local checkout, `git diff <base>...<head>`, when `gh pr diff` refuses it for changing more than 300 files, and requests changes for a diff it cannot read only when neither source yields it — however the reviewer was started (#1190). The bodies also describe the verdict marker as it is posted, naming the reviewed head and base, and tell a reader to match its `<!-- pkit-verdict` prefix rather than the bare string. ([#1205])
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The README, the `software-engineer` agent and the `docs-reviewer` agent say that a link takes some of a document's anchors as its targets, rather than that the document lists them (#1659, COR-050 point 16). A link may name the fields that list what stays out, so the `docs-reviewer` reads a revisit whose `links` is empty, and whose line names no field, as one such link takes.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The software engineer commits what the friction writers write with its change, a new answer file among it, and no longer owes an answer after resolving a conflict in an artefact's block. Where the change check flags one of its revalidations as replacing revalidations answered apart, it makes that revalidation's reason judge their combined state (COR-062 points 1 and 7).

### Fixed
- Sync and upgrade refuse to propagate over the methodology's source repository when the running code is not the repository's own: where sync's self-host test says no but the checkout's markers (the package source beside the in-tree dispatcher) say source, propagating would copy a foreign tree over the source it is built from (ADR-059). The refusal names both tests and how the run got there — routing bypassed with `PKIT_NO_ROUTE=1` (by hand, or by a `pin` to a newer release or a pinned `upgrade`), an inherited `PKIT_ROUTED=1`, a dispatcher present but not executable, or a start that never went through the router — and points at running the checkout's own code. Nothing overrides it, `--force` included. `pkit pin` refuses in the source repository, where the router runs the checkout's own code before any pin is read. The router's warning for a non-executable dispatcher now points at repairing it, not at `pkit sync`.
- Upgrading now untracks the runtime-local files an adopter committed before their `runtime_ignore` declaration shipped (#288). The rendered `.pkit/.gitignore` ignores them, but an ignore rule does not untrack a file git already tracks, so a committed `sandbox-provenance.yaml`, diagnose log or `__pycache__/` stayed tracked for good. The upgrade to this release runs a backbone migration that removes every tracked file the rendered `.pkit/.gitignore` ignores from the index with `git rm --cached` alone: the working copy, which is per-machine state, is never touched, and the removal is staged for the operator to commit. Only declared files move — git's own matcher decides, against that file alone, so a shared `config.yaml` or a file the adopter's own `.gitignore` ignores but tracks on purpose stays tracked, and private visibility does not hide the declared ones. It is skipped while a merge, rebase, cherry-pick or revert is in progress, a path whose staged content git would refuse to drop stays tracked, and both are reported with the command to finish by hand. A re-run finds nothing to untrack and says so. Once the removal is committed, another clone that pulls it while still tracking the files has git delete its copies; the migration's report says to untrack them there first. In another clone that pulls the committed removal while still tracking the files, git deletes the working copies; the bytes are the pre-pull commit's, and `git restore --source=ORIG_HEAD --worktree -- <paths>` right after the pull restores them without re-tracking them.
- A command a query starts now stays inside that query's bound. When a validator reads a data point through `pkit connections resolve` and the point's filler hangs, the filler is given only the time the validator has left, it is stopped in time for the validator to answer, and the finding names the filler rather than the validator — "did not answer within the 0.4 s its caller had left" when that time was short; whatever the filler started — a `pkit friction artefacts` it runs, say — no longer outlives `pkit validate`, even when something else killed the reading command, and an interrupt ends the whole tree. Every command the backbone runs is told its deadline in `PKIT_COMMAND_DEADLINE` (seconds since the epoch), so a well-behaved command can tell; a deadline left over in a shell changes nothing. Within one `pkit validate`, each data point now resolves once: whichever of the `connections` member and a validator reading the point asks first resolves it, and the others get that resolution, its fillers not started again — unless the first asker ran out of time, when the next one resolves it. `pkit connections resolve --json` says where the point came from in a new `from` field: `run-cache` when a validate run had already resolved it, `resolution` otherwise.
- A request to the hosting service that gets no answer is no longer reported as refused, and `pkit release merge` no longer waits for ever on a `gh` that never answers. Every `gh` call `pkit pull-request` and `pkit release merge` make is bounded: 15 seconds for a reading and 30 for a request that changes the service, after which `gh` alone is ended. `gh` now runs in the command's own process group, so a caller that ends that group ends a `gh` request in flight too. A request is taken as refused only on an answer the backbone recognises: GraphQL errors, a `GraphQL:` line, an HTTP 4xx, or `gh`'s own refusal before it sent anything. Anything else, a `gh` ended by a signal included, is no usable answer. Then `merge`, `enqueue` or `dequeue` says on standard error that the request is in flight, and reads the pull request. If the request's end state shows (merged or queued, or dropped by the queue since it was sent; out of the queue on two readings for a dequeue), it was made: `accepted` is true. If two readings, the second at least 40 seconds after the request, do not see it, it was not seen made: `accepted` is false, with `reason_kind: not-made`, and the reason says what the readings saw rather than that it was not made. A dequeue not seen made is sent once more. If the pull request cannot be read, the request is unconfirmed: `accepted` is null, with `reason_kind: unanswered`, and the command exits 1 and names `pkit pull-request read <n>`. `dequeue` now confirms a pull request out of the queue on two readings even when the service answered it. One it finds merged has `reason_kind: merged`, and one the service accepted that cannot be read since is unconfirmed, with `reason_kind: unreadable`. `pkit release merge` reports an unconfirmed merge or enqueue plainly, claiming neither that the release merged nor that it did not, and exits 4 with nothing deleted. A request not seen made exits 1, saying this run saw nothing merged and naming the reading and the re-run that tell. ([#1303])
- The backbone tells a repository with no commit yet from one whose commit git cannot read, such as a missing object or a checkout git refuses as unsafe. `pkit repository base --json` gains `head`: HEAD's `commit`, `unborn` (true only when HEAD is a branch before its first commit) and `problem` (why git cannot read HEAD here). Its `schema_version` stays 1. A data point's filler reads `head` to learn whether there is history (COR-052 point 6). For a filler that declares `reads: [history]`, the `connections` member of `pkit validate` and `pkit status` used to say `reads history (HEAD, no commit yet)` whenever git could not answer. They now say `reads history (git cannot read HEAD here)` in that case, and `pkit connections resolve --json` carries that filler's history read with `ref` and `commit` both null. A default branch whose commit git cannot read no longer counts as having no commit yet, so a filler that declares `settled` is not started there. The `default_branch` problem in `pkit repository base` names only the fixes a filler has, the fetch and the declaration. The `base` problem still offers naming a base.
- A migration never targets a version already released. A project already at that version never runs a script in its folder, and nothing reported it. `pkit new migration` without `--version` now picks the next version past the one the default branch records for the tier, `.pkit/VERSION` or the component's `package.yaml`, and prints it with its reason. It used to take that released version itself. It joins the next major's folder where one already waits, and takes the version the working tree records where a branch bumped it above the default branch's. A `--version` at or below the default branch's version is refused, naming the version to give. The script's number counts the default branch's scripts in its folder too. `pkit migrations check-diff` now fails a migration script a change adds, or moves in, whose folder is at or below the version its tier records at the base's tip, and names the version to move it to. A script your project receives by sync is not judged.
- Installing or updating a capability no longer copies the source project's own `project/` tree into the adopter (#812). That subtree is adopter-owned by tier, but it was being seeded from wherever the capability was distributed from — so every fresh adopter received the source project's instance data, and because seed-once is per *file* while `pkit sync` re-copies installed capabilities, each new file the source accrued arrived on the next update too. Four consequences, all verified by running the real install path into an empty directory: the default-agent activation file shipped already present, and since the adapter activates on file *presence*, a fresh adopter silently got `project-manager` as their default agent — contradicting DEC-030's guarantee that installation "creates the template but no activation file" and the capability README's "off by default"; the bootstrap stamp shipped, so an adopter whose repo identity could not be resolved had their setup gate read it as completion and run every gated verb against foreign defaults with no labels provisioned; per-issue audit journals shipped, keyed by issue number, so an adopter's issue #446 inherited another project's #446 governance history; and the config and workstream taxonomy shipped, making the source project's own work areas the adopter's allowed classification values. Install now stubs an empty `project/` directory instead, mirroring what the area install path has always done. Adopter content is never touched — the change removes a copy and adds no delete, which the regression tests assert explicitly. Files already seeded into existing adopters are not cleaned up here; that needs content-based discrimination to avoid deleting an adopter's own work and is tracked separately.
- The distribution no longer carries adopter-owned content, and is reproducible (#813). The methodology tree is bundled by `force-include`, and hatchling's `exclude` cannot filter force-included paths — so eleven `.pkit/` trees were included wholesale, carrying each capability's adopter-owned `project/` subtree plus the project-side trees of several areas: 51 such files in a 1.149.0 wheel. That is how one project's config, its default-agent activation switch, its bootstrap stamp and its per-issue audit journals reached adopters (the copy side of which was fixed separately). Worse, some of that content is git-ignored runtime state, so what shipped depended on whatever untracked files sat on the build machine: the released 1.149.0 wheel carries 14 journals where a wheel built from a working tree carried 36 — two builds of one commit produced different artifacts. The force-includes for those trees are now rebuilt file by file by a build hook that asks the project's own ownership predicate, rather than enumerating what to keep in a hand-maintained list which had already drifted from the rule it was meant to mirror. A test asserts the built artifact against that same predicate, so the two cannot diverge again, and separately asserts that kit-owned content still ships and no build cache does — the per-file force-include bypasses `exclude`, so the hook reapplies those patterns itself. Both distributions are covered, by different filters — the wheel through the ownership predicate, the source distribution through a list of withheld globs the same build hook applies, kept separate on purpose so each can check the other — and a test compares the two artifacts against each other rather than only against the predicate, which is what caught a hole in the predicate itself: `project/` was matched only at the depths then known, so the wheel was still shipping this project's own harness permission allow-list. Nothing an adopter needs is removed: verified that no kit-owned file is missing from either artifact, and a build from a clean clone produces the same file list as one from a working tree carrying 36 untracked journals. The bundle also still carries the adopter-tier DIRECTORIES the installer reads to decide whether to stub an adopter's `project/` scaffolding — as empty markers, declared in code so both build paths emit the same set, since discovering them from the filesystem made the artifact depend on untracked state and produced no markers at all when the wheel was built from a source distribution.
- An adopter's own `project/` tier is recognised at every position it occupies, not only directly under an area (#823). The rule matched `.pkit/<area>/project/` alone, so two deeper positions fell through to the closing "everything else under `.pkit/` is the kit's" rule: the adapter settings pair (`.pkit/adapters/<harness>/settings/project/`, which holds an adopter's own permission additions) and the per-component adapter manifest (`.pkit/adapters/<harness>/project/manifest.yaml`). Both were reported as kit-owned. The consequence was not what it first appeared, and the difference is worth stating because the predicate's name (`is_sync_managed`) describes a consumer it does not have: `pkit sync` never calls it, and decides what it writes through the copy path, which already treats adopter-owned files as seed-once — verified by installing a scratch adopter, writing a sentinel into that settings file and running a sync, which left it byte-identical. The one production consumer is the agent-overlay write-authority guard, so the real effect was that an adopter could not grant an agent write authority over their own allow-list: the guard refused the path as kit content. It now accepts it, while still refusing the kit's half of the same settings pair. The tier is enumerated by position rather than by searching for a `project` component at any depth: a `project/` directory inside a kit-owned refresh root — an area's `core/`, or below a capability's top level — belongs to the kit and is overwritten and pruned like any other kit content, which ADR-012 Decision 2 already pinned for capabilities and which this change leaves unchanged. Tests assert the two ownership predicates never contradict each other, over the real tree and over declared paths this repo does not contain — the adapter positions exist only in installed adopters, so a tree walk alone cannot see them. The predicate's docstring, the lifecycle README, the agents README and the packaging comments now state what the predicate actually governs, since the previous wording is what produced the misfiled report.
- The `release` and `version` commands now operate on the repository at the working directory, so `pkit release check` run from a git worktree reports that worktree's diff and changesets rather than those of the checkout that owns the `pkit` binary, and prints a one-line notice when the two differ (#877). ([#880])
- `pkit schemas add` and `pkit schemas rename` now find a namespace stamped into the core schemas area (`.pkit/schemas/`) by `pkit new schema core`, the whole-tree `pkit schemas validate` / `schemas list` / `schemas show` cover that area alongside the capability ones, and an unknown namespace is reported naming both homes searched (#879). ([#884])
- The bundled methodology tree is built from tracked files, so a local checkout's untracked files no longer ship (#909). The build walked the filesystem, so an untracked, non-ignored file under `.pkit/` (an editor backup, a `.DS_Store`) went into the source distribution and, because the default build makes the wheel from the source distribution, into the wheel as well. That broke the claim that two builds of one commit carry the same content. Both artifacts now list `.pkit/` content with `git ls-files` whenever the source tree has its own `.git`. A wheel built from a source distribution walks the unpacked tree, which is sound because that tree was assembled from tracked files. If a `.git` is present but git cannot answer, the build fails with a message rather than falling back to the walk. That includes git refusing a checkout owned by a different user, as in CI containers and Docker volume mounts; the message gives the `git config --global --add safe.directory` remedy. The editable install (`uv sync`) runs the same build step, so in such a container `uv sync` now fails the same way until the checkout is marked safe. A build from a tree with neither a `.git` nor source-distribution metadata (a Docker context without `.git`, a copied tree) still succeeds but warns that untracked files may ship. Installs from the git URL build from a clean checkout, so their file set is unchanged; verified by comparing kit file lists before and after, which differ only by the stray file. ([#926])
- `pkit upgrade` no longer crashes when started with no stdin or a closed one (as under some process supervisors); it treats that as non-interactive and prints the tool-update instruction instead of self-updating (#913). ([#928])
- `pkit release merge` no longer reports a failure after the release PR has already merged when run from a worktree or a detached HEAD; it deletes the head branch through the GitHub API, treats local cleanup as best-effort warnings, and never deletes a branch named by a fork's head (#897). ([#936])
- `pkit new capability`, `pkit capabilities install` and `pkit capabilities register` now refuse a capability named `core`. That name is reserved because it names the core schemas area, so a capability called `core` would have had its schemas silently unreachable (#919). ([#939])
- `pkit agents adopt` no longer refuses an agent whose only unmet category is an optional read with no conventional default (such as `project-conventions`): it leaves the category undefined, names it as optional, and deploys the agent (#916). ([#940])
- Every file in the wheel and the source distribution now comes from tracked files, not only the bundled `.pkit/` tree (#930). The Python package in the wheel and the rest of the source distribution (tests, docs, scripts) were taken from the build backend's own walk, so an untracked module under `src/project_kit` shipped as importable code; both are now enumerated from tracked files like `.pkit/`. The licence file is declared explicitly (`license = "MIT"` with `license-files = ["LICENSE"]`), so an untracked `LICENSE.orig` or similar at the root no longer ships in the source distribution or the wheel's metadata; the package metadata now states the licence as the SPDX expression `MIT`. A build from a clean checkout ships the same files as before. The editable install is unchanged in effect: it still points at `src/` through a `.pth` file, now named `_project_kit.pth`. A wheel build mode other than the standard or editable wheel now fails the build rather than producing a wheel with no package. The build requires hatchling 1.32.4 or later. ([#946])
- A hung workstream lookup no longer hangs `pkit report` (#1053). The report asks the project-management capability's `context-workstream` verb for the workstream on its context line; that call now runs through the backbone's one command runner, under the same thirty-second bound as every other command the backbone runs on a component's behalf. A verb that overruns is stopped with everything it started, the report prints a warning saying the workstream was omitted because the verb did not answer in time (pass `--workstream` to name it), and the report is composed without it. Every other miss still omits the workstream silently, and a verb that prints output that is not UTF-8 now omits the workstream instead of stopping the report with a decoding error.
- The core skills now declare in their front matter what their body cites, so `pkit validate --only refs` reports no drift warnings on them: the `decision-author` skill declares the agents overlay it points ADR authors at, `report-author` declares COR-043, and the `schema` skill stops citing the superseded COR-022.
- `pkit validate`, the friction writers and `pkit friction check` now find the same artefacts in the same working tree (#1034, ADR-057 point 2). Validation walked the filesystem while the change check read git's listing, and the two disagreed: validation read files git ignores, documents beneath a nested repository, and documents through a link (a linked file, or everything beneath a linked folder), none of which the change check ever saw; it read globs by the running interpreter's pathlib, so `docs/**.md` matched nothing before Python 3.13; the change check missed a document with Windows line endings, which validation read; and an unreadable file in a place was a validation finding but aborted the change check. Every reader now takes one listing of the working tree — the files git sees, and outside a git work tree every file — in which a link is listed but never followed or read as a document, globs read as pathlib 3.13 reads them on every interpreter, line endings are read as a text file is read, and an unreadable file is reported as unreadable.
- The configuration pass of `pkit validate` now judges the friction patterns and the contributor selections as the rest of the backbone reads them (#1034, ADR-057 point 2), removing three disagreements. A friction pattern is judged as friction discovery reads it — without surrounding spaces, and an entry of spaces alone, which discovery ignores, not at all — and counts as matching something only when it covers a file of the working tree's listing by the reading the friction checks apply, so a glob reaching only folders, or only files git ignores, is now warned as matching nothing, as the checks never see anything under it. A contributor selection is checked against the data point the active provider of its role defines: a point declared only by a provider that is not selected is not defined in the project and is now an error, a point the active provider declares as not `single` is refused even when another provider's declaration says `single`, and while the role has no active provider the selection is not reported a second time beside the role conflict.
- Every comparison of a version range — a component's `requires_backbone` against a backbone, a `requires_capabilities` range against a capability — now goes through the wiring resolver's one relation, the one `pkit validate` reports (#1034, ADR-057 point 2): the capability install and register gates, the capability upgrade's dependant check, the dependency check they share and the backbone upgrade's compatibility check all call it. The backbone upgrade read `requires_backbone` out of the package file with a pattern that saw only a double-quoted value, so a single-quoted range was ignored and a commented-out line could be taken for the range; it now reads the file as YAML, as validation does, so a single-quoted range outside the target backbone refuses the upgrade and a comment is never read as one.
- The `data` member of `pkit validate` now lists the repository through the working tree's one listing, the one friction discovery reads (#1034, ADR-057 point 2), instead of walking the filesystem on its own: a YAML file git ignores, or one beneath a nested repository, is no longer taken for the repository's data. Outside a git work tree every file is listed, as before. The focused `pkit data validate <path>` still checks whatever path it is given.
- `pkit new agent <namespace> <name> --with-storyboard` declares the storyboard on the agent as it stamps the pair: the agent's `storyboards:` front matter names the sibling `storyboard.md` and its body cites it, so the fresh pair passes `pkit refs validate` without the author adding the line. The stamped placeholder under "Files you own" no longer cites the overlay file as a path, which `pkit refs validate` reported as an undeclared reference on every fresh agent.
- `pkit agents`, the ownership check of `pkit refs validate` and `pkit new storyboard` without `--namespace` resolve a name shipped by both a capability and core to the capability's agent, the one the Claude Code deploy now writes — the agents README's precedence: project, then an installed capability, then core. The agents README states how the deploy realises it and what happens when two capabilities ship one name.
- `pkit capabilities register <name>` in the methodology's source repository no longer prints "a capability named <name> also ships from kit source … the kit-shipped one is not installed": there the capability's subtree is the kit source, so there is no second copy to name. The note still appears for an adopter whose incubated capability later ships from the kit too.
- A revalidation written in a merge commit is now the artefact's revalidation point. The whole-repository friction check (`pkit friction check --all`), and `friction debt`, `friction explain` and `friction record-status` with it, read the paths a merge commit changed against every parent, so a merge that writes a new `at`, as when resolving a conflict by revalidating the combined state, is the point, and a deferral a merge introduces has its point there. Before, the merge was skipped and an older commit taken as the point, so a page revalidated while merging the base branch read as stale there although the change check had accepted it. A merge that only keeps one side's `at` is still not the point: that side's commit is.
- In the methodology's source repository the capability verbs no longer delete or copy onto a capability's authored subtree. Run by the checkout's own code, `pkit capabilities uninstall` of a kit-shipped capability says which subtree it would have deleted, asks, and unregisters it only (`--yes` answers for a non-interactive run); `--purge` is refused there. `capabilities install` of an unregistered capability registers it in place without copying, and `capabilities upgrade` re-deploys it and provisions its query commands with nothing copied and no migration run, where both used to fail with `SameFileError`. Run by other code, `capabilities uninstall` now refuses in the source repository as `install`, `upgrade` and `register` do. A capability copy whose source is its destination is refused before its migrations run.
- A source checkout whose dispatcher `.pkit/cli/pkit` has been deleted is still recognised as the methodology's source repository: the marker test is the package source beside `.pkit/` (ADR-059 point 3), so the router's warning says the checkout carries the package source but no dispatcher and names `git checkout -- .pkit/cli/pkit`, and a sync or upgrade run by other code refuses rather than propagating over it. `pkit capabilities install`, `upgrade` and `register` refuse the same way in the source repository when the running code is not its own, with the same message, before any other pre-flight. The refusal's first line now reads "refusing to run `<command>` in <root>". `capabilities install`, `register` and `upgrade` provision the capability's query commands after deploying it, printing the lines `sync` prints, so `pkit validate` answers offline without a `pkit sync` first. `pkit process hand-off` resolves a role-addressed upstream through the wiring, as `health` and `couple` do, validating the trigger against the offered process instead of warning that it does not resolve.
- The read command `pkit connections resolve <address>` now resolves only the point asked for. Before, it resolved every data point and so started every command filler, and through them any nested checks, to answer for one. A script reading the readers point no longer runs the documentation-check fillers. `pkit validate` and `pkit status` still resolve every point. Within one run each point resolves once, whichever reader asks first, so the answer is unchanged.
- A capability older than the backbone no longer keeps committed process journals out of commits (#1134). Such a package still declares its own journal ignore line in `runtime_ignore`, and the rendered `.pkit/.gitignore` used to keep every component's lines, so with `process.journal.committed: true` new journal files stayed silently ignored until the capability was upgraded. The backbone owns the journal pattern, and its choice now takes precedence: while the project commits its journals, the render leaves out any component entry that declares them and lists it in a comment line (`# dropped: <component> '<entry>' — the backbone owns the journal pattern while journals are committed`). `pkit config set` prints one `dropped` line per such component when it re-renders the file. The `pkit validate` warning on the entry now fires only when the journals are committed, the one case the render drops it; while they stay ignored the entry is redundant. The journal migration this release's upgrade runs says the next `pkit sync` drops a component line it finds left behind. Documented in ADR-009 rule 7, the process README ("The journal — optional audit"), the CLI reference (`config set`, the `packages` check) and the lifecycle README ("Validation: the package schema").
- The friction measures now leave out artefacts under a `friction.exclude` path, as COR-050 point 7 says (#1136). `pkit friction check --all` counted and listed every artefact without anchors as unanchored, excluded ones included, and judged excluded artefacts stale or deferred, so `pkit friction debt` listed them too. An excluded artefact is now neither listed nor counted as unanchored and is never judged stale or deferred; the human view counts the rest and says how many were left out, and the JSON `counts` gain `excluded`. What such an artefact declares is still checked: its dead anchors, unresolved kinds and over-broad anchors are reported, since a dead anchor is never silence. `pkit friction explain` on it shows the state `excluded` and names the `friction.exclude` entry that leaves it out, with its file and JSON Pointer (`excluded_by` in `--json`). Artefact discovery decides the exclusion once, as it walks the places, and the checks read it from the artefact. Documented in the CLI reference ("friction check --all", "friction debt", "friction explain") and the schemas README.
- A collection entry's content now takes the body section headed by its whole id (COR-050 point 1). The section was the first heading that merely started with the id, so entry `uc-login` took the section headed `uc-login-sso …` when that one came first, and the friction checks compared the wrong prose for it. A heading now opens an entry's section only when the id is followed by the end of the heading, whitespace, or punctuation that continues no id — a hyphen or a dot before a letter or digit does. The same section is a rule's in a rule set.
- Friction discovery and its writers now read a file whatever its line endings (COR-050 points 1 and 5). The front-matter reader matched its `---` fences only with `\n` line endings, so a file checked out with `\r\n` — a clone with `core.autocrlf=true` — read as having no front matter wherever that reader was used directly, and the friction writers refused such a file outright. A file's front matter and body are now read with `\r\n` and a lone `\r` as `\n`, so an artefact whose line endings changed while its text did not has the same content; `pkit friction revalidate`, `defer` and `record-status` edit a `\r\n` file and write it back with `\r\n`, byte for byte outside the key they replace. A file that mixes line endings is a new `pkit validate` finding (`mixed-line-endings`), and the writers refuse it, rather than reading a carriage return inside a value as a line break.
- `pkit release check` now recognises a release pull request by what the release writes, so it passes one on its content alone, with no `skip-changeset` label and whatever the branch is called. It read the self-host manifest's `backbone_version`, which a backbone release also moves, as an ordinary surface change. The release step and the check now read one list of the files a release writes and the lines it changes in them, and the release rewrites that manifest line in place, leaving every other byte of the manifest as it was.
- A changeset's `pr` given as a bare number now links its changelog entry to that pull request of the repository instead of writing a broken link, and `pkit release lint` refuses a `pr` that is neither a pull-request number nor a URL.
- `pkit refs rename` with an empty old or new value refuses with a message instead of failing with a Python traceback.
- `pkit config set` changes only the lines of the key it sets (#1198). Every write to `.pkit/project/config.yaml` used to lay the whole file out again, re-indenting every list, so a one-key change showed as a rewrite of the file. The writer now edits the file in place: a value on one line keeps its line, quoting and trailing comment; a list or mapping replaces only its own key's lines, in its own layout; a new key goes after its last sibling, in the file's layout. Lists, comments, blank lines, indentation, key order and `\r\n` line endings elsewhere stay byte for byte. A change the edit cannot make exactly — removing a key, a file that mixes line endings, a key shared through a YAML alias — is refused, and nothing is written. Setting a value already set writes nothing and asks nothing; `config set` says so. The same writer serves `pkit connections providers set` and the saved project name.
- `pkit friction check --all` takes as an artefact's revalidation point the commit that wrote the `at` the artefact carries (#1217). After a merge that kept one side's revalidation where both sides revalidated, the check could take the other side's revalidation instead, when its commit was dated later: a commit whose `at` the file no longer carries. It then read that side's changes as covered and the kept side's as stale, where the change check, comparing with the base, read the opposite. Such a commit is now passed over, so the two checks agree on the point. `pkit friction debt`, `pkit friction explain` and `pkit friction record-status` read the same walk and follow. ([#1227])
- `pkit sync` and `pkit upgrade` run by a pkit older than the project's content (`.pkit/manifest.yaml`'s `backbone_version`) or its pin (`.pkit/version-pin`) now refuse before writing anything (#1212). They used to sync the older bundle over the newer content, and `upgrade` also moved the pin down: the state the router's offline fallback, which runs the installed tool when a pin cannot be fetched, now reaches often. The refusal exits non-zero, names the versions and the way to the right pkit (the pinned version through the router or `uvx`, `pkit pin <version>` to move the pin, git to roll back), and no flag overrides it, `--force` and `--dry-run` included. Read-only commands keep working. The router's fallback notice now says, once per command, when the pkit it runs is older than the pin. ([#1218])
- Every write of a project's version pin (`.pkit/version-pin`) is now whole or not at all (#1211): the pin `pkit upgrade` writes by default, the pin it raises, `pkit pin` and `pkit unpin` all go through one writer, which writes the new pin to a temporary file beside it, flushes it to disk and renames it into place. The default pin after an upgrade and `pkit pin` used to write the file in place, so a write cut short could leave a truncated pin, which the router then ran as the version to use. A write that fails now leaves the previous pin as it was. ([#1226])
- A process predicate that cannot be evaluated now says why, instead of every failure reading the same "couldn't evaluate detection predicate 'x'" (#752). The reason names how its run ended — exited 2, ended by a signal, did not answer within the bound, could not start, printed no JSON object — and the tail of what the predicate wrote on stderr is shown beside it, attributed ("the predicate said:"), in `pkit process status`, `validate`, `cascade`, `can-move` and `move`, and in a `stderr_tail` field of its own in the `--json` views (legal moves, invariants, blocked, subprocess, cascade, and a new `position.unevaluated` list naming the detections behind an indeterminate position). So an un-bootstrapped project's refusal reaches the operator with its "run `pkit project-management bootstrap`" hint. The tail is bounded (the last 10 non-blank lines, at most 1500 bytes) and stripped of terminal escape sequences and control characters. Fail-closed is unchanged: an unevaluable predicate is still indeterminate. `pkit report` likewise stops omitting the workstream silently when the project-management workstream verb fails: absent stays silent, but a verb that cannot start, exits non-zero, overruns or prints undecodable output is named in a warning with what it said. ([#1240])
- `pkit release check` now counts only the changesets a pull request itself adds or edits. It counted every pending changeset, so a pull request that changed a component and declared nothing passed whenever another pull request had already left a changeset naming the same component, and the change could reach a release with no line in the changelog. A pending changeset the pull request leaves alone, or one that merging the base branch brought in, no longer counts, and the failure message says so (#782). ([#1236])
- `pkit process hand-off` no longer tells you to pass `--state` when that flag cannot help (#711). When one state carries two `depends_on` entries on the same upstream — one upstream depended on in two ways, which `pkit process couple` legally appends — the stamp used to answer "couples to X on several states (s, s); pass --state", a dead end, since `--state` picks a state and both entries sit on it. It now says that no flag tells entries on one state apart, names both entries by place and relation, names the definition file, and prints the exact `handoff:` block to add to the intended entry by hand, then the `health --interpretation-only` run that confirms it. Entries on different states still get the `--state` advice, and where one of those states holds two entries the message says `--state` cannot settle that one.
- A `pkit process new` that fails part-way no longer leaves a half-made process behind (#711). It writes the definition, the predicate stubs and the `package.yaml` registrations in turn, and a failure after the first write — a `commands:` block that is not a mapping, say, which only the last write finds — used to strand a definition plus unregistered stubs, after which the one-shot refusal blocked every re-run. The run now takes itself back on any failure, interrupts included: every file and directory it created is removed, `package.yaml` is restored byte for byte, nothing that existed before is touched, and the re-run works. The written definition is also re-linted, as `couple` already did. A run killed outright, which cannot take itself back, is recognised on the re-run — the scaffold's definition with none of its commands registered — and the refusal names the exact `rm` that clears it. `pkit process hand-off` gets the same take-back, so a seam stub or registration that fails after its edit no longer leaves a contract naming seams nothing registers.
- `pkit process health --json` and `pkit process health --interpretation-only --json` now say which view they are, in a top-level `view` key: `full` and `interpretation-only` (#711). The two payloads differed only by keys the interpretation view leaves out, so a consumer reading `totals.get("missed", 0)` got `0` from it and concluded "clean", even when the full run had a miss and exited 1 — a false green on the machine surface. Read `view` before any total. The key is added to both payloads and no existing key is renamed or removed, so current consumers keep working.
- A detection stub scaffolded by `pkit process new` shows the JSON to print alone on its line (#1276). The note "(the `inferred` answer its detection declares)" used to follow the JSON on that line and read as part of it; it is now on the line below. Only stubs scaffolded from this release on change; stubs already on disk are left as they are, and what a predicate must print is unchanged.
- `pkit process health` says why a predicate could not be evaluated (#1244). A finding for a `candidates` or `resolve` seam that gave no answer named only a list of possible causes; it now names the predicate and how its run ended — `couldn't evaluate resolve predicate 'x': it exited 2` — or, for an answer without its list, what the answer lacked, and an upstream position no detection could place names its first unevaluated state and why. What the predicate wrote on standard error follows under `the predicate said:`, bounded and stripped of escape sequences, and each entry of an `indeterminate` list in `--json` (both views) gains a `stderr_tail` key beside `reason`, null when there is nothing to show. A validator's no-answer finding and an inert command filler's reason, for a command that exited, show the runner's shared tail of what it said on standard error — its last lines, bounded, escape sequences and control characters removed, joined on the one line with ` | ` — where they showed its last line raw. No verdict, total or exit code changes.
- Copying a source tree into a project leaves out Python's caches — any `__pycache__` directory and any compiled `.pyc` or `.pyo` file the source holds (#1325). Importing a script of the source in place writes them there, so `pkit init`, `pkit sync`, and a capability's install and refresh used to copy whatever had run in the source before them; what a project receives no longer depends on it. One rule decides it for every copy path.
- `pkit friction check --all` sees a change made by a merge that takes a file whole from one side (#1336) — a fix to the check's verdict in a rare history. git's combined diff lists a path for a merge only where the merge differs from every parent, so a merge resolved with `-s ours`, or a conflict resolved to one parent's file, lists nothing for the file it took. Where such a merge brought a file back to what it was before an artefact's revalidation, or removed it, and no commit since touched it, the check read no change, though the change check on that merge asked. Where a merge lies between an answering state and HEAD, the state's trees are now compared with HEAD's for every file an anchor stands on, so the artefact is reported stale, dated from that merge; and debt is dated along the lines that carry HEAD's file, so an edit such a merge discarded no longer dates it. `pkit friction debt`, `explain` and `record-status` follow, and `explain` names the file behind such a merge.
- `pkit init` ends by recommending the install the CLI reference recommends (#1376). It recommended a symlink to the `pkit` of a source checkout. When `init` ran from a `uv tool` install, that symlink pointed inside the tool's own environment. A test now holds `init`'s advice and the CLI reference's to the same command.
- The rule-set pass of `pkit validate` and the friction pass find the same sections in a rule-set file (#1372). Both now read a body's headings one way, with fenced code skipped. Before, the friction pass read a heading inside fenced code as a heading. It could take such a heading for an entry's section, or end a section early at it, as at a `# comment` line in a shell example. This holds for every collection file, not only rule sets. A heading such as `RS-DOC-001-draft` no longer counts as the section of `RS-DOC-001` in validation, as it never did for friction. Validation now reports it as a heading that opens like a rule id but is not one.
- `pkit upgrade` takes the latest release from the release tags only (#1472). It counted a pre-release or dev release tag as a release, so a pre-release tagged above the latest release would be the version the tool step installed and the version a pinned project's pin was raised to. It now skips such a tag, testing each tag as the release step tests a release version, so what it installs or pins is a release `pkit pin` accepts. ([#1477])
- `pkit upgrade` installs a newer pkit tool only when the running pkit is the `uv`-installed tool (#1473). Run through `uv run`, a pip install, or a `uvx` that uv serves from its cache, it used to install the `uv` tool at a terminal and hand the upgrade to whichever `pkit` the `PATH` found, overriding the version the operator chose. Now it says it is not the installed tool, prints the newer release and its install command, and goes on under the pkit that started it. When `uv` cannot say where its tools live, it prints the command too. And when the install succeeds but the hand-over fails, because `pkit` cannot be started or the `pkit` the `PATH` runs first is another, older pkit, the upgrade stops before it syncs or pins anything and names the command to re-run. It used to go on under the old pkit and pin the project at the old release. ([#1481])
- `pkit validate` names the right shape when it refuses a capability's place or folder of held documents that is not an object (#1411). A place's message gave `{path, location?}`, though the package schema's place is `{path, location?, type?, description?}`. A held folder's message gave the same place shape, though a held folder is `{location, path, description?}`, and its closing words gave `{location, path}`. Each message now gives its own shape. ([#1510])
- Choosing `override` on a naming collision at `pkit capabilities install` now replaces the project's artefact with the capability's, as COR-017 says (#1528). Before, the install copied the capability's skill or agent beside the project's, and the project's still deployed. Now the project's own artefact of that name is removed from `.pkit/skills/project/` or `.pkit/agents/project/`, so it no longer comes first at deploy. Git keeps the removed artefact. While git does not hold it as it stands, `override` is refused and asked again. An artefact that core or another capability ships is left in place, and the deploy's name precedence picks among them. Before it asks, the prompt names each artefact `override` would remove and each it would leave. `pkit capabilities upgrade --interactive` overrides the same way. `skip` and `inspect` are unchanged. ([#1550])
- Validation's decisions member reports nothing on a synced copy of a record in an adopter's project, as COR-055 point 1 asks (#1542). The front-matter check and the id check of `pkit validate` and `pkit decisions validate` leave out a core record, or a kit-shipped capability's, as the narration warnings and the point checks already did. A synced copy is still read for its id, so a project's record that takes a synced copy's id is reported, on the project's record. The count of records checked is the project's own. ([#1556])
- `pkit new decision` numbers a new record past every record the default branch holds in the namespace's folder, as well as past the working tree's, as COR-054 point 3 asks (#1501). A branch cut before another branch's record merged no longer takes that record's number again, once the default branch is fetched. This holds for every namespace: core, project, ADR and each capability's DEC. `$PKIT_CHECK_BASE` never changes the number. Where the default branch cannot be read, the command says so on standard error, with the reason and any fixes, and numbers past the working tree alone. It also warns when it read the default branch from the local branch, in a repository with no remote. When the default branch has no commit yet, a line on standard output says so, before the `Stamped:` line. ([#1590])
- `pkit upgrade` and `pkit sync` no longer copy the core `lifecycle` area over a project's own area of that name. A project at a release before v1.149.0, which first shipped the core area, could create its own at `.pkit/lifecycle/`. Both commands now stop before they write anything, under `--dry-run` too, and nothing overrides the stop, `--force` included. The core area holds `ownership.py`, and a project's own area does not, which is how the two are told apart. The message names the path, the running release and the ways on. Move the project's area to another name, then run the command again. After an upgrade's stop, an unpinned project can stay at its release with `pkit pin`. Where the core area has lost `ownership.py`, restore that file from git. Until #1600 builds the full check, no release adds a core area or a core adapter. ([#1589])
- Where git cannot run, or cannot read the branches here, such as outside a git repository, every reader of the default branch says so in plain words: the default branch cannot be read because git cannot read the branches here, with git's own reason, or because git could not be run. It no longer says there is no remote and no local branch, or offers to declare another name or a base in their place. Only the message changes.
- A rule that fills an extension point binds only where the rule it fills binds, as COR-051 point 7 says, also when that rule is itself a fill (#1616). Take an accepted rule that fills a point of a proposed rule. It binds nowhere, so a rule that fills a point it offers binds nowhere either. Under `--preview` such a fill binds only where the rule it fills would bind, marked as previewed, and a fill that binds without the preview is never marked. A fill conflict at a point of a rule that binds nowhere is no validation error. Every reader of the walk gives this answer: `pkit rule-sets reach`, `pkit rule-sets altered`, validation's `rule-sets` member, and a component's check that reads `pkit rule-sets reach --json`, such as the living-docs validator.
- `pkit report`'s environment block reads a capability's version as every other reader of a capability's record reads it (ADR-065 point 2): the manifest at the capability's root, then the one under its `project/`, then its installed `package.yaml`. A shipped capability with no manifest used to show as "unknown". It now shows its installed package's version. "unknown" is left for a capability none of the three gives a version for. A manifest that does not parse no longer stops the report: the block reads past it. A capability `package.yaml` whose `schema_version` is not a number reads as unreadable, as one that does not parse does, where it raised a Python error.
- A capability registered as `externally-sourced` is a synced copy, as a `kit-shipped` one is (COR-041, COR-050 point 14). Its files outside its `project/` tier are never a documentation place: a declared place that matches them fails `synced-place` in `pkit validate`. `pkit decisions validate` and `pkit decisions check-diff` leave its records to the repository that authors them. This holds in every repository, the methodology's source included, since such a capability is authored elsewhere. COR-010 says that renaming or removing a section's heading in a document the methodology or a component ships needs no migration, since no project that receives the document can anchor its sections.
- `pkit permissions sandbox enable --dangerously-allow-unconfined` no longer writes `failIfUnavailable: false` into the committed `.claude/settings.json`. The fail-open goes to the gitignored `.claude/settings.local.json`, for this machine only, and the committed floor stays `true`. The run warns when the repository does not ignore that file. A run without the flag, `setup autonomy` included, removes any `failIfUnavailable` other than `true` from `settings.local.json`, a hand-set one too. A committed `false` that an earlier version wrote is reported by `sandbox status` and `overview`, with the `sandbox enable` step that moves it, and `setup autonomy` restores it. On macOS, where the box is gated off, the flag records the local `false` too. It has no effect while the box is off, takes effect if the box is turned on later, and is reported meanwhile. There a committed value is corrected, but a missing one is not added.
- `pkit friction check --all` takes about half the time it did on a large repository, with the same answer, so a command that reads it, such as living-docs' `fill-doc-check`, answers well within the 30-second bound.
- `pkit friction since` finds a `revalidated` block or a `revisited` list under the keys discovery reads it by, each rendered as text, never a key merely written the same (#1750). Where two keys on its path are read alike, as `1` and `"1"` are, or a mapping on the path holds a merge key (`<<` or `!!merge`), the file now lies outside, with why, where a change to text no tool reads could count as inside. Front matter holding a value YAML cannot build, such as the key `? [{a: 1}]` or the value `!!int x`, now reads as front matter that does not parse, where it raised an error.
- `pkit release apply` no longer writes a `none` changeset's note to the changelog when another changeset moves its component, as PRJ-002 decides. `pkit release plan` no longer lists that note either.
- The core rules now tell an agent that it writes the revisit entries its own change owes, again after a merge conflict, that a reviewer checks them, and that any other entry is a person's decision.
- Each migration folder numbers its scripts once. The runner runs a folder's scripts in the order of their names, so two scripts of one number ran in the order their slugs gave. `pkit migrations check-diff` now fails a script a change adds, or moves in, whose number another script in its folder holds, including one the base took since the branch left it, and names the number to take. A script your project receives by sync is not judged. `pkit release plan` now also warns of an adapter's or a capability's migration folder that does not match the version the release computes for it, as it did for the backbone's.
- `pkit new decision <capability>` refuses a capability whose `decisions/` folder arrives in the project as a synced copy, as the lifecycle's ownership predicate answers (`is_synced_copy`). That is a kit-shipped capability in a project that is not the methodology's source repository, or an externally-sourced one in any project. The next sync overwrites what a project writes there, so the command now writes nothing, exits 1, and names `pkit new decision project <slug>` and `pkit new decision adr <slug>` as where the project's own decision goes. A capability incubated in the project, and a kit-shipped one in the methodology's source repository, stamps as before.
- `pkit release merge` now names the backbone's own landing, `pkit pull-request land <n> --head <sha> --subject <title>`, when it refuses a pull request whose head branch is no `release/` branch. It named the project-management capability's `merge-pr`, which a project may not have installed (COR-014). The help of `pkit release merge` names the same command.
- Every `pkit new` command that stamps into a folder a sync may write now refuses one whose files the project receives as synced copies, as `pkit new decision <capability>` did alone before. That covers `new decision core`, `new agent` and `new storyboard` for a core or capability agent, `new schema` for the core schemas area or a capability, and `new migration` for the backbone or a capability. Each asks the lifecycle's ownership predicate (`is_synced_copy`) before it writes anything, in a dry run too. Where the folder holds synced copies, the next sync would overwrite what the project writes there, so the command writes nothing, exits 1, and names the folder and where the project's own artefact goes. In the methodology's source repository, and for a capability incubated in the project, each stamps as before.
- A question that guards a person's decision is now asked only at a terminal, so an answer piped into stdin, as in `echo y | pkit …`, is never read (#2003). Off a terminal without `--yes`, `release apply`, `settings consolidate` and `capabilities uninstall`, both its `--purge` and its unregister in the methodology's source, refuse, write nothing and name the command with `--yes`. A report's post and `report submit` refuse too, and `--yes` still stages a report without posting it. `report inbox --resolved` lists the reports and then refuses to close any. `visibility untrack` and `visibility private` leave the footprint tracked. A naming collision in `capabilities install` or `capabilities upgrade --interactive` is refused before anything is written. `permissions setup autonomy` leaves the overrides in place unless `--remove-overrides` is given. At a terminal, and with `--yes`, each command works as before.
- The commands `pkit process new` and `pkit permissions scaffold` now refuse a capability whose files the project receives as synced copies, as every `pkit new` command does. Each asks the lifecycle's ownership predicate (`is_synced_copy`) before it writes anything, `process new` in a dry run too. Where the capability's folder holds synced copies, the next sync would overwrite what the project writes there, so the command writes nothing, exits 1, and names the folder and where the project's own artefact goes: a capability incubated in the project, and for a grant of the project's own, `pkit permissions grant`. In the methodology's source repository, and for a capability incubated in the project, each writes as before.
- The commands `pkit process couple` and `pkit process hand-off` now refuse a process definition whose capability the project receives as synced copies. Each asks the lifecycle's ownership predicate (`is_synced_copy`) before it writes anything, in a dry run too. The next sync would overwrite the entry, the contract, the seam stubs and their registrations written there, so the command writes nothing, exits 1, and names the folder. It names a capability incubated in the project as where the project's own process goes, which may name the refused one as its upstream. In the methodology's source repository, and for a capability incubated in the project, each writes as before.
- The help of `pkit process graph` now says how its presets combine, as the command does it (#2038). It said every preset AND-combines with the atomic filters. But `--advisory`, `--connectors`, `--declared` and `--derived` each add the relations, mode or source they name to those `--relation`, `--mode`, `--source` or another of them names, so `--connectors --mode pull` keeps both modes. The command reference already said so. What the command keeps is unchanged.
- The next steps `pkit init` prints now hold for the project it set up (#2083). It leaves out the install of pkit where a `pkit` is installed on the PATH already. A `pkit` that `uv run` or `uvx` puts there for one run does not count. It names no capability's configuration, since first install installs no capability, and names the `.claude/settings.json.pre-pkit` backup only where it made one. The `.gitignore` step says why the lines are left to the project: pkit never writes the project's own `.gitignore` (ADR-009). The steps are numbered in order whichever are printed.
- A collection entry removed from its file and added back starts its answers again, as COR-050 point 3 says its history does (#2063). An answer file added before the entry was last added answers it no more. `pkit friction check`, `check --all`, `debt` and `explain` give the entry no live answer from before, and judge it from where it was added back. The check counts such a file among those a prune would remove. A document deleted and restored is read the same way, and an entry its file kept keeps its answers.
- `pkit status` lists each skill and agent under `.claude/` under where pkit deployed it from: core, each capability by name, then the project namespace (#2085). A capability's skills and agents, such as `pm` and `project-manager`, were listed as "user-managed (untouched by pkit)". Now only what the project added itself is listed as the project's own, which pkit does not deploy. A marked agent copy that nothing ships any longer is listed apart, as the next `pkit sync` removes it. The adapter section's labels change with it: `skills` and `agents`, each group reading `<n> deployed from <where>:`. No migration.
- `pkit friction revalidate`, `defer` and `revisit` follow a moved artefact, as COR-062 point 10 asks (#2108). The answer they write names, in `read`, the answer files on the artefact at every path its file had. They follow a rename in HEAD's history and a move not yet committed, as the change check does. A move answered through the writers alone now passes the change check, with no answer file named in `read` by hand.
- The authoring skills no longer send their reader to sections of project-kit's own `CONTRIBUTING.md`, which no install writes, nor to project-kit's example projects (#2156). The disciplines a decision record follows now live in the decisions README, `.pkit/decisions/README.md`, under "Writing a record". Every record leads with meaning, holds principles, not inventory, and names the role rather than its current holder (COR-046). An ADR adds architectural fit, and a core record, the methodology maintainers' case, the axiom discipline and project-neutrality. `decision-author` leads with the records a project writes, PRJ and ADR, and picks a record's kind by COR-025's scope test. `agent-author`, `capability-author` and the `schema` skill point to the README too. The axiom discipline now binds core records and the schemas core ships only, no longer a project's own PRJ records, capability records or schemas. No migration.
- The core agents `methodology-reviewer`, `critic` and `architect` no longer read project-kit's own `CONTRIBUTING.md`, which no install writes, and point to the decisions README's "Writing a record" instead (#2167). The `capability-author` and `report-author` skills no longer cite records of project-kit's own project, which no install writes either. `capability-author` points to the project's own version-bump policy, by the core rules' rule 10, and `report-author` to the CLI reference's "Report commands". No migration.
- Right after `pkit upgrade` moves a project's answers into answer files, and before the upgrade is committed, `pkit friction check --all`, `debt` and `explain` no longer ask you to run the move again (#2186). They read the last commit, which still holds the answers in their blocks. Where the working tree holds the answer files the move wrote, `moved-<hash>.yaml`, and the last commit does not, they now say the move has run and the upgrade is to be committed with `git commit`. `pkit validate` gives the same words where a component runs one of them, as living-docs' documentation check does.
- After a merge, `pkit release merge` switches its clone to the release PR's base and pulls it only where the clone is on the PR's head branch with a clean working tree, as project-management's merge verbs do. A clone on another branch or a detached HEAD, or with uncommitted changes, untracked files included, is now left where it is, nothing pulled, and the merged branch's local copy is kept, said in one line. Before, the clean-up checked out the base whatever the clone held, so a clone in the middle of other work was moved onto it with its changes, and its next commit could land there. A clean clone already on the base is fast-forwarded with no switch, said in one line, and a fast-forward that fails warns and changes nothing. Where the switch fails, the clone stays on the head branch and its local copy is kept, with a warning, rather than a delete that cannot succeed. The deletion of the head branch on GitHub is unchanged.
- The core rules state two built parts as built (#2201). Rule 7 no longer says `pkit migrations check-diff` runs "when available". Rule 12 names `deploy-agents.sh` beside `deploy-skills.sh` as the Claude Code adapter's deploy primitives, no longer as future, and says that skills deploy as symlinks and agents as copies with their placeholders resolved. Neither rule asks anything new. No migration.
- `pkit permissions setup autonomy` now reports a local `autoAllowBashIfSandboxed: false` in `.claude/settings.local.json` as an override of the autonomy posture, beside the other overrides it detects. That local value, which Claude Code's `/sandbox` panel writes, wins over the committed `true`, so the box confined while every command still prompted and setup reported success. Removing it takes the same consent as the other overrides, a yes at a terminal or `--remove-overrides`, and keeps the rest of the local file. On macOS, where the box is intended off, it is not reported.
- In a project made private with `pkit visibility private`, install, sync and upgrade now render pkit's region of `.git/info/exclude` again from the footprint, so a component installed since then no longer reaches the shared tree. The rest of the file is kept, and a project that is not private is left as it is.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — An optional overlay category set with no value (a bare `project-conventions:` key) is now reported as a warning when agents deploy, instead of being dropped silently; the agent still deploys without it (#916). ([#940])
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — When a capability ships an agent with the same name as a core agent, `deploy-agents.sh` deploys the capability's, as the agents README's name-collision precedence has always said: the project's agent, then an installed capability's, then core's. It used to look in core before the capabilities, so a capability's specialisation of a core agent never deployed — not even after `override` at the install collision prompt.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — `deploy-skills.sh` deploys a capability's skills only when the capability is registered in `.pkit/manifest.yaml`, not for every directory under `.pkit/capabilities/`, and its stale-removal pass drops the deployed skills of a capability that is no longer registered. A capability unregistered in place keeps its subtree on disk, so the deploy that follows — the uninstall's own, or the next `pkit sync` — used to put its skills back. A skill that resolves to a flat source also no longer carries the sub-procedure links of a lower-precedence composite skill of the same name. Adopter content under `.claude/skills/` — anything but the deploy's own symlinks into `.pkit/` — is never touched.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — `merge-settings.sh` keeps every key under `permissions`. It used to rebuild `permissions` from `allow` and `deny` alone, so every `pkit init`, `pkit sync`, `pkit upgrade` and `pkit merge` erased `ask`, `defaultMode`, `additionalDirectories` and any other key there from `.claude/settings.json`, and ignored them in the project's additions. Now `ask` and `additionalDirectories` are unioned across the baseline, the additions and the existing file, without duplicates, as `allow` is. Every other key, such as `defaultMode`, takes the precedence top-level keys have: the additions over the baseline, and the existing file over both. A file with only `allow` and `deny` merges byte for byte as before. A capability overlay still cannot set anything under `permissions`, and a managed region still replaces `permissions` whole. The adapter README documents each key under "How adopters use this adapter". ([#1466])
- **demo-recording 0.2.0** — The `demo-recording` skill declares COR-016, which its storyboard-author operation cites, and names the recording and control window bounds as `record.yaml` keys rather than hook-shaped tokens, so `pkit validate --only refs` reports no drift warnings on it.
- **demo-recording 0.2.0** — A fenced block whose tag no loaded plugin provides is a warning, not an error, as DEC-001 decides (#1904). `pkit demo-recording validate` lists it with its step and line under the warnings and exits 0, and `run` and `record` go on past it, skipping that block with a warning. A block whose directive content is wrong is still an error. The README and the `hook-author` skill now say that a `--no-recording` run runs no after-record hook, so `DCR_VIDEO_PATH` always names the saved video. The README names Python 3.9 as the floor, and says that a `ready` directive that times out warns and goes on.
- **evidence 0.5.2** — The `evidence` skill declares COR-023, whose binding rule its add operation applies, and drops a background citation of COR-007, so `pkit validate --only refs` reports no drift warnings on it.
- **evidence 0.5.2** — The evidence check does what DEC-002 and DEC-003 decide (#1910). Run on a scope, it leaves out each directory below that holds an `evidence.yaml` of its own, so a nested scope's citations no longer resolve against the outer records. It skips a fenced code block whose fence stands indented in a list item or set in a block quote. A record with no `id` is a finding rather than a crash, and an `evidence.yaml` with no top-level `records` list fails the check. Named a directory that holds no `evidence.yaml`, it checks the scope that directory lies in, and errors only where no directory above holds one. The schema's `id` pattern now lets an id start with a digit, as DEC-001's slug does. The skills and the README say the same.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs fill-doc-check` gives a `page-stale` obligation for a page the whole-repository friction check reports stale, no longer for one it reports deferred: a deferral is the page's answer (project-management DEC-053 point 4), so a deferred page owes the documentation check nothing, and `pkit friction debt` keeps reporting it until the page is revalidated. Before, the check refused every pull request that left a deferred page untouched. A stale page's description names only its changed anchors, not the ones it has deferred.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `fill-doc-check` no longer answers "nothing owed" when git cannot read a commit that is there, such as a missing object or a checkout git refuses as unsafe. It used to ask git itself whether HEAD named a commit and took any failure for a repository with no commit. It now reads that from the backbone, `head` in `pkit repository base --json`, and asks git nothing of its own. Where git cannot read HEAD it exits 1 and prints nothing, so a documentation check that reads it reports itself unresolved. A repository with no commit yet still owes nothing. It needs the backbone this release ships, the first whose reading carries `head`.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `fill-doc-check` gives no answer when the friction check could not do its work on a page. It read only `unreachable` as not judged, so a page in any other state that is not `stale` owed nothing, as a current page does. Now a page whose anchor's resolver gave no answer (`no-answer`), or in a state this capability does not know, exits 1, naming the page and why, so a documentation check that reads it reports itself unresolved rather than pass on fewer obligations. A page left unjudged only by an anchor whose kind nothing installed resolves (`unresolved-kind`) owes what its other anchors owe — a `page-stale` obligation when one of them changed — and the human view names the anchor: such an anchor is the project's to mend, which `pkit validate` reports under `friction` and the change check fails where a pull request adds it, so it never makes every later check fail.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs fill-doc-check` runs its three readings at once, so with the backbone of this release it answers well within the 30-second bound on a large repository, with the same answer, and `pkit validate` no longer fails for want of it.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — `pkit living-docs validate` no longer fails with `assignment-missing` on a place of rule sets outside every documentation root, such as the old `rule-sets/` folder `pkit config set docs.internal` declares to keep a project's rule sets found. Such a place joins a space only when the project assigns it one (DEC-001 point 1). Until then it gives no space and owes no assignment, so its `README.md` is neither a page nor an unclassified document. A document there that declares a page kind is reported, never failed, naming the assignment that would make it a page. A place of rule sets that gives a type owes an assignment as any other. The place is read from the backbone's answer, `pkit friction artefacts --json`, where it is declared to hold rule sets, so it needs the backbone this release ships.
- **living-docs 0.2.0** — The `living-docs` agent's reader-review reads the reading-evidence point, as DEC-001 points 6 and 7 say it does. It resolves `pkit::documentation:reading-evidence` and takes the entries for the page, each the result of an executed check that followed it. It reads them as advice that never replaces its own reading, and its findings record names each entry it read, by its `id`. Where the point holds no entry for the page, or does not resolve, the review runs as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Parent-ref recognition now honours the DEC-013 `Integration: integration/<slug>` marker (#763). The marker is defined as the first body line on a marked descendant — above the parent-ref, no blank line between — but every parent-ref recognizer read the parent-ref off the literal first content line, so a marked descendant hard-rejected with "first body line does not match the parent-ref form". That made DEC-013's marker propagation unusable: the project-manager could not stamp the marker onto descendants without breaking their validation (the originating symptom was `edit-issue` rejecting every marked Feature/Task). A single shared helper (`strip_integration_marker`) now drops a leading valid marker before the parent-ref is read, consumed uniformly by validate-issue, create-issue, edit-issue, move-issue, close-issue, containment, show-tree, show-issue, and the inference layer. The helper's pattern is kept byte-identical to `body-format.yaml`'s `integration_marker.pattern` (drift-guarded by a test), so a line that only *attempts* the marker but is malformed is left in place and still hard-rejects — validate-issue and edit-issue now surface that as a precise `body.integration-marker` finding naming the expected form, rather than the misleading `body.parent-ref` message the fall-through produced.
