The two schemas that carry the marker's slug (`body-format.yaml`, `git-conventions.yaml`) are reconciled to one kebab shape (they previously disagreed on the trailing character and single-character slugs).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A migration retires state seeded into adopters from the source project's `project/` tree (#814). The seeding itself was stopped in the backbone (#812), but that removed nothing already on disk — the copy primitive never prunes adopter-owned paths, by design — so without this an adopter who installed earlier keeps every seeded file and behaves differently from a fresh install of the same version. It acts only on a POSITIVE signal of kit provenance, following the precedent the reviewer-rename migration set in the same directory: a lifecycle journal byte-identical to one this version shipped is provably foreign and is removed (a foreign journal is actively harmful — `pm history <N>` reads it as this project's history). Where no signal is reliably available the file is reported and left alone. A bootstrap stamp is reported rather than removed even though a foreign one is genuinely harmful: judging it needs the same normaliser that wrote the value (case-folding, userinfo, ports, ssh:// forms), a migration is shell-only by convention here, and a divergent re-implementation would delete stamps that are legitimately the adopter's — while the prerequisite gate already refuses a foreign-repo stamp on every gated verb using that canonical normaliser, so removing it here would be tidiness rather than protection. The default-agent activation file is byte-identical whether it was seeded or the adopter ran `enable-default-agent`, and enabling leaves no record, so removing it could silently disable a feature they chose — the migration prints the remediation instead; and `config.yaml` / `workstreams.yaml` are kept because they may have been edited on top of the seed and their values are frequently correct anyway, so deleting an adopter's only working config to purify its provenance would do more harm than the residue. Known limit, stated rather than hidden: the embedded hashes are the set this build shipped, which is not a property of the repo — the journals are git-ignored while packaging includes the tree wholesale, so what ships depends on the build machine's working tree. An adopter may therefore hold journals this migration has no digest for, which is reported rather than removed.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The READ and presence gates now ask `_lib/axis_carriage` which substrate carries an axis, closing the reader half of the ordering inversion DEC-051 settles. The writers were rewired first; until now `validate-issue` and `pre-check` still resolved board-versus-label BEFORE consulting the adopter's substrate-map, so under a configured board `priority` and `workstream` never reached the seam at all and the adopter's own binding was not consulted by the reader — the reported failure (#708) seen from the other side. The `type` axis already resolved in the pinned order in both files and was the in-tree oracle; it now asks the same accessor as the other two rather than a second predicate that happens to agree.
What changes for an adopter whose map binds an axis to their own labels under a configured board: `validate-issue` finds the label the writer actually wrote (their `P0`, not the kit's `priority:High`) and hard-rejects its ABSENCE — a state that previously passed clean because the gate did not run; `pre-check` reports the kit-label check per axis, so one axis can be board-carried while another is label-bound, which the old combined "board configured" skip line could not express, and the `state:*` check now SAYS why it skipped instead of being skipped silently by its caller.
Where an axis resolves to the BOARD, the presence gate demands no label and does not claim the value is missing: it reports `classification.<axis>.unverified` at `warning` — the severity `board_membership.unverified` already uses for the same distinction — saying the value is board-carried and was not read here. This commit deliberately does NOT read the board field; that read and its failure semantics belong to a record still under review. No new severity, verdict token or exit code: a board adopter's exit status is unchanged.
Two remaining map-blind readers are fixed with it. `promote-issue`'s idempotent-skip read its state through the kit's `state:` prefix regardless of substrate — so a `label`-bound state never matched and an already-promoted issue failed with "no transition backlog → backlog", while under a board a STALE kit label was trusted and silently skipped a promotion that had not happened. And `pkit pm history --check-drift` scanned the timeline for the kit prefix alone, showing zero state changes on a repo full of them; it now reads the adopter's own vocabulary, and where no label carries state at all it reports that the check could not run rather than printing a clean bill of health off an empty scan.
Board MEMBERSHIP (is this issue on the board, DEC-019) and board IDENTITY stay with the flag, unrouted through carriage — routing them would silently drop the membership requirement for any board adopter with a single label binding. ([#712])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `back-fill --emit-script`'s value guards now fail **closed** (#816). The milestone and board-field re-reads ended in `|| echo ""`, which collapsed a FAILED read and a genuinely-empty value into the same empty string — and the empty branch is the branch that WRITES, so a rate limit, a network drop, or a token without the `project` scope silently overwrote a value the script never read. Both fragments now use `if ! current=$(...)`, which separates a non-zero `gh` exit from a successful empty read, and the board-field re-read's `--jq` raises on every response that does not positively confirm a value (a populated GraphQL `errors` array; a null `data`/`node`/`fieldValues` at any hop) — mirroring the rule `--apply` already applied. An unreadable **board** is an ERROR naming the issue, the field and the likely causes, and it stops the run: a board that will not answer is a broken tool, not a missing value. An unreadable milestone or label skips that one write and the run continues, as `--apply` does. A board that *answers* "unset" is still a genuine missing value and is still written, and a project with no board emits no board read at all, so a milestone-only back-fill gains no new failure mode. The emitted header's "guard fails OPEN" admission is retracted, and its claim that every substrate skips drift is corrected: the milestone write seeds one milestone corpus-wide and does write over a differing one, as `--apply` does.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The per-reviewer merge-gate override shipped in 0.54.0 reported itself dishonestly in three states, which is the one thing DEC-050 exists to get right (#755). `passed_via`, the gate's refusal text and the audit comment each re-derived "was this slot overridden?" and disagreed: a reviewer with a genuine fresh APPROVED who was also named in `--bypass-reviewer` was reported as `satisfied-by-override`; the audit comment asserted "every other required reviewer still gated" even when the override was redundant or when EVERY slot was overridden; and the audit's "state at override time" was computed with the permissive verdict reader, so the PR author's own self-approval — which the gate correctly refuses to count — described what the override waived (ADR-042's named anti-pattern). One per-reviewer satisfaction record now backs all three surfaces, and the audit's scope sentence is derived from it rather than asserted. Also fixed: `--bypass` silently swallowed `--bypass-reviewer` (skipping the DEC-050 name validation and the audit) and is now refused as incoherent; the idempotency stamp is keyed to HEAD as DEC-050 scopes it, and hashes the reviewer name so a name containing `-->` can no longer break the marker; a repeated `--bypass-reviewer X X` posts one audit; the all-slots nudge survives every refusal path; and the audit renders DEC-049's canonical line from the schema template through a shared `_lib/audit.py`. That comment is posted **irrespective of `audit.projection`**, and writes no journal entry: the engine journal has no entry shape for a gate override yet, so honouring `off` here would delete the override trail rather than quieten it. `--bypass-reason` on `done-work` is superseded by `--bypass-reviewer-reason` (the old spelling retained as a deprecated alias) to end the collision with its meaning on `move-issue`/`promote-issue`; the released spelling stays as a deprecated alias sharing one destination (no second code path), warns, and refuses if both are supplied.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Structural-type inference now agrees across every command that reports it (#793). An issue's type is read from its title prefix, and that read was implemented nine times across seven divergent bodies — so the same title resolved differently depending on which command you asked. A kind-prefixed Task resolved under `move-issue` and returned nothing under `show-issue`, `show-tree`, and `close-issue`, which is most of the issues an adopter files: every `[Bug]`, `[Docs]`, `[Test]`, `[Refactor]`, and `[Chore]` issue displayed as `<unrecognised prefix>` and rendered as `[?]` in the hierarchy, despite those prefixes being exactly what the title schema prescribes for a Task carrying that kind label. The resolver is now a single implementation in `_lib/structural_type.py` consulting four vocabularies in a fixed precedence — adopter prefixes under a brownfield substrate map (which short-circuits, so a map binding the type axis elsewhere still resolves to nothing rather than falling through to the kit's vocabulary), the kit's own structural prefixes, the kind-driven prefixes from `classification.yaml`, and the `type:*` label as a fallback for a title whose prefix was edited away. Each vocabulary beyond the kit prefixes is opt-in per caller, so wiring the nine call sites through one implementation preserved each one's behaviour; the fix is the four commands that now supply the classification schema they previously never loaded. Two callers deliberately abstain and are unchanged: `create-issue` infers a *parent's* type, where containers carry no kind prefix, and `validate-issue` stays brownfield-strict so a title matching no adopter prefix still resolves to nothing. This is the parity pass `move-issue`'s own docstring called for and nobody performed.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `start-work` now creates the Task's branch off the correct **base** rather than the incidental checked-out `HEAD` (#835). Previously it ran a bare `git checkout -b <name>`, so running `start-work` while another feature branch was checked out produced a branch **stacked on that branch**, carrying its commits — which, on squash-merge, would land unrelated work on `main` under the Task's subject and empty the other branch's PR. The base is now resolved to the adopter's `default_branch` (default `main`), or to the DEC-013 `integration/<slug>` branch when the issue body carries the integration marker, and is fetched from `origin` so the branch is cut from an up-to-date ref (degrading to the local ref when offline). Base resolution takes only `(config, body)` — never `HEAD` — so the checked-out branch can no longer leak into it.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `project-manager` agent now dispatches reviewer helpers **without** a `name`, closing a silent review-truncation defect (#851). Under Claude Code's experimental agent-teams feature, passing a `name` to the `Agent` tool promotes the spawn from a fire-once subagent to a persistent teammate, and a teammate's result reaches the lead **hard-truncated at 4064 characters** (terminated by `[result truncated — ask the agent for the rest via SendMessage]`). The remainder is reachable only via `SendMessage`, which the agent's tool grant does not include. Because reviewer output routinely runs 8–12 KB, more than half of every `critic` / `architect` review was being discarded — and the surviving prefix reads as a complete answer, so the loss was invisible. Measured on three named teammates in one real session, each capped at exactly 4064 characters. The agent body states the discipline as a **capability check rather than a remembered convention**: if the `Agent` tool exposes a `name` parameter, teams is enabled and the hazard is live; if it does not, naming is not expressible — and the correct action (dispatch unnamed) is identical either way. No settings are written and no harness toggle is authored. This is **standing discipline**, not an interim measure: a harness-level check can detect that agent teams is enabled, but it cannot stop a dispatcher from naming a spawn — only the acting agent at the moment of dispatch can.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The pre-check title-prefix alignment check no longer blocks pm operations over already-existing issue titles — an unrecognised or missing prefix is now an advisory warning — and issues filed through `pkit report` are excluded from the sample instead of being counted as drift (#852).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Reconstructing a saved back-fill plan no longer collapses two intents of the same kind (#826). Intents were keyed by change `kind` alone, so an adopter with two `set-board-field` hooks — one seeding Priority, one Workstream — had the second intent's changes recover the FIRST intent's target, writing a wrong value at apply time after a plan that reviewed correctly. The same held for two `assign-milestone` hooks. Intents are now keyed by the write they declare (the milestone title; the field id plus the value written there; the axis for a label write), derived on the proposed side from the plan's exact reviewed argv, and the degenerate `(kind, "")` arm that preserved the old behaviour for the two hook-driven kinds is gone. **Plans saved before this fix apply correctly rather than being refused** — nothing was added to the plan document, so the key is derived from what every `schema_version: 1` plan already carries. A proposed entry no declared intent accounts for is now reported as BLOCKED rather than applied against an unrecoverable target.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The project-manager agent no longer assumes it cannot dispatch reviewers when running as a subagent: the harness allows nesting up to a configured depth, so the agent now checks for its `Agent` tool and only falls back to recommending a reviewer when it is at the limit (#853).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Containers can be closed again in trackers with more than 500 issues (#846). Resolving "does this parent have open children?" meant downloading the whole tracker and filtering locally, with each consumer fetching its own copy and judging completeness differently — so one seam carried four ceilings, only one of which was checked. Past the smallest of them the close gate refused every container, correctly but permanently. The unchecked path in the same command — the hint listing which children to close, printed after that refusal — could omit still-open children while reading as the complete set; it decided nothing, but a short list served as a whole one is worth refusing over too. Acquisition now belongs to the containment seam, which enumerates to exhaustion and returns a completeness verdict with the child set; a gate holds on an incomplete answer instead of treating a short list as the whole one, and the hint is omitted rather than shown short. The seam also stops collapsing two different native-read outcomes: an endpoint that is absent means textual-only is the complete answer, while an endpoint that could not be reached does not — collapsing them let a transient error silently drop natively-linked children whose bodies carry no parent-ref line. A caller may still supply its own corpus, but must say whether it is complete.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A repository the caller cannot see is no longer mistaken for one without native sub-issues (#869). GitHub answers 404 both for an absent endpoint and for a repository you may not know exists, and the containment seam read that status as "this instance has no native sub-issues" — a determinate answer that let a close gate resolve a parent's children from body parent-refs alone, so any child linked natively but carrying no parent-ref line went unseen. The fault is quiet and selective rather than exotic: a fine-grained token missing `Issues: read`, or unauthorised SSO, returns 404 rather than 401 on a private repository, so public-repo work keeps succeeding while private repositories silently report no native children. No reading of the error text can separate the two causes, since both produce the same status and the same message, so the seam now asks the API: 410 and 422 still name feature-absence outright, while a 404 is attributed by probing the parent issue itself — visible parent means the endpoint really is absent, an unreachable one means the answer cannot be vouched for and the gate holds. One extra call, only when a read has already failed. The link-writing path routes through the same classifier, so a broken token there now reports a failure instead of announcing the feature unsupported and hiding the cause.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `show-tree` and the children-view refresh no longer present a partial answer as a complete one (#863). Both built their own issue corpus — `show-tree` at the operator's `--limit`, the refresh at a fixed 1000 rows — and neither could tell a truncated fetch from an exhaustive one, so a bounded tree looked identical to a whole one and a parent's children comment could omit children with no hint that anything was missing. Acquisition now goes through the containment seam, which reports whether it saw the whole tracker. `show-tree --limit` stays a view control with its default unchanged, and a render that struck it is marked partial in every format it emits — `[partial]` in text, a note in markdown, `"complete": false` in JSON — while a complete render carries no such mark, so the label carries information. The note names WHICH fact made the view short, because the remedies differ: a struck limit is fixed by raising it, while a child set the seam could not vouch for is not, and telling an operator otherwise sends them round the same loop. JSON gains `incomplete_reason` alongside the verdict for the same reason — a bare false sends a machine consumer back to guessing the cause. The children-view refresh takes the stricter line and refuses rather than publishing a partial list — including when the corpus was *filtered* rather than cut short, which the completeness verdict cannot see: `--state open` is complete for what it asked while every closed child is missing from it, so the default `show-tree --refresh-children-views` had been rewriting each parent's children comment with closed children silently dropped. that comment is the only parent-side view of children on trackers without native sub-issues and it carries no hedge of its own, so a stale view is better than a confidently wrong one. The same change fixes a failed fetch there rendering as an *empty* children list, which was indistinguishable from a parent that genuinely has none.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work` now transitions the issue right after the squash-merge and treats every local branch step as a warning, so a worktree on a detached HEAD or one whose `main` is checked out elsewhere no longer reports a landed merge as an error and no longer leaves the issue stranded at Review (#878). ([#881])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `merge-pr` now merges, deletes the remote head branch, and cleans up the local checkout the same way `done-work` does, so a worktree on a detached HEAD or one whose `main` is checked out elsewhere no longer turns a landed merge into an error; both verbs now return to the project's configured default branch rather than assuming `main` (#882).
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `show-tree` now marks its render partial when the PR fetch strikes `--limit`, not only the issue fetch (#906). A tracker with more PRs than the limit previously rendered as complete while PR-to-issue links and orphan-PR detection silently missed PRs. The `[partial]` label (text), the `**Partial view**` note (markdown) and `complete` / `incomplete_reason` (JSON) now name the PR half; when an issue-side cause also holds, both reasons are reported. `--refresh-children-views` is not blocked by a truncated PR list, since it writes from issues alone. ([#923])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A retried `move-issue --bypass` no longer posts a second audit comment. The comment is still posted before the label write, so the justification survives a failed move. It now ends with a hidden idempotency key (`<!-- pkit-audit-key: move-issue:<digest> -->`), hashed from the transition, the reason and the issue's engine-journal length, and a retry that finds its own key posts nothing. A different reason still posts, and so does the same transition made again after the move landed. Wrappers that compose over `move-issue`, such as `promote-issue`, inherit this. The capability README now describes how each audit comment behaves on a retry (#901). ([#924])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `validate-issue` now accepts a Task titled with a kind-driven prefix — `[Bug]`, `[Docs]`, `[Test]`, `[Refactor]`, `[Chore]` — instead of hard-rejecting it on `title.format`; the error lists every accepted prefix. It also reports a new `title.kind-prefix-mismatch` finding when a Task's prefix disagrees with its `type:*` label: a hard reject at `--phase create`, a warning at `--phase transition` (#895). ([#898])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The PR-opening verbs now target the base branch DEC-013 specifies for the closing issue (#903). `open-pr` ignored the issue's `Integration:` marker, and `create-draft` and `review-work` hardcoded `main` as their `--base` default, so a PR for integration-branch work targeted `main` even though `start-work` had cut its branch off `integration/<slug>` (the #835 symptom through another door). All four verbs now resolve the base through one shared resolver: an explicit `--base` wins; else the closing issue's `Integration:` marker names `integration/<slug>`; else the adopter's `default_branch`. `create-draft`'s "commits beyond base" gate measures against the same resolved base, and `review-work` prints the base it will use. ([#929])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Readers that scanned for the kit's own `<axis>:` label prefix now read through the substrate map, so an adopter whose map binds an axis to their own labels gets the value instead of silence. `context-workstream` reports a remapped workstream; `start-work` and `review-work` derive the branch prefix, and `open-pr` the PR type, from a remapped type label; and `validate-pr`'s PR-title type cross-check now runs for a remapped type instead of being skipped (it can now report a title/type mismatch it used to miss). Under a map that rebinds an axis, a leftover kit `<axis>:*` label no longer decides the value. Projects with no substrate map are unchanged (#910). ([#925])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `pm-reviewer` and `project-manager` agents now send code review to the installed code-review panel (when the software-engineering capability is installed) or the project's own reviewers. Before, they sent it to `software-engineer` / `qa-engineer`, which are producer agents that emit no verdicts, and `qa-engineer` is not shipped at all. The panel is named as optional, so project-management still does not depend on software-engineering being installed (#900). ([#937])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `pre-check`'s title-prefix alignment line no longer claims "all" sampled issues have recognised prefixes when some carry no prefix; it now states how many were recognised out of how many were sampled, and how many were set aside as prefix-less. The documented exit contract now says what the script always did: exit zero means no check failed — passed, skipped, or warned (#904). ([#935])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `back-fill --set` now exits non-zero when it could apply only some of the requested values (#915). Before, a run in which one `--set` resolved and another did not (an unknown axis, a value your `remap` has no entry for, an axis carried by a board, or the losing half of a conflicting repeat) printed the report and exited 0, so a script driving the repair saw success while an axis it named was never written. The report (human and `--json`) still shows the values that could be applied; `--apply` and `--emit-script` refuse the whole run before the confirmation prompt, so nothing is written until every `--set` value can be applied. The ones that could not are named on stderr in a closing `error: --set could not apply …` line, and the exit is 2. ([#941])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Every bypass `done-work` and `merge-pr` perform, and every `handoff-issue`, now leaves its own audit comment, and no comment another actor posts can stop one being written. Before, `--bypass`, `--bypass-ci` and `handoff-issue` skipped posting when any comment on the issue or PR contained a fixed stamp string. So a second, honest act (a different reason, new commits, or a handoff back and forth) recorded nothing. Anyone who posted the stamp also suppressed the record, and for a gate override that comment is the only record. Each audit comment from `done-work`, `merge-pr`, `move-issue` and `handoff-issue` now ends with a hidden key, `<!-- pkit-audit-key: <writer>:<digest> -->`, hashed from what identifies the act. For `--bypass` and `--bypass-ci` that is the reason and the PR head commit. For `handoff-issue` it is from, to, the reason and the issue's count of assignment events. A retry skips only when a comment with exactly the body it would post (line endings and trailing whitespace aside) was posted, unedited, by the account `gh` posts as. That account also covers other workflows on the same CI token and comments the operator typed by hand, so a comment that merely quotes a key, such as a multi-line bypass reason, does not count. The per-reviewer override and `move-issue`'s transition audit get the same guard, and the per-reviewer override's stamp moves to the new key form. The whole-gate and CI bypass comments now name the short PR head commit, so two bypasses either side of a force-push read differently. Any doubt resolves toward posting again. The first retry of an act audited under the old stamps therefore posts one fresh comment. `done-work`'s documented side-effect order now states that its audit comments post before the merge. The capability README says what makes an audit comment unique and what stops suppression (#902). ([#953])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `start-work` now checks that the issue can move to In Progress before it changes anything (#942). Before, it created and checked out the branch and set the assignee, and only then called `move-issue --to in-progress`. On an issue in Todo, where that move is not legal, the run left the branch and assignee in place, the issue stayed where it was, and the output ended on the branch-creation line. It now refuses with exit 2, names the state it found and the move to make first (from Todo, `move-issue <N> --to backlog`), and creates no branch and writes no assignee. The check reads the same state and the same `workflow.yaml` transitions `move-issue` does, so an issue already In Progress still re-runs cleanly. If `move-issue` still fails after the branch is cut (a network error, for example), the run now ends on a `[failed]` block that names the branch and any assignee it left behind, with the commands to undo them, and passes `move-issue`'s exit code through as before. ([#948])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The `project-manager` agent declares COR-047, whose requirement its dispatch discipline upholds; `pm-reviewer` and the `pm` skill's validate-body operation word a configuration key and two validator finding names so they no longer read as hooks. `pkit validate --only refs` reports no drift warnings on them, and `pkit sync` redeploys the two changed agents.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The native containment link now respects that an issue has one native parent (#1040). Linking a child that is already natively under a different parent is reported as a conflict naming the child, the parent it has and the parent asked for; before, GitHub's HTTP 422 for it read as "the instance does not support sub-issues". The seam reads the child's current native parent before the add, and GitHub's error body when the add is refused, so `link-parent` shows the conflict in its plan, `--dry-run` included, and leaves the link alone. `set-field --parent` now moves the native link along with the first line: it reads the issue's native parent while validating, moves the link in one write, and only then rewrites the first line; a move GitHub refuses, or a native parent that cannot be read, stops the call before anything is written and says why. In `textual` containment and for a milestone parent it still changes only the first line. `link-parent` also reads each parent's sub-issue list once per run instead of once per child. ([#1061])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `check-criterion` and `uncheck-criterion` take `--section doc-impact` to tick the checkboxes of a Task's `## Doc impact` section, addressed like the acceptance criteria — a 1-based index with an optional expected-text guard, numbered as the new `show-issue --field doc-impact` lists them (#1015). `done-work`'s checkbox close-gate counted those boxes but no verb could tick them, so closing such a Task forced a whole-body rewrite. Its refusal now prints, under each unticked box, the command that ticks it.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `open-pr` takes the closing issue number positionally, as `review-work` and `done-work` do (`open-pr 1017`), keeping `--closes` as the explicit form; with both, the positional issue is the primary one (#1017). Its help now states how the PR title is composed — `<type>(<scope>): <summary>`, with `--summary` as the description part only — so a first invocation no longer fails on the asymmetry with its sibling verbs.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Attaching an existing issue to a Milestone, or moving it to another, has its own path (#1016): `edit-issue --milestone` / `--clear-milestone` now take a required `--reason`, post an audit comment naming the old and new milestone and the reason before writing (once, even on a retry), and refuse an issue whose type `issue-types.yaml` does not let sit under a milestone; the milestone must still be open. `promote-issue` without `--milestone` now says the milestone is unchanged instead of printing "no milestone", and on an issue already past Todo it points at `edit-issue` for a milestone change on its own.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work` now gates and closes every issue a pull request closes, not only the one it runs for (#1086). It reads each `Closes #N` the PR body carries, with the reader `open-pr` and `validate-pr` use, and runs the checkbox close-gate on each before the merge, refusing at the first issue with an unticked box and naming it. After the merge it runs each through `close-issue --mode pr-merge --pr <PR>`, the issue it ran for first, so each is closed as completed and its closure cascade runs. An issue already closed before the merge is skipped with a note, and a PR body that cannot be read fails closed. `merge-pr` reads closing references through the same shared reader.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A `code-undocumented` obligation of the `pkit::work-tracking:doc-check` point is met by anchoring its code from a page, no longer by any page changing. The companion schema `doc-check.schema.json` makes `document` optional for a `code-undocumented` obligation, whose subject is its `path` (now required), and keeps it required for `page-stale`. `check-doc-mapping` reports an obligation that names no `document` as unmet for as long as the point gives it, whatever the diff changed: its filler reads the pull request's head, so the obligation leaves the point once a page anchors the path. Its line names the fix, anchor `<path>` from a page, and each source counts such obligations as unanchored, under that source's own setting.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The deployed `project-manager` agent finds its batch-planning storyboard. Its source cites the storyboard by the sibling name `storyboard.md` and no longer calls it the file beside the agent, which the deployed copy in `.claude/agents/` is not; the deploy writes the storyboard's source path into that copy, which is where the agent loads it from.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work` on an issue that never reached Review no longer ends on a false warning (#1162). A Task whose PR was opened without `review-work` is still In Progress when `done-work` runs, and `workflow.yaml` declares no In Progress → Done for a Task, so the `move-issue --to done` after the merge was refused. `done-work` then warned "PR merged but move-issue exited 2" and advised a re-run that would fail the same way, while the pr-merge close went on to set `state:done`, and the run exited 2. `done-work` now moves such an issue to Review as the last step before the merge, so the merge makes the declared Review → Done as it does for an issue already in Review. Both moves go through `move-issue`. If the move to Review fails, nothing is merged. An issue already in Review is handled as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `close-milestone` no longer records a rollforward it did not perform (#387). Closing a Milestone moves none of its open children — a date-based close, or a `--force`d content-based or `either` one, leaves them assigned to the closed Milestone — but the audit line appended to the description counted them as "rolled forward". It now counts them as "still open, not rolled forward". The close's warning pointed at `set-field --parent` or a raw `gh issue edit --milestone` to reassign them by hand; the raw edit changes only the native field, so the closed Milestone kept counting the child through its first-line ref. The warning now names `edit-issue <n> --milestone <next> --reason`, which moves both. The pm README's Milestone section states what a close does with open children.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `close-milestone` sees a Milestone's children however many issues the repository has. It read only the newest 500 issues, so in a larger tracker a child filed earlier was missed, and a content-based Milestone could close over an open child it never saw. It now reads the issues through the containment seam, which fetches up to its 5000-issue ceiling and says whether it saw every issue; a read that reached the ceiling is refused, naming it, rather than answered from.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-pr` and `done-work` read a pull request's changed files whatever its size. They read them with `gh pr diff`, which GitHub refuses for a PR changing more than 300 files, so with a diff-floor reviewer installed (such as software-engineering's code panel) both refused that PR and called it a transient gh failure. They now read every page of GitHub's pull-request files API, which lists up to 3000 files, and count a renamed file under its old path as well as its new one. A read that reaches the 3000-file ceiling is still refused, now naming that cause and a split-or-bypass remedy instead of a retry. `review-pr`'s brief tells each reviewer where to read a diff `gh pr diff` refuses: the PR's range in the local checkout.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `check-mesh` compares against its configured peers again: with any mesh peer configured it failed with a Python error before comparing the first one.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `promote-issue` on a Todo issue no longer prints "the process engine refused this move: refused: no transition from 'backlog' to 'backlog'" under its successful promotion. `move-issue` asks the engine to record a move after writing the state label, so the engine read the label just written and took the target for the origin. It refused every move into a state with no transition to itself (Todo → Backlog, and Review → Done), and journaled every move into a state that has one as that self-loop (Backlog → In Progress as `create-draft`, In Progress → Review as `back-to-draft`). `move-issue` now passes the position it read before the label write as the origin (`pkit process move --from`), so each move is journaled once, as the transition it is, and the warning appears only when the engine refuses the move itself — a gate that does not pass, say. Entries journaled before this release keep their recorded transitions. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the backbone release that adds `--from`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `promote-issue <N> --milestone <M> --reason "<R>"` on a Todo issue now makes the whole Todo → Backlog move. It attached the milestone first, and the workflow reads an issue with a milestone and no state label as Backlog, so the `move-issue` that followed found nothing to move: the issue ended with the milestone but no state label, no audit comment carrying the reason and no recorded move. `promote-issue` now moves the issue first and attaches the milestone second, so the result is the same as promoting without a milestone and then scheduling. If the milestone write fails after the move, re-running the same command attaches it. Where a label carries the state (the default), the re-run does not move the issue again or post a second audit comment; where a Projects-v2 board carries it, the re-run makes the move again, and with journal logging on that posts a second audit comment. Without `--milestone`, `promote-issue` is unchanged.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A native sub-issue link GitHub refuses with HTTP 422 is no longer reported as "native sub-issues unsupported on this instance" (#808). GitHub answers 422 for a request it refused on instances where sub-issues work, so a 422 is now never read as an instance without sub-issues, on its status or on any wording: only a 410, or a 404 pm attributes to the endpoint by probing the parent issue, counts as one. A child that already has a parent is the conflict it is, naming that parent and saying its existing link must be removed first; a malformed `sub_issue_id` and any other 422 are failures. Each report quotes GitHub's own message after pm's sentence (gh's own line, attributed to gh, when GitHub sent none), and a validation error that carries only a field and a code is shown as `field: code` instead of being dropped. What that changes per command: `link-parent` and `set-field --parent` now fail with exit 3 where a 422 other than the one-parent rule or a duplicate used to pass as "unsupported" — `link-parent` reports the link as failed instead of "not linked (unsupported)", and `set-field --parent` stops before the first line moves instead of rewriting it alone; `create-issue` still files the issue on its textual ref and names `pkit pm link-parent <N>` for making the link once the cause is fixed. A move GitHub refuses for a reason other than the one-parent rule is a failure, no longer a conflict with a remedy. On an instance whose GitHub genuinely offers no sub-issues and answers 422, set `containment: textual` in `project/substrate-map.yaml`: pm then stops attempting the native link, and each refused write now says so. On the read side a 422 from a parent's sub-issue list is an unreadable list (an incomplete answer, quoting what GitHub said) rather than a determinate textual-only one, so a gate holds instead of answering from the first lines alone. 404 and 410 behave as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A parent the forward cascade moves is now journaled. When an issue moved forward, `move-issue` bumped a parent that was behind and recorded nothing for it, so with journal logging on the parent's move existed on the tracker and nowhere in the journal, and `pkit pm history <parent> --check-drift` reported drift for a move pkit itself made. Each parent the cascade moves now gets one entry, journaled the way the issue's own move is: after its label write, from the state the parent held before it, by the same actor, with the child's move named as the reason (`forward cascade from #12: backlog → in-progress`), which `pkit pm history` shows. A cascaded move the engine refuses warns exactly as a direct move's refusal does. One such refusal is a known defect, not intended behaviour: a parent in Todo is taken straight to In Progress, which the workflow does not declare, so the engine does not record it and `--check-drift` still reports that parent (#1229). A parent already at or beyond the target is left alone, so a re-run or a sibling's move adds no second entry. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the backbone release that adds `pkit process move --reason`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A `post-comment` hook now posts one comment per firing, and only its own earlier comment can stop it. It used to skip whenever any comment on the issue or PR contained its fixed `<!-- pkit-hook: <id> -->` stamp, so every firing after the first on a subject was dropped (a second transition, the same hook declared on another event), and anyone could suppress it by posting the stamp. The comment now opens with `<!-- pkit-hook: <id>:<firing> -->`, where `<firing>` is a digest of the hook's id, the event, the issue or PR and, on `after_move_issue`, the transition's from and to states. A retry of the same firing skips only when a comment opening with that line was posted, and not edited since, by the account `gh` posts as. A transition an issue makes a second time, and a second close after a reopen, still count as the same firing. Comments posted under the old bare stamp do not hold a post back, so a hook can post once more on an issue or PR that already has one.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-work` now checks that the issue can move to Review before it touches the pull request (#947). Before, it opened a ready PR or flipped a draft to ready and requested reviewers, and only then called `move-issue --to review`. On an issue in Backlog, where that move is not legal, the run left a ready PR with reviewers against an issue that stayed where it was, and the output gave no closing message. It now refuses with exit 2, names the state it found and the moves to make first (from Backlog, `move-issue <N> --to in-progress`; from Todo, Backlog first), and opens, flips and requests nothing. The check reads the same state and the same `workflow.yaml` transitions `move-issue` does, so an issue already in Review still re-runs cleanly, for example to flip the PR ready again after `back-to-draft`. If `move-issue` still fails after the PR was opened or made ready (a network error, for example), the run now ends on a `[failed]` block naming the PR, its ready state and the reviewers it requested, with the commands to undo them, and passes `move-issue`'s exit code through as before. ([#1235])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `set-field <N> --parent <P>` on an issue whose body opens with an `Integration: integration/<slug>` marker now rewrites the parent-ref on the line below the marker and leaves the marker as the first line, with no blank line between the two. It wrote the new parent-ref above the marker, so the issue's integration branch was no longer read from the first line and the branch and PR verbs fell back to the default branch; a parent-ref already under the marker stayed too, leaving the body naming two parents. A marked body with no parent-ref now gets one directly below the marker. Setting the parent an issue already has writes nothing, and unmarked bodies are rewritten as before. ([#1234])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `start-work` and `review-work` check that their move is legal before they touch a branch or pull request from one shared reading of the issue's state: its labels and milestone, with no state label and no milestone reading as Todo (#1242). The two verbs also share one implementation of that refusal, with its "move it first" hint, and of the failure that names what a run left behind when the composed `move-issue` fails after the verb's own changes, for instance because the state changed since the check and `move-issue` refuses the move. `review-work`'s documented exit codes now include 3 for a `gh` failure opening the pull request or making it ready, and its closing line, `[ok] PR ready; #<N> in Review`, no longer claims a move from In Progress; `move-issue`'s own output says whether it moved the issue.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Each close `close-issue` makes is now journaled. A won't-do close, a cascade-eligible container's close and a pr-merge close — the one `done-work` runs on each issue a merged pull request closes — wrote the Done label and recorded nothing, so with journal logging on `pkit pm history <N> --check-drift` reported drift for closes pkit itself made. Each now records one move to Done, after the close and its label reconcile, from the state the issue held before the close: its state label, else its milestone, else Todo, so an issue GitHub closed when a pull request merged is recorded from where the merge found it. The close mode is the entry's reason, which `pkit pm history` shows (`wont-do close: <reason>`, `cascade-eligibility close: …`, `pr-merge close: closed by merged PR #<M>`). `move-issue --to done` on an issue GitHub already closed — the move `done-work` makes on its own issue after a merge — records the close as it rewrites the stale label. An issue already labelled Done is not recorded again, so a re-run adds nothing. A close the engine refuses, or one the workflow does not declare for the issue's type (a Task closed from In Progress), is warned about as a refused move is; the close stands and the exit code is unchanged. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release that adds `pkit process move --reason`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work`, `merge-pr` and `land-work` pass the operator's cross-repository confirmation on to the backbone, which now runs its own guard on every merge, enqueue and dequeue it makes for them. When the operator gave `--allow-foreign-repo` on the verb's command line — whatever the verb's own comparison found — or answered yes at its prompt, the verb passes `--allow-foreign-repo` on, and never otherwise, so a confirmed landing in another repository is not refused by the backbone, and nothing is confirmed that the operator did not confirm. `done-work` hands the same confirmation to the `move-issue` and `close-issue` runs it starts, so a confirmed landing is not refused, or asked about again, after its merge. `land-work` at a terminal now asks once where it asked twice — its own guard and `done-work`'s — since its confirmation is passed on to `done-work`. Should the backbone's guard refuse all the same, because its comparison and the capability's disagree and the operator confirmed nothing, the landing ends refused, naming both verdicts, with nothing asked of GitHub; giving the flag settles it. A dequeue the backbone's guard refused names the command that takes the PR out. The capability's guard now reports how it passed; its other verbs behave as before. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release whose `pkit pull-request` takes `--allow-foreign-repo`. ([#1277])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The forward cascade reaches every ancestor of a moved issue (#1230, #1229). An EPIC that stayed behind when a Task under its Feature started now moves too. An ancestor in Todo shows Backlog and then In Progress — two moves the workflow declares — where it was written straight to In Progress, a move the engine refused to record and `pkit pm history <N> --check-drift` then showed as drift. And `move-issue <N> --to done` from Todo or Backlog, a won't-do close, used to bring the issue's ancestors to In Progress and no longer moves them. Each ancestor behind is brought up to the moved issue's state, capped at In Progress, with no milestone, no hook and no comment; where a journal is kept, each step is recorded with that move as its reason. An ancestor at or past the target is left alone and the walk goes on above it, so the first forward move after upgrading brings a chain left behind level. The preview and `--dry-run` list each ancestor's steps. The walk stops, with a warning, at an ancestor it cannot read, one that is not a container, one it has passed, one whose first line is a parent-ref its type does not allow, and one whose native parent is not the parent its first line names — the moved issue's own included, settled by `set-field <N> --parent <P>`. A cascade cut short ends with a `[warn]` block, the issue's move stands and the exit code stays 0; running the same `move-issue` again finishes it, with no prompt — on an issue already at Done, once it has closed as completed. `close-issue`'s closure cascade quotes a first line that looks like a parent-ref its type does not allow, with the allowed forms, instead of reporting that the body names no parent. `done-work`'s warning after a failed move to Done names the issue in the `move-issue` line it prints. Where state is not written as a label, the cascade does not run and one line says so.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work` and `merge-pr` no longer delete a merged pull request's head branch on GitHub themselves. After their own steps that follow the merge, they ask the backbone (`pkit pull-request delete-branch`), which deletes the branch only while its tip is the head the pull request merged at. A push made to the branch after the merge was deleted with it, and completing an old pull request could delete the branch of a newer one that reused its name; now such a branch is kept, and so is one another open pull request uses as its head or as its base. What became of the branch — deleted, kept, not there, refused, or not known to be deleted — is said in one line and never fails the verb. Where the branch was not deleted, the line names the command that deletes it later (`pkit pull-request delete-branch <PR> --expect <sha>`); `merge-pr`'s line says a re-run of it does not retry the deletion. The local branch is now deleted only once the branch on GitHub was deleted or is gone, and is kept, said in one line, otherwise — including when no merged head is known, where it used to be force-deleted. The operator's cross-repository confirmation is passed on to the backbone's guard as it is for the merge. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release that has `pkit pull-request delete-branch`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A `post-comment` hook now posts again when an issue makes the same transition a second time, or is closed again after a reopen. Its stamp identified a firing by the hook, the event, the issue or PR and, on a move, the from and to states, so a repeat of a transition (in-progress → review, closed, reopened, then in-progress → review again) and a second close read as retries of the first and posted nothing. The firing now includes which occurrence it is: on `after_move_issue` the issue's count of landed moves — the journal's length where the project keeps one, its state-label events on the GitHub timeline where it does not — and on `after_close_issue` the time GitHub reports the issue closed. A retry of the same firing, such as `close-issue --mode=pr-merge` run again on the same close, still posts once. Where the occurrence cannot be read, the firing is told without it, as before. A run reads the issue's or PR's comments once, and only when a `post-comment` hook is about to post, however many fire; the occurrence costs no read where a journal is kept or the issue was already closed, and one otherwise. Comments that cannot be read still let the hook post, and it now prints a `[warn]` line saying it could not check. The hook engine's `gh` calls, imported by a script, fell back to a plain `gh` and ignored the configured `gh.host`; they now go through the helper that sets it.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work`, `merge-pr` and `land-work` no longer conclude that a merge or an enqueue was not made from one reading. When the backbone settles a request that got no answer, the verbs take its word. If the backbone says the request was made, the landing goes on. If two readings did not see it made, the landing fails, the verbs say the request may still show, and a re-run reads the pull request first and completes it if it has merged since. `merge-pr` now records the after-merge steps as owed whenever its run sent a merge or an enqueue GitHub did not refuse and did not see merged, so its re-run completes a merge that showed late instead of refusing it as merged by someone else. If the backbone says the request is unconfirmed, the verbs report it unconfirmed, exit 4, and name `pkit pull-request read <PR>`. When the backbone gives no answer at all, the verbs read the pull request once. Merged is the merge and queued is waited for; anything else is now unconfirmed, exit 4, where it used to be "not made". When taking a pull request out of the queue ends unconfirmed, the verbs say whether it left is not known, never that it failed. When it finds the pull request merged, they say it merged and at which head. The backbone's `[warn]` line that a request is in flight is passed on as it comes. The bounds on the backbone's answers now hold the longest the backbone states each command can run: a minute and a half for a reading, three and a half minutes for a merge or an enqueue, and ten for a dequeue. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release that settles an unanswered request. ([#1303])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The verbs that pass on the process engine's answer say why it could not evaluate a predicate, with the predicate's own words (#1244). `close-issue --mode=cascade-eligibility-close`, holding a container whose fold the engine could not resolve, prints what the predicate behind it said on standard error under the fold's reason. `move-issue`'s forward cascade, stopping at an ancestor the engine cannot place, prints why under that ancestor: each cause the engine gives and what its predicate said, or the reason a classifier placed it in none of its states. A move the engine refused to record warns as before, and where the refusal names a predicate it could not evaluate, the causes and the predicate's words now follow on the lines after the warning instead of running on into it. No verdict or exit code changes. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: the words come from the engine's `stderr_tail` and `position.unevaluated`, which the release writes.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A move's why now reaches its journal entry, and a move the forward cascade makes is projected at `audit.projection: full` like any other governed move (#1232). A bypass's justification — `promote-issue --reason`, `move-issue --bypass --bypass-reason` — was written into the audit comment only, so with journal logging on the journal lacked the why of exactly the moves that needed one. It is now the reason on the move's journal entry too (`bypass: <reason>`), at every projection, and `pkit pm history` shows it; a move no gate was bypassed for carries none. At `full`, each ancestor the forward cascade moves now gets the provenance comment a governed move gets, naming the move that caused it on a line of its own: one comment on each ancestor per run, covering the steps that landed in it (`todo → backlog → in-progress`), so a re-run never repeats one. At `audit` and `off` a cascaded move still posts nothing. A comment that fails to post is one `[warn]` line, and the move stands. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release that adds `pkit process move --reason`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `set-field <N> --parent <P>` refuses an issue whose body's first line attempts an `Integration:` marker but is not the valid `Integration: integration/<slug>` form (#1241). It wrote the new parent-ref above the malformed line, which moved it off the first line, where `validate-issue` hard-rejects it, so the bad marker then passed validation. The refusal names the line and the required form, exits 1, and writes nothing — neither the body nor the native sub-issue link — under `--dry-run` too. A valid marker and an unmarked body are rewritten as before, except that a parent-ref prepended to an unmarked body opening with blank lines now replaces them, leaving one blank line before the content instead of the old blank lines below the new one.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The issue a landing completes now journals its move to Done with the reason every other issue its pull request closes gets (#1296). `done-work` — and `land-work`, which lands through it — moved its own issue to Done after GitHub had closed it, and that move was journaled with no reason, while each other issue the pull request closed got `pr-merge close: closed by merged PR #<PR>` from `close-issue`. `done-work` now names the merged pull request to `move-issue` (`--to done --merged-pr <PR>`), and the one entry that move writes carries the same reason — `completed by merged PR #<PR>` where the issue is still open when it moves, as `close-issue` words a close it makes through the pull request — so `pkit pm history` reads the same for each. `--merged-pr` with any other target is refused as a usage error. With journal logging off nothing is recorded, as before. Upgrade the capability together with the backbone: it needs the release that adds `pkit process move --reason`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A child linked natively under a container now holds the container open until the child closes, whatever the child's first line says (#1304). The close gate lists a container's children through the containment seam, native sub-issues included, but then asked each one whether its first line names a parent and left out one that names none — a sub-issue linked in GitHub's UI, or one filed with `create-issue --parent <N> --milestone <M>`, whose first line is the `Milestone:` ref — so the container could close while that child was open. A container may therefore now hold where it closed before: `close-issue <N> --mode=cascade-eligibility-close` refuses, naming each open child that holds it (`#<n>`) with what releases the container, and `pkit process cascade project-management:issue-lifecycle --subject <N>` names the member the fold stopped at (`member '<n>'`, with the state it is in); `pkit pm show-tree` shows that child under the container, whatever its first line names. Close that child — or, if it does not belong under the container, move it with `set-field <n> --parent <P>` — and the container closes as before. A child whose issue cannot be read still holds the container, as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The merge gate no longer counts a remote reviewer or a commenter as independent of a pull request's author it could not read. When `gh pr view` names no author (no login, a blank one, or `app/` alone, which is how it writes an author GitHub returns as null), the agent-mode gate counts no remote reviewer's verdict, and refuses, naming the unread author, where a required reviewer is registered only on the remote path or a `--bypass-reviewer` override names a reviewer on the remote path; only the whole-gate `--bypass` gets past that refusal, and a local reviewer's verdict counts as before. The human-mode gate counts no `Approved` comment and says why, while an APPROVED review still counts; and `land-work` stops at its findings step, with nothing merged, when a remote reviewer's verdict carries a `[person]` finding. A readable author is handled as before. ([#1489])
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The workflow schema migrations `0.15.0/001` and `0.24.0/001` no longer warn wrongly on an upgrade that crosses more than one version. `pkit upgrade` now runs a capability's migrations after its copy, so the shipped `workflow.yaml` they read is the newest. They now read a schema at or above their own, in the shipped file and in a project's override, as one their change already holds. `0.26.0/001` reads it the same way, so a later schema bump does not bring the warning back. The README says that `pkit sync` never changes `project/config.yaml`, and that only an upgrade's migrations do.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A sentence the author wrote in a form the commands cannot read is reported as unreadable, not passed over in silence (#1523). Under a pull request's `## What changed in each updated document`, the commands read a sentence only from a top-level list item that names its document in a code span, ``- `path`: sentence``. An item in another form, such as the document in bold, gave no sentence without a word. `edit-pr` said "validation: clean", and `land-work` told the person who authorises the merge that the author had not said what changed. `open-pr`, `edit-pr` and `land-work` now warn of each such item, naming its line in the description as the command leaves it, and the form a sentence takes. In `edit-pr` the warning is a `warning` finding of its validation, which still writes the edit. Nothing inside an HTML comment is read as a sentence any more, since it does not render.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — An issue's branch is the one with its open pull request (#1608). The verbs that find an issue's branch by name (`start-work`, `create-draft`, `review-work`, `back-to-draft`, `review-pr`, `done-work` and `land-work`) each took the first local branch matching `<type>/<N>-<slug>`. A stale branch an earlier attempt left behind could take the place of the one carrying the pull request, as on #1367, where `review-work` validated an empty branch and refused on a body it never read. They now read it one way, `_lib/issue_branch`. Of several matching branches, the one with the issue's open pull request wins, read in one `gh` call. With none open, more than one open, or no answer from `gh`, the verb refuses before it changes anything, naming each branch and why. `done-work` and `land-work` still complete a pull request that has merged, found from the issue itself. `land-work` hands the branch it read to `review-pr` and `done-work`, so a run reads the open pull requests once. `start-work` refuses a branch naming the issue in another shape only when no branch of the right shape matches.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work` refuses to merge a pull request whose checks it cannot read (#1946). A failed call to GitHub, an answer that is not JSON, or one that carries no `statusCheckRollup` was read as a rollup of no checks, and passed the CI-status gate. Now it refuses as unreadable, with exit 2, names the cause and writes nothing, and `--bypass-ci` does not clear it. `land-work` stops on it as on any other reading that fails. A rollup GitHub answers with no checks, as for a project that runs none, still passes, and the run now says `ci-status: green (no checks configured)`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `create-issue` checks a body against `body-format.yaml` before it writes anything, `--dry-run` included, as DEC-010 decides (#1932). A body that lacks a section its issue type requires, or holds a heading of the first level, is refused, naming what is wrong, and nothing is filed. A `file:line` reference and a `## Implementation` or `## How` section are warned of, and filing goes on. `create-issue`, `edit-issue` and `validate-issue` now read these rules through one module, `scripts/_lib/body_rules.py`. Each universal rule takes its severity from the schema entry whose new `id` names it. The `## Implementation` and `## How` warning is new to all three, and a `# ` line in a fenced code block is no longer read as a heading.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `merge-pr` refuses to merge a pull request whose checks it cannot read (#1952), as `done-work` does since #1946. A `statusCheckRollup` that the answer from GitHub leaves out or carries as null was read as a rollup of no checks, and passed the CI-status gate. Now it refuses with exit 2, names the cause and writes nothing, and `--bypass-ci` does not clear it. An answer that is not a pull request's JSON is said, rather than refused in silence. A rollup GitHub answers with no checks, as for a project that runs none, still passes, and the run now says `ci-status: green (no checks configured)`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `review-pr` runs the reviewers an installed capability contributes where the project registers no local reviewer of its own (#1934), as DEC-032 point D4 decides. It used to exit 2 with `no agents configured in review.agents.local_registered:` before it worked out the required set, and `land-work`'s review step skipped the review. Now both work out the set first and run each reviewer in it, so a project that registers only a remote reviewer has software-engineering's reviewers run on a change to code. Where the pull request requires no local reviewer at all, `review-pr` says so, runs nothing and exits 0, and `land-work` leaves the approval to `done-work`'s gate.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `done-work`'s agent-mode gate works out the reviewers a pull request requires before it judges whether any is required (#1979), as DEC-032 point D3 decides. It used to refuse with `no agents configured` where the project registers no remote or local reviewer, though a capability contributes one the pull request requires. Now it merges on that reviewer's approval. A required set with no reviewer in it refuses the merge and never passes. Before, entries under `review.agents.*` that name no reviewer, such as a `local_registered:` entry with no `name`, passed the gate with no reviewer at all. The refusal names those entries, or says that nothing is registered.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `close-issue` closes an issue as won't do, or closes a container, only on the person's authorisation (#1936). It asked only at a terminal, so with no terminal and no `--yes` it closed in both modes and nobody was asked. Now it refuses there with exit 1 and writes nothing, as `move-issue` refuses a move the person holds. `--yes` authorises the close, a terminal still asks, and a dry run needs no authorisation.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `close-issue --mode=pr-merge` moves an issue to Done only through a merged pull request that relates to it (#1968). Without `--pr` it wrote `state:done` on an open issue, and with `--pr <M>` it closed an open Task through any merged pull request. Now an open issue without `--pr` is refused with exit 1, writing nothing, and the labels of an issue GitHub closed are still reconciled. With `--pr <M>` the pull request must close the issue in its body or come from the issue's branch, `<type>/<N>-<slug>`. The close names the relation it found, in its output and its comment, and a pull request with neither is refused with exit 1. Like a won't-do close, the close through `--pr` is the person's: with no terminal and no `--yes` it is refused, writing nothing. `done-work` passes `--yes` after the merge.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — When the merge gate finds no standing approval, `done-work`'s refusal names `review-pr` with the issue's number, the number that command takes (#1935). It named the pull request's number, so `review-pr <PR> --force` found no branch, or reviewed another issue's pull request.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The closure cascade that ends every `close-issue` mode reports a parent eligible to close only once its last open child has closed (#1937). It read the parent's checkboxes alone, and said "eligible to close pending sibling check" while a sibling was still open. It now reads the process engine's fold over the parent's children (`pkit process cascade`), as `--mode=cascade-eligibility-close` does. A parent with no open child left and every box ticked is reported eligible, with the `close-issue <P> --mode=cascade-eligibility-close` command that closes it. A parent with an open child is reported not eligible, each open child named. Where the fold cannot be read, the report says whether a child is open is not known, and the parent is not reported eligible.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The commands `create-issue` and `set-field` check `--workstream` against the project's workstreams file, `project/workstreams.yaml`, as DEC-012 and DEC-018 decide (#1933). Until now they read only the legacy `workstreams:` key of `project/config.yaml`. An empty key let any value through, and a list the 0.5.0 migration left there refused a workstream added since. They now take only a workstream the file holds as active, and refuse any other, a deprecated one included, naming the active ones. The legacy key is read only where the file is absent, and where it is empty too nothing is checked.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — A step that is the person's is now taken only with their authorisation: a yes typed at a terminal, or `--yes` (#2015). Off a terminal without `--yes`, `close-milestone` refuses the close, `add-workstream`, `rename-workstream`, `merge-workstream`, `split-workstream` and `remove-workstream` refuse the change, and `edit-workstream` refuses a status flip. `add-member` and `remove-member` refuse to write `members.yaml`. Each refuses with exit 1 before anything is changed, and names the command to run with `--yes`. An answer piped into stdin is never read. A re-run of `close-milestone` that finishes a rollforward still goes on without `--yes`. At a terminal, and with `--yes`, each command works as before.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — `create-issue`, `edit-issue` and `validate-issue` warn of a predicted decision id, as DEC-010 decides (#1970): an id that no record holds, written in a body as if one did. They read a core, project or architecture record's id, and a capability's decision named with its capability, `[<capability>:DEC-NNN-<slug>]` or `<capability>:DEC-NNN`. A bare `DEC-NNN` names no capability and is not checked. The ids are looked up in one run of `pkit refs lookup --json`, only for a body that cites one. Where `pkit` gives no answer, one notice says which ids were not checked, and why. Neither refuses anything or changes an exit code. The rule's entry in `body-format.yaml` now carries its `id`, `body.predicted-decision-id`.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The command `bootstrap` refuses when no GitHub repository resolves for the checkout, `--dry-run` included, and exits 2 (#2087). It names what is missing, with the reason `gh` gave, and how to add it: a remote on GitHub and `gh auth login`, or `gh` itself. Until now a dry run printed `target repo: <unresolved>`, planned its labels and exited 0, and a missing `gh` ended in a traceback. The README's setup now says the capability needs a GitHub repository, and its config example declares the workstreams in `project/workstreams.yaml`, so `pre-check` no longer calls it a legacy fallback. The config reference names no version that has not shipped.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The doc check (`check-doc-mapping`) meets a page's friction obligation by an answer the change adds on the page, as the change check lists and accepts it against the same base, as well as by a change to the page itself (#2095). The check reads a pull request as the one squash commit it lands as, so a fix round that edits an anchor after the commit that answered for it no longer fails the check while the change check asks nothing. Where the change check gives no list, the check says why and meets a page only by its change in the diff.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — The doc check (`check-doc-mapping`) meets a page's friction obligation by a deferral the change rewrote, which the change check lists as edited, where the change check counts it for its anchor and its reason names an open follow-up (#2135). The check asks the host through `gh` whether each follow-up the reason names is open. Where the host cannot be asked, as in a check run with no token, a follow-up counts as open, with a warning, and the reviewer checks it. A bump, or an answer the change check refuses, still meets nothing.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Every command that reads the topology now says which commit of the default branch it read, as DEC-059 point 1 decides (#2177). Each command that confirms the clone's seat before it writes, and `next-task`, prints a note naming that commit, once in a run: a command that another runs, such as the `move-issue` that `start-work` runs, repeats no commit the run named already. A project with no topology prints no such note.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — After a merge, `done-work` and `merge-pr` switch their clone to the default branch and pull it only where the clone is on the merged pull request's head branch with a clean working tree. A clone on another branch or a detached HEAD, or with uncommitted changes, untracked files included, is now left where it is, nothing pulled, and the merged branch's local copy is kept, said in one line. Before, the clean-up checked out the default branch whatever the clone held, so a worker's clone in the middle of a Task was moved onto it with its changes, and its next commit could land there. A clone already on the default branch with a clean working tree is still brought up to date, by a fast-forward only and with no switch, and the merged branch's local copy there is now kept, said in one line. A fast-forward that fails warns and changes nothing. Where the switch fails, the clone stays on the head branch and the local copy is kept, with a warning, rather than a delete that cannot succeed. The deletion of the head branch on GitHub is unchanged. `land-work` lands through `done-work`, so it treats its clone the same way.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — In a project with a topology, a claim on an issue now counts only where its clone made it after its current take of the claim's seat (DEC-059 point 5, #2182). Before, a clone that took its seat again after a release, by itself or by force, revived every claim it made before the release. A Task another seat took over while the seat was free then had two owners, and the clash guard refused the taker's next write. The take is the claim on the seat's state issue that made the clone its holder, and the claim's comment must have a higher id than the take's, since GitHub numbers the comments of every issue from one sequence. A Task whose claim no longer counts returns to the pool, and its clone claims it again from its seat.
- **project-management 0.55.0** — Where `gh.default_owner` is unset, as a single-owner adopter whose board lives in the repository's owner leaves it, `set-field` finds the board under the repository's owner, which the issue's url names. It read the board with no owner, so `gh` looked among the boards of whoever ran it, and an organisation's board was not found. Its refusal for an issue with no card now names that owner in the `gh project item-add` command it gives.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The analysis' id discipline now holds against every history, not only the working tree (#1132). `pkit analysis new` numbers a use case or journey past every number the default branch's history ever gave a file of its place, so deleting the highest-numbered one frees nothing, and names that file when it is the number it follows; the history counts the files the tree reading counts — a Markdown file of the place, judged by the reading at the commit that added it — and a shallow clone is said. It reads a file's number from its name as well as its front matter, so a file whose id cannot be read still holds it. A file `friction.exclude` leaves out is no part of the analysis, for the stamp and the check alike. `pkit analysis validate` reports such files — front matter that does not parse, or a name carrying a number the front matter does not give — and fails on a placeholder the stamp left unfilled, in an own field or the body, matched against every placeholder the templates and the skill's commands ever shipped (`<Title>` and `<Term>` included), so a reworded template never hides an old one and words of an artefact's own in angle brackets are never refused; the stamp refuses a title, name or reason still holding one. `pkit analysis check-numbers` knows an artefact by its id, not its path, and reads a number from a file's name as well as its front matter on both sides; a number the default branch took since the fork and removed is still taken, as the stamp counts it. A move, or a stacked branch's own work landed byte for byte, is no collision; the default branch's file of the name this branch gives the number, with other bytes — a parent edited after the fork and then squash-merged, or two branches stamping the same slug — is a warning, "possibly your own work landed: merge the default branch and keep one file; otherwise renumber", never an error; a file of another name is a collision. A stacked branch that moved its landed file into an area is not reported there: the duplicate check catches the two files after the merge. The stamp writes each file whole — to a temporary file beside it, then moved over it — records the analysis location only once the file is written and only while it is not recorded yet, puts the file back if recording fails, refuses a file or folder reached through a link, and keeps a collection file's bytes outside the entry it adds — its line endings included: a file whose every line ends CRLF, as a `core.autocrlf` checkout writes it, stays CRLF, and one with mixed line endings is refused, saying so.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — The resolver's `pkit analysis propose` reads what an anchor matches from `pkit friction explain --json` and matches no anchor of its own (#1157): the files each path anchor stands on at the revalidation point and at HEAD, the commits behind each finding with the paths behind them — for a dead anchor, where its files went — and the artefact's body, for an actor or a term its own section. A quote surviving only in a file `friction.exclude` leaves out now reads as gone rather than kept, and such a file is never taken for where code moved. Each commit in its `--json` document carries its `paths`, and the human view lists them, so `git show <commit> -- <paths>` is what changed under the anchor.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A revalidation record under a path `friction.exclude` leaves out is no longer counted or checked by `pkit analysis validate`, as an excluded artefact is no part of the analysis. The check reads the `excluded` key the backbone this release ships adds to each held file in `pkit friction artefacts --json`, so the capability requires that backbone.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis propose` proposes nothing for an artefact the friction checks did not judge. It refused only an artefact whose revalidation point lies beyond a shallow clone, and read every anchor state but `stale`, `deferred` and `dead-anchor` as current — so for an artefact in the backbone's `unresolved` state, whose anchor of a registered kind cannot be resolved, it could propose `holds` from its other anchors and hand over `revalidate --outcome unchanged` for the anchor nobody read.
