It now refuses an `unresolved` artefact, and one in any state it does not know, with exit 1 and what `pkit friction explain` says clears it; and an anchor in any state but `current` or a change — `unresolved-kind`, `no-answer`, one a later backbone adds — is not judged, so the verdict is the new rule `not-judged`: read, with nothing proposed and no command to run.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate` reads a journey's part anchor on a use case, such as `UC-001#`, as an anchor on that use case, the one named before its last `#` (COR-050 point 2). The check that a journey's use-case anchors match its `steps` and its `relies-on` now counts it, where it slipped past before. It matches no entry of the two lists, which name each use case whole, since a part anchor flags the journey only when its part changes. So a journey with a part anchor on any use case fails the check, and the message names it among the anchors (DEC-001 point 4).
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — Onboarding a brownfield project's analysis now starts from the surface the project declares, and ends once that surface is covered (DEC-001 point 9, #1930). Before an agent derives any candidate, the `analysis-author` skill's `build-analysis.md` has it run `pkit friction check --all` and read the declared surface. Where the check reads `no surface declared`, the agent asks the person which paths the analysis ought to cover, and derives nothing until they are declared under `friction.surface`. After the last step, onboarding ends only once the check counts no path of the surface uncovered, and lists none of the analysis's artefacts as unanchored without a reason a person accepted.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — `pkit analysis validate` finishes well inside the 30 seconds `pkit validate` gives a component's command, with the same findings (#2025). On project-kit's own analysis, with other work running, it takes about a quarter of the time it did, some 7.5 seconds where it took 30. It asks the backbone where every record it reads lies in one run of `pkit refs lookup --json`, not one run for each record. It searches a text for a term only where the text holds a run of the term's name, and reads the kinds' declaration and each component's package metadata once. The lookup in one document is new in the backbone this release ships, so the capability requires it.
- **software-analysis 0.2.0** — A freshly stamped analysis artefact tells its writer once what to write (#2088). `pkit analysis validate` reports a part that still holds its hint once, by the placeholder check, and no longer also as empty or missing where the format rule binds. Each hint's example is now written in a form the check accepts. The hint of a use case's *Main path*, and of a use case's or a journey's *Variants*, stands on a line of its own below its label, where the list goes, and its example is the list's first item. So a writer who puts the example in the hint's place writes a list item the check reads.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The code-review panel now states when a pre-existing defect blocks: only when the change asserts it away — the PR title or body claims to fix or guard against that behaviour, or text the diff adds or edits says the case is handled — and the defect survives in the reviewer's remit. Defects the diff newly exposes, moves, copies or widens count as the diff's own. Every other pre-existing finding stays advisory and is tagged `[advisory] (pre-existing: <reason>)` (#883). ([#899])
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — `docs-reviewer` reads a pull request's `## What changed in each updated document` as the commands that write the list of answers read it (#1523). They read a sentence only from a top-level list item that starts with its document in a code span, and the form to write is ``- `path`: sentence``. Take an `updated` document whose only sentence they cannot read, such as one naming the document in bold. The person who authorises the merge is told its author has not said what changed. The reviewer now judges it as having no sentence, and its `[block]` quotes the item and gives the form it needs.
- **software-engineering 0.3.0** — The code-review panel's three reviewers tag a finding `[person]`, as project-management DEC-028 decides for every reviewer's verdict (#1976). Such a finding judges a passage the change owns against an accepted rule of the project's conventions, cited by id, that binds on what the change alters. The rule needs a reading it does not settle there, or a fact it does not name. The finding withholds no approval and is shown to the person who authorises the merge. The README says the same.

### Security
- A deny of the permission decision core, a guardrail's included, also matches the forms of its command that programs read (ADR-025 point 5): a short flag inside a bundle (`git push -uf`), a long flag cut to a prefix (`--force-w`), with its value after `=` (`--force-with-lease=main`) or behind one dash (`-force`), a program named by its path or in another case (`/usr/bin/sudo`, `Git`), and a subcommand behind the options before it (`git -C . push --force`). The privilege catalog's Bash matcher gains an optional `word_prefix_any` field, through which the `vcs-history-rewrite` guardrail names a refspec opening with `+`, so `git push origin +main` is denied. The guardrail also names `--mirror`, and the `destructive-fs` pattern spells `--recursive` cut down to `--r`. Where an unquoted glob, or an assignment that changes the program's configuration, such as `HOME`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, a name holding `CONFIG` or `GIT_DIR`, may make a command one a deny names, no grant allows it: the core defers, and a strict posture denies (ADR-025 point 2). So where `git` is granted, `git push origin --forc[e]`, `HOME=/tmp/x git status` and `export GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/tmp/x && git push` are no longer allowed unasked. Claude Code's adapter baseline allows `git` by prefix, so a deferred glob led by `git` may still run unasked there. Grants still match a command as it is written, and a git alias or refspec set in a configuration git reads, or through `git -c`, stays the grant's breadth.
- The permission decision core names the guardrails a deferred Bash command may hide, through `hidden_guardrails()` and, for a PreToolUse payload, `hook_hidden_guardrails()`. Each names the guardrails among the subject's denies that may bind on the command in a form the core cannot see, such as a pathname pattern or an assignment that changes the program's configuration, with what keeps the core from seeing it. An adapter whose harness may allow what the core defers asks the person about such a command (ADR-025 point 2). No verdict of `decide()` changes, and `hook_decide()` still returns it.
- The permission decision core no longer allows a compound Bash command by a grant on one of its segments (ADR-025 point 2). Each segment it splits a command into now needs an allow of its own. A segment that matches no privilege, or only privileges no allow grant reaches, makes the core defer the call to the harness, and a strict posture denies it. So where `gh` is granted, `gh pr list | sh` and `gh pr list ; python3 x.py` are no longer allowed unasked. **A pipe into a filter is deferred too,** such as `gh pr list | jq .` or `git log | head`, since no shipped profile grants a filter, so the harness may ask about it. A compound whose every segment is granted, such as `git status && gh pr list`, is allowed as before. The reason the core gives names the program of each segment no grant covers.
- The permission decision core no longer allows a Bash command that holds a construct its splitter cannot trust, anywhere in the command (ADR-025 point 2). Those are a quote, a backtick, a `<` / `>` redirection and a `(`, which opens `$(…)`, `<(…)`, zsh's `=(…)` and zsh's glob qualifiers. It used to look for them only after a leading `cd`. The core now defers such a command to the harness, a strict posture denies it, and a deny still wins. It also splits a command at a newline and a background `&`, and keeps a segment that only assigns, such as `X=$(./x.sh)`, as one that needs a grant. So where `gh` is granted, `gh pr list > ~/.bashrc`, `gh pr list $(python3 x.py)` and `gh pr list & python3 x.py` are no longer allowed unasked. **A quoted argument is deferred too,** such as `git commit -m "fix: x"`, so the harness's own allow list decides it. Claude Code's adapter baseline allows `git`, `gh` and `pkit` by prefix, so those still run unasked there, while a quoted `docker` command is now asked about.
- The permission decision core reads a Bash command's words as the shell does before it matches a grant or a deny (ADR-025 point 2). It removes quotes and backslashes by the standard library's POSIX quoting rules, so `git push origin main "--force"` and `git push origin main \--force` are denied as `--force` is, and a quoted argument with no other construct, such as `git commit -m "fix: x"`, is allowed where a grant covers its segment. It never allows a command whose words, expansions or program it cannot be sure of: a `$`, which opens every expansion, a quote that does not close, whitespace other than a space, a tab or a newline, a comment, a brace expansion, a leading assignment that changes which program runs, such as `PATH=/tmp/x gh pr list`, or one written with a quote or a backslash. A segment led by `export` runs no program, so `export git PATH=/tmp/x` no longer passes as `git`, and a `cd` parted from its path by a newline is no longer stripped. It defers such a command to the harness, a strict posture denies it, and a deny still wins. So where `git` and `gh` are granted, `gh pr view $N`, `git checkout $BRANCH`, `git log $BASE..HEAD` and `git status # note` are no longer allowed unasked. Claude Code's adapter baseline allows `git`, `gh` and `pkit` by prefix, so a deferred command led by one of them may still run unasked there.
- **claude-code 0.7.0** — The permission hook asks the person where the decision core defers on a Bash command that may hide a form of a guardrail, such as `git push origin --forc[e]` or `HOME=/tmp/x git push origin main`. Claude Code's own rules, such as the baseline's `Bash(git:*)`, would otherwise run such a command unasked. The hook answers `ask`, and its reason names the guardrail and what keeps the core from seeing the form, never the command's text. A strict posture still denies such a command, a project's own deny that the core cannot tell still defers, and a fault in the check, or a backbone without it, keeps the deferral. Requires backbone >=2.0.0.
